# The plain tone. Cliff's notes: what happens, in order, no jokes.
#
# Format, tab-separated:   BOOK CH:FIRST-LAST<TAB>summary
#
# Sister file to summaries.txt (dry) and summaries-cheeky.txt (cheeky). All
# three are validated against the same day splits by ./build-summaries.py, and
# the page lets a reader flip between them. A day present in one file and not
# another renders whichever exists, labelled.
#
# This is the only tone with no attitude at all. Someone who missed a day and
# wants to know what they missed reads this one. State the events, the people
# and the outcome, in the order the passage gives them. No wry framing, no
# asides, no editorial. Being dull is allowed. Being wrong is not.
GEN 1:1-31	God creates the heavens and the earth, which begin formless and dark. Over six days he makes light and separates it from darkness; the sky; dry land and seas; vegetation; the sun, moon and stars; sea creatures and birds; land animals; and finally humans, male and female, in his own image, giving them authority over the other creatures. Plants are given to both humans and animals for food. At the end of the sixth day God reviews everything he has made and calls it very good.
GEN 2:1-25	God finishes his work on the seventh day, rests, and makes that day holy. God forms a man from the dust, breathes life into him, and plants a garden in Eden, from which four rivers divide. The man may eat from any tree except the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, on pain of death. It is not good for him to be alone, but none of the animals he names will do. God takes a rib from the sleeping man and forms a woman.
GEN 3:1-24	The serpent tells the woman the forbidden tree will not kill her but make her like God. She eats and shares with her husband. They realise they are naked and sew fig leaves. God finds them hiding; the man blames the woman, the woman blames the serpent. God curses the serpent, tells the woman she will bear children in pain and be ruled by her husband, and curses the ground. He clothes them in skins and drives them out, cherubim guarding the tree of life.
1CH 1:1-54	The book opens with nine generations listed as bare names, Adam to Noah, then the descendants of Japheth, Ham and Shem. Nimrod gets one line as a mighty man. The line narrows through Eber, whose son Peleg is named for the earth being divided, to Abraham. Then come Ishmael's twelve sons, the sons of Keturah, Isaac, and Esau's descendants with the clans of Seir. It closes with eight kings who reigned in Edom before Israel had any king, and eleven Edomite chiefs.
1CH 2:1-55	The twelve sons of Israel are listed, then Judah's line in detail. Er is killed by Yahweh for wickedness, Tamar bears Perez and Zerah, and Achar is named the troubler of Israel. Perez's line runs through Nahshon and Boaz to Jesse, whose sons are given with David seventh. Zeruiah's sons Abishai, Joab and Asahel follow. Hezron fathers a son at sixty; Sheshan, having no sons, marries his daughter to his Egyptian servant Jarha. Caleb's families close the chapter with the scribes of Jabez.
1CH 3:1-24	David's six sons born in Hebron are listed with their mothers, followed by the four sons of Bathshua including Solomon, and nine more born in Jerusalem, with Tamar named as their sister. He reigned seven and a half years in Hebron and thirty-three in Jerusalem. The kings of Judah follow in order from Solomon to Josiah, then Josiah's four sons, Jeconiah the captive, Shealtiel and Zerubbabel. The list keeps going for six more generations past the exile, ending with Anani.
1CH 4:1-43	More families of Judah, including the potters and linen workers who lived with the king for his work. Jabez, named for the sorrow of his birth, prays for blessing and enlarged borders and God grants it. Simeon's line follows: Shimei has sixteen sons and six daughters, but the tribe as a whole did not multiply like Judah. Their towns are listed as theirs until David's reign. In Hezekiah's day they seize good pasture at Gedor, and five hundred men take Mount Seir from the surviving Amalekites.
1CH 5:1-26	Reuben was the firstborn, but his birthright went to Joseph's sons after he defiled his father's couch, while the ruling prince came from Judah. The Reubenites, Gadites and half-tribe of Manasseh east of the Jordan are listed with their territories. Their forty-four thousand seven hundred sixty fighting men beat the Hagrites and take fifty thousand camels, two hundred fifty thousand sheep, two thousand donkeys and a hundred thousand captives. Later they worship the local gods, and God stirs up two Assyrian kings to deport them.
1CH 6:1-48	Levi's three sons head the chapter, then the high-priestly line from Aaron through Zadok down to Jehozadak, who went into captivity when Nebuchadnezzar carried Judah away. The families of Gershom, Kohath and Merari follow, including Samuel and his two sons. Then come the men David put over the music of Yahweh's house once the ark had a resting place: Heman the singer, Asaph on his right, Ethan on his left, each traced back generation by generation to Levi.
1CH 6:49-81	Aaron and his sons are the ones who offer on the altars and make atonement, as Moses commanded. His line is given again, Eleazar to Ahimaaz. Then the Levites' dwelling places: Hebron and twelve other towns to Aaron's sons, with the surrounding fields given to Caleb instead. Kohath's remaining families get ten cities, Gershom thirteen, Merari twelve, all assigned by lot from the other tribes. The rest of the chapter names each town with its pasture lands, cities of refuge included.
1CH 7:1-40	Issachar, Benjamin and Naphtali are listed with their fighting strength, Naphtali in a single verse. Manasseh's line includes Zelophehad, who had daughters. Ephraim's sons Ezer and Elead are killed by men of Gath while raiding livestock; Ephraim mourns for days, then fathers Beriah, named for the trouble in his house. His daughter Sheerah builds Upper and Lower Beth Horon and Uzzen Sheerah. The line runs on to Nun and Joshua. Asher closes with twenty-six thousand men fit for war.
1CH 8:1-40	Benjamin's descendants are given again, at greater length. Shaharaim fathers children in Moab after sending away his wives. Elpaal's sons build Ono and Lod, and Beriah and Shema drive out the inhabitants of Gath. Long strings of names follow, heads of households who lived in Jerusalem. The chapter ends with Saul's family: Ner fathered Kish, Kish fathered Saul, and Saul fathered Jonathan, Malchishua, Abinadab and Eshbaal, with Jonathan's line traced nine generations further to Azel and his six sons.
1CH 9:1-20	All Israel had been recorded by genealogy, and Judah went to Babylon for disobedience. The first to resettle their own towns were Israelites, priests, Levites and temple servants. Those living in Jerusalem are counted by family: six hundred ninety of Zerah's line, nine hundred fifty-six Benjaminites, and a thousand seven hundred sixty priests described as very able men for the service of God's house. Levites and gatekeepers follow, Shallum chief among them, with Phinehas named as their ruler in the past.
1CH 9:21-44	Two hundred twelve gatekeepers were appointed, ordained by David and Samuel the seer, posted on all four sides and relieved every seven days by their brothers from the villages. Four chief gatekeepers held the storerooms and treasuries and opened the house every morning. Others counted the vessels in and out, handled the flour, wine, oil and spices, baked in pans, or prepared the showbread each Sabbath. The singers lived in the rooms and were excused all other duty. The chapter closes by repeating Saul's genealogy.
1KI 1:1-27	David is old and cannot keep warm, so Abishag the Shunammite is brought to attend him. Adonijah declares himself king, acquires chariots, horsemen and fifty runners, and holds a feast at the stone of Zoheleth with Joab and Abiathar behind him. He invites his brothers and the men of Judah, but not Nathan, Benaiah or Solomon. Nathan warns Bathsheba that her life and Solomon's are at stake and sends her to remind David of his oath. Nathan follows and asks whether the king has named Adonijah.
1KI 1:28-53	David swears again that Solomon will reign. Zadok, Nathan and Benaiah set him on the king's own mule, take him to Gihon, anoint him and sound the trumpet. The people come up piping until the earth shakes. Adonijah's guests hear the noise as they eat, and Jonathan brings the report. They scatter. Adonijah takes hold of the horns of the altar and asks not to be killed. Solomon promises that if he proves worthy not a hair will fall, and sends him home.
1KI 2:1-23	Dying, David charges Solomon to keep the law of Moses. Then the instructions: Joab, who killed Abner and Amasa, must not go to the grave in peace; Barzillai's sons are to eat at the king's table; and Shimei is not to be held guiltless. David dies after forty years, seven in Hebron and thirty-three in Jerusalem. Adonijah asks Bathsheba to request Abishag as his wife. She takes it to Solomon, who says she may as well ask the kingdom for him.
1KI 2:24-46	Solomon has Benaiah kill Adonijah that day. Abiathar is spared for carrying the ark but banished to Anathoth, fulfilling the word spoken against the house of Eli. Joab flees to the Tent and takes hold of the altar, so Benaiah strikes him there for the blood of Abner and Amasa. Benaiah is given the army and Zadok the priesthood. Shimei is confined to Jerusalem on pain of death; three years later he goes to Gath after two runaway slaves, and Solomon has him killed for breaking the oath.
1KI 3:1-28	Solomon marries Pharaoh's daughter. The people still sacrifice at high places because no temple exists, and Solomon offers a thousand burnt offerings at Gibeon. God appears in a dream and invites him to ask for anything. Calling himself a little child, Solomon asks for an understanding heart. God grants wisdom, riches and honour. Two prostitutes then dispute over a living and a dead baby. Solomon orders the child divided; the real mother gives it up, and he awards it to her.
1KI 4:1-34	Solomon's officials are listed: Azariah the priest, two scribes, Jehoshaphat as recorder, Benaiah over the army, and Adoniram over forced labour. Twelve district officers each supply the court for one month. Solomon rules from the Euphrates to Egypt. Daily provisions run to thirty cors of fine flour, ten fatted cattle and a hundred sheep, with forty thousand stalls of chariot horses. He speaks three thousand proverbs and a thousand and five songs, and foreign kings send men to hear him.
1KI 5:1-18	Hiram of Tyre, who had always loved David, sends greetings. Solomon replies that his father could not build the temple because of constant war, and asks for cedar cut in Lebanon, since nobody cuts timber like the Sidonians. Hiram agrees to float cedar and cypress by sea as rafts in exchange for food. Solomon sends twenty thousand cors of wheat and twenty of pure oil yearly, and the two make a treaty. He raises thirty thousand men in monthly shifts under Adoniram, plus carriers and stonecutters.
1KI 6:1-18	In the four hundred and eightieth year after the exodus, Solomon begins the temple. It is sixty cubits long, twenty wide and thirty high, with a porch, latticed windows, and three storeys of side rooms resting on offsets so no beams pierce the walls. The stone is dressed at the quarry, so no iron tool is heard at the site. Yahweh promises that if Solomon keeps the commandments the word given to David will stand. The interior is lined with carved cedar, no stone visible.
1KI 6:19-38	The inner sanctuary is prepared for the ark, twenty cubits each way, overlaid with pure gold, with gold chains drawn across in front of it. Two cherubim of olive wood, ten cubits high, stand inside with wings outstretched so that one wing touches each wall and the inner wings meet. The walls are carved with cherubim, palm trees and open flowers, and the floor is gold. The doors are olive wood and cypress, carved and gilded. Begun in Ziv and finished in Bul, it took seven years.
1KI 7:1-26	Solomon spends thirteen years on his own buildings: the House of the Forest of Lebanon on rows of cedar pillars, a porch of judgment, his residence, and a house for Pharaoh's daughter. He then brings Hiram of Tyre, son of a widow of Naphtali and a bronze worker filled with skill. Hiram casts two bronze pillars eighteen cubits high, with capitals of network, chainwork, lilies and two hundred pomegranates each, named Jachin and Boaz. He also casts the round bronze sea on twelve oxen, holding two thousand baths.
1KI 7:27-51	Hiram makes ten bronze stands, panelled with lions, oxen, cherubim and palm trees, each on four bronze wheels. On each sits a basin holding forty baths, five stands to a side of the house, with the sea to the south-east. He finishes the bronze work, cast in the clay ground of the Jordan plain between Succoth and Zarethan, and Solomon leaves it unweighed. The gold work follows: the altar, the showbread table, ten lampstands, tongs, cups, spoons, fire pans and the door hinges.
1KI 8:1-24	Solomon assembles the elders and tribal heads in the seventh month to bring the ark up from Zion. The priests carry the ark, the Tent of Meeting and the holy vessels, and set the ark under the wings of the cherubim, its poles visible from the holy place. It holds nothing but the two stone tablets from Horeb. A cloud fills the house so the priests cannot minister. Solomon says Yahweh had spoken of dwelling in thick darkness.
1KI 8:25-44	Solomon asks that the promise of a man on David's throne be kept, then admits heaven itself cannot contain God, much less this house, and asks only that God's eyes be open toward the place where his name is. He works through cases: a man swearing an oath at the altar, defeat, drought, famine, plague, blight, locust and siege, asking each time that God hear and forgive.
1KI 8:45-66	Solomon's last petitions cover captivity: since no one is without sin, if they are carried off and repent, praying toward this land and this house, he asks God to hear, forgive, and move their captors to compassion. The peace offerings come to twenty-two thousand cattle and a hundred and twenty thousand sheep, the bronze altar being too small. The feast lasts fourteen days, and he sends the people home glad.
1KI 9:1-28	Yahweh appears to Solomon a second time, having heard his prayer, but warns that if he or his children turn to other gods, Israel will be cut off and the house cast away. Hiram, given twenty cities in Galilee, is unimpressed, calls them the land of Cabul, and sends a hundred and twenty talents of gold. The forced levy built the temple. Solomon conscripts the surviving Canaanites but not Israelites. Hiram's sailors bring four hundred and twenty talents of gold from Ophir.
1KI 10:1-29	The queen of Sheba comes to test Solomon with hard questions, bringing camels loaded with spices, gold and jewels. He answers everything, and when she has seen his wisdom, his house, his table and his officials, there is no spirit left in her. Solomon's annual gold income is six hundred and sixty-six talents; he makes gold shields and an ivory throne with twelve lions, silver being reckoned worthless. He keeps fourteen hundred chariots, trading horses out of Egypt.
1KI 11:1-23	Solomon loves many foreign women from the nations Yahweh had warned about, seven hundred wives and three hundred concubines, and in old age they turn his heart to Ashtoreth, Milcom, Chemosh and Molech. Yahweh, angry because he had appeared to Solomon twice, says he will tear the kingdom away and give it to a servant, though not in Solomon's lifetime, leaving one tribe for David's sake. Adversaries are raised up: Hadad the Edomite, who escaped Joab's slaughter as a child and grew up in Pharaoh's house.
1KI 11:24-43	Rezon takes Damascus and reigns over Syria, hostile to Israel all Solomon's days. Jeroboam son of Nebat, whom Solomon had put over the forced labour of the house of Joseph, also rebels. Ahijah the Shilonite meets him alone in a field, tears his new garment into twelve pieces, and tells him to take ten: Yahweh will give him ten tribes, leaving one so David may have a lamp in Jerusalem. Solomon tries to kill him, and he flees to Egypt until Solomon dies.
1KI 12:1-17	The assembly asks him to lighten the heavy yoke his father laid on them, and they will serve him. He sends them away three days. The elders who served Solomon advise him to answer kindly. He rejects that and asks the young men he grew up with, who tell him to say his little finger is thicker than his father's waist, and that where his father used whips he will use scorpions. He answers roughly, and Israel goes home.
1KI 12:18-33	Rehoboam sends Adoram and Israel stones him to death, while the king flees to Jerusalem by chariot. Israel makes Jeroboam king, and only Judah follows David's house. Rehoboam musters a hundred and eighty thousand men, but Shemaiah the man of God tells them not to fight their brothers, and they go home. Jeroboam, fearing the people will return to Rehoboam if they sacrifice in Jerusalem, makes two golden calves for Bethel and Dan, appoints priests who are not Levites, and invents a feast in the eighth month.
1KI 13:1-17	A man of God comes from Judah to Bethel while Jeroboam is burning incense, and cries out against the altar that a son named Josiah will be born to David's house who will sacrifice its priests on it. The sign is that the altar will split. Jeroboam points at him and orders his arrest, and his hand withers; the altar splits. Invited home for a reward, he refuses, having been commanded to eat no bread, drink no water and not return by the way he came.
1KI 13:18-34	An old prophet claims an angel told him to bring the man of God home to eat, which is a lie, and the man of God goes back and eats. At the table Yahweh's word comes to the old prophet, who announces that because his guest disobeyed, his body will not reach his fathers' tomb. On the road a lion kills him and stands beside the body with the donkey, eating neither. Jeroboam does not turn from his way.
1KI 14:1-14	When Jeroboam's son Abijah falls ill, Jeroboam tells his wife to disguise herself and ask the prophet Ahijah at Shiloh what will happen to the boy. God raised Jeroboam up and tore the kingdom from David's house for him, yet he has made molten images and cast God behind his back; his whole house will be swept away like dung. Her son will die as she reaches the city, mourned because some good was found in him.
1KI 14:15-31	Yahweh will shake Israel like a reed and scatter it beyond the River because of its Asherah poles and Jeroboam's sins. The child dies as his mother crosses the threshold, and all Israel mourns. In Judah, Rehoboam reigns seventeen years, his mother an Ammonite, and Judah builds high places and pillars on every high hill. In his fifth year Shishak of Egypt takes the treasures of the temple and palace, including Solomon's gold shields, which Rehoboam replaces with bronze ones carried by the guard.
1KI 15:1-19	removes the cult prostitutes and the idols his fathers made, deposes his mother Maacah for making an Asherah image, and burns it at the Kidron, though the high places remain. When Baasha of Israel fortifies Ramah to blockade Judah, Asa sends the temple and palace treasure to Ben Hadad of Syria to break his treaty with Baasha.
1KI 15:20-34	Ben Hadad agrees, striking Ijon, Dan, Abel Beth Maacah and all Naphtali, so Baasha abandons Ramah. Asa conscripts all Judah, nobody exempt, to carry off Ramah's stone and timber and build Geba and Mizpah. In Israel, Nadab son of Jeroboam reigns two years until Baasha of Issachar kills him at the siege of Gibbethon, then destroys every last member of Jeroboam's house, as Ahijah had said. Baasha reigns twenty-four years in Tirzah, walking in Jeroboam's way himself.
1KI 16:1-17	Yahweh's word comes through Jehu son of Hanani against Baasha: raised out of the dust and made prince, he walked in Jeroboam's way, so his house will be swept away like Jeroboam's. Zimri, captain of half his chariots, kills him while he is getting drunk in Arza's house, then wipes out the whole house of Baasha, fulfilling Jehu's word. Zimri reigns seven days. The army at Gibbethon hears of the conspiracy, makes its commander Omri king, and marches to besiege Tirzah.
1KI 16:18-34	Seeing the city taken, Zimri burns the palace down over himself. Omri reigns twelve years, six in Tirzah, then buys the hill of Samaria from Shemer for two talents of silver and builds a city there. He does evil, worse than any before him. Ahab his son reigns twenty-two years and does worse still: he marries Jezebel daughter of Ethbaal, serves Baal, and builds him a temple in Samaria. In his time Hiel of Bethel rebuilds Jericho, losing two sons, as Joshua had said.
1KI 17:1-24	Elijah the Tishbite tells Ahab there will be no dew or rain except by his word, then hides by the brook Cherith, where ravens bring him bread and meat until the brook dries up. Sent to Zarephath in Sidon, he meets a widow with a handful of meal and a little oil, preparing a last meal for herself and her son. He tells her to bake for him first, and the meal and oil do not run out. Elijah prays, and the child revives.
1KI 18:1-24	In the third year Yahweh sends Elijah to show himself to Ahab, promising rain. Elijah meets Obadiah, who is afraid to announce him in case Elijah vanishes and Ahab kills the messenger. Ahab greets Elijah as the troubler of Israel; Elijah replies that Ahab's house are the troublers, and calls for all Israel and four hundred and fifty prophets of Baal at Carmel. He proposes two bulls and no fire: the god who answers by fire is God.
1KI 18:25-46	Baal's prophets call from morning to noon and leap around the altar with no answer. Elijah suggests their god may be thinking, travelling or asleep. Elijah repairs Yahweh's altar with twelve stones, digs a trench, and has twelve jars of water poured over the wood and the bull. At his prayer fire falls and consumes the offering, the stones, the dust and the water. Elijah has the prophets of Baal killed at the Kishon, then runs ahead of Ahab's chariot to Jezreel.
1MA 1:1-29	Alexander the Macedonian beats Darius, conquers to the ends of the earth, and divides his kingdom among his officers before he dies twelve years later. His successors crown themselves and multiply evils. Antiochus Epiphanes takes the throne. Some in Israel make a covenant with the Gentiles and build a gymnasium in Jerusalem, undoing their circumcision. Antiochus defeats Ptolemy in Egypt, then strips the temple of its golden altar, lampstand, table and vessels. Israel mourns. Two years later he sends a tribute collector with a large force.
1MA 1:30-64	The collector speaks peaceably, then attacks the city, kills many, burns and plunders it, and fortifies the city of David with renegades. Jerusalem empties, her feasts turned to mourning. Antiochus decrees one people, one law: no offerings, Sabbaths profaned, swine sacrificed, sons left uncircumcised, all on pain of death. On the fifteenth of Chislev, an abomination of desolation is built on the altar. The law is torn up and burned, mothers who circumcised their sons are executed with them, and many choose death over unclean food.
1MA 2:1-34	Mattathias, a priest, leaves Jerusalem for Modin with five sons, among them Judas Maccabaeus. Seeing the desecration, he mourns. The king's officers offer him silver, gold and the rank of the king's Friend if he sacrifices first. He refuses for himself and his house. When a Jew steps up to the altar, Mattathias kills him and the officer, pulls down the altar, and calls everyone zealous for the law to follow him into the mountains. Others flee to the wilderness, where troops corner them on the Sabbath.
1MA 2:35-70	The refugees will not fight or block the entrances, and about a thousand die with their wives, children and cattle. Mattathias and his friends decide they will fight if attacked on the Sabbath rather than be wiped out. Hasidaeans join them; they tear down altars and circumcise uncircumcised children by force. Dying, Mattathias tells his sons to be zealous and recites the examples of Abraham, Joseph, Phinehas and David among others. He names Simon as counselor and Judas as commander, blesses them, and dies, buried at Modin.
1MA 3:1-31	Judas Maccabaeus succeeds his father, a lion roaring for prey. He kills Apollonius in battle and keeps his sword for life. Seron of Syria comes looking to make a name, and Judas meets him at the pass of Bethhoron with a small hungry company. Judas says strength comes from heaven, not numbers, and routs Seron, leaving about eight hundred dead. Antiochus, furious, musters his forces and pays them a year in advance, then finds his treasury short and heads for Persia to collect tribute.
1MA 3:32-60	Antiochus leaves Lysias in charge from the Euphrates to Egypt with half the army and the elephants, ordered to destroy Israel and settle strangers on the land. Lysias sends Ptolemy, Nicanor and Gorgias with forty thousand foot and seven thousand horse to Emmaus, and slave dealers arrive with silver and fetters to buy Jewish captives. Judas gathers the people at Mizpeh to fast and pray, appoints captains, and sends home the newly betrothed, the builders, the planters and the fearful as the law allows.
1MA 4:1-33	Gorgias takes five thousand foot and a thousand horse to fall on Judas by night, guided by men from the citadel. Judas slips past him and hits the camp at Emmaus instead, so Gorgias finds nobody. At dawn Judas appears with three thousand poorly armed men, reminds them of the Red Sea, and routs the enemy, killing about three thousand. He forbids looting until Gorgias sees the smoke and flees. The next year Lysias returns with sixty thousand foot to Bethsura, and Judas meets him with ten thousand.
1MA 4:34-61	About five thousand of Lysias's men fall, and Lysias withdraws to Antioch to hire more. Judas goes up to Mount Zion and finds the sanctuary desolate, gates burned. Priests cleanse the place and pull down the profaned altar, storing its stones until a prophet can rule on them. New altar, new vessels, and on the twenty-fifth of Chislev, the day it was profaned, rededicate it with songs and cymbals. The feast is made annual, and they fortify Mount Zion and Bethsura.
1MA 5:1-23	The surrounding nations, angry the altar has been rebuilt, begin killing the Jews living among them. Judas defeats Esau's children, the sons of Baean, and Timotheus and the Ammonites. Letters arrive from Jews besieged at Dathema in Gilead, and messengers come from Galilee with clothes torn. Judas sends Simon with three thousand to Galilee and takes eight thousand to Gilead, leaving Joseph and Azarias to hold Judea with orders not to fight. Simon drives the enemy to Ptolemais and brings the Galilean Jews home.
1MA 5:24-44	Judas and Jonathan march three days into the wilderness, where friendly Nabathaeans warn them Jews are shut up in several towns, due to be killed the next day. Judas takes Bosora, marches all night, and reaches another stronghold as it is being stormed. He hits the besiegers from behind in three companies with trumpets, and about eight thousand of Timotheus's men fall. More cities are taken. Timotheus regroups at Raphon; Judas crosses the brook first, and the enemy flee to the temple at Carnaim, which is burned.
1MA 5:45-68	Judas gathers every Jew in Gilead, with wives, children and goods for Judah. Ephron shuts its gates against his offer of peaceful passage, so he storms it and razes it. They cross the Jordan and reach Mount Zion without losing a man. Joseph and Azarias, wanting a name of their own, disobey orders and attack Jamnia; Gorgias routs them and about two thousand fall, for they were not of the family through whom deliverance came. Judas strikes Hebron and Azotus, burns the idols, and goes home.
1SA 1:1-28	Elkanah has two wives: Peninnah, who has children, and Hannah, who has none and is provoked every year at the Shiloh sacrifice. Praying silently, Hannah vows a son to Yahweh with no razor touching his head, and Eli takes her for drunk before blessing her. She bears Samuel, and once he is weaned she gives him to Yahweh for life.
1SA 2:1-20	Hannah sings that the bows of the mighty break, the full hire themselves out for bread, and Yahweh raises the poor to sit with princes. Eli's sons send a servant to take meat with a three-pronged fork before the fat is burned, by force if refused. Samuel ministers in a linen ephod, in a new robe from his mother each year.
1SA 2:21-36	Eli hears that his sons sleep with the women serving at the Tent of Meeting and rebukes them, but they do not listen. A man of God tells him the promise that his house would serve forever is revoked: no old man will be left in it, and Hophni and Phinehas will die on one day as the sign. A faithful priest will replace them.
1SA 3:1-21	Samuel serves Eli at a time when Yahweh's word is rare. One night Yahweh calls him three times; he runs to Eli, who tells him to answer that his servant is listening. Yahweh says he will carry out everything he said against Eli's house, because Eli knew what his sons were doing and did not restrain them.
1SA 4:1-22	Israel loses about four thousand men to the Philistines, so the elders send to Shiloh for the ark. Israel is routed anyway: thirty thousand fall, the ark is captured, and both of Eli's sons are killed. Eli, ninety-eight and blind, hears the ark is taken, falls backward and breaks his neck, and Phinehas' wife dies in labour naming the child Ichabod.
1SA 5:1-12	The Philistines take the ark to Ashdod and set it beside Dagon. The next morning Dagon has fallen face down before it; set up again, he falls again with his head and both hands broken off on the threshold. Tumors follow, so the ark is sent to Gath, then to Ekron, with the same result, until the lords agree to send it home.
1SA 6:1-21	After seven months the priests advise returning the ark with five golden tumors and five golden mice. They put it on a new cart drawn by two milk cows never yoked, with the calves shut up at home, and the cows go straight to Beth Shemesh. Men there are struck for looking into the ark, so the town sends for Kiriath Jearim.
1SA 7:1-17	The ark goes to Abinadab's house at Kiriath Jearim, where Eleazar keeps it for twenty years. Samuel tells Israel to put away the foreign gods, and they gather at Mizpah to fast and confess. When the Philistines march up, Yahweh thunders and throws them into confusion, and Samuel sets up a stone called Ebenezer.
1SA 8:1-22	Samuel makes his sons judges at Beersheba, but they take bribes, so the elders ask for a king. Yahweh tells Samuel it is God they have rejected, not him, but to warn them plainly. Samuel lists it: their sons for his armies, their daughters as cooks, the best of their fields, a tenth of their flocks, and no answer when they cry out.
1SA 9:1-13	Kish, a Benjamite, has a son named Saul, taller than anyone in Israel from the shoulders up. When Kish's donkeys go missing, Saul and a servant search four districts without finding them. Saul wants to turn back before his father starts worrying about him instead, but the servant knows of a man of God in the city and has a quarter shekel to give him.
1SA 9:14-27	Samuel comes out to meet them, having been told the day before that a man from Benjamin would arrive to be anointed prince over Israel. Saul, not recognising him, asks where the seer lives. Samuel says he is the seer, says the donkeys are found, and hints that everything desirable in Israel is his; Saul protests that he is from the smallest tribe.
1SA 10:1-27	Samuel anoints Saul with three signs: two men at Rachel's tomb reporting the donkeys found, three men at the oak of Tabor giving him two loaves, and prophets among whom the Spirit will come on him. Those who know him ask whether Saul is also among the prophets. At Mizpah the lot falls to him, and he is found hiding in the baggage.
1SA 11:1-15	Nahash the Ammonite besieges Jabesh Gilead, offering terms only if every right eye is gouged out. Saul hears of it behind his oxen, the Spirit comes on him, and he cuts a yoke of oxen in pieces and sends them through Israel. Three hundred and thirty thousand come; he scatters the Ammonites and then refuses to execute the men who had doubted him.
1SA 12:1-25	Samuel tells Israel he has given them the king they asked for and challenges anyone to name an ox or a bribe he ever took; they clear him. He reviews the history from Egypt to their demand for a king when Nahash came, though Yahweh was already king. He calls for thunder and rain in wheat harvest, and gets it.
1SA 13:1-23	Jonathan attacks the Philistine garrison at Geba. The Philistines muster thirty thousand chariots, and Israel hides in caves, thickets, rocks, tombs and pits. Saul waits the seven days Samuel set, and as his men scatter he offers the burnt offering himself; Samuel arrives and tells him his kingdom will not continue. There is no blacksmith in Israel, so only Saul and Jonathan have a sword.
1SA 14:1-26	Without telling his father, Jonathan and his armour bearer cross toward the Philistine garrison between the crags Bozez and Seneh, agreeing that an invitation to come up is the sign. The garrison calls them up, and the two kill about twenty men in a small space. Panic spreads through the camp, and Saul binds the people by oath to eat nothing until evening.
1SA 14:27-52	Jonathan, who never heard the oath, dips his staff in honeycomb; told of it, he says his father has troubled the land. The starving people slaughter plunder and eat it, so Saul rolls up a great stone and builds his first altar. God does not answer about a night pursuit, so Saul casts lots; the lot falls on Jonathan, and the people rescue him.
1SA 15:1-35	Samuel sends Saul to destroy Amalek completely. Saul strikes Amalek but keeps Agag alive along with the best livestock, destroying only what was worthless. Asked about the bleating, he blames the people and says the animals were for sacrifice. Samuel answers that obedience is better than sacrifice and that Yahweh has rejected him as king, then kills Agag at Gilgal.
1SA 16:1-23	Yahweh sends Samuel to Jesse of Bethlehem, one of whose sons is the new king. Samuel assumes Eliab is the one and is told that people look at appearance while Yahweh looks at the heart. Seven sons pass and none is chosen, so the youngest is brought in from the sheep and anointed. The Spirit leaves Saul, and his servants find a harpist: David.
1SA 17:1-31	In the valley of Elah, Goliath of Gath, six cubits and a span tall, in bronze armour and with a spear like a weaver's beam, offers single combat and defies Israel for forty days. David, sent with grain and cheeses for his brothers, hears the challenge and asks what the man who kills the Philistine will get. Eliab accuses him of pride.
1SA 17:32-58	Saul objects that he is only a boy, so David recounts killing a lion and a bear that took lambs from his flock. Saul dresses him in armour, which David takes off because he has not tested it, and takes his staff, five smooth stones and his sling. Goliath despises him and curses him by his gods. David answers that Yahweh does not save with sword and spear, slings a stone into his forehead, then takes Goliath's sword and cuts off his head. The Philistines flee to Ekron.
1SA 18:1-30	Jonathan binds himself to David in a covenant and gives him his robe, sword, bow and sash. But the women sing that Saul killed thousands and David ten thousands, and Saul wonders what is left for David but the kingdom. Twice he throws his spear at David while David plays the harp. He offers Merab, then marries her elsewhere, then offers Michal for a dowry of a hundred Philistine foreskins, hoping the Philistines will kill him. David brings two hundred, and Saul becomes his permanent enemy.
1SA 19:1-24	Saul tells Jonathan and his servants to kill David. Then the evil spirit comes and Saul throws his spear while David plays, striking the wall. Saul posts men to kill him in the morning, but Michal lets him down through a window and puts a household idol in the bed, saying he is sick. David reaches Samuel at Ramah. Three sets of messengers sent to seize him prophesy instead, and so does Saul, who lies stripped a day and a night.
1SA 20:1-20	David comes to Jonathan asking what he has done to deserve being hunted. Jonathan insists his father tells him everything, but David swears there is only a step between him and death. They agree on a test: David will miss the new moon meal and hide in the field, and Jonathan will say he asked leave for the family sacrifice at Bethlehem. If Saul is angry, the intent is settled. He will signal with three arrows near the stone Ezel.
1SA 20:21-42	Jonathan explains the code: arrows on this side mean safe, arrows beyond mean flee. At the feast David's seat is empty and Saul assumes ritual uncleanness. On the second day he asks where the son of Jesse is, hears the Bethlehem story, and erupts: he insults Jonathan's mother, says Jonathan will never be established while David lives, and orders David killed. Asked what David has done, Saul throws his spear at his son. Next morning Jonathan shoots beyond the boy, calls the words out, and the two weep and part.
1SA 21:1-15	David comes alone to Ahimelech the priest at Nob, who meets him trembling. David says the king sent him on secret business and asks for bread. The only bread is the holy showbread, which Ahimelech gives once assured David's men have kept from women. Doeg the Edomite is there that day. David also asks for a weapon and is given Goliath's sword. He then flees to Achish of Gath, whose servants recognise him from the song, so he feigns madness, drooling into his beard until Achish dismisses him.
1SA 22:1-23	David escapes to the cave of Adullam, where his family joins him and about four hundred men in distress, debt or discontent make him their captain. He settles his parents with the king of Moab. Saul accuses his Benjamites of conspiring with his son. Doeg reports that Ahimelech gave David bread, an oracle and Goliath's sword. Ahimelech protests his ignorance, and Saul condemns him. The guards refuse to kill priests, so Doeg does it: eighty-five men, then all of Nob, people and livestock. Only Abiathar escapes.
1SA 23:1-29	Hearing the Philistines are raiding Keilah, David asks Yahweh twice and is told to go; he saves the town. Saul, told David is shut inside a walled town, musters an army, so David asks through the ephod whether Saul will come and whether Keilah will hand him over. Both answers are yes. He and his six hundred leave. Jonathan comes to him in the woods and says David will be king. The Ziphites offer to betray him, and Saul is closing in when a Philistine raid calls him away.
1SA 24:1-22	At En Gedi, Saul takes three thousand men and goes into a cave to relieve himself, not knowing David and his men are at the back of it. David cuts off the edge of Saul's robe instead, then regrets even that, refusing to raise his hand against Yahweh's anointed. When Saul leaves, David calls after him and shows the robe as proof he could have killed him, asking why the king is chasing a dead dog. Saul weeps, admits David will be king, and asks him to spare his descendants.
1SA 25:1-23	Samuel dies and Israel mourns him at Ramah. Nabal, a rich man shearing three thousand sheep at Carmel, is surly and harsh, while his wife Abigail is intelligent and beautiful. David sends ten men with a courteous greeting, noting that his men protected Nabal's shepherds, and asks for whatever he can spare. Nabal asks who David is and refuses. David straps on his sword and takes four hundred men. A servant warns Abigail, who loads donkeys with bread, wine, sheep, grain and fruit and rides out without telling her husband.
1SA 25:24-44	Abigail takes the blame herself, notes that Nabal's name means folly, and argues that Yahweh has kept David from bloodguilt he would regret as king. She asks only to be remembered. David blesses her, admits she has saved him from killing every male there, and sends her home. Nabal is drunk at a feast, so she waits until morning; when she tells him his heart fails, and ten days later Yahweh strikes him dead. David marries her, and Ahinoam. Saul has given Michal to Palti.
1SA 26:1-25	The Ziphites betray David again and Saul comes to Ziph with three thousand men. David and Abishai walk into the camp at night, where Saul lies asleep, a deep sleep from Yahweh on everyone. Abishai asks to pin him; David refuses to touch Yahweh's anointed and takes his spear and water jar. From a hilltop he mocks Abner for failing to guard the king. Saul recognises his voice, admits he has played the fool and promises no more harm, and they part.
1SA 27:1-12	Concluding that Saul will kill him eventually, David crosses into Philistine territory with six hundred men and settles with Achish of Gath. Saul stops searching. David asks for a country town rather than the royal city, and Achish gives him Ziklag. He stays a year and four months, raiding the Geshurites, Girzites and Amalekites, leaving no survivors and taking their livestock. Asked where he has raided, he names the south of Judah, leaving nobody alive to contradict him. Achish counts him a servant for life.
1SA 28:1-25	The Philistines muster and Achish tells David to march with him. Samuel is dead, and Saul has expelled the mediums. At Shunem, Saul is terrified and gets no answer at all, so he seeks a medium at Endor. Disguised, he asks her to bring up Samuel, swearing she is safe. Samuel appears, she recognises Saul, and cries out. Samuel tells him the kingdom is David's because he spared Amalek, and tomorrow he and his sons will join him. Saul collapses, and the woman feeds him before he leaves.
1SA 29:1-11	The Philistines gather at Aphek, Israel camps at Jezreel, and David marches in the rear with Achish. The Philistine commanders object to the Hebrews being there. Achish defends David as blameless, but they insist, arguing he could turn on them mid-battle and that the cheapest peace with Saul would be their own heads, and they quote the song about his ten thousands. Achish sends him away in peace. David protests, then rises early and returns to Philistine territory.
1SA 30:1-15	David reaches Ziklag on the third day and finds it burned, every wife, son and daughter carried off, though nobody was killed. They weep until they have no strength, and talk of stoning him. Abiathar brings the ephod, and the answer is that he will recover everything. Two hundred of the six hundred are too exhausted to cross the brook Besor. They find an Egyptian slave abandoned when he fell ill, revive him with bread and figs, and he offers to lead them to the raiders.
1SA 30:16-31	The Egyptian brings them to the Amalekites, spread out over their plunder. David attacks from twilight until the following evening; only four hundred on camels escape. He recovers everything, including his two wives. Back at the brook Besor, some of his men want to give the two hundred nothing but their families, and David refuses: the man who stays with the baggage shares equally with the man who fights. He makes it a lasting statute, and sends plunder to the elders of Judah.
1SA 31:1-13	Israel is defeated on Gilboa and the Philistines kill Jonathan, Abinadab and Malchishua. Saul asks his armour bearer to kill him so the Philistines cannot abuse him; the man is too afraid, so Saul falls on his sword, and the bearer does the same. Saul, his sons and all his men die that day. They cut off his head and fasten his body to the wall of Beth Shan. The men of Jabesh Gilead march all night, take the bodies down, burn them and fast seven days.
2SA 1:1-27	Two days after David returns to Ziklag, a man arrives with torn clothes and dirt on his head and reports that Israel has fled and that Saul and Jonathan are dead. He says he found Saul leaning on his spear, finished him at his request, and took the crown and bracelet. David and his men mourn and fast until evening. Learning the man is an Amalekite, David asks why he was not afraid to kill Yahweh's anointed, and has him executed. He then sings the song of the bow.
2SA 2:1-32	David asks Yahweh where to go, is sent to Hebron, and is anointed king over Judah, reigning there seven and a half years. Abner makes Saul's son Ishbosheth king at Mahanaim. At the pool of Gibeon the two set twelve men from each side to fight; all twenty-four kill each other, and a battle follows in which Abner is beaten. Asahel chases Abner, who twice tells him to turn aside and then kills him with the butt of his spear.
2SA 3:1-21	The war between the houses drags on, David growing stronger and Saul's house weaker. Six sons are born to him in Hebron, including Amnon, Absalom and Adonijah. Ishbosheth accuses Abner of sleeping with Saul's concubine, and Abner, furious at being reproached over a woman, swears to transfer the kingdom to David. His one condition is that Michal be returned first. She is taken from Paltiel, who follows her weeping as far as Bahurim.
2SA 3:22-39	Joab returns, learns Abner came and went in peace, and tells David he has been deceived. Without David's knowledge he brings Abner back and kills him in the gateway for the blood of his brother Asahel. David declares himself guiltless and curses Joab's house, orders public mourning, walks behind the bier, and laments that Abner died as a fool dies. He swears to eat nothing until sundown, which convinces all Israel the king had no part in it. He admits the sons of Zeruiah are too hard for him.
2SA 4:1-12	Abner dead, Ishbosheth loses his nerve. Two captains, Rechab and Baanah, enter at midday, kill him on his bed, behead him, and carry the head to David at Hebron, claiming Yahweh has avenged him. David reminds them that he executed the man who reported Saul's death, and asks what he owes men who murdered an innocent man in his own bed. He has them killed and hung by the pool, and buries the head in Abner's grave. The chapter also notes Jonathan's son Mephibosheth, lamed at five.
2SA 5:1-25	All the tribes come to Hebron, call David their own flesh, and anoint him king. He is thirty, and reigns forty years. He attacks the Jebusites, who taunt that the blind and lame will keep him out, and takes the stronghold of Zion. Hiram of Tyre sends cedar and craftsmen. He takes more wives and has more children, including Solomon. The Philistines come up twice into the valley of Rephaim: the first time Yahweh says go up, the second to circle behind and wait for marching in the mulberry trees.
2SA 6:1-23	David gathers thirty thousand men to bring the ark up on a new cart. At Nacon's threshing floor the oxen stumble, Uzzah steadies the ark, and God strikes him dead. David, angry and afraid, names the place Perez Uzzah and leaves the ark at Obed-Edom's three months, where it brings blessing. Hearing this, he brings it up with sacrifices every six paces, dancing in a linen ephod. Michal mocks him for exposing himself before servants; he says he danced before Yahweh. She has no child ever after.
2SA 7:1-29	Settled in cedar, David tells Nathan the ark should not live in curtains. Nathan approves, then receives Yahweh's word that night: God never asked for cedar, and has moved in a tent since Egypt. He reminds David he was taken from the sheep, and promises a great name and a house of his own line. His offspring will build the temple, and that throne will stand forever; God will discipline him but not withdraw his kindness as from Saul. David sits before Yahweh and prays.
2SA 8:1-18	David subdues the Philistines, then Moab, measuring the captives by line, two lines to death and one spared. He strikes Hadadezer of Zobah, taking seventeen hundred horsemen and twenty thousand foot, hamstringing the chariot horses but for a hundred. When Damascus helps, he kills twenty-two thousand and garrisons the country. Toi of Hamath sends silver, gold and bronze, dedicated to Yahweh with plunder from Syria, Moab and Ammon. Eighteen thousand die in the Valley of Salt. He reigns with justice, Joab over the army, Zadok and Ahimelech priests.
2SA 9:1-13	David asks whether anyone of Saul's house is left to treat kindly for Jonathan's sake. Ziba reports that Jonathan has a son, lame in both feet, living at Lo Debar. Mephibosheth comes and bows, and David tells him not to fear: Saul's land is restored and he will eat at the king's table continually. Mephibosheth calls himself a dead dog. David orders Ziba, with his fifteen sons and twenty servants, to farm the land for him. Mephibosheth lives in Jerusalem, eating like one of the king's sons.
2SA 10:1-19	When the Ammonite king dies, David sends envoys to comfort his son Hanun, whose advisers persuade him they are spies. Hanun shaves off half of each man's beard, cuts their garments off at the buttocks, and sends them away, so David has them wait at Jericho until their beards grow. The Ammonites hire thirty-three thousand Syrian mercenaries. Joab, caught between two forces, splits the army with Abishai, and both enemies flee. David later destroys Hadadezer's reinforcements at Helam.
2SA 11:1-27	In the season when kings go to war, David sends Joab to besiege Rabbah and stays in Jerusalem. From his roof he sees Bathsheba, wife of Uriah the Hittite, sends for her, and she conceives. David recalls Uriah, but he refuses to sleep in his own house while the army camps in the field. So David sends him back carrying a letter telling Joab to put him at the front and withdraw. Uriah is killed at the wall. David then takes Bathsheba as his wife, and Yahweh is displeased.
2SA 12:1-16	Nathan tells David a story about a rich man with many flocks who took a poor man's one beloved ewe lamb to feed a traveller. David declares the man deserves death and fourfold restitution. Nathan answers that he is the man: God gave him everything, yet he struck down Uriah and took his wife. Therefore the sword will never leave his house, and his wives will be taken in public because he acted in secret. David says he has sinned, and is told the child will die.
2SA 12:17-31	David refuses to be raised from the ground or to eat, and on the seventh day the child dies. His servants are afraid to tell him, but he sees them whispering and asks. On hearing it he washes, worships, and eats, saying that while the child lived he hoped God might be gracious, and now he cannot bring him back but will go to him. Bathsheba bears Solomon, whom Nathan names Jedidiah. Joab calls David to Rabbah to finish the siege himself.
2SA 13:1-18	Amnon, David's son, becomes obsessed with his half-sister Tamar and makes himself ill over her. His cousin Jonadab advises him to feign sickness and ask David to send Tamar to make food for him. David does so. When she brings the cakes into the room, Amnon has everyone sent out and takes hold of her. She refuses and tells him such a thing is not done in Israel. He rapes her. His love then turns to hatred, and he has her put out and the door bolted.
2SA 13:19-39	Tamar puts ashes on her head, tears her robe and goes away crying aloud. Absalom tells her to stay quiet, and she remains desolate in his house. David is very angry but does nothing. Two years later Absalom holds a sheep shearing and presses David into sending the king's sons. His servants kill Amnon when the wine has made him merry, and the rest flee on their mules. David is told all his sons are dead until Jonadab explains that only Amnon is. Absalom flees to Geshur.
2SA 14:1-17	Joab, seeing the king's heart turned toward Absalom, brings a wise woman from Tekoa, dresses her as a long-time mourner, and puts words in her mouth. She comes to David with a case: a widow whose two sons fought, one killing the other, and now the family demands the survivor's life, leaving her husband no heir. David swears no hair of her son will fall. She then presses further: why has the king not brought home his own banished one? God devises means so the banished are not cast off.
2SA 14:18-33	David asks whether Joab's hand is in this, and the woman admits it. He tells Joab to bring Absalom back, but Absalom is sent to his own house and may not see the king's face. Absalom is the most admired man in Israel for his looks, and cuts his heavy hair once a year, weighing two hundred shekels. After two years he summons Joab twice, and when Joab will not come he has his barley field set on fire. David finally calls him in and kisses him.
2SA 15:1-19	Absalom acquires a chariot, horses and fifty runners, then stands by the gate intercepting anyone coming to the king for judgment, telling each that his case is good but nobody has been appointed to hear it, and embracing whoever bows to him. So he steals the hearts of Israel. Under cover of a vow at Hebron he has himself proclaimed king and summons David's counsellor Ahithophel. David orders immediate flight to spare the city, leaving ten concubines behind, and tells Ittai the Gittite to turn back.
2SA 15:20-37	Ittai swears to be wherever the king is, in death or life, and crosses over with his men. The country weeps as the people cross the Kidron. Zadok brings the ark, and David sends it back, saying that if he finds favour God will bring him back to see it; the priests are to stay in the city with their sons as messengers. David climbs the Mount of Olives barefoot and weeping, and prays that Ahithophel's counsel be turned to foolishness. He sends Hushai back to feign loyalty.
2SA 16:1-23	Past the summit, Ziba meets David with donkeys, bread and wine, and says Mephibosheth stayed in Jerusalem expecting the kingdom back; David grants him everything of Mephibosheth's. At Bahurim, Shimei of Saul's family curses David as a man of blood and throws stones. Abishai asks to take his head, and David refuses: if his own son seeks his life, why not a Benjamite. Absalom enters Jerusalem, and Hushai greets him as king. On Ahithophel's advice Absalom sleeps with his father's concubines publicly, on the roof.
2SA 17:1-29	Ahithophel advises taking twelve thousand men to strike David that night while he is exhausted, killing only the king. Hushai argues instead that David is a seasoned warrior in hiding and that all Israel should be gathered with Absalom leading. Absalom prefers Hushai, because Yahweh had ordained to defeat Ahithophel's counsel. Hushai sends word through the priests, and a woman at Bahurim hides the couriers in a well under grain. David crosses the Jordan by dawn. Ahithophel goes home, sets his house in order and hangs himself.
2SA 18:1-17	David organises his men in three divisions under Joab, Abishai and Ittai, and is talked out of going himself. He orders all three commanders, in the army's hearing, to deal gently with Absalom. The battle is fought in the forest of Ephraim, and the forest kills more than the sword. Absalom, riding his mule, is caught by the head in a great oak. A man who sees it refuses Joab's silver, citing the king's order. Joab drives three darts through Absalom's heart.
2SA 18:18-33	Absalom had raised a pillar in the king's valley for himself, having no son to keep his name. Ahimaaz asks to carry the news, and Joab refuses because the king's son is dead, sending a Cushite instead; Ahimaaz insists and outruns him. He arrives with news of victory but says only that he saw a great tumult. The Cushite answers plainly. David goes up to the room over the gate and weeps for Absalom, wishing he had died in his place.
2SA 19:1-22	The victory turns to mourning because the king is grieving, and the troops slip into the city like men ashamed of fleeing. Joab confronts David: he has shamed the men who saved his life, and if he does not speak to them, nobody will stay. David sits in the gate and the people come. He works on Judah through the priests, promising Amasa command of the army. Shimei hurries down with a thousand Benjamites and confesses, and David refuses to execute anyone that day.
2SA 19:23-43	David swears Shimei will not die. Mephibosheth comes down unwashed and untrimmed since the king left, and says Ziba deceived and slandered him. David divides the land between them, and Mephibosheth says Ziba may take it all now the king is home. Barzillai the Gileadite escorts David over the Jordan but declines to come to Jerusalem: at eighty he can no longer taste food or hear singers, and sends Chimham in his place. Israel then complains that Judah stole the king away, and Judah answers the fiercer.
2SA 20:1-26	Sheba son of Bichri blows a trumpet and declares Israel has no share in David, and all Israel follows him while Judah stays. Back in Jerusalem David puts the ten concubines in custody, provided for but shut up for life. Amasa is late mustering Judah, so David sends Abishai after Sheba. At Gibeon Joab greets Amasa, takes his beard as if to kiss him, and kills him. Sheba is besieged in Abel, where a wise woman ends it: his head comes over the wall.
2SA 21:1-22	A three-year famine is attributed to Saul's killing of the Gibeonites, whom Israel had sworn to spare. The Gibeonites refuse silver and ask for seven of Saul's sons instead, who are hanged at Gibeah at the start of barley harvest. David spares Mephibosheth because of his oath to Jonathan. Rizpah spreads sackcloth on the rock and guards the bodies from birds and animals until the rains come. David then buries the bones of Saul and Jonathan, and God answers prayer for the land.
2SA 22:1-51	David's song of deliverance, sung when Yahweh had rescued him from all his enemies and from Saul. It opens by piling up names: rock, fortress, deliverer, shield, horn of salvation, high tower. Death's waves had closed around him, and his cry reached God's temple, at which the earth shook and he rode a cherub through thick darkness until the sea floor lay bare. The rest is a soldier's thanks: trained hands, a bow of bronze, and feet that do not slip.
2SA 23:1-39	David's last words: a ruler who governs justly is like morning light after rain, while the ungodly are thorns nobody can gather by hand. Then the roll of his mighty men. Josheb Basshebeth killed eight hundred at once. Eleazar fought until his hand froze to his sword. Shammah held a lentil field alone. Three broke through the Philistine lines to fetch water from Bethlehem, and David poured it out rather than drink it. The list ends with Uriah the Hittite.
2SA 24:1-25	Yahweh's anger moves David to number Israel and Judah. Joab objects and is overruled, and the census takes nine months and twenty days, returning one million three hundred thousand fighting men. David's conscience strikes him, and through Gad he is offered famine, flight or plague. He chooses to fall into Yahweh's hand, and seventy thousand die. The angel stops at the threshing floor of Araunah the Jebusite, where David builds an altar, insisting on paying fifty shekels, and the plague is stopped.
ACT 1:1-26	Luke writes to Theophilus again. Jesus tells the disciples to wait in Jerusalem for the Spirit and to be his witnesses to the ends of the earth, then is taken up in a cloud. Two men in white ask why they stare. The eleven pray upstairs, and the lot to replace Judas falls on Matthias.
ACT 2:1-24	At Pentecost wind fills the house, fire rests on each of them, and they speak in other languages. Devout Jews from every nation hear their own tongue. Some are bewildered, others say they are drunk. Peter notes it is nine in the morning, quotes Joel, and says God raised the crucified Jesus because death could not hold him.
ACT 2:25-47	Peter argues from David's psalm about not being left in Hades: David died and his tomb is still there, so he spoke of the Christ. He says God made the crucified Jesus Lord and Christ. The crowd asks what to do, and about three thousand are baptized. They devote themselves to teaching, breaking bread and prayer, and sell property to meet needs.
ACT 3:1-26	Peter and John pass a man lame from birth carried daily to the Beautiful Gate to beg. Peter has no silver or gold, and in the name of Jesus Christ of Nazareth tells him to walk. He goes into the temple leaping. In Solomon's porch Peter says it was not his own power, and calls the crowd to repent.
ACT 4:1-37	The priests and Sadducees arrest Peter and John for teaching resurrection; believers reach about five thousand. Asked by what power they acted, Peter names Jesus of Nazareth, whom they crucified and God raised, and says there is no other saving name. With the healed man standing there, the council settles on threats. The group prays for boldness, and Barnabas sells a field.
ACT 5:1-21	Ananias, with Sapphira's knowledge, sells property and keeps back part of the price. Peter says he lied to the Holy Spirit, not to men, and Ananias falls dead and is buried. Three hours later Sapphira repeats the figure and falls dead beside him. Great fear comes on everyone. The Sadducees jail the apostles, and an angel opens the doors at night.
ACT 5:22-42	The officers find the prison locked and guarded but empty, until someone reports the men are teaching in the temple. They are fetched without force, for fear of stoning. Peter answers that they must obey God rather than men. The council wants them dead until Gamaliel cites Theudas and Judas of Galilee, whose followers came to nothing. They beat the apostles and release them.
ACT 6:1-15	As numbers grow, the Hellenists complain their widows are neglected in the daily distribution. The twelve say it is not right to leave the word to serve tables, and seven men full of the Spirit are chosen, including Stephen and Philip. Stephen works wonders, and men from several synagogues cannot withstand his wisdom, so they produce witnesses claiming he spoke against the temple.
ACT 7:1-31	Asked whether the charges are true, Stephen answers with a history. God sent Abraham to a land he was given no foothold in, and foretold four hundred years of slavery. The brothers sell Joseph into Egypt, where he becomes governor. A new king forces them to expose their babies. Moses, raised by Pharaoh's daughter, kills an Egyptian at forty and flees to Midian.
ACT 7:32-60	The voice names itself the God of Abraham and sends Moses back to Egypt. Stephen argues that Israel rejected the deliverer God sent and made a calf, and that the Most High does not live in houses made by hands. When he says he sees the Son of Man standing at God's right hand, they stone him, laying their coats at Saul's feet.
ACT 8:1-40	Saul consents to Stephen's death, and the persecution scatters the church. Philip preaches in a Samaritan city, where the lame are healed and Simon the sorcerer is baptized. Simon offers money for the power to give the Spirit and is refused sharply. An angel sends Philip to the Gaza road, where he explains Isaiah to an Ethiopian official and baptizes him.
ECC 1:1-18	The Preacher, son of David and king in Jerusalem, opens with vanity of vanities. Generations come and go while the earth remains. The sun hurries back to where it rose, the wind circles, the rivers run to a sea that is never full. Nothing is new, nobody is remembered, and in much wisdom there is much grief.
ECC 2:1-26	He runs the experiment on himself: mirth, wine, houses, vineyards, gardens, pools, herds, silver and gold. He withholds nothing his eyes desire and becomes greater than anyone before him in Jerusalem, then calls it all a chasing after wind. Wise man and fool meet the same end, and his heir may be a fool.
ECC 3:1-22	The poem of times opens the chapter: a time to be born and to die, to weep and to laugh, for war and for peace. God has made everything beautiful in its time and set eternity in human hearts, yet nobody can grasp the whole work. So rejoice, eat and drink. He then finds wickedness where justice should sit.
ECC 4:1-16	The Preacher looks at oppression and sees people weeping with no comforter. The dead, he concludes, are better off than the living, and the unborn better than both. Achievement runs on envy of a neighbour. A man with no son or brother works endlessly and asks who for. Then: two are better than one, and a threefold cord is not quickly broken.
ECC 5:1-20	Advice on worship: guard your steps at God's house, listen rather than offer the sacrifice of fools, keep your words few, and pay what you vow. Do not be amazed when justice is taken away, since every official has another above him. Whoever loves silver is never satisfied, and the labourer sleeps sweetly while the rich man lies awake.
ECC 6:1-12	The Preacher names a heavy evil: a man given riches, wealth and honour who lacks nothing he desires, then has no power to enjoy any of it, so a stranger eats it instead. A man with a hundred children and long years but no satisfaction and no burial is worse off than a stillborn child, which at least has rest.
ECC 7:1-29	A run of sayings preferring the sober to the pleasant: a good name beats fine perfume, the day of death beats the day of birth, the house of mourning beats the house of feasting. A fool's laughter is thorns crackling under a pot. Do not ask why the former days were better. Be neither excessively righteous nor wicked, since no righteous man never sins.
ECC 8:1-17	Advice on serving a king: keep his command, do not leave his presence in haste, and remember nobody can ask him what he is doing. Sentence against evil is not carried out quickly, so people set their hearts on doing it. He commends eating, drinking and joy, and concludes that no one can find out the work of God.
ECC 9:1-18	One event comes to everyone, righteous and wicked. A living dog is better than a dead lion, since the living at least know they will die. So eat your bread with joy, wear white, live joyfully with the wife you love, and work hard, since there is no work in Sheol. The race is not to the swift: time and chance happen to all.
ECC 10:1-20	Dead flies spoil the perfumer's oil, and a little folly outweighs wisdom and honour. A fool announces himself to everyone as he walks along. The Preacher has seen servants on horses and princes on foot. Then occupational hazards: digging a pit, breaking a wall, quarrying stone, and a snake that bites before it is charmed. Do not curse the king even in your thoughts.
ECC 11:1-10	Cast your bread on the waters; give a portion to seven and even to eight, since you do not know what evil is coming. Anyone who watches the wind will not sow. You do not know how bones grow in the womb, so sow morning and evening. Rejoice in your youth, and know that God will bring you into judgment.
ECC 12:1-14	Remember your Creator while you are young. Then old age: the keepers of the house tremble, the strong men bow, the grinders stop because they are few, those who look out of the windows go dark, a bird's voice wakes a man, and a grasshopper is a burden. The silver cord is severed, dust returns to earth and the spirit to God.
EXO 1:1-22	Jacob's seventy descendants have become a nation, and a new Egyptian king who never knew Joseph decides they are a security risk. He puts them to forced labour building Pithom and Raamses, but the harder they work the more they multiply. He orders the midwives Shiphrah and Puah to kill every newborn boy. They refuse, saying Hebrew women give birth before they arrive, and God gives them households of their own. Pharaoh then orders every Hebrew boy thrown into the Nile.
EXO 2:1-25	A Levite couple hide their newborn son three months, then set him in a tarred basket in the reeds. Pharaoh's daughter finds him, and his sister arranges for his own mother to nurse him. She names him Moses. Grown, he kills an Egyptian beating a Hebrew and buries the body; a Hebrew throws it in his face the next day, and Moses flees to Midian. He defends seven sisters at a well, marries Zipporah, and fathers Gershom. God hears Israel's groaning.
EXO 3:1-22	Tending Jethro's flock at Horeb, Moses sees a bush burn without being consumed. God calls to him, tells him to remove his sandals, and names himself the God of Abraham, Isaac and Jacob. He has seen the affliction in Egypt and is sending Moses to bring the people out. Moses asks who he is to go, and what name to give. God answers I AM WHO I AM. Pharaoh will refuse until struck with wonders, and the people will leave carrying Egyptian silver and gold.
EXO 4:1-31	Moses objects that no one will believe him, so God gives him three signs: a rod that becomes a snake, a hand that turns leprous, and river water that becomes blood. Moses pleads that he is slow of speech, then asks God to send someone else. God's anger burns, and he appoints Aaron as spokesman. Moses takes leave of Jethro and returns to Egypt. At a lodging place God moves to kill him, and Zipporah circumcises their son with a flint. Aaron meets him, and the people believe and worship.
EXO 5:1-23	Moses and Aaron deliver Yahweh's demand to let the people go and hold a feast in the wilderness. Pharaoh says he does not know Yahweh and refuses. He accuses the people of idleness and orders that straw no longer be supplied while the daily brick quota stays the same. The people scatter to gather stubble, and the Israelite officers are beaten for falling short. They appeal to Pharaoh, who repeats the order, then turn on Moses and Aaron. Moses complains to God that nothing has been rescued.
EXO 6:1-30	God answers that Pharaoh will not merely release Israel but drive them out. He declares his name Yahweh, unknown by that name to Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, and repeats the covenant promise of land with a chain of I will statements. The people are too crushed to listen. Moses protests that he has uncircumcised lips. A genealogy follows: Reuben, Simeon, then the Levites in detail down through Kohath and Amram to Aaron and Moses, and on to Eleazar's son Phinehas. Moses repeats his objection.
ISA 1:1-31	Yahweh calls heaven and earth to witness that his children have rebelled: the ox knows its owner, Israel does not. He rejects their sacrifices and festivals because their hands are full of blood. Wash, seek justice, defend the widow, and scarlet sins can go white as snow. The faithful city is a prostitute now, and God will smelt her clean.
ISA 2:1-22	Isaiah sees the mountain of Yahweh's house raised above the hills, nations streaming to it, swords beaten into plowshares. Then the present: a land full of silver, horses and idols people made with their own fingers. A day of Yahweh comes against everything high, cedars, towers, ships, and people hide in caves and throw their gold to the moles and bats.
ISA 3:1-26	Yahweh strips Jerusalem of every support: bread, water, soldier, judge, prophet, elder and enchanter. Boys will rule, and a man drafted because he owns a coat refuses. The elders are tried for grinding the faces of the poor. The women of Zion lose an itemised inventory of finery and get rottenness for spices, rope for a belt, baldness for styled hair.
ISA 4:1-6	Seven women take hold of one man, offering to feed and clothe themselves if they can carry his name. Then the tone turns: whoever is left in Zion will be called holy, the filth washed away by a spirit of justice and burning. Over Mount Zion God creates cloud by day and fire by night, a shelter from heat and storm.
ISA 5:1-30	A love song about a friend's vineyard: best vines, watchtower, winepress, a crop of wild grapes. The hedge comes down and the clouds are told to withhold rain. Israel is the vineyard, and he looked for justice and got bloodshed. Six woes: property hoarders, dawn drinkers, those who call evil good, judges who acquit for bribes. He whistles for a nation that never tires.
ISA 6:1-13	The year King Uzziah dies, Isaiah sees the Lord enthroned, his robe filling the temple, seraphim calling holy, holy, holy until the thresholds shake. He calls himself a man of unclean lips, and a seraph touches his mouth with a live coal to take his guilt away. He volunteers, then hears the job: preach to people who will not understand, until the cities stand empty.
ISA 7:1-25	Syria and Israel march on Jerusalem and Ahaz's heart shakes like trees in wind. Isaiah meets him with his son Shearjashub: the two kings are only smoking stubs of torches. Offered any sign, Ahaz refuses, so God gives one anyway: a young woman will bear a son called Immanuel, and before the child knows right from wrong both kingdoms will be abandoned.
ISA 8:1-22	Isaiah writes Maher Shalal Hash Baz on a large tablet, then gives the name to his newborn son: before the boy can say father or mother, Assyria will carry off the wealth of Damascus and Samaria. The people rejected the gentle waters of Shiloah, so the River will flood them to the neck. God will be a sanctuary to some and a trap to others.
ISA 9:1-21	The gloom lifts for Zebulun and Naphtali: people walking in darkness see a great light, the oppressor's yoke is broken and the bloodied battle gear burned as fuel. A child is born, called Wonderful, Counselor, Mighty God, Everlasting Father, Prince of Peace, on David's throne. Then judgment resumes: Samaria boasts it will rebuild in cut stone, and Manasseh and Ephraim eat each other.
ISA 10:1-34	Woe to legislators who write oppressive laws to make widows their plunder. Assyria is the rod of God's anger but does not know it: its king boasts of gathering the earth like eggs from an abandoned nest. God asks if an axe brags against the man swinging it, and burns Assyria's forest down to a remnant a child could count.
ISA 11:1-16	A shoot comes out of the stump of Jesse, carrying the spirit of wisdom, counsel, might and knowledge. He judges not by appearance but by righteousness, and kills the wicked with the breath of his lips. Then the animals change: wolf with lamb, lion eating straw, a nursing child at a cobra's hole. Yahweh gathers the remnant and splits the River into seven streams.
ISA 12:1-6	A short song of thanks. The speaker thanks Yahweh because his anger has turned away, calls him strength and song, and refuses to be afraid. Water will be drawn from the wells of salvation. Then the song turns outward: tell the nations what he has done, sing and shout, because the Holy One of Israel is great in the middle of Zion.
ISA 13:1-22	The burden of Babylon. Yahweh musters an army from a far country, and the day of Yahweh arrives with the sun, moon and stars giving no light. He will make people rarer than the gold of Ophir. The sack is brutal: infants killed before their parents' eyes, houses looted, wives raped. The Medes cannot be bribed, and Babylon is left to jackals and ostriches.
ISA 15:1-9	In a single night Ar and Kir are laid waste. Moab weeps on the high places, heads shaved and beards cut, wailing in sackcloth on the rooftops, the noise carrying from Heshbon to Jahaz. Even the armed men shake. The prophet's own heart cries out for Moab. Refugees carry their savings over the brook of the willows, and the waters of Dimon run with blood.
ISA 16:1-14	Moab is told to send tribute lambs to Zion. Judah is asked to shelter the outcasts and not betray the fugitive. But Moab's pride is worth nothing, so Moab wails for Moab and mourns the raisin cakes of Kir Hareseth. The prophet waters Heshbon with his tears, and nobody treads the winepresses. Three years, and its glory is gone.
ISA 17:1-14	Damascus stops being a city and becomes a ruined heap. Ephraim's fortress ends too, and Jacob's glory grows thin, two or three olives left at the top of the tree. Then people will look to their Maker instead of to altars their own fingers made. Nations roar like the sea, are rebuked, and blow away like chaff.
ISA 18:1-7	An oracle to the land of rustling wings beyond the rivers of Ethiopia, which sends ambassadors in papyrus boats to a tall and smooth people. Watch for the banner, listen for the trumpet. Yahweh says he will stay still and watch, like clear heat in sunshine. Then, just as the flower becomes a grape, he cuts off the shoots and leaves them to the birds.
ISA 19:1-25	Yahweh rides into Egypt on a swift cloud, the idols tremble, and Egypt turns on itself until its counsel fails and it consults wizards. The Nile dries up, ruining fishermen, flax workers and weavers. The princes of Zoan and Memphis are fools staggering in their own vomit. Then it turns: an altar to Yahweh in Egypt, a highway to Assyria, Israel a third alongside them.
ISA 20:1-6	In the year Sargon of Assyria sent his commander against Ashdod, Yahweh tells Isaiah to take off his sackcloth and sandals, and Isaiah goes naked and barefoot for three years. Assyria will lead Egyptian and Ethiopian captives away, young and old, naked and barefoot with buttocks uncovered. Those counting on Egypt will be dismayed, and the coastland people ask how they themselves can escape.
ISA 35:1-10	The desert blossoms like a rose and is given Lebanon's glory. Weak hands are strengthened and the fearful told God is coming to rescue them. The blind see, the deaf hear, the lame leap like a deer, and water breaks out in the wilderness. A road called The Holy Way runs through it, and the ransomed come back to Zion singing.
ISA 39:1-8	Merodach Baladan of Babylon sends letters and a gift to the recovered Hezekiah, who gives the envoys the full tour: treasury, spices, armoury, nothing held back. Isaiah asks what they saw. Everything, says Hezekiah. Then everything goes to Babylon, Isaiah tells him, and his own sons will be eunuchs there. Hezekiah answers that there will be peace in his days.
ISA 14:1-32	Yahweh restores Jacob, and captives rule their captors. Israel takes up a taunt over the king of Babylon: the oppressor has stopped, and even the cedars are relieved no lumberjack is coming. Sheol stirs and dead kings rise to ask whether he is weak like them, his bedding maggots. Called morning star, he meant to set his throne above the stars and ends up unburied.
ISA 21:1-17	A vision comes like a whirlwind and the prophet gets labour pains, deafness and blindness. A banquet is laid, then the order comes to oil the shield. A watchman sees horsemen in pairs and reports that Babylon has fallen. Then Dumah, where a voice asks about the night and is told morning comes, and also night. Then Arabia, and Kedar's glory ends within a year.
ISA 22:1-25	A city on its rooftops, its dead not killed in battle, its rulers caught fleeing. It inspects the breaches, tears down houses for the wall, builds a reservoir, and never once looks at the one who planned it. Called to sackcloth, it feasts instead. Shebna, caught carving himself a rock tomb, is thrown into a wide country, and Eliakim gets the key of David's house.
ISA 23:1-18	The ships of Tarshish howl because their harbour is gone, the news reaching them from Kittim. Sidon is shamed and the sea itself says it never bore children. Tyre, whose merchants were princes, was planned against by Yahweh to stain the pride of all glory. After seventy years forgotten, Tyre takes up her trade again like a prostitute, and her earnings end up holy.
ISA 24:1-23	Yahweh empties the earth, and the levelling is universal: priest like people, servant like master, creditor like debtor. The land is polluted because its people broke the everlasting covenant, so the curse eats it. Wine mourns, harps stop, drink turns bitter. Escape the noise and you fall in the pit; climb out and the snare takes you. The earth staggers like a drunk.
ISA 25:1-12	God is exalted for making a fortified city a heap that is never rebuilt. He has been a stronghold to the poor, and silences the ruthless. On this mountain he makes all peoples a feast of rich meat and refined wine, swallows death forever and wipes tears off every face. Then Moab is trampled like straw in a dung pit.
ISA 26:1-21	A song for Judah: we have a strong city, and its walls are salvation. Whoever's mind is steadfast is kept in perfect peace, because Yahweh is an everlasting rock.
