He has flattened the lofty city, and the feet treading it down are the feet of the poor. Israel admits it was in labour and gave birth only to wind. Then the dead will live.
ISA 27:1-13	Yahweh punishes leviathan the fleeing serpent and the twisted serpent, and kills the dragon in the sea. Then a kinder vineyard song: he keeps it, waters it every moment, guards it night and day, and says there is no wrath in him. Jacob's guilt is forgiven when he grinds his altar stones to chalk. Then a trumpet brings the outcasts back.
ISA 28:1-29	Woe to the drunkards of Ephraim and their crown of fading flowers. Priest and prophet reel with wine, and every table is covered in vomit. They mock Isaiah's teaching as baby talk, precept on precept, line on line, so that is what God's word becomes to them. Jerusalem's rulers boast of a covenant with death, and God answers with a tried cornerstone in Zion.
ISA 30:1-18	Woe to rebellious children who make an alliance without asking God. Treasure goes south through lion and serpent country to a people who cannot help, so God nicknames Egypt Rahab Who Sits Still. Their guilt is a bulge in a high wall that collapses and leaves no shard worth carrying coals in. Offered quietness, they chose horses, so their pursuers will be faster.
ISA 31:1-9	Woe to those who go down to Egypt for help, counting horses and chariots instead of looking to the Holy One of Israel. Egyptians are men, not God, so helper and helped fall together. Yahweh will defend Zion like a lion over its prey. Israel must throw away its silver and gold idols. Assyria falls by no man's sword.
ISA 32:1-20	A king will reign in righteousness with just princes, and a man will be shelter from the storm, streams in dry ground. Stammerers will speak clearly and fools no longer be called noble. Isaiah then turns on the women at ease: the vintage will fail, so put on sackcloth. Thorns take the houses of joy, until the Spirit is poured out.
ISA 33:1-24	Woe to the destroyer who has never been destroyed and the betrayer nobody has betrayed: when he finishes, it will be done to him. The highways are empty, the covenant broken, the envoys of peace weeping. Sinners in Zion ask who can live with everlasting burning, and the answer is whoever refuses a bribe and shuts his eyes to evil.
ISA 34:1-17	The nations are called in to listen. Yahweh is enraged with all of them, the mountains melting with blood and the sky rolled up like a scroll. His sword, drunk in heaven, comes down on Edom as a sacrifice at Bozrah. Edom's streams become pitch. Owl and raven take possession, all recorded in Yahweh's book, none missing her mate.
ISA 29:1-24	Ariel is besieged until it whispers out of the dust like a medium, and then the besiegers become blowing chaff. The vision is a sealed book: the literate cannot read it because it is sealed, the illiterate cannot read at all. Soon the deaf hear the book and the blind see.
ISA 30:19-33	Zion stops weeping and gets an answer. Still the bread of adversity, but the teachers are no longer hidden, and a voice behind says this is the way, walk in it. The plating is stripped off the idols. Then his name arrives in thick smoke, and every blow of the rod on Assyria falls in time with tambourines and harps.
ISA 36:1-22	Sennacherib takes all the fortified cities of Judah and sends Rabshakeh to Jerusalem. Egypt is a bruised reed that pierces the hand leaning on it, he tells Hezekiah's officials, and the Yahweh they trust is the God whose high places Hezekiah demolished. He offers two thousand horses if Judah can find riders. Asked to speak Aramaic, he shouts in Hebrew.
ISA 37:1-21	Hezekiah tears his clothes and goes into the temple, sending word to Isaiah that the children have come to the birth with no strength to deliver. Isaiah answers that the Assyrian king will hear news, go home and fall by the sword there. Sennacherib sends a letter listing places whose gods failed, and Hezekiah spreads it out before Yahweh.
ISA 37:22-38	Zion's virgin daughter has shaken her head at Sennacherib, and his boasts are quoted back: with my chariots I climbed Lebanon and will dry up the rivers of Egypt with my feet. God will put a hook in his nose. His angel kills a hundred and eighty five thousand men, and Sennacherib's own sons kill him while he worships.
ISA 38:1-22	Isaiah tells the sick Hezekiah to set his house in order because he will die. Hezekiah turns his face to the wall and weeps bitterly. God adds fifteen years, and the shadow on Ahaz's sundial goes back ten steps. His own prayer follows: his life cut from the loom, his bones broken as by a lion. The treatment was a cake of figs.
ISA 40:1-31	Comfort my people: tell Jerusalem her warfare is over and she has received double for her sins. A voice calls for a highway through the wilderness. All flesh is grass, but God's word stands forever. God comes as a mighty one and also carries lambs in his arms. Who measured the sea in his palm? Those who wait for him rise on wings like eagles.
ISA 41:1-29	God calls the islands to court and asks who raised up the conqueror from the east. The answer is Yahweh, the first and the last. Frightened nations encourage each other over an idol, the soldering pronounced good, the thing nailed down so it will not fall over. Israel, called a worm, is made a threshing sledge. Then he challenges the idols to predict anything.
ISA 42:1-25	Behold my servant, chosen, with the Spirit on him. He brings justice to the nations without shouting in the street, will not break a bruised reed or snuff a smouldering wick, and will not give up. God, silent a long time, now cries out like a woman in labour. Then the reproach turns inward: who is as blind as my own servant?
ISA 43:1-28	The one who created Jacob says not to fear: through water he will be with them, through fire they will not be burned. Egypt, Ethiopia and Seba are the ransom. The witnesses called are the blind and the deaf. Forget the old things: he is making a road in the wilderness. Israel brought only sins, blotted out for his own sake.
ISA 44:1-28	God tells Jacob not to fear: he will pour water on dry ground and his Spirit on their offspring. Then a long dismantling of idol-making. The blacksmith goes hungry and faint at the coals, and the woodcutter burns half a tree to bake bread and roast meat, says aha, I am warm, and makes a god of the leftovers. Cyrus is named as God's shepherd.
ISA 45:1-25	Yahweh calls Cyrus his anointed, breaks the bronze doors ahead of him, all for Jacob's sake, though Cyrus does not know him. God forms light and creates darkness, makes peace and creates calamity. Then the potter argument: woe to the clay that argues with its maker. The ends of the earth are called to look to him and be saved.
ISA 46:1-13	Bel bows down and Nebo stoops, their idols loaded onto pack animals and going into captivity with everyone else. Israel, by contrast, has been carried by God from the womb, and he will keep carrying them to grey hair. A hired goldsmith's god is set where it cannot move or answer. God is calling a bird of prey from the east.
ISA 47:1-15	Babylon, addressed as a pampered virgin daughter, is told to sit in the dust with no throne, take up the millstones, and lift her skirt to wade the rivers. God handed his people over and she showed no mercy, telling herself she would be a princess forever. Widowhood and childlessness arrive in a single day, and her astrologers cannot save her.
ISA 48:1-22	These people swear by God's name but not in truth. He announced things in advance because he knew they were obstinate, iron-necked and brass-browed, and would otherwise credit their idols. He defers his anger for his own name's sake. If only they had listened, their peace would have been like a river. There is no peace for the wicked.
ISA 49:1-26	The servant was called from the womb, his mouth made a sharp sword. He complains he has laboured in vain, and is told Jacob alone is too small a job: he will also be a light to the nations. Zion says she has been forgotten, and the answer is a nursing mother who cannot forget her child, and hands with Zion engraved on the palms.
ISA 50:1-11	God asks where the certificate of divorce is, or which creditor he sold them to: nobody bought them, they were sold for their own sins. Then the servant speaks: God wakens his ear morning by morning, and he gave his back to those who beat him and did not hide his face from spitting, having set it like flint.
ISA 51:1-23	Those who seek righteousness are told to look at the rock they were cut from, at Abraham and Sarah, who were one before being made many. The heavens vanish like smoke and the earth wears out like a garment, but that salvation lasts forever. Jerusalem, who drank the cup of staggering, has it taken from her and given to those who walked over her back.
ISA 52:1-15	Wake up, Zion, put on your best clothes, shake off the dust and untie your neck. You were sold for nothing and will be redeemed without money. The feet of the messenger who says your God reigns are called beautiful on the mountains. This time they will not go out in haste, because God goes ahead of them and behind.
ISA 53:1-12	The servant grew up like a shoot from dry ground, with no beauty to attract anyone, despised and rejected. He carried the sickness that was theirs while onlookers assumed God had struck him. He was pierced for their transgressions, and by his wounds they are healed, since all of them strayed like sheep. He stayed silent, like a lamb led to slaughter.
ISA 54:1-17	The barren woman is told to sing, because the desolate will have more children than the married wife, so she should enlarge her tent and drive the stakes in harder. Her Maker is her husband. He forsook her for a small moment but gathers her with great mercies. The city is rebuilt in sapphire, and no weapon made against her will succeed.
ISA 55:1-13	Everyone thirsty should come to the water, and whoever has no money should buy wine and milk without price. Why spend money on what is not bread? Seek Yahweh while he can be found, and let the wicked abandon his way and be freely pardoned. God's word works like rain and snow, which do not go back up empty.
ISA 56:1-12	Keep justice, because salvation is near, and blessed is the one who keeps the Sabbath. The foreigner who has joined himself to Yahweh should not say he will be separated out, and the eunuch should not call himself a dry tree: he gets a name better than sons and daughters. Then the tone drops: the watchmen are blind, mute dogs that cannot bark.
ISA 57:1-21	The righteous die and nobody notices that they are being spared the coming evil. Then the accusation: they inflame themselves under every green tree, kill children in the valleys, pour drink offerings to the smooth stones of the wadi, and never once said it is useless. Then the high and lofty One who inhabits eternity says he lives with the contrite.
ISA 58:1-14	A people complain that they have fasted and God has not noticed. The reply: on your fast day you pursue your own pleasure and drive your workers, fasting for strife. The fast he chose is to undo the yoke, share bread with the hungry, house the homeless poor and not hide from your own family. Then he answers with here I am.
ISA 59:1-21	Their sins separated them, not any shortness in God's arm. They hatch adders' eggs and weave spiders' webs: eating the eggs kills you, crushing one produces a viper, and the webs will never make clothing. They grope along the wall like the blind, and truth has fallen in the street. God found no intercessor, so his own arm brought salvation.
ISA 60:1-22	Zion is told to get up and shine, because her light has come while thick darkness covers the peoples. Camels from Midian, Ephah and Sheba arrive with gold and frankincense. Foreigners rebuild her walls and her gates stay open day and night. Gold replaces brass, silver replaces iron, and peace becomes her governor. Sun and moon are no longer needed.
ISA 61:1-11	The Spirit of the Lord is on the speaker, anointed to bring good news to the humble, bind up the broken hearted, proclaim liberty to captives, and announce the year of Yahweh's favour and the day of vengeance. Mourners get a garland instead of ashes, oil of joy instead of mourning. They will rebuild ruins derelict for generations, and instead of shame they get double.
ISA 62:1-12	For Zion's sake the speaker will not keep quiet until her righteousness shines like the dawn. She will get a new name from Yahweh's own mouth: not Forsaken or Desolate, but Hephzibah and Beulah, and as a bridegroom rejoices over his bride, so God rejoices over her. Those who call on Yahweh are to give him no rest.
ISA 63:1-19	Someone comes from Edom in stained clothing and identifies himself as mighty to save. Asked why his clothes are red, he says he trod the winepress alone, trampling the peoples until their lifeblood stained him. The speaker then recounts God's kindnesses: he carried them all the days of old, but they grieved his holy Spirit and he became their enemy.
ISA 64:1-12	The prayer continues with a wish that God would tear the heavens open and come down so the mountains shake. Then the confession: all of them are unclean, their righteousness like a filthy garment, fading like leaves, and nobody calls on his name. But he is their Father, they the clay and he the potter. Zion is a wasteland and the beautiful house is burned.
ISA 65:1-25	God says he was found by people who were not looking, while spreading his hands out all day to people who sacrifice in gardens, sit among graves, eat pig's meat, and tell others they are holier. But as one keeps a cluster because there is wine in it, he will not destroy them all. He creates new heavens where wolf and lamb feed together.
ISA 66:1-24	Heaven is his throne and the earth his footstool, so what house could anyone build him. The man he looks at is poor and contrite. Sacrifice offered by the self-willed is equated with murder and pig's blood. Then a nation is born in one day, and God says he will comfort them as a mother comforts her child. The last verse is grim.
JDG 1:1-18	Joshua is dead, so Israel asks Yahweh who attacks first. Judah goes with Simeon. They kill ten thousand at Bezek and cut off Adoni-Bezek's thumbs and big toes; he says he did that to seventy kings. Judah burns Jerusalem and takes Hebron, Debir, Gaza, Ashkelon and Ekron. Othniel wins Caleb's daughter Achsah, who asks her father for springs.
JDG 1:19-36	Yahweh is with Judah in the hills, but the valley holds out because its people have iron chariots. Caleb takes Hebron. Benjamin leaves the Jebusites in Jerusalem. Joseph's house takes Bethel after a man shows them the way in. Manasseh, Ephraim, Zebulun, Asher, Naphtali and Dan all fail to clear their land, and the Canaanites who remain are put to forced labour.
JDG 2:1-23	Yahweh's angel comes to Bochim: Israel made covenants with the inhabitants instead of tearing down their altars, so those nations stay as a snare. The people weep. Joshua dies at a hundred and ten, and the next generation does not know Yahweh. They serve the Baals, are handed to raiders, are rescued by judges, and relapse worse each time a judge dies.
JDG 3:1-31	The leftover nations are left to test Israel. Israel serves their gods and is sold to Cushan Rishathaim for eight years, until Othniel frees them. Then Eglon of Moab, eighteen years. Ehud, a left-handed Benjamite, hides a sword on his right thigh, asks for a private word, and kills the very fat king. Shamgar kills six hundred Philistines with an ox goad.
JDG 4:1-24	Twenty years under Jabin of Hazor, whose commander Sisera has nine hundred iron chariots. Deborah, a prophetess judging under her palm tree, sends Barak with ten thousand men to Mount Tabor. Barak will only go if she comes, so she says the honour will go to a woman. Sisera's army is routed, and Jael drives a tent peg through his temple while he sleeps.
JDG 5:1-31	Deborah and Barak sing a victory song. It recalls Yahweh marching from Seir with the earth shaking, and names the tribes who came: Ephraim, Benjamin, Machir, Zebulun, Issachar and Naphtali. Reuben stayed among the sheepfolds, Dan by the ships, Asher at the coast, and Meroz is cursed. Jael is blessed above women, while Sisera's mother waits for a chariot that is late.
JDG 6:1-21	Midian oppresses Israel seven years until they hide in caves. A prophet reminds them what Yahweh did in Egypt. Yahweh's angel then finds Gideon threshing wheat in a winepress to hide it, and greets him as a mighty man of valour. Gideon objects that his clan is the poorest and he the least in it, then asks for a sign.
JDG 6:22-40	Fire consumes the offering and the angel vanishes. Gideon fears that seeing the angel will kill him, and is told it will not. That night he and ten servants pull down his father's Baal altar. The town demands his death, but Joash says a real god can defend his own altar, and nicknames him Jerub-Baal. Gideon then tests God twice with a fleece.
JDG 7:1-12	Gideon camps at the spring of Harod. Yahweh says the army is too large, or Israel will claim it saved itself, so everyone afraid is sent home and twenty-two thousand leave. Ten thousand is still too many, so only the three hundred who lap water like dogs are kept. That night Yahweh sends Gideon to scout the enemy camp.
JDG 7:13-25	Gideon overhears a dream of a barley loaf flattening a tent, read as the sword of Gideon. He splits his three hundred into three companies with trumpets and torches hidden in jars. At the middle watch they blow, smash the jars and shout. Midian turns its swords on itself. Ephraim seizes the fords and brings Gideon the heads of Oreb and Zeeb.
JDG 8:1-17	Ephraim complains at not being called to the fight, and Gideon flatters them into calm. Exhausted but still pursuing, he asks Succoth for bread; its princes refuse until the Midianite kings are caught, and Penuel says the same. He surprises the fifteen thousand survivors at Karkor and captures Zebah and Zalmunna, then punishes Succoth's elders with desert thorns and breaks down Penuel's tower.
JDG 8:18-35	Gideon asks Zebah and Zalmunna about the men they killed at Tabor and learns they were his brothers. His firstborn Jether is too afraid to execute them, so Gideon does it. Israel asks him to rule as a dynasty; he refuses, then asks for the plunder's gold and makes an ephod that Israel worships. He fathers seventy sons, plus Abimelech by a Shechemite concubine.
JDG 9:1-28	Abimelech persuades Shechem that one ruler beats seventy, hires men with temple silver, and kills his seventy brothers on one stone. Only Jotham escapes. From Mount Gerizim he tells a fable: olive, fig and vine all refuse the crown, so the bramble takes it and threatens fire. Three years on, God sends an evil spirit between Abimelech and Shechem, and Gaal arrives mocking him.
JDG 9:29-57	Zebul warns Abimelech to ambush, then tells Gaal the approaching troops are only shadows and taunts him into fighting. Gaal loses and is expelled. Abimelech takes Shechem, kills its people, sows salt, and burns a thousand in the tower. At Thebez a woman drops a millstone on his skull, and he has his armour bearer finish him so nobody says a woman killed him.
JDG 10:1-18	Tola judges twenty-three years, then Jair twenty-two, whose thirty sons ride thirty donkey colts. Israel then serves the gods of Syria, Sidon, Moab, Ammon and the Philistines, and is oppressed eighteen years. When Israel cries out, Yahweh lists every past rescue and tells them to call on the gods they chose. They put away the foreign gods, and he is grieved by their misery.
JDG 11:1-20	Jephthah, a fighter and the son of a prostitute, is driven out by his half-brothers so he would inherit nothing. When Ammon attacks, Gilead's elders come to hire him; he asks why they want him now, and they agree before Yahweh that he will be their head. He asks Ammon's king why he has come, and answers his land claim with the whole exodus itinerary.
JDG 11:21-40	Jephthah ends his case: Israel has held this land three hundred years. The king ignores him. The Spirit comes on Jephthah and he vows to sacrifice whatever comes out of his door to meet him. He defeats twenty cities. What comes out is his daughter, his only child. She asks for two months in the mountains to mourn, returns, and he does as he vowed.
JDG 12:1-15	Ephraim confronts Jephthah for fighting Ammon without them and threatens to burn his house down; he answers that he did call and they did not come. Gilead beats Ephraim and holds the Jordan fords, making anyone who denies being an Ephraimite say Shibboleth. Those who say Sibboleth are killed: forty-two thousand of them. Ibzan, Elon and Abdon follow.
JDG 13:1-25	Israel is under the Philistines forty years. Yahweh's angel tells the barren wife of Manoah of Zorah that she will bear a son, a Nazirite from the womb: no razor on his head, and no wine or unclean food for her. Manoah asks the visitor to return, hears the same instructions, then watches the angel rise in the altar flame. She bears Samson.
JDG 14:1-20	Samson demands a Philistine woman at Timnah. A lion attacks on the way; he tears it apart bare-handed, and later eats honey from the carcass. At the feast he sets thirty companions a riddle worth thirty outfits. They threaten his wife until she gets the answer from him. He pays by killing thirty men at Ashkelon, and his wife is given to his friend.
JDG 15:1-20	At wheat harvest Samson finds his wife given to his companion. He burns the Philistine grain and olive groves with three hundred torch-tailed foxes; they burn his wife and her father. Judah binds him, but at Lehi the ropes fall apart and he kills a thousand with a donkey's jawbone. God splits a spring for his thirst. He judges Israel twenty years.
JDG 16:1-16	In Gaza Samson visits a prostitute while men wait at the gate to kill him. At midnight he carries the gate doors, posts and bar to a hill facing Hebron. Each Philistine lord offers Delilah eleven hundred pieces of silver for the secret of his strength. He names cords, then ropes, then his hair woven into the loom, and breaks free each time.
JDG 16:17-31	Samson tells Delilah his strength is in his unshaven Nazirite hair. She has it shaved as he sleeps and calls the lords in; he wakes not knowing Yahweh has left him. The Philistines blind him and set him grinding at Gaza while his hair grows back. Brought out at Dagon's feast, he braces on the two central pillars and brings the house down.
JDG 17:1-13	Micah admits taking his mother's eleven hundred pieces of silver. She blesses him and gives two hundred pieces to a silversmith for a carved image and a molten image. Micah keeps a shrine with an ephod and teraphim and ordains one of his sons. A young Levite from Bethlehem passes through and is hired as priest for ten pieces a year, clothing and food.
JDG 18:1-15	Dan is still looking for territory and sends five men to scout. They lodge at Micah's house, recognise the young Levite's voice, and ask for an oracle; he tells them their journey is before Yahweh. At Laish they find people living quietly and far from help. Six hundred armed Danites set out, and the scouts mention the ephod, teraphim and images at Micah's.
JDG 18:16-31	While six hundred armed men stand at the gate, the five scouts take the images, the ephod and the teraphim. The priest objects, is told he would rather serve a tribe than one man, and goes gladly. Micah protests and is told to be quiet or lose his household. Dan kills the people of Laish, burns the city and rebuilds it as Dan.
JDG 19:1-16	A Levite from the hill country of Ephraim takes a concubine from Bethlehem. She leaves for her father's house, and he travels to bring her back. Her father keeps him three days, then presses him to stay a fourth and a fifth. He leaves late, refuses to lodge at Jebus because it is foreign, and reaches Gibeah of Benjamin, where nobody takes them in.
JDG 19:17-30	An old man finds them in the street and takes them in. Men of the city surround the house and demand the Levite. The old man offers his own virgin daughter and the concubine instead. The Levite puts his concubine out, and they rape her all night. She collapses on the threshold. He carries her home and cuts her body into twelve pieces.
JDG 20:1-25	Four hundred thousand armed men assemble at Mizpah. The Levite says the men of Gibeah meant to kill him and raped his concubine until she died. Benjamin refuses to hand the guilty men over and musters twenty-six thousand swordsmen and seven hundred left-handed slingers. God says Judah goes first. Benjamin kills twenty-two thousand Israelites on the first day and eighteen thousand on the second.
JDG 20:26-48	Israel fasts and weeps at Bethel, and Yahweh promises Benjamin tomorrow. On the third day Israel feigns flight, drawing Benjamin onto the highways. The ambush burns the city, and the smoke is the signal. Benjamin breaks for the wilderness and twenty-five thousand fall; six hundred reach the rock of Rimmon. Israel then destroys the Benjamite towns by sword and fire.
JDG 21:1-25	Israel had sworn to give no daughter to Benjamin, and now weeps that a tribe is being cut off. Jabesh Gilead had sent nobody to the assembly, so twelve thousand men destroy it, sparing four hundred virgins for the Benjamites at Rimmon. It is not enough, so the rest are told to seize wives from the girls dancing at the yearly feast at Shiloh.
JER 1:1-19	Jeremiah, a priest's son from Anathoth, is called in Josiah's thirteenth year. God set him apart before the womb and appointed him prophet to the nations. Jeremiah objects that he is only a child; God touches his mouth and sets him over nations to uproot and to plant. Two visions follow: an almond branch, and a cauldron tipping from the north.
JER 2:1-19	God tells Jeremiah to proclaim that he remembers the kindness of Jerusalem's youth, how she followed him through unsown wilderness. Then the question: what fault did your fathers find in me? He tells them to check Kittim and Kedar for any nation that swapped its gods. Two evils: they abandoned the spring of living water and dug themselves cracked cisterns.
JER 2:20-37	God broke their yoke and was told they would not serve. He planted a noble vine and it went degenerate, and no lye or soap hides the stain. They are likened to a wild donkey in heat, calling wood their father and stone their mother, with as many gods as they have towns. A bride does not forget her jewellery, but they forgot him.
JER 3:1-25	The law bars a remarried divorcee from returning, yet God tells Israel, who has had many lovers, to come back. Israel went up every high mountain and got a certificate of divorce; Judah watched, did the same, and returned only in pretence, so Israel is called the more righteous. The north is told to return: God promises shepherds after his own heart, a Jerusalem called Yahweh's Throne, and an ark nobody remembers. The people confess the hills are useless.
JER 4:1-31	If Israel puts away her abominations the nations will bless themselves in Yahweh. Judah must break up fallow ground and circumcise their hearts. Then the alarm: blow the trumpet, get into the fortified cities, a destroyer of nations comes from the north with chariots like a whirlwind. Jeremiah's anguish is physical. He sees the earth waste and void, the heavens dark, no people and no birds, though God will not make a full end. Zion decks herself for lovers who want her dead.
JER 5:1-31	God tells Jeremiah to search Jerusalem for one person who does justice, and he will pardon the city. Jeremiah tries the poor, then the great men, and both have broken the yoke, so lion, wolf and leopard wait for them. They deny sword or famine will come and call the prophets wind, so God makes his words fire and the people wood. A nation whose language they do not know will eat their harvest. Prophets prophesy falsely, priests rule by their own authority, and the people love it so.
JER 6:1-30	Flee Jerusalem and blow the trumpet, because evil looks out from the north. Enemy shepherds camp around Zion and debate attacking at noon or by night, and siege mounds are ordered against a city that produces wickedness the way a well produces water. From least to greatest everyone is greedy; prophets and priests patch the wound saying peace, peace, when there is no peace, unable to blush. Told to ask for the old paths, they refused to walk in them. Jeremiah, set as a tester of metals, reports rejected silver.
JER 7:1-19	In the temple gate, Jeremiah tells worshippers to amend their ways and not trust the lying words Yahweh's temple, Yahweh's temple, Yahweh's temple. If they do justice, stop oppressing the foreigner, orphan and widow, and stop following other gods, they can stay. Instead they steal, murder and swear falsely, then shelter in his house as though it were a den of robbers. He points to Shiloh, and forbids Jeremiah to pray for them. Whole families bake cakes for the queen of the sky.
JER 7:20-34	God's wrath will pour out on man, animal, tree and crop. What he commanded at the exodus was obedience, not sacrifice, and since Egypt he has sent prophets and been ignored; Jeremiah is told they will not listen to him either. They built the high places of Topheth in the valley of the son of Hinnom and burned their sons and daughters there, which he never commanded and which never entered his mind. So it will be renamed the Valley of Slaughter, and every wedding voice in Judah silenced.
JER 8:1-22	The bones of Judah's kings, priests and prophets will be dug up and spread before the sun, moon and stars they worshipped, left unburied like dung, and survivors will prefer death to life. God asks why a people that falls will not get up. Stork, turtledove, swallow and crane keep their seasons; his people do not know his law. Prophet and priest say peace, peace, when there is no peace. Jeremiah's heart faints: the harvest is past, and he asks whether there is no balm in Gilead.
JER 9:1-26	Jeremiah wishes his head were water and his eyes a spring of tears, and wishes he could leave for a lodge in the wilderness, because they all bend their tongues like bows for falsehood. God will melt and test them, feed them wormwood, scatter them, and make Jerusalem a heap for jackals. Mourning women are summoned to teach their daughters wailing, since death has climbed in through the windows. Then the rule: boast of nothing but knowing the God who does loving kindness and justice.
JER 10:1-25	Do not learn the way of the nations or fear signs in the sky. Their customs are empty: a tree is cut, a workman shapes it, they plate it with silver and nail it down so it cannot fall over. Such gods must be carried, cannot speak, and can do neither harm nor good. Yahweh made the earth and brings the wind out of his storehouses, and every goldsmith is disappointed by his own image. Jeremiah admits a man cannot direct his own steps.
JER 11:1-23	Jeremiah proclaims the covenant terms: cursed is anyone who will not obey what God commanded when he brought their fathers out of the iron furnace of Egypt. Judah now has as many gods as cities. Israel was a green olive tree, and God has set it on fire. Jeremiah then learns of a plot against himself: the men of his own town, Anathoth, tell him to stop prophesying or die.
JER 12:1-17	Jeremiah concedes God is righteous but argues the case anyway: why do the wicked prosper? God planted them, and he is near their mouths and far from their hearts. The reply is not an answer: if running with footmen tires you, how will you race horses? Even Jeremiah's own family have betrayed him. Then God's grief, at having handed the beloved of his soul to her enemies because his heritage roared at him like a lion. Neighbouring nations will be uprooted too, then restored if they swear by Yahweh's name.
JER 13:1-27	Jeremiah buys a linen belt, wears it unwashed, hides it in a rock cleft by the Euphrates, and digs it up ruined: so God will ruin the pride of Judah. A saying about filling wine bottles becomes God filling kings, priests and prophets with drunkenness and smashing them together. The king and queen mother are told to sit down, their crowns fallen. Can an Ethiopian change his skin, or a leopard his spots? Neither can people trained in evil.
JER 14:1-22	The word concerning the drought. Judah mourns, jars come back from the cisterns empty, and the doe abandons her fawn. The people admit their rebellions but ask why God acts like a stranger passing the night. He answers that they loved to wander, refuses Jeremiah's prayers, and promises sword, famine and plague. The prophets promising assured peace were never sent and will die by the sword they denied. The chapter ends pleading: no idol makes rain, so we wait for you.
JER 15:1-21	God says that even Moses and Samuel standing before him would not change his mind, and appoints four destroyers, sword, dogs, birds and animals, because of what Manasseh did in Jerusalem. He says plainly that he is weary of showing compassion. The woman who bore seven sons gives up her spirit, her sun setting while it is still day. Jeremiah then curses his own birth as a man of strife cursed by everyone, and asks whether God will be a brook that runs dry. The promise back is conditional.
JER 16:1-21	Jeremiah is forbidden to marry or have children here, since children born in this place die unlamented and unburied. He is barred from houses of mourning, because God has withdrawn his peace, and barred from feasts, because the voices of bridegroom and bride will be silenced. Asked what they did wrong, he is to say their fathers forsook God and they did worse. Then a reversal: God will send fishermen and hunters to bring them back, and the nations will admit their fathers inherited only lies.
JER 17:1-27	Judah's sin is engraved with an iron pen and a diamond point on their hearts and their altars' horns. Cursed is the man who trusts in flesh, a desert bush in a salt land; blessed is the one who trusts Yahweh, a tree by water whose leaf stays green in drought. The heart is deceitful above all things, and wealth got wrongly is a partridge sitting on eggs she did not lay. Then a Sabbath oracle at the gates: keep it holy, or fire devours Jerusalem's palaces.
JER 18:1-23	Sent down to the potter's house, Jeremiah watches a vessel go wrong on the wheel and the potter make it into something else. God asks whether he cannot do the same with Israel, and states the principle both ways: he relents when a threatened nation turns, and withdraws good from a nation that does evil. Judah answers that amending is useless, they will follow their own plans. His people have left the ancient paths. When they plot to strike him with the tongue, Jeremiah prays in detail against them.
JER 19:1-15	Carrying a potter's earthen bottle, Jeremiah takes elders and priests to the valley of the son of Hinnom and announces disaster that will make ears tingle, because they filled the place with innocent blood and built high places of Baal to burn their sons, which God never commanded and which never entered his mind. It will be renamed the Valley of Slaughter, and in the siege they will eat the flesh of their own children. He smashes the bottle: God will break this people the same way, beyond mending.
JER 20:1-18	Pashhur son of Immer, chief officer in the temple, has Jeremiah beaten and locked in the stocks at the upper gate of Benjamin. Released, Jeremiah renames him Magormissabib and tells him he will die in Babylon. Then Jeremiah turns on God: you persuaded me and prevailed, and I am a laughing stock all day. Keeping silent only makes the word a fire shut up in his bones. He affirms that Yahweh is with him, breaks into praise, then curses the day he was born.
JER 21:1-14	Zedekiah sends Pashhur and Zephaniah the priest to ask Jeremiah for a wonder that makes Nebuchadnezzar withdraw. The answer is the opposite: God will turn their own weapons back into the city, fight against them himself, and hand Zedekiah and the survivors over without mercy. The people are offered the way of life and the way of death: stay and get sword, famine and plague, surrender to the Chaldeans and live. The house of David is told to do justice in the morning or watch its forest burn.
JER 22:1-30	Jeremiah takes terms to the royal palace: do justice, protect the foreigner, orphan and widow, and kings will keep riding through these gates; refuse, and God swears the house will be desolate. Weep not for the dead but for Shallum, exiled and never to see his country again. Woe to the king who panels a spacious house in red cedar on unpaid labour, when his father did justice for the poor. Jehoiakim gets a donkey's burial. Coniah is recorded as childless, with no descendant ever ruling in Judah.
JER 23:1-18	Woe to the shepherds who scatter the flock and never visit them: God will visit their evil on them, gather the remnant himself, and appoint shepherds who actually feed the sheep. He will raise for David a righteous Branch called Yahweh Our Righteousness. Then, concerning the prophets, Jeremiah's bones shake like a drunk man's, because prophet and priest alike are profane and their wickedness is found in God's own house. Samaria's prophets prophesied by Baal; Jerusalem's are worse. Do not listen: they speak visions out of their own hearts.
JER 23:19-40	A whirling storm of wrath is already out and will not return until it has done what God intended. He did not send these prophets, yet they ran; had they stood in his council they would have turned people from evil. He has heard them announcing I had a dream, I had a dream. Let the dreamer tell his dream and the one with the word speak it faithfully: what is straw to wheat? His word is fire, and a hammer that shatters rock.
JER 24:1-10	After Jeconiah, the craftsmen and the smiths are carried off to Babylon, God shows Jeremiah two baskets of figs before the temple, one of first-ripe figs and one too bad to eat. The interpretation reverses the obvious expectation. The good figs are the exiles already in Chaldea: God will bring them back and give them a heart to know him. The bad figs are Zedekiah, his princes and the remnant left behind, consumed by sword, famine and plague.
JER 25:1-18	In Jehoiakim's fourth year, Nebuchadnezzar's first, Jeremiah reckons up twenty three years of speaking to a people who would not listen, to him or to any prophet before him. So God will bring the families of the north and Nebuchadnezzar, called his servant, against the land, and these nations will serve Babylon seventy years, after which Babylon itself is punished. Then God hands Jeremiah a cup of the wine of wrath to make the nations drink until they go insane. He starts with Jerusalem.
JER 25:19-38	The cup goes round: Pharaoh, Uz, the Philistine cities, Edom, Moab and Ammon, Tyre and Sidon, the desert kingdoms, Elam and the Medes, all the kings of the north, and every kingdom on earth, with the king of Sheshach drinking last. They are told to drink, be drunk, vomit and not get up, and refusing changes nothing, since judgment has begun at the city that bears God's name. Yahweh roars like a man treading grapes. The slain will lie unburied like dung.
JER 26:1-24	Early in Jehoiakim's reign, Jeremiah stands in the temple court and warns that if Judah will not walk in the law, this house will become like Shiloh. The priests, prophets and people seize him and say he must die. Tried before the princes, he answers that God sent him and that killing him puts innocent blood on the city; the princes and people rule that he does not deserve death. Elders recall Micah of Moresheth, whom Hezekiah spared, and Uriah, whom Jehoiakim killed. Ahikam son of Shaphan protects him.
JER 27:1-22	Wearing bonds and bars on his neck, Jeremiah sends word by visiting envoys to the kings of Edom, Moab, Ammon, Tyre and Sidon: God gives the earth to whomever he pleases, and has given these lands to Nebuchadnezzar, his servant. Any nation refusing the yoke gets sword, famine and plague. He tells Zedekiah the same, and warns the priests not to believe prophets promising the temple vessels back soon. The pillars and the bronze sea will go to Babylon.
JER 28:1-17	In Zedekiah's fourth year, Hananiah son of Azzur announces in the temple that God has broken Babylon's yoke and that within two years the vessels, Jeconiah and all the captives will be home. Jeremiah says he hopes so, then points out that a prophet promising peace is only proved genuine when it happens. Hananiah breaks the wooden bar off Jeremiah's neck; Jeremiah returns with word that bars of wood are now bars of iron, and that Hananiah will die this year. He dies.
JER 29:1-17	Jeremiah writes to the exiles in Babylon. The instructions are practical: build houses, plant gardens, marry, have children, and seek the peace of the city you were carried into, since your peace depends on its peace. Their prophets, diviners and dreams were never sent. After seventy years God will visit them and bring them back, because his thoughts toward them are of peace, to give them hope and a future. The king and people still in Jerusalem get sword, famine and plague, and become rotten figs.
JER 29:18-32	Those left in Jerusalem will be pursued with sword, famine and plague and tossed among the kingdoms, because they would not listen to the prophets God sent. Two named liars, Ahab son of Kolaiah and Zedekiah son of Maaseiah, will be killed in front of the exiles and become a curse formula. Shemaiah the Nehelamite, who wrote to Zephaniah the priest asking why Jeremiah had not been put in the stocks for telling the exiles to settle down, hears that he and his offspring will be punished.
JER 30:1-24	Jeremiah is told to write all these words in a book, because God will reverse the captivity of Israel and Judah. First the terror: men clutching their waists like women in labour, faces gone pale, the day of Jacob's trouble, out of which he will be saved. Then the diagnosis: your hurt is incurable, no one pleads your cause, and it was God who wounded you because your sins increased. But those who devoured Israel will be devoured, and the city they called an outcast will be rebuilt.
JER 31:1-20	The survivors of the sword found favour in the wilderness, and God says he has loved them with an everlasting love. The virgin of Israel will be rebuilt, dancing with tambourines and planting vineyards on the mountains of Samaria, until watchmen on the hills of Ephraim call people up to Zion. He will bring them from the ends of the earth, the blind, the lame and pregnant women among them. Then Rachel is heard weeping in Ramah for children who are no more.
JER 31:21-40	Set up road signs and turn back along the road you came by, backsliding daughter, because God has created a new thing: a woman will encompass a man. Judah will again bless the habitation of righteousness, with farmers and shepherds living there together. Jeremiah notes he woke at this point and his sleep had been sweet. Nobody will repeat the proverb about sour grapes, since each will die for his own sin. Then the new covenant: the law written on their hearts, everyone knowing him, their sin remembered no more.
JER 32:1-23	In Zedekiah's tenth year, with Babylon besieging Jerusalem, Jeremiah is shut in the court of the guard for prophesying that the city will fall. God tells him his cousin Hanamel will come offering him the field at Anathoth, and when Hanamel arrives Jeremiah knows the word is genuine. He buys the field for seventeen shekels, seals the deed before witnesses, and hands both copies to Baruch to store in a jar, because fields will be bought in this land again.
JER 32:24-44	Jeremiah finishes his prayer by pointing at the siege ramps: the city is already handed over, and yet you told me to buy a field. God answers with Jeremiah's own words as a question, is there anything too hard for me, then confirms that the Chaldeans will burn a city that has provoked him since it was built. Then the turn: he will gather them from every country, give them one heart, and make an everlasting covenant. Fields will be bought again throughout Benjamin and Judah.
JER 33:1-26	Still shut in the court of the guard, Jeremiah gets a second word: call to me and I will answer and show you great and difficult things you do not know. The houses torn down for siege defences will fill with the dead, and yet God will bring the city health and cure, reverse the captivity, and make it a name of joy. Where they call it waste, the voices of bridegroom and bride will be heard again. A righteous Branch will grow for David.
JER 34:1-22	While Nebuchadnezzar's army fights Jerusalem, Jeremiah tells Zedekiah the city will burn and he will speak to the king of Babylon mouth to mouth, though he will die in peace. Lachish and Azekah are the only fortified cities left. Then a covenant to free every Hebrew slave, which everyone kept and promptly reversed. God notes that the law required release after six years, and proclaims a liberty of his own: liberty to the sword, plague and famine. The Babylonian army, which had withdrawn, will come back and burn the city.
JER 35:1-19	Jeremiah brings the Rechabites into a room of the temple, sets out bowls and cups, and tells them to drink. They refuse: their ancestor Jonadab son of Rechab commanded them never to drink wine, never to build houses or plant vineyards but to live in tents, and they have kept all of it. The point is the comparison. Jonadab is still obeyed generations on, while God has sent prophet after prophet to Judah and been ignored. The Rechabites are promised they will never lack a man to stand before him.
JER 36:1-15	In Jehoiakim's fourth year Jeremiah is told to write everything he has been given since Josiah's day on a scroll, in the hope Judah will hear and turn. Baruch writes it from dictation. Jeremiah, banned from the temple, sends Baruch to read it there on a fast day. In the fifth year a fast is proclaimed and Baruch reads it in Gemariah's room. Micaiah reports to the princes in the palace, who send for Baruch and have him read it again.
JER 36:16-32	The princes hear it, take fright, and tell Baruch to hide with Jeremiah. They report to the king, who sends for the scroll and has it read aloud. Sitting by a brazier fire, he cuts it up with a penknife every few columns and burns it. Nobody tears their clothes. He orders Jeremiah and Baruch arrested, but God hides them. Jeremiah dictates it again to Baruch, with many similar words added.
JER 37:1-21	Zedekiah sends Jehucal and Zephaniah to ask Jeremiah to pray for the city. Egypt's army draws the Chaldeans off, but Jeremiah's word is that Pharaoh will go home and the siege will return and burn Jerusalem. Leaving for Benjamin to see to his property, Jeremiah is arrested at the gate as a defector, beaten, and imprisoned. Brought out secretly, he tells Zedekiah he will be handed to Babylon, and begs not to go back to that prison. He is moved to the court of the guard.
JER 38:1-14	Four officials hear Jeremiah telling the people that whoever stays in the city dies and whoever surrenders to the Chaldeans lives, and ask the king to have him killed. Zedekiah says he cannot stop them, so they lower Jeremiah by ropes into Malchijah's dungeon, which holds no water but mire, and he sinks into the mud. Ebedmelech the Ethiopian, a palace eunuch, tells the king he will starve there and is sent with thirty men. He fetches rags to pad Jeremiah's armpits under the ropes, and they haul him up.
JER 38:15-28	Jeremiah warns Zedekiah that answering will get him killed and the advice ignored anyway, so the king swears secretly not to hand him over. The terms: surrender and the city stands, refuse and it burns. Zedekiah admits his real fear is being mocked by the Judeans who already defected. If he refuses, the palace women will be led out and his wives and children taken. Told to keep it secret, Jeremiah tells the princes only that he begged not to return to Jonathan's house.
JER 39:1-18	In Zedekiah's eleventh year the wall is breached and the Babylonian officers take their seats in the middle gate. Zedekiah flees by night through the king's garden, is overtaken near Jericho, and is brought to Nebuchadnezzar at Riblah, who kills his sons in front of him, then blinds him and takes him in fetters to Babylon. The city is burned and the people deported. Nebuchadnezzar orders Jeremiah protected, and Ebedmelech is told he will be spared because he trusted God.
JER 40:1-16	Nebuzaradan finds Jeremiah in chains among the captives at Ramah, takes them off, and offers him a choice: Babylon, or anywhere he likes. He recommends Gedaliah son of Ahikam, whom Babylon has made governor, gives him food and lets him go. Jeremiah joins Gedaliah at Mizpah, where the captains left in the countryside are told that serving the Chaldeans is safe. Scattered Jews return from Moab, Ammon and Edom to a large harvest. Johanan warns that Baalis of Ammon has sent Ishmael to kill Gedaliah, who will not believe it.
JER 41:1-18	In the seventh month Ishmael son of Nethaniah comes to Mizpah, eats with Gedaliah, then kills him along with the Jews and Chaldean soldiers there. The next day eighty pilgrims arrive with offerings; Ishmael goes out weeping to meet them and kills them too, throwing the bodies into Asa's cistern. Ten survive by revealing hidden food stores. He takes the rest of Mizpah captive and heads for Ammon, but Johanan son of Kareah catches him at Gibeon and the captives change sides.
JER 42:1-22	Johanan son of Kareah and all the captains ask Jeremiah to pray and show them the way to go, swearing to obey whatever comes. After ten days the answer arrives: stay in the land and God will build and plant them, and they need not fear Babylon. Go to Egypt instead and the sword they fear will overtake them there, with famine close behind. Jeremiah tells them plainly that they have dealt deceitfully with themselves, and will die in the place they want to go.
JER 43:1-13	Azariah, Johanan and all the proud men accuse Jeremiah of lying, and blame Baruch for turning him against them. They take the whole remnant, including Jeremiah and Baruch, into Egypt as far as Tahpanhes. There God tells Jeremiah to hide large stones in the brickwork at Pharaoh's entry and announce that Nebuchadnezzar will set his throne on them. He will strike Egypt, burn the houses of its gods, wrap himself in the land the way a shepherd puts on his coat, and leave in peace.
JER 44:1-16	The word comes to the Jews living at Migdol, Tahpanhes, Memphis and in Pathros. They have seen Judah left desolate, and the cause was burning incense to gods they did not know. So why, God asks, do it again in Egypt, cutting off man, woman and nursing child and leaving no remnant? He sets his face against them: the remnant in Egypt will be consumed by sword and famine, from least to greatest, and only a few will see Judah again. The people answer that they will not listen.
JER 44:17-30	The people say they will keep every vow, burning incense to the queen of the sky as their fathers and kings did in Judah, because back then they had plenty of food and famine only came after they stopped. The women add that their husbands knew about the cakes. Jeremiah answers that God remembered that incense, which is exactly why the land became a desolation. He tells them to go ahead, and swears that no man of Judah in Egypt will say As the Lord Yahweh lives again.
JER 45:1-5	A short word to Baruch, dated to the fourth year of Jehoiakim, when he wrote Jeremiah's words in a book. God quotes Baruch's complaint back to him: sorrow added to my pain, worn out with groaning, no rest. The answer offers no comfort, only perspective: what God built he is breaking down, across the whole land. So Baruch should not seek great things for himself. The one promise is small: wherever he goes, he will escape with his life.
JER 46:1-28	The oracles against the nations begin with Egypt, beaten at Carchemish. Shields are readied and spears polished, then the question: why do I see them turning back, terror on every side? Egypt rises like the Nile in flood, but the day belongs to Yahweh. Go up to Gilead for balm, virgin daughter of Egypt: there is no healing. Then Nebuchadnezzar invades Egypt itself, and the beautiful heifer meets destruction from the north, her hired soldiers running like stall-fed calves. Jacob is told not to fear.
JER 47:1-7	A word concerning the Philistines, given before Pharaoh struck Gaza. Waters rise out of the north into an overflowing stream that covers the land and everyone in it. At the sound of stamping hooves, fathers do not turn back for their children, their hands too feeble. Every helper from Tyre and Sidon is cut off, Gaza is shaved bald and Ashkelon brought to nothing. The sword of Yahweh is asked how long until it rests, and cannot, having been given a command.
JER 48:1-26	The oracle against Moab names town after town: Nebo laid waste, Kiriathaim taken, Heshbon plotting to end Moab as a nation. Flee and save your lives, because your treasures will not help and Chemosh himself goes into captivity. Moab has sat undisturbed like wine settled on its lees, never poured from vessel to vessel, so his taste is unchanged; now men will pour him off and smash the jars. He will be ashamed of Chemosh as Israel was of Bethel, and will wallow in his own vomit.
JER 48:27-47	God asks whether Moab did not mock Israel, shaking his head whenever he spoke of him. Now Moab is told to leave the cities and live in the rocks like a dove nesting over a chasm, since his boasting has accomplished nothing. Then God says he will wail for Moab himself, weeping more than Jazer wept for the vine of Sibmah, because the wine presses are silent. The terror, the pit and the snare wait, yet Moab's captivity will one day be reversed.
JER 49:1-19	Concerning Ammon: God asks why Malcam possesses Gad if Israel has heirs, and promises war on Rabbah until it is a desolate heap. Concerning Edom: is wisdom gone from Teman? Grape gatherers leave gleanings and thieves stop when they have enough, but Esau is stripped bare, his hiding places uncovered. Those who never deserved the cup are drinking it, so Edom will drink too, and Bozrah becomes a curse. Edom nested as high as an eagle and will be brought down like Sodom.
JER 49:20-39	Edom will be dragged off like the little ones of the flock, an eagle spreading its wings over Bozrah. Concerning Damascus: Hamath and Arpad melt at the news, and fire devours the palaces of Ben Hadad. Concerning Kedar and Hazor, struck by Nebuchadnezzar: tents, flocks and camels carried off, a people living at ease with neither gates nor bars scattered to the winds. Concerning Elam: God breaks its bow and brings the four winds against it.
JER 50:1-23	The word concerning Babylon, announced openly: Babylon is taken, Bel disappointed, her idols shamed, because a nation from the north will empty her land. Israel and Judah will come back together weeping. God calls his people lost sheep whose shepherds led them astray, and whose devourers claimed innocence on the grounds that the sheep had sinned. So flee out of Babylon like goats ahead of the flock. Babylon's king will be punished as Assyria's was, and the hammer of the whole earth lies broken.
JER 50:24-46	God says he laid a snare and Babylon walked into it without noticing. He has opened his armoury and brought out the weapons of his indignation, and the attackers are told to come from the farthest border, open her storehouses and leave nothing; those who escape are to announce in Zion the vengeance taken for his temple. Israel's captors refuse to release them, but their Redeemer is strong and will plead their cause. A sword is announced against her princes, soldiers, horses and treasures, and a drought against her waters.
JHN 1:1-28	John begins before the beginning: the Word was with God and was God, and everything was made through him. A man called John is sent to testify to the light, not to be it. The world did not recognise him. The Word became flesh and lived among them. Questioned, John denies being the Christ, Elijah or the prophet: he is the voice in the wilderness.
JHN 1:29-51	John calls Jesus the Lamb of God who takes away the world's sin, and says he saw the Spirit descend on him. Two of his disciples follow Jesus. Andrew fetches Simon, whom Jesus renames Cephas; Philip fetches Nathanael, who asks whether anything good comes out of Nazareth. Told he was seen under the fig tree, Nathanael calls him Son of God.
JHN 2:1-25	At a wedding in Cana the wine runs out. Jesus says his hour has not come; his mother tells the servants to do whatever he says. Six purification jars are filled with water and drawn out as wine, the first sign. In Jerusalem he drives the animals from the temple and overturns the money changers' tables, speaking of raising a destroyed temple in three days.
JHN 3:1-36	Nicodemus, a Pharisee and ruler, comes at night. Jesus tells him no one sees the kingdom without being born anew, and that the Spirit is like wind. He speaks of the Son of Man lifted up as Moses lifted the serpent, of God giving his only Son, and of judgment as light coming into a world that preferred darkness. John says he must decrease.
JHN 4:1-27	In Samaria, Jesus stops at Jacob's well and asks a Samaritan woman for a drink, which she notes Jews do not do. He offers living water. Told to fetch her husband, she says she has none, and he says she has had five. He answers her question about which mountain with worship in spirit and truth, and tells her he is the Messiah.
JHN 4:28-54	The woman leaves her water pot and tells the town about a man who told her everything she did. The disciples urge Jesus to eat; he points to fields white for harvest. He stays two days, and many Samaritans believe. At Cana a nobleman begs him to come for his dying son; Jesus says the boy lives, and the fever broke at that hour.
JHN 5:1-22	At the pool of Bethesda the sick wait for the water to stir. A man ill thirty-eight years has no one to put him in. Jesus tells him to take up his mat and walk; it is the Sabbath, and he is told carrying the mat is unlawful. Jesus says his Father is still working, which they take as making himself equal with God.
JHN 5:23-47	Whoever does not honour the Son does not honour the Father. Anyone who believes has passed out of death, and the hour is coming when the dead will hear his voice. His own testimony would not count, so he lists John, the works the Father gave him, and the Father. They search the scriptures but will not come to him. Their accuser will be Moses.
JHN 6:1-34	He asks Philip where bread can be bought, to test him. Philip prices it at two hundred denarii, and Andrew mentions a boy with five barley loaves and two fish. Jesus seats five thousand men, feeds them, and has twelve baskets of scraps gathered. When they try to make him king he withdraws. That night the disciples see him walking on the sea.
JHN 6:35-71	Jesus says he is the bread of life. The crowd objects that they know his parents. Their fathers ate manna and died; the bread he gives is his flesh, and unless they drink his blood they have no life. He says this in the synagogue, and many disciples stop following him. Asked whether they will leave too, Peter says there is nowhere else to go.
JHN 7:1-26	Jesus stays in Galilee because Judea wants him dead. His brothers, who do not believe in him, tell him to go to the Feast of Booths; he says his time has not come, then goes up privately. The crowds are split between good man and deceiver. Halfway through the feast he teaches in the temple, and they wonder how an uneducated man knows so much.
JHN 7:27-53	They object that they know where he comes from, whereas the Christ's origin will be unknown. On the last day of the feast he calls the thirsty to drink, promising rivers of living water, which John says means the Spirit. The crowd divides over whether the Christ can come from Galilee. The officers return empty-handed, and Nicodemus asks whether the law condemns a man unheard.
JHN 8:1-29	Jesus is brought a woman caught in adultery, with a question about stoning meant to trap him. He stoops and writes on the ground, then says the one without sin should throw first; the accusers leave from the oldest. He does not condemn her. He says he is the light of the world, and answers the objection about self-testimony by naming the Father.
JHN 8:30-59	To those who believed him he says the truth will free them. They reply that they have never been in bondage, and he answers that everyone who sins is sin's slave. He tells them their father is the devil, a murderer and liar from the start. They call him a Samaritan. He says that before Abraham was, I AM, and they pick up stones.
JHN 9:1-41	Asked whose sin caused a man's blindness, Jesus says neither his nor his parents', makes mud, and sends him to wash in Siloam; he comes back seeing. They take him to the Pharisees, since it happened on a Sabbath. His parents, afraid of expulsion, refer them to their son. Questioned, he says he knows that he was blind and now sees, and is thrown out.
JHN 10:1-42	Jesus describes a sheepfold where the sheep know the shepherd's voice. He says he is the door, and the good shepherd who lays down his life, unlike a hired hand who runs from the wolf. He has other sheep not of this fold. At the Feast of Dedication he says he and the Father are one, and they take up stones.
JHN 11:1-31	Lazarus of Bethany falls sick, and Jesus stays two days where he is. He then proposes going back to Judea, which alarms the disciples, since people there tried to stone him. He says Lazarus has fallen asleep, then tells them plainly that he is dead. Martha meets him and says he could have prevented it; he tells her he is the resurrection and the life.
JHN 11:32-57	Mary falls at his feet and says the same thing her sister said. Jesus is troubled and weeps. At the tomb he orders the stone taken away; Martha objects that four days means a stench. He calls Lazarus out, and the dead man comes out. The council fears Rome will act, and Caiaphas says it is better for one man to die for the people.
JHN 12:1-25	Jesus is at Bethany, where Lazarus is at the table. Mary anoints his feet with a pound of nard. Judas asks why it was not sold for the poor, which John says was because he stole from the money box. Jesus says she kept it for his burial. The next day palm branches greet him riding a young donkey.
JHN 12:26-50	Jesus says his soul is troubled, and asks the Father to glorify his name. A voice answers from the sky; the crowd hears thunder or an angel. He speaks of being lifted up and drawing all people, then hides. John explains their unbelief from Isaiah, and notes that many rulers believed but kept quiet, preferring human praise.
JHN 13:1-38	Before Passover, Jesus ties on a towel and washes the disciples' feet. Peter objects, then asks for a full wash. He tells them to do the same for one another, says one of them will betray him, and gives the dipped bread to Judas, who goes out into the night. Then the new commandment, and the warning about Peter's denials.
JHN 14:1-31	His Father's house has many rooms, and he goes to prepare a place. Thomas says they do not know the way; he answers that he is the way, the truth and the life. Philip asks to see the Father and is told that seeing him is enough. He promises the Spirit of truth, and leaves them peace unlike the world's.
JHN 15:1-27	Jesus calls himself the true vine and the Father the farmer, who prunes the fruitful branches and removes the rest. Apart from him they can do nothing. He tells them to love one another as he loved them, no love being greater than dying for your friends. He calls them friends, not servants, then warns that the world hated him first.
JHN 16:1-33	They will be put out of synagogues, and killed by people who think it service to God. Jesus says his going is to their advantage, since otherwise the Counselor will not come. A little while and they will not see him; a little while and they will. Their sorrow will turn like a woman's in labour. They will scatter, but he has overcome the world.
JHN 17:1-26	Jesus prays that the hour has come, and asks the Father to glorify the Son. Eternal life, he says, is knowing the only true God and the one he sent. He asks that those given him be kept in the Father's name and be one, none lost except the son of destruction. He prays too for those who will believe through them.
JHN 18:1-21	Jesus crosses the Kidron into a garden Judas knows. Judas arrives with soldiers and lanterns; at Jesus' answer they fall back, and he tells them to let the others go. Peter cuts off the ear of the high priest's servant Malchus and is told to sheathe the sword. Annas questions Jesus while Peter, at the fire outside, denies being a disciple.
JHN 18:22-40	An officer slaps him, and Annas sends him bound to Caiaphas. Peter denies him twice more, once to a relative of the man whose ear he cut off, and the rooster crows. At the Praetorium the priests stay outside to avoid defilement, and say they cannot execute anyone. Jesus tells Pilate his kingdom is not of this world. Offered the custom, they call for Barabbas.
JHN 19:1-22	Pilate has him flogged, and the soldiers crown him with thorns and dress him in purple. Pilate presents him with the words behold the man; the priests say their law condemns him for making himself the Son of God. Jesus says Pilate's power is given from above. Pilate tries to release him until they mention Caesar, then sends him to Golgotha.
JHN 19:23-42	The soldiers divide his clothes and cast lots for the seamless coat. Four women stand by the cross, and he gives his mother and the disciple he loved to each other. He says he is thirsty, is given vinegar, says it is finished, and dies. A soldier pierces his side. Joseph of Arimathaea and Nicodemus bury him with a hundred pounds of spices.
JHN 20:1-31	Mary Magdalene finds the stone gone before dawn and reports the body taken. Peter and the disciple Jesus loved run to the tomb and find the linen and the head cloth folded separately. Mary takes Jesus for the gardener until he says her name. That evening he shows the disciples his hands and side. Thomas, absent, later answers my Lord and my God.
JHN 21:1-25	At the sea of Tiberias, Peter and six others fish all night and catch nothing. At daybreak a man on the beach tells them to cast on the right side, and the net fills with a hundred and fifty-three fish. Peter jumps in. After breakfast Jesus asks him three times whether he loves him, and tells him how he will die.
JOB 1:1-22	Job is blameless and rich, with seven thousand sheep and ten children, and sacrifices in case his sons have sinned. In the divine council Satan asks whether Job fears God for nothing, hedged about as he is. God permits an attack on everything but Job himself. Four messengers arrive: the oxen, the sheep, the camels, all ten children. Job tears his robe and worships.
JOB 2:1-13	The council meets again. Satan argues that skin is given for skin, so God permits an attack on Job's body but not his life. Job is struck with sores from sole to head and sits in the ashes scraping himself with broken pottery. His wife tells him to renounce God and die. Three friends sit with him seven days in silence.
JOB 3:1-26	Job breaks the silence by cursing the day he was born. He wants that day blotted from the calendar. He asks why he did not die at birth, since in the grave kings and prisoners and slaves lie at rest together. Why is light given to people who long for death and dig for it like treasure? The thing he feared has come.
JOB 4:1-21	Eliphaz answers first, gently. He points out that Job has strengthened others in trouble and now collapses when trouble reaches him. His argument is that the innocent never perish, and that lions still starve. Then he reports a night vision, a spirit passing before his face that made his hair stand up, asking whether a mortal can be more just than God.
JOB 5:1-27	Man is born to trouble as sparks fly upward. Eliphaz recommends taking the case to God, who catches the wise in their own cleverness. Then the pivot: the man God corrects is happy, because God wounds and binds up the same wound. He promises deliverance from six troubles and seven, peace with the animals, and a grave reached in full age.
JOB 6:1-30	Job wishes his grief could be weighed against the sand of the seas. God's arrows are in him. He asks whether a donkey brays over good grass, and asks God to finish the job and crush him. His friends, he says, are a wadi: full of ice in winter, gone by summer, so the caravans that counted on them find nothing.
JOB 7:1-21	Job compares life to forced labour, a hired hand watching for the shadow of evening. His nights are appointed misery, and his days run out faster than a weaver's shuttle. He asks whether he is the sea or a sea monster that God needs a guard on him, then asks God to look away long enough for him to swallow.
JOB 8:1-22	Bildad answers, less gently. He asks how long Job will blow words about like wind, and states that God does not pervert justice, which means Job's children died for their own sin. His advice is to make supplication and be pure. He appeals to earlier generations, then gives his image: the godless man's confidence is a spider's web that will not hold his weight.
JOB 9:1-35	Job agrees that no man can win a case against God, who moves mountains without their noticing and made the Bear, Orion and the Pleiades. Since God is both the stronger party and the one who summons the court, even a righteous man's own mouth would condemn him. God destroys the blameless and the wicked alike, and there is no umpire between them.
JOB 10:1-22	Job says he will give his complaint free rein. He asks whether God has eyes of flesh or years as short as a man's, that he needs to hunt out sin. He reminds God that these hands poured him out like milk and curdled him like cheese, and then set about destroying the work. Guilty or innocent, he cannot lift his head.
JOB 11:1-20	Zophar answers, offended by the volume of talk. He wishes God would speak up, and states that God is exacting less than Job's sin deserves. God's mystery is higher than heaven and deeper than Sheol. An empty-headed man will become wise when a wild donkey's colt is born a man. His terms: set your heart right, and misery will pass like water gone by.
JOB 12:1-25	Job opens sarcastically, granting that wisdom will die with them. The blameless man is now a joke to his neighbours while robbers' tents prosper. Ask the animals, the birds and the fish, he says, and they will tell you Yahweh's hand did this. Then his own catalogue of God's power, aimed at the powerful: counsellors stripped, judges made fools, chiefs left staggering like drunks.
JOB 13:1-28	Job says he has heard all of it and would rather take his case to the Almighty than to them. He calls them forgers of lies and worthless physicians whose best move would be complete silence, and asks whether they mean to lie on God's behalf, since he will examine them too. Their memorable sayings are proverbs of ashes. Why harass a driven leaf?
JOB 14:1-22	Job describes human life as short and full of trouble, growing like a flower and cut down, its months already counted. He contrasts man with a tree: a felled tree sprouts again at the scent of water, but a man lies down and does not rise until the heavens are no more. He wishes God would hide him in Sheol until the wrath passed.
JOB 15:1-35	Eliphaz returns, sharper. He accuses Job of filling himself with the east wind and of being condemned by his own mouth, and asks whether Job was born before the hills or sat in on God's private council. His doctrine: God does not even trust his holy ones. Then a portrait of the wicked man, hearing terrors, wandering for bread, his tents consumed by fire.
JOB 16:1-22	Job calls all three of them miserable comforters and says he could easily make their speeches if their positions were swapped, though he would use his mouth to strengthen instead. He describes God as an attacker: gnashing teeth, taking him by the neck and dashing him to pieces, setting him up as a target with archers around him. He has sewn sackcloth to his skin.
JOB 17:1-16	Job says his spirit is used up, his days are out and the grave is ready. He asks for a pledge, someone to strike hands and stand as his collateral, since nobody else will. He has been made a byword and people spit in his face. If he calls the worm his mother and sister, where is his hope, and who will see it?
JOB 18:1-21	Bildad objects to being treated as an animal and asks how long Job will hunt for words. Then his own portrait of the wicked man's end, built out of traps: his lamp goes out, his feet carry him into a net, a snare takes him by the heel, a noose waits hidden in the ground. His name vanishes, with no son or grandson behind him.
JOB 19:1-29	Job says they have reproached him ten times. God, he says, has walled up his path, uprooted his hope like a tree and built a siege ramp against his tent. Then the social damage: brothers gone, servants treating him as a stranger, his breath offensive to his wife. He wishes his words were engraved in rock, and declares that his Redeemer lives.
JOB 20:1-29	Zophar answers, stung by the rebuke. His thesis is that the triumph of the wicked is brief. The imagery is digestive: wickedness is sweet in his mouth and he keeps it under his tongue, but in his belly it turns to cobra venom, and the riches he swallowed he vomits up. A bronze arrow comes out through his liver.
JOB 21:1-34	Job asks them to listen and mock afterwards. His question is why the wicked live long and grow powerful: their children are established, their bulls always breed, their children dance to tambourine and harp, and they go down to Sheol in an instant after a comfortable life. How often, he asks, does the lamp of the wicked actually go out?
JOB 22:1-30	Eliphaz asks whether a man's righteousness benefits God at all, and then brings specific charges for the first time: Job took pledges for nothing, withheld water from the weary and bread from the hungry, sent widows away empty and broke the arms of the fatherless. Then the offer: lay your gold in the dust, and God himself will be your treasure.
JOB 23:1-17	Job wishes he knew where to find God, so he could lay out his case in order and fill his mouth with arguments. He believes God would listen rather than overpower him. But God cannot be found east, west, north or south. Still, God knows the way Job takes, and when tried he will come out like gold.
JOB 24:1-25	Job asks why God does not set fixed times for judgment, and catalogues what goes unpunished: men move boundary markers, drive off the orphan's donkey and take the widow's ox as a pledge. The poor sleep naked in the cold and hug rocks for shelter. Then the night workers: the murderer at dawn, the adulterer at twilight, burglars digging through houses.
JOB 25:1-6	Bildad's last speech, and the shortest in the book. Dominion and fear belong to God, who makes peace in the heights and whose armies cannot be counted. Given that, he asks how any man can be just with God. Even the moon has no brightness in God's sight, so a human being, twice called a worm, has no standing at all.
JOB 26:1-14	Job replies sarcastically, thanking them for the help given to the powerless and the wisdom supplied to a man with none. Then he outdoes Bildad at his own hymn: God stretches out the north over empty space and hangs the earth on nothing, binds water in clouds that do not burst, and pierces the swift serpent. All that is only the outskirts of his ways.
JOB 27:1-23	Job swears by the God who has taken away his right that he will not speak falsely and will hold his integrity until he dies. Then he takes over their material: this is the portion of the wicked. His children multiply for the sword, the silver he heaps up like dust is divided by better men, and he builds his house like a moth.
JOB 28:1-28	A poem about mining and wisdom. Men sink shafts far from anywhere, dangling on ropes, overturning mountains by the roots and bringing hidden things to light, in places no falcon has seen. But wisdom is not found that way. The deep says it is not here, and it cannot be bought with gold. God alone knows its place.
JOB 29:1-25	Job remembers his former life. God's lamp shone on his head, his children were around him, and rock poured out oil for him. When he came to the city gate young men hid and princes stopped talking. He earned it, he says, by rescuing the poor, making the widow's heart sing, being eyes to the blind and feet to the lame.
JOB 30:1-31	Now, Job says, he is mocked by younger men whose fathers he would not have trusted with his sheep dogs, outcasts who live in rocky holes and eat broom roots. He has become their song, and they spit in his face. He cries out and gets no answer. He is brother to jackals and companion to ostriches, his harp turned to mourning.
JOB 31:1-40	Job's final speech is a long oath of innocence, each charge answered with a curse on himself if it is true. He made a covenant with his eyes not to look at a young woman. If he committed adultery, let his wife grind for another. On his servants: the same God made them both in the womb. The words of Job are ended.
JOB 32:1-22	The three friends stop answering, since Job is righteous in his own eyes. A younger man, Elihu son of Barachel the Buzite, is angry at Job for justifying himself rather than God, and at the three for condemning Job without finding any answer. He held back out of respect for their age. He is full of words, like new wineskins ready to burst.
JOB 33:1-33	Elihu addresses Job directly, insisting he is clay like him and will not frighten him. He quotes Job's claim to be clean and treated as an enemy, and answers that God owes no account. God speaks in dreams and night visions, to keep a man's soul from the pit. Then an interpreting angel, one in a thousand, and God says he has found a ransom.
JOB 34:1-37	Elihu addresses the wise men present, quoting Job's claim that God has denied him his right. His argument is that God cannot commit injustice, since nobody appointed him over the earth, and if he withdrew his breath all flesh would perish. He asks whether Job has ever simply said he was guilty, and concludes that Job adds rebellion to his sin.
JOB 35:1-16	Elihu takes up Job's question about what profit there is in righteousness. He tells Job to look at the skies, higher than he is, and argues that sin does nothing to God and righteousness gives God nothing: both land on other people. When the oppressed cry out, nobody asks where God their Maker is, the one who gives songs in the night.
JOB 36:1-33	Elihu asks for more patience, since he still speaks on God's behalf. When people are bound in fetters God opens their ears and delivers the afflicted by means of their affliction. He warns Job not to long for the night, then turns to weather: God draws up water that falls as rain.
JOB 37:1-24	Elihu's own heart trembles at the storm. God's voice thunders under the whole sky and does great things nobody can comprehend. He tells snow to fall, which seals up men's hands from work and drives animals into their dens. Then he presses Job: can you spread out the sky like a cast metal mirror?
JOB 38:1-41	Yahweh answers Job out of the whirlwind, asking who darkens counsel with words without knowledge. Where was Job when the earth's foundations were laid and the morning stars sang together? Who shut the sea behind doors? The questions run through the gates of death, the treasuries of snow, and rain on wilderness where no one lives.
JOB 39:1-30	The questioning turns to animals. Does Job know when mountain goats give birth? Who set the wild donkey free in the salt land?
