The ostrich leaves her eggs in the dust because God withheld wisdom from her, and yet she outruns horse and rider. The horse mocks fear, snorts at the trumpet and smells battle from far off.
JOB 40:1-24	God asks whether the one who argues will contend with the Almighty, and Job answers briefly: he is of small account and lays his hand on his mouth. God resumes anyway, asking whether Job means to condemn him in order to justify himself. Then behemoth, with brass tubes for bones, unbothered while the river rises to his mouth.
JOB 41:1-34	God describes Leviathan at length. Can Job hook him, rope his nose, keep him as a pet for the girls? Anyone who lays a hand on him does not try twice. His scales are sealed so no air passes between them, his eyes are like the eyelids of morning, and torches come from his mouth. He is king over all the sons of pride.
JOB 42:1-17	Job answers that he spoke of things too wonderful for him, and that where he had only heard of God he now sees him, so he repents in dust and ashes. God is angry at Eliphaz and the other two for not speaking rightly as Job did, and Job prays for them. He is restored double, and his three daughters inherit alongside their brothers.
LUK 1:1-35	Luke dedicates his account to Theophilus. The priest Zacharias and his wife Elizabeth are old and childless. While he burns incense, Gabriel promises a son named John. Zacharias asks how he can be sure and is struck silent. Six months later Gabriel comes to Mary, promising a son named Jesus who will reign forever.
LUK 1:36-80	Gabriel points to Elizabeth's pregnancy, and Mary consents. At her greeting Elizabeth's baby leaps, and Mary sings of God scattering the proud, pulling princes off thrones and sending the rich away empty. Elizabeth gives birth; Zacharias writes the name John on a tablet, his speech returns, and he prophesies about a horn of salvation in David's house.
LUK 2:1-25	A census sends Joseph from Nazareth to Bethlehem with Mary. She gives birth and lays the child in a feeding trough, there being no room in the inn. An angel announces a Savior to shepherds, with the trough as the sign. The shepherds go and tell everyone; Mary ponders it all. On the eighth day he is named Jesus.
LUK 2:26-52	Simeon, promised he would not die before seeing the Lord's Christ, takes the child and asks to be released in peace. He tells Mary a sword will pierce her own soul. Anna, a prophetess widowed eighty-four years, gives thanks. At twelve Jesus stays behind after Passover, and his parents find him among the temple teachers.
LUK 3:1-38	Luke dates John's ministry by Tiberius, Pilate and the high priests. John preaches repentance at the Jordan, calls the crowds a brood of vipers, and tells them to share a spare coat and collect no more tax than appointed. He points to one who will baptize with Spirit and fire. Herod jails him. Jesus is baptized, and a voice names him Son.
LUK 4:1-23	In the wilderness Jesus fasts forty days and is tempted three times: bread from a stone, the kingdoms of the world for an act of worship, and a leap from the temple pinnacle. He answers each from scripture. At Nazareth he reads Isaiah, sits down, and says the scripture is fulfilled today. The crowd asks whether he is not Joseph's son.
LUK 4:24-44	No prophet is accepted at home, Jesus says, and reminds them Elijah was sent to a widow in Sidon and Elisha cleansed only Naaman. The synagogue drives him to the brow of the hill, but he walks through them. At Capernaum his authority astonishes people. He casts out an unclean spirit, heals Simon's mother-in-law, and at sunset heals everyone brought to him.
LUK 5:1-39	Jesus teaches from Simon's boat. Simon, who caught nothing all night, nets enough to break the nets, and he, James and John leave everything. A leper is cleansed by touch. A paralyzed man is lowered through the roof tiles; Jesus forgives his sins, is accused of blasphemy, and heals him as proof. Then Levi, and a question about fasting.
LUK 6:1-26	Disciples pick grain on a Sabbath, and Jesus answers with David eating the show bread. Watched by Pharisees, he restores a man's withered right hand. After a night of prayer he chooses twelve apostles. On a level place he blesses the poor, the hungry, the weeping and the hated, with matching woes on the rich.
LUK 6:27-49	Love your enemies, bless those who curse you, offer the other cheek. Treat others as you want to be treated. Do not judge or condemn, since the measure you use is measured back. Deal with the beam in your own eye first. Each tree is known by its fruit, and calling him Lord without obeying is a house without a foundation.
LUK 7:1-24	A centurion whose servant is dying sends elders. As Jesus approaches, he sends friends to say a word will do. Jesus says he has found no such faith in Israel. At the gate of Nain he meets the funeral of a widow's only son, touches the coffin and raises him. John sends two disciples to ask whether he is the one.
LUK 7:25-50	Jesus asks what they went out to see: not fine clothing, but a prophet. Tax collectors agree God is just; the Pharisees do not. At a Pharisee's table a woman known as a sinner wets his feet with tears. Jesus tells Simon about two debtors, notes Simon offered no water, and tells her that her faith has saved her.
LUK 8:1-27	Jesus travels the towns with the twelve Mary Magdalene, Joanna and Susanna, who fund the group. He tells of seed falling, then explains it. A lamp is not lit to be hidden. Told his mother and brothers are outside, he says his family are those who hear the word and do it. He stills a storm on the lake.
LUK 8:28-56	The man gives his name as Legion. The demons ask to be sent into a herd of pigs, which drowns. The townspeople find him clothed and sane, and ask Jesus to leave. Jairus begs him to come for his dying daughter. On the way a woman touches his cloak and is healed. Word comes that the girl is dead; Jesus calls her and she rises.
LUK 9:1-31	The twelve are given authority and sent out with nothing. Herod, hearing the reports, is perplexed by talk that John has risen. At Bethsaida five loaves and two fish feed five thousand men. Jesus asks who they say he is; Peter answers, the Christ of God. He predicts his death. On a mountain, Moses and Elijah speak of his departure at Jerusalem.
LUK 9:32-62	proposes three tents without knowing what he is saying. A cloud covers them. Next day Jesus frees a boy. He says the Son of Man will be handed over, which they fear to ask about. They argue about who is greatest, and he sets a child beside him. He rebukes James and John for offering to call down fire.
LUK 10:1-21	Jesus sends seventy others out in pairs. They carry no purse, greet no one on the road, stay in one house, eat what they are given, heal the sick. Where refused, they wipe off the dust. Chorazin, Bethsaida and Capernaum are warned. The seventy return delighted that demons obey them, and are told to rejoice instead that their names are written in heaven.
LUK 10:22-42	A lawyer asks what he must do, answers his own question from the law, then asks who counts as a neighbour. Jesus describes a man robbed on the Jericho road, a priest and a Levite who pass by, and a Samaritan who binds his wounds. The lawyer says the neighbour showed mercy. At a village Martha complains that Mary sits listening.
LUK 11:1-28	Asked to teach them to pray, Jesus gives a short prayer. He follows it with a man banging on a friend's door at midnight, who gets bread through persistence. After he casts out a mute demon, some credit Beelzebul; he answers that a divided kingdom falls. An evicted spirit returns with seven worse.
LUK 11:29-54	Jesus calls it an evil generation seeking a sign. The eye is the lamp of the body, so check that the light in you is not darkness. Dining with a Pharisee, he is criticised for not washing, and answers with cups clean outside. The woes follow: tithing mint while bypassing justice, loving the best seats, being hidden graves.
LUK 12:1-30	Jesus warns the disciples against the yeast of the Pharisees, since nothing hidden stays hidden. They need not fear those who can only kill the body: five sparrows sell for two coins and none is forgotten. Asked to settle an inheritance dispute, he refuses and warns against greed, telling of a rich man whose harvest outgrows his barns and who dies that night.
LUK 12:31-59	Seek the kingdom. Sell what you have and give it away, since the heart follows the treasure. Lamps stay lit for a master, who will serve the servants he finds awake. Jesus says he brings division rather than peace, and the crowd can read the weather but not the times.
LUK 13:1-35	Told about Galileans killed by Pilate, Jesus asks whether they were worse sinners and warns of the same end without repentance. A fig tree gets one more year. On a Sabbath he straightens a woman, and asks the objecting ruler whether they do not water animals that day. Warned that Herod wants him dead, he calls him a fox.
LUK 14:1-35	At a Pharisee's house, Jesus asks whether healing is lawful, gets silence, and heals a man. He tells the host to invite those who cannot repay. He describes a feast whose guests all make excuses, so the servant fills the house from the streets. A disciple must put him before family, carry a cross, and count the cost.
LUK 15:1-32	The Pharisees complain that he eats with tax collectors, so Jesus tells three stories. A shepherd leaves ninety-nine sheep to find one. Then a younger son takes his share and wastes it abroad; his father sees him far off and runs to meet him. The elder brother refuses to come in, listing his years of service.
LUK 16:1-31	A manager about to be dismissed cuts the bills of his master's debtors, and is commended for shrewdness. Nobody serves God and money. Then a rich man in purple and a beggar named Lazarus both die: Lazarus is carried to Abraham, the rich man is in torment. They have Moses and the prophets, Abraham says.
LUK 17:1-37	Anyone who makes a little one stumble would be better off drowned with a millstone. Asked for more faith, Jesus says mustard-seed faith could uproot a sycamore. Ten lepers are sent to the priests and cleansed; the one who comes back to thank him is a Samaritan. As in the days of Noah and Lot, ordinary life will be going on.
LUK 18:1-43	A widow wears down a judge who fears neither God nor man. A Pharisee lists his fasts while a tax collector asks only for mercy, and the second goes home justified. A ruler who has kept the commandments is told to sell everything, and leaves sad. Near Jericho a blind beggar shouts over those hushing him and is given his sight.
LUK 19:1-23	In Jericho, Zacchaeus, a rich chief tax collector, climbs a sycamore to see over the crowd. Jesus calls him by name, which the crowd resents. Zacchaeus promises half his goods to the poor and fourfold restitution. Near Jerusalem, Jesus tells of a nobleman who goes abroad to receive a kingdom, leaving ten servants a mina each, and returns to call for accounts.
LUK 19:24-48	The unused mina goes to the servant with ten, and the king's enemies are killed before him. Jesus rides into Jerusalem on a colt, cloaks on the road, disciples praising God. Told to quiet them, he says the stones would cry out instead. Seeing the city, he weeps, foretelling a siege. In the temple he drives out the traders and teaches daily while the priests look for a way to kill him.
LUK 20:1-47	Asked by what authority he acts, Jesus counters with a question about John's baptism; unable to answer safely, the leaders plead ignorance. He tells of tenants who kill the owner's son, and the vineyard goes to others. Spies ask about paying tax to Caesar; he asks whose image is on the coin. Sadducees pose a woman married to seven brothers; he answers that the resurrected do not marry. He asks how David can call the Christ his Lord, and warns against scribes who devour widows' houses.
LUK 21:1-38	Watching the rich give to the treasury, Jesus says a poor widow's two coins are worth more, since she gave everything she had. He says the temple's stones will all come down. Believers will be arrested first and should not prepare a defense, since he will give them words. When armies surround Jerusalem, Judea should flee, and pregnant and nursing women will suffer most. Then come signs in sun, moon and stars, and the Son of Man in a cloud.
LUK 22:1-36	As Passover approaches, the chief priests look for a way to kill Jesus, and Satan enters Judas, who agrees on a price. Peter and John prepare the meal in a borrowed upper room. At the table Jesus gives bread as his body and a cup as the new covenant, and says his betrayer's hand is on the table. They argue about who is greatest; he says the greatest must serve. He tells Simon that Peter will deny him three times, then tells them to buy a sword.
LUK 22:37-71	Told two swords are enough, Jesus goes to the Mount of Olives and asks that the cup be taken away; an angel strengthens him, and his sweat is like drops of blood. He finds the disciples asleep from grief. Judas arrives leading a crowd and kisses him. Someone cuts off a servant's ear, and Jesus heals it. In the courtyard Peter denies him three times; the rooster crows, and he weeps. Blindfolded and beaten, he is asked to prophesy.
LUK 23:1-28	Before Pilate they accuse Jesus of subverting the nation and claiming to be king. Pilate finds no case and sends him to Herod, who mocks him and sends him back; Herod and Pilate become friends that day. Pilate finds nothing worthy of death and offers to release him after a beating. The crowd calls instead for Barabbas, jailed for insurrection and murder, and shouts until it wins. Simon of Cyrene is made to carry the cross, and Jesus tells the mourning women to weep for themselves.
LUK 23:29-56	Jesus tells the women that harder days are coming. At the Skull he is crucified between two criminals and asks the Father to forgive them, since they do not know what they are doing. Rulers and soldiers mock him. One criminal insults him; the other says he has done nothing wrong, and is promised Paradise. Darkness covers the land, the temple veil tears, and he dies. The centurion calls him righteous, and Joseph lays him in an unused tomb.
LUK 24:1-26	At dawn the women find the tomb empty, and two men in dazzling clothes ask why they seek the living among the dead. They report to the eleven, who call it nonsense, though Peter runs to the tomb, sees the linen lying alone, and goes home wondering. That day two of them walk to Emmaus, and Jesus joins them unrecognized. Cleopas explains about the crucified prophet and their dashed hope. Jesus calls them slow to believe the prophets.
LUK 24:27-53	Starting from Moses and the prophets, Jesus explains the scriptures about himself. At Emmaus they press him to stay, and when he breaks the bread their eyes are opened and he vanishes. They return to Jerusalem and find the eleven saying the Lord has appeared to Simon. Jesus stands among them, and they take him for a spirit until he eats broiled fish before them. He says forgiveness will be preached to all nations. At Bethany he blesses them and is carried up.
MAT 1:1-25	Three sets of fourteen generations run from Abraham through David to Joseph, naming Tamar, Rahab, Ruth and the wife of Uriah. Mary is found pregnant by the Holy Spirit. Joseph plans a quiet divorce until an angel tells him in a dream to marry her and name the child Jesus.
MAT 2:1-23	Wise men from the east ask Herod where the king of the Jews has been born. The chief priests name Bethlehem, and Herod sends them on to report back. They find the child, give gold, frankincense and myrrh, and go home another way. Joseph is warned to Egypt, and Herod kills the boys of Bethlehem two and under. The family returns after his death and settles in Nazareth.
MAT 3:1-17	John the Baptizer preaches repentance in the desert in camel hair, eating locusts and wild honey, and the whole Jordan region comes to be baptized. He calls the arriving Pharisees and Sadducees a brood of vipers, saying descent from Abraham counts for nothing. Jesus comes to be baptized; John objects, then agrees. The heavens open, the Spirit descends as a dove, and a voice names him God's Son.
MAT 4:1-25	Fasting forty days, Jesus is tempted three times: bread from stones, a leap from the temple, and every kingdom in exchange for worship. He answers each with scripture, the devil leaves, and angels attend him. Hearing John is arrested, he moves to Capernaum and preaches repentance. He calls Peter and Andrew, then James and John, from their nets, and crowds follow from across the region.
MAT 5:1-24	Jesus goes up a mountain and teaches, blessing the poor in spirit, the mourners, the gentle, the hungry for righteousness, the merciful, the pure in heart, the peacemakers and the persecuted. His hearers are salt and light, meant to be seen. He has not come to abolish the law but to fulfill it. Anger toward a brother carries the weight of murder, and a gift at the altar waits until the quarrel is settled.
MAT 5:25-48	Settle with your accuser before court. A lustful look is already adultery, and an offending eye or hand is better lost than kept. Divorce is restricted and oaths dropped for a plain yes or no. Retaliation gives way to the turned cheek, the surrendered cloak and the second mile. Enemies are to be loved, since God sends sun and rain on everyone alike.
MAT 6:1-34	Charity, prayer and fasting are to be done unseen, because a performance already has its reward. Jesus gives a short prayer, then warns that unforgiving people are not forgiven. Treasure on earth rots or is stolen, and the heart follows the treasure. No one can serve both God and money. Do not be anxious: birds do not sow, lilies outdress Solomon, and worry adds nothing.
MAT 7:1-29	Judging invites judgment by the same measure, and the speck in a brother's eye waits until the beam in your own is gone. Ask, seek and knock, since a father asked for bread does not hand over a stone. Do to others what you want done to you. The gate is narrow, false prophets are known by their fruit, and the sermon ends with two houses, one on rock and one on sand.
MAT 8:1-34	Jesus cleanses a leper by touch and sends him to the priest. A centurion asks for a healing at a distance and is praised for a faith greater than any in Israel. Peter's mother-in-law is healed of a fever. Two would-be followers are warned the Son of Man has nowhere to lay his head. He stills a storm, then sends two demoniacs' demons into pigs, which drown, and the town asks him to leave.
MAT 9:1-38	A paralyzed man is brought on a bed; Jesus forgives his sins, and when scribes call it blasphemy he heals the man as proof. He calls Matthew from the tax office and eats with tax collectors, wanting mercy rather than sacrifice. On the way to raise a ruler's dead daughter, a woman bleeding twelve years is healed by touching his clothes. Two blind men and a mute demoniac are healed, and the workers are few.
MAT 10:1-42	Jesus gives the twelve authority over unclean spirits and disease and names them, ending with Judas Iscariot. They are sent to Israel with no money, bag, spare coat or staff, and told to shake the dust off their feet at any town that refuses them. He warns of floggings, trials before governors and betrayal within families, and says the Spirit will supply their words. Sparrows are not forgotten and their hairs are counted.
MAT 11:1-30	From prison John sends to ask whether Jesus is the one, and is answered with a list: the blind see, the lame walk, lepers are cleansed, the deaf hear, the dead rise, the poor hear good news. Jesus praises John to the crowd as more than a prophet, and Elijah for those willing to hear it. He denounces Chorazin, Bethsaida and Capernaum, then invites the weary to take his yoke, which is easy.
MAT 12:1-28	Hungry disciples pick grain on the Sabbath, and Jesus answers the Pharisees with David eating the show bread and priests who work in the temple, calling himself Lord of the Sabbath. In the synagogue he asks who would not lift a sheep out of a pit, and restores a man's withered hand. The Pharisees begin plotting to destroy him. When he heals a blind and mute demoniac they credit Beelzebul.
MAT 12:29-50	Plundering a strong man's house requires binding him first. Every other sin can be forgiven, but blasphemy against the Holy Spirit cannot. A tree is known by its fruit, and every idle word will be accounted for. Asked for a sign, Jesus offers only Jonah: three days in the whale, three days in the heart of the earth. An evicted spirit returns with seven worse. Told his mother and brothers are outside, he points to his disciples.
MAT 13:1-30	From a boat, Jesus tells of a farmer whose seed falls on the roadside, on rocky ground, among thorns and on good soil. Asked why he teaches in parables, he quotes Isaiah on people who hear without understanding, then explains it: the word snatched away, joy without root, cares and riches, and soil that understands. In a second parable an enemy sows weeds among the wheat, and the owner leaves both until harvest.
MAT 13:31-58	More comparisons follow: a mustard seed that grows into a tree birds nest in, and yeast a woman hides in three measures of meal. Indoors, Jesus explains the weeds: the sower is the Son of Man, the field the world, the harvest the end of the age. He adds hidden treasure, a costly pearl and a dragnet sorted on the beach. At home he is dismissed as the carpenter's son.
MAT 14:1-36	Herod hears about Jesus and assumes John the Baptizer has risen. John had called his marriage to Herodias unlawful; at Herod's birthday her daughter danced and asked for John's head. Jesus withdraws by boat, is followed, and feeds five thousand men from five loaves and two fish. That night he walks on the water, and Peter walks, sinks, and is pulled up.
MAT 15:1-39	Pharisees and scribes complain that the disciples eat without washing their hands. Jesus answers that their tradition voids God's command, since money declared devoted to God excuses a man from supporting his parents. What comes out of the mouth defiles, not what goes in. A Canaanite woman answers his line about dogs with the crumbs, and her daughter is healed. He then feeds four thousand from seven loaves.
MAT 16:1-28	Pharisees and Sadducees ask for a sign from heaven, and Jesus offers only the sign of Jonah: they can read the sky for weather but not the times. He warns about the yeast of the Pharisees, and the disciples think he means bread. At Caesarea Philippi Peter answers that he is the Christ, and is called the rock and given the keys. When Jesus says he must suffer and be killed, Peter objects and is called a stumbling block.
MAT 17:1-27	Jesus takes Peter, James and John up a high mountain, where his face shines and his clothes turn white, and Moses and Elijah appear. Peter offers to build three tents. A cloud covers them and a voice names Jesus his beloved Son. Coming down, they are told Elijah has already come in John. Jesus cures an epileptic boy the disciples could not, and sends Peter to find the temple tax in a fish's mouth.
MAT 18:1-35	Asked who is greatest, Jesus stands a child among them and says the kingdom belongs to those who become like children. Anyone who makes a little one stumble would be better off drowned with a millstone. A shepherd leaves ninety-nine to find one stray. A brother who sins gets private correction, then witnesses, then the assembly. Peter asks whether seven times is enough forgiveness and is told seventy times seven.
MAT 19:1-30	Jesus is tested on whether divorce is lawful for any reason. He answers from creation, that the two become one flesh, and says Moses permitted divorce because of hard hearts. Children are brought and the disciples try to stop them. A rich man asks what he must do for eternal life, is told to sell everything, and leaves sad. A camel passes a needle's eye more easily than a rich man enters the kingdom.
MAT 20:1-34	A landowner hires laborers through the day and pays everyone the same, starting with the last. The early workers complain and are told they got what they agreed to. Going up to Jerusalem, Jesus tells the twelve he will be condemned, flogged and crucified, and raised on the third day. The mother of James and John asks for the two seats beside him; he says the seats are not his to give. Leaving Jericho he restores the sight of two blind men.
MAT 21:1-23	Near Jerusalem, two disciples fetch a donkey and colt, which Matthew ties to the prophecy of a humble king. Cloaks go on the road, the crowds shout Hosanna. In the temple he drives out the buyers and sellers, calling it a den of robbers instead of a house of prayer. He heals the blind and lame there. Next morning he curses a fruitless fig tree, which withers. Back in the temple the chief priests demand to know by what authority he acts.
MAT 21:24-46	Jesus offers to answer if they first say whether John's baptism came from heaven or from men. Cornered either way, they claim not to know, so he declines too. He tells of two sons: one refuses to work and goes, the other agrees and does not. Next, a landowner leases a vineyard, and the tenants kill the servants sent for the fruit, then kill his son to take the inheritance. They realize the parables are about them, but fear the crowd too much to arrest him.
MAT 22:1-46	A king's wedding invitations are refused, some guests killing the servants sent to call them, so the king destroys them and fills the hall from the highways. The Pharisees ask about paying tax to Caesar; Jesus asks whose image is on the coin. Sadducees pose a woman married to seven brothers, and are told the resurrected do not marry. A lawyer asks for the greatest commandment and gets two. Jesus then asks how David can call the Christ his Lord, and nobody dares question him again.
MAT 23:1-39	Jesus tells the crowds to do what the scribes and Pharisees say but not what they do, since they load burdens on others and want the best seats and the title Rabbi. His followers are to take no such titles. Then come the woes: shutting the kingdom, tithing mint and dill while neglecting justice and mercy, straining out gnats and swallowing camels, and honoring the prophets their ancestors killed. He laments over Jerusalem as a hen whose chicks would not come, and says her house is left desolate.
MAT 24:1-27	Shown the temple buildings, Jesus says not one stone will be left on another. On the Mount of Olives the disciples ask when. He warns first of deception: many will claim to be the Christ. Wars, famines, plagues and earthquakes are only the beginning of birth pains. His followers will be handed over, killed and hated by all nations, but the good news will reach all nations first. When the abomination Daniel spoke of stands in the holy place, Judea should run for the mountains.
MAT 24:28-51	The coming will be as unmistakable as vultures over a carcass or lightning across the sky. Sun and moon go dark, stars fall, the Son of Man appears, and angels gather the chosen. A fig tree in leaf means summer is near; the hour is unknown, even to angels. As in Noah's flood, life goes on until it doesn't: one taken, one left. A servant left in charge is found feeding the household, or beating it.
MAT 25:1-25	Ten virgins take lamps to meet a bridegroom; five bring extra oil, five do not. He is delayed, they sleep, and at midnight the cry goes up. The five without oil go to buy more, and the door is shut on them. The lesson: watch, since the hour is unknown. A man going abroad entrusts three servants with five, two and one talents. The first two trade and double their share and are praised. The third, calling his master harsh, buries his and returns it untouched.
MAT 25:26-46	The master calls the third servant wicked and lazy: he should have banked the money for interest. His talent goes to the servant with ten, and he is thrown out. Jesus then describes the Son of Man in glory, separating all nations as a shepherd separates sheep from goats. Those on the right inherit the kingdom for feeding, clothing and visiting him: whatever was done for the least of his brothers was done for him. Those on the left hear the same, for the same left undone.
MAT 26:1-39	Jesus predicts his crucifixion in two days; the priests plot to seize him quietly. At Bethany a woman anoints his head with costly oil, which he calls preparation for burial. Judas takes thirty pieces of silver to betray him. At the Passover meal Jesus names his betrayer and gives the disciples bread and wine as his body and blood. He predicts the disciples will scatter and Peter will deny him three times. In Gethsemane he asks that the cup pass from him.
MAT 26:40-75	Three times Jesus prays and finds Peter, James and John asleep. Judas arrives with an armed crowd and marks him with a kiss. A disciple cuts off a servant's ear; Jesus tells him to put the sword away, and the disciples flee. Before Caiaphas, testimony fails until two witnesses cite his words about the temple. Asked if he is the Christ, he says they will see the Son of Man at God's right hand. He is condemned, spat on and beaten. Outside, Peter denies him three times and weeps bitterly.
MAT 27:1-32	The chief priests hand Jesus to Pilate. Judas returns the silver, is told it's his problem, throws it into the sanctuary and hangs himself; the priests buy the potter's field with it. Before Pilate, Jesus answers about being king of the Jews, then says nothing more. Pilate offers Barabbas or Jesus; his wife sends word of a dream. The crowd picks Barabbas and calls for crucifixion. Pilate washes his hands and hands Jesus over after flogging. Soldiers mock him and compel Simon of Cyrene to carry the cross.
MAT 27:33-66	At Golgotha he refuses the wine mixed with gall and is crucified between two robbers, his clothes divided by lot. Passers-by mock him. After three hours of darkness he cries out asking why God has forsaken him, cries out again, and dies. The temple veil tears top to bottom, the earth quakes, tombs open. The centurion says he was truly the Son of God. Women who followed him watch from a distance. Joseph of Arimathaea lays the body in his own tomb; the priests seal it and post a guard.
MAT 28:1-20	At dawn the two Marys reach the tomb, where an earthquake accompanies an angel who rolls the stone away and sits on it, and the guards go rigid with fear. The angel says Jesus has risen and sends the women to tell the disciples to go to Galilee. Jesus meets them on the way. The guards report to the priests, who pay them to claim the disciples stole the body. The eleven worship him in Galilee, though some doubt. He claims all authority and sends them to disciple all nations.
PRO 1:1-33	The book states its purpose: to teach wisdom, justice and discretion. The fear of Yahweh is the beginning of knowledge. A father tells his son to refuse sinners who invite him to ambush the innocent and share one purse, since their trap catches themselves. Then Wisdom shouts at the city gates, asking how long the simple will love simplicity.
PRO 2:1-22	The instruction is conditional: if you receive these words and search for wisdom like silver and hidden treasure, then you will understand the fear of Yahweh. Once wisdom enters the heart, discretion stands guard against men who take pleasure in evil, and against the woman who flatters and whose house leads down to the dead.
PRO 3:1-35	The father promises length of days to the son who keeps his teaching. Then the famous instruction: trust Yahweh with all your heart, do not lean on your own understanding, and he will make your paths straight. Discipline is a father's love, not rejection. Wisdom is worth more than rubies. The chapter closes on neighbour rules: pay what is owed today.
PRO 4:1-27	The father passes on what his own father taught him: get wisdom, and buy it with everything you own if that is the price. He urges his son to avoid the path of the wicked entirely rather than walk carefully on it, since such men cannot sleep unless they have made someone fall. The righteous path is like dawn brightening toward full day.
PRO 5:1-23	A warning about adultery. The adulteress speaks lips that drip honey and a mouth smoother than oil, but the end is bitter as wormwood and her steps go straight down to Sheol. The positive advice uses water: drink from your own cistern, keep your springs to yourself, and rejoice in the wife of your youth, a loving doe.
PRO 6:1-35	If you have guaranteed a stranger's debt, get out of it without sleeping, like a bird escaping a snare. The sluggard is sent to study the ant, who has no overseer and still stores food in summer, while a little folding of the hands brings poverty like an armed robber. Then six things Yahweh hates and seven that are an abomination.
PRO 7:1-27	The father illustrates the alternative with a story he claims to have watched through his window lattice. A young man without understanding walks past a corner at twilight. A woman meets him and delivers a full pitch: her couch is perfumed with myrrh, and her husband is away with a bag of money until the full moon. He follows like an ox to slaughter.
PRO 8:1-36	Wisdom speaks for herself, standing at the crossroads and the city gates. She insists her words are worth more than silver or rubies, and that kings reign by her. Then the famous passage on her age: Yahweh possessed her at the beginning of his work, before the depths, the mountains or the dust of the world. She was the craftsman at his side.
PRO 9:1-18	Two invitations, one at each end. Wisdom builds her house with seven carved pillars, sets the table and sends her maids to call the simple in to eat and live. Then the mirror image: the foolish woman sits at her own door and uses the same words, adding that stolen water is sweet. Her guests are already in the depths of Sheol.
PRO 10:1-32	Solomon's collection of individual proverbs begins, most of them a single contrast between the righteous and the wicked. The themes are work and speech. Diligent hands bring wealth while a lazy hand brings poverty. Too many words guarantee sin. Love covers all wrongs where hatred stirs up strife. The sluggard is vinegar to the teeth and smoke in the eyes of whoever sent him.
PRO 11:1-31	The chapter opens on commercial honesty, since a false balance is an abomination and accurate weights are Yahweh's delight. Generosity is treated as arithmetic that works backwards: the one who scatters increases, and hoarding grain earns a public curse. Riches are no help on the day of wrath. A beautiful woman without discretion is a gold ring in a pig's snout.
PRO 12:1-28	More paired sayings, weighted toward speech, work and temperament. Loving correction is knowledge and hating reproof is stupidity, stated bluntly. A righteous man looks after his animal. Tilling your land brings bread while chasing fantasies does not. Rash speech pierces like a sword and the wise tongue heals. Anxiety weighs the heart down and a kind word lifts it.
PRO 13:1-25	The subjects are the mouth, money and discipline. Guarding your mouth guards your life, while opening your lips wide brings ruin. Some pretend to be rich with nothing and some pretend to be poor while wealthy. The best known line arrives here: hope deferred makes the heart sick, and fulfilled longing is a tree of life. Walking with wise men makes you wise.
PRO 14:1-35	A wise woman builds her house and a foolish one pulls it down with her own hands. Several sayings admit complication rather than stating a rule: the heart knows its own bitterness, and there is a way that seems right to a man which ends in death. Where there are no oxen the stall is clean, but the harvest comes by the ox's strength.
PRO 15:1-33	The chapter is mostly about how things are said. The gentle answer turns away wrath while the harsh word stirs anger, and a gentle tongue is a tree of life. Two comparisons carry the values: better a little with the fear of Yahweh than great treasure with trouble, and better a dinner of herbs where there is love than a fattened calf with hatred.
PRO 16:1-33	A cluster of sayings about human planning and divine direction: a man's heart plans his course but Yahweh directs his steps, and all a man's ways look clean to himself while Yahweh weighs the motives. A block on kings follows, since their wrath is a messenger of death. Then the familiar lines: pride goes before destruction, and ruling your own spirit beats capturing a city.
PRO 17:1-28	It opens with a dry morsel eaten in quiet beating a feast full of strife. Mocking the poor insults their Maker. Covering an offence promotes love while repeating the matter separates friends. It is safer to meet a bear robbed of her cubs than a fool in his folly. Starting a quarrel is like breaching a dam, so stop it early. A friend loves at all times. Even a fool is thought wise while he keeps his mouth shut.
PRO 18:1-24	The chapter is largely about talk and its consequences. A fool takes no delight in understanding, only in airing his own opinion, and answering before you have heard is folly and shame. Death and life are in the power of the tongue. Yahweh's name is a strong tower, while a rich man's wealth is a strong city only in his own imagination. Whoever pleads first sounds right until someone cross-examines him. One friend can stick closer than a brother.
PRO 19:1-29	Poverty runs through this chapter. Better a poor man with integrity than a perverse fool; wealth adds friends while the poor lose even their relatives; and whoever pities the poor lends to Yahweh, who will repay him. Zeal without knowledge is no good. A hot-tempered man rescued from the consequences will only need rescuing again. Many plans are in a man's heart but Yahweh's counsel prevails. The sluggard cannot get his hand from the dish to his mouth.
PRO 20:1-30	It opens with drink: wine is a mocker, beer is a brawler, and anyone led astray by them is not wise. Several sayings are about self-knowledge and its limits: who can say his heart is pure, and the spirit of man is Yahweh's lamp searching his innermost parts. Observational humour appears in the buyer who says it is no good and then boasts once he has left. Do not repay evil; wait for Yahweh. Grey hair is the splendour of old men.
PRO 21:1-31	The king's heart is in Yahweh's hand like a watercourse, turned wherever he wishes, and doing righteousness and justice pleases him more than sacrifice. Whoever stops his ears at the cry of the poor will himself cry out unheard. Two sayings recommend the corner of a roof, or even desert land, over a house shared with a contentious woman. The sluggard's own desire kills him because his hands refuse to work. The horse is prepared for battle, but victory belongs to Yahweh.
PRO 22:1-29	A good name beats great riches, and rich and poor share one maker. Train up a child in the way he should go and he will not depart from it. The borrower is servant to the lender, and the sluggard claims there is a lion in the street. Halfway through, the collection changes shape into longer instruction introduced as thirty excellent sayings: do not exploit the poor in court, because Yahweh will plead their case, and do not move the ancient boundary stone.
PRO 23:1-35	Advice for eating with powerful people: treat a stingy host's delicacies as food you will end up vomiting, along with your compliments. Do not wear yourself out getting rich, since wealth sprouts wings like an eagle. Do not encroach on the fields of the fatherless, whose Defender is strong. Buy the truth and do not sell it. Then the long closing passage on wine, which warns against the wine that goes down smoothly and then bites like a snake.
PRO 24:1-34	Do not envy evil men. A house is built by wisdom and its rooms filled by knowledge. Then a demanding verse: rescue those being led away to death, and do not say you did not know, since the one who weighs hearts knows. A righteous man falls seven times and gets up. Do not rejoice when your enemy falls. The chapter closes with the field of the sluggard, overgrown with thorns and nettles and its wall broken down.
PRO 25:1-28	A new collection, copied out by the men of Hezekiah. It opens at court: God's glory is to conceal a matter and a king's is to search it out, and it is better to be told to come up than to be moved down in front of a prince. Then a run of images. A word fitly spoken is apples of gold in settings of silver. A boaster is clouds and wind without rain. A man without self-restraint is a city with its walls broken down.
PRO 26:1-28	A chapter on fools, sluggards and troublemakers, built out of comparisons. Honour suits a fool as badly as snow in summer. Two consecutive verses contradict each other on purpose: do not answer a fool according to his folly, and answer a fool according to his folly. A fool repeating his folly is a dog returning to its vomit. Meddling in someone else's quarrel is grabbing a dog by the ears, and whoever digs a pit falls into it.
PRO 27:1-27	Do not boast about tomorrow, and let someone else praise you rather than your own mouth. Open rebuke is better than hidden love; the wounds of a friend are faithful while an enemy's kisses are plentiful. A near neighbour is better than a distant brother. Iron sharpens iron. Sheol, Abaddon and human eyes are never satisfied. The chapter ends on husbandry: know the state of your flocks, since riches do not last forever.
PRO 28:1-28	The wicked flee when nobody is chasing them while the righteous are bold as a lion. Wealth and its uses dominate the chapter. A rich man is wise in his own eyes while a poor man with understanding sees through him, and a poor man with integrity beats a rich man who is crooked. Concealing sins does not prosper, and confessing and renouncing them finds mercy. A wicked ruler over helpless people is a roaring lion or a charging bear. Whoever gives to the poor lacks nothing.
PRO 29:1-27	Politics runs through this chapter. When the righteous thrive people rejoice and when the wicked rule they groan; a king who judges the poor fairly has an established throne, while one who takes bribes tears the land down. Flattery spreads a net for a man's feet. A fool vents all his anger while a wise man controls himself. Where there is no revelation the people cast off restraint. Being hasty in words is worse than being a fool. Pride brings a man low. The fear of man is a snare.
PRO 30:1-33	The words of Agur son of Jakeh, who opens by calling himself the most ignorant of men and asking who has gathered the wind in his fists. His prayer asks for two things: no falsehood, and neither poverty nor riches, in case wealth makes him deny God or poverty makes him steal. Then numbered lists: four things that never say enough, four too amazing to understand, and four small creatures that are exceedingly wise, including the lizard in a king's palace.
PRO 31:1-31	The words of King Lemuel, taught him by his mother. She warns him off women and wine, since drinking makes a king forget the law, and tells him to speak for the mute and judge righteously for the poor. Then the closing poem on the worthy woman, whose price is above rubies: she rises before dawn, buys a field and plants a vineyard, opens her hands to the needy, and sells linen to merchants. Charm is deceitful and beauty vain; let her works praise her in the gates.
PSA 51:1-19	A plea for mercy and cleansing. The speaker admits his transgressions, says he has sinned against God alone, and asks to be washed whiter than snow and given a clean heart and a right spirit. He asks not to be cast from God's presence or lose the holy Spirit, promises to teach transgressors God's ways once restored, and says God wants a broken and contrite heart rather than sacrifice. It closes asking God to build the walls of Jerusalem, after which proper offerings will please him.
PSA 52:1-9	An accusation against a powerful man who boasts of mischief. His tongue is called a sharp razor that plots destruction, and he loves lying more than truth. God will uproot him from the land of the living, and the righteous will see it, fear, and laugh, saying this is the man who trusted his riches instead of God. The speaker contrasts himself as a green olive tree in God's house, trusting God's loving kindness and giving thanks before the assembly.
PSA 53:1-6	The fool says in his heart there is no God. God looks down from heaven to see if anyone understands or seeks him, and finds that all have turned back and become corrupt, with no one doing good. The wicked eat up God's people like bread and never call on him. They are seized by terror where no cause for fear exists, because God scattered the bones of their besiegers. The psalm ends wishing salvation would come out of Zion.
PSA 54:1-7	A short prayer for rescue. The speaker asks God to save him by his name and vindicate him, because strangers and violent men have risen against him and pay God no attention. He then states his confidence that God is his helper and sustains his soul, and asks that the evil his enemies intend be repaid to them. He promises a freewill offering and thanks to God's name, and says God has delivered him from all trouble.
PSA 55:1-23	A man in terror prays, restless and moaning, wishing he had a dove's wings so he could fly away and rest in the wilderness. He asks God to confuse the language of the violent city, where malice and lies patrol the streets. The deepest grievance: the attacker is a close friend and worship companion, his words smoother than butter, his heart war. He calls on God evening, morning and noon, and expects the bloodthirsty not to live out half their days.
PSA 56:1-13	Men hound the speaker all day long, twisting his words and lurking to take his life. He answers his own fear with a refrain: when afraid, he trusts God, and what can flesh do to him? He asks God to cast down the peoples, and says God counts his wanderings and keeps his tears in a bottle and in his book. He pays his vows with thank offerings, sure God kept his feet from falling.
PSA 57:1-11	The speaker takes refuge in the shadow of God's wings until disaster passes, crying out to the God who accomplishes his requests. He describes lying among lions whose teeth are spears and arrows and whose tongue is a sharp sword. His enemies dug a pit and fell into it themselves. Twice he asks that God be exalted above the heavens and his glory over all the earth. He declares his heart steadfast, wakes lute and harp, says he will wake the dawn, and sings thanks among the nations.
PSA 58:1-11	An attack on corrupt judges who claim to speak righteousness while plotting injustice. The wicked go astray from the womb, poisonous as a deaf cobra that will not hear the charmer. The psalm asks God to break their teeth, drain them away like water, blunt their arrows, and make them like a snail that melts. When vengeance comes the righteous will wash their feet in the blood of the wicked, and people will say there is a God who judges the earth.
PSA 59:1-17	A prayer for deliverance from men lying in wait, and the speaker insists it is not for any sin of his. The enemies return at evening, howling like dogs and prowling the city, spewing swords from their lips and assuming nobody hears. God laughs at them. He asks that they not be killed outright but scattered, so his people will not forget, and ends singing of God as his high tower and refuge.
PSA 60:1-12	A national complaint: God has rejected his people, broken them down, shaken the land and made them drink wine that leaves them staggering. The speaker asks him to mend the fractures. God speaks from his sanctuary claiming Shechem, Succoth, Gilead, Manasseh, Ephraim and Judah as his own, and calls Moab his wash basin, throws his shoe on Edom and shouts over Philistia. The psalm asks who will bring him into the strong city, notes that God no longer marches with their armies, and says human help is worthless.
ROM 1:1-32	Paul introduces himself as an apostle set apart for the gospel, descended from David and declared Son of God by the resurrection. He greets the Romans, praises their well-known faith, and says he is not ashamed of the gospel, God's power for salvation, Jew first and also Greek. Then the turn: God's wrath is revealed against people who knew God from what he made, swapped his glory for images of humans and animals, and were handed over to their own desires and a mind full of every kind of wrong.
ROM 2:1-29	Paul turns on the reader who agreed with all that: judging others while doing the same condemns you, and God's kindness was meant to lead you to repentance. God judges impartially by works, no favouritism between Jew and Greek. Gentiles without the law who do what it requires show it written on their hearts. He challenges the one who relies on the law: do you steal, commit adultery, rob temples? Circumcision counts only if the law is kept. The real Jew is inward, the real circumcision of the heart.
ROM 3:1-31	Paul asks what advantage the Jew has: they were entrusted with God's words, and human unfaithfulness does not cancel God's faithfulness. He rejects sinning to make grace look better. Scripture is quoted at length: no one is righteous, no one seeks God, their throats are open graves. The law's job is to shut every mouth, not to justify. Now a righteousness from God has appeared apart from law, through faith in Jesus Christ, for all who believe, since all sinned. Boasting is excluded: God is God of Gentiles too.
ROM 4:1-25	Abraham is the test case. If he was justified by works he could boast, but scripture says he believed God and it was credited to him as righteousness. David likewise blesses the man whose sin God does not count. Abraham was credited righteous while still uncircumcised, so circumcision sealed a faith he already had, making him father of believing Gentiles as well as Jews. The promise came through faith, not the law. Roughly a hundred and facing Sarah's dead womb, he did not waver but grew strong in faith.
ROM 5:1-21	Justified by faith, we have peace with God and access into grace. Paul says believers even rejoice in suffering, since suffering produces perseverance, perseverance character, character hope. People rarely die for a righteous person, but Christ died for us while we were still sinners. He sets Adam against Christ: sin and death entered through one man and spread to all. Through one man's disobedience many were made sinners, and through one man's obedience many will be made righteous. Law came in so the trespass would increase, but grace increased more.
ROM 6:1-23	Should we keep sinning so grace can abound? Paul says absolutely not, arguing from baptism: those baptised into Christ were baptised into his death, to walk in newness of life. The old self was crucified so the body of sin would be broken. Christ, raised, dies no more. Believers should count themselves dead to sin, alive to God, and stop offering their bodies to sin. Grace is not licence: you are slave to whatever you obey. The wages of sin is death; God's gift, eternal life.
ROM 7:1-25	Paul argues from marriage law: death ends the binding, and believers likewise died to the law through Christ. Is the law then sin? No: he would not have known coveting except that the law forbade it, and sin used the commandment to kill him. The law is holy; sin used it. He describes an inner split, doing the evil he hates rather than the good he wants, delighting in God's law while another law takes him captive. He cries out for deliverance and thanks God through Jesus Christ.
ROM 8:1-39	There is now no condemnation for those in Christ Jesus. What the law could not do, God did by sending his own Son. Paul contrasts the mind set on the flesh, which is death, with the mind set on the Spirit, life and peace. Believers received a Spirit of adoption and cry "Abba, Father," heirs with Christ if they suffer with him. Creation and believers alike groan while the Spirit intercedes wordlessly. God works all things for good for those he called, and nothing can separate them from Christ's love.
SIR 1:1-30	Sirach opens by asserting that all wisdom comes from the Lord and stays with him forever. Nobody can count the sand of the seas, the drops of rain or the days of eternity. Wisdom was created before all things, and God poured her out on all his works. The fear of the Lord is then called glory, gladness, the beginning of wisdom, its fullness, its crown and its root. Warnings against unjust anger and self-exaltation close the chapter.
SIR 2:1-18	Advice for anyone starting out in God's service: prepare your soul for temptation, endure steadily and do not act hastily in calamity. Gold is tried in fire and acceptable men in the furnace of humiliation. Three times he addresses those who fear the Lord, telling them to wait for mercy, trust him and hope for good things. Woes follow for fearful hearts, faint hands and those who have lost patience. He would rather fall into the hands of the Lord than of men.
SIR 3:1-31	A chapter on honouring parents. Honouring a father makes atonement for sins, and a father's blessing establishes his children's houses. He is specific about old age: help your father, do not grieve him, and if he fails in understanding be patient rather than dishonouring him while you are still strong. That kindness melts sins away like fair weather on ice. The second half turns to humility: the greater you are, the more you should humble yourself.
SIR 4:1-31	The first half concerns the poor. Do not deprive them of their living, and give no one occasion to curse you, since his maker will hear him. Be a father to the fatherless and you will be like a son of the Most High. Then wisdom appears as a hard teacher: she walks a man in crooked ways, torments him with discipline until she can trust him, and only then returns by the straight way and reveals her secrets.
SIR 5:1-15	A chapter of things not to say. Do not set your heart on your goods or call them sufficient, do not ask who has dominion over you, and above all do not say that you sinned and nothing happened, since the Lord is patient rather than indifferent. Do not winnow with every wind or walk in every path, which is what the double-tongued sinner does. If you have no understanding, keep your hand over your mouth.
SIR 6:1-37	The chapter is mostly about friendship. Let those at peace with you be many and your counsellors one in a thousand. Acquire a friend by testing him, since some turn to enmity and publicise the quarrel, and some are table companions who match your prosperity and hide from you when you are brought low. A faithful friend is a medicine of life. The second half describes wisdom as harsh to the unlearned, and tells the son to put his neck in her chain.
SIR 7:1-36	A long chain of prohibitions covering ambition, worship and household. Do not seek preeminence or a seat of honour from the king, and do not seek to be a judge in case you fear a powerful man's face. Do not repeat words in prayer. Then domestic instructions: correct your children, watch over your daughters, do not mistreat a servant who works truly, honour your parents, give the priest his portion, and visit the sick. Remember your last end.
SIR 8:1-19	A chapter of conflicts to avoid. Do not contend with a mighty man, a rich man or a man full of tongue, since heaping wood on his fire only helps him. Do not dishonour the old or rejoice over the dead, since we all age and die. Do not lend to a man mightier than yourself, and if you do, treat it as lost. Do not travel through the desert with a wrathful man or open your heart to everyone.
SIR 9:1-18	A chapter of warnings about women and about company. Do not be jealous over your own wife, avoid the harlot, and do not gaze at a girl or another man's wife, since beauty has led many astray and kindles love like a fire. Then friendship again: never abandon an old friend, since a new one is like new wine and only good once it has aged. Keep well away from a man who has power to kill.
SIR 10:1-31	The chapter opens on government. A wise judge instructs his people, ministers resemble their judge and citizens their ruler, and an uninstructed king destroys his people. Then a sustained attack on pride: sovereignty passes from nation to nation because of injustice, violence and greed, and a man who is king today dies tomorrow and inherits creeping things and worms. Pride was never created for humans, and honour belongs to those who fear the Lord.
SIR 11:1-34	The chapter argues that appearances mislead. Do not judge a man by his looks, since the bee is small among flying things and produces the best of sweets. Many kings have ended sitting on the ground while someone nobody considered has worn a crown. A man who saves by pinching and finally decides to enjoy his goods does not know how little time is left. Call no one blessed before his death, and beware the proud man, who is like a decoy partridge in a cage.
SIR 12:1-18	On giving and on enemies. Help the godly man and the lowly and you will be repaid, if not by him then by the Most High, but keep back bread from the ungodly in case he uses it to overmaster you. Then a warning never to trust an enemy, since his wickedness is like rust on brass: even if he humbles himself, you will be like a man who has wiped a mirror and finds the rust still there. He speaks sweetly while planning a pit.
SIR 13:1-26	Whoever touches pitch is defiled, and whoever keeps company with a proud man becomes like him. Do not associate with someone mightier and richer than yourself, since the earthen pot and the kettle collide only one way. The rich patron is described in detail: he trades on you while you are useful, shames you with his food until he has stripped you two or three times, then laughs. When a rich man falls there are many helpers; when a poor man falls he is rebuked.
SIR 14:1-27	A portrait of the miser, whose eye is never satisfied with his portion, who grudges bread at his own table, and who gathers by depriving himself so that others can revel in his goods. Against that, do well to yourself according to what you have, be generous to your friend before you die, and do not let a good day pass, since there is no seeking luxury in the grave. Generations come and go like leaves on a thick tree. The chapter ends with wisdom pursued like game.
SIR 15:1-20	The man who fears the Lord and holds the law obtains wisdom, who meets him like a mother and receives him like a bride, feeds him bread of understanding, and opens his mouth in the congregation. Then the sharpest section: do not say that it was through the Lord that you fell away, since he has no need of a sinful man. God made man and left him in the hand of his own counsel, setting fire and water before him.
SIR 16:1-30	The chapter begins with children: one is better than a thousand, and dying childless is better than having wicked ones. Then a list of God's past judgments, on the giants of old, the people among whom Lot lived, and the six hundred thousand footmen who hardened their hearts. Do not imagine you can be hidden in a boundless creation, since heaven, the deep and the earth shake when he visits. Creation itself, in the last section, never hungers, never tires and never disobeys.
SIR 17:1-32	A short account of humanity. The Lord created man from the earth and turns him back to it, giving him counted days, authority over what is on the earth, and the image of God, along with counsel, tongue, eyes, ears and heart. He showed them good and evil, gave them a law of life as an inheritance and made an everlasting covenant. Their ways are never hidden. Almsgiving is kept like a signet. Turn back now, since thanksgiving perishes from the dead.
SIR 18:1-33	God alone will be justified: when a man has finished he is only at the beginning. Human days reach a hundred years at most, a drop from the sea or a pebble from the sand compared with eternity, which is why God is patient and brings people back as a shepherd does his flock. Then practical advice. Add no grief of words to your giving, since a word is better than a gift. Do not run up debts on banqueting.
SIR 19:1-30	A drunken workman will never get rich, and whoever despises small things falls little by little. Then a long section on keeping quiet. Never repeat what you are told, whether it came from a friend or an enemy, and let a word die with you, since it will not burst you: a fool labours with a word as a woman in childbirth, and it sticks in him like an arrow in the thigh. Reprove a friend before threatening him, since he may not have said it.
SIR 20:1-32	A chapter of paired opposites, mostly about silence and speech. One man is silent because he has no answer and another because he knows his time, while the braggart misses his moment. Then a series of observations: prosperity found in misfortune, gain that turns to loss, a gift that profits nothing and a gift repaid double, a man who buys cheap and pays sevenfold. The fool's gift is demanded back the next day. A slip on the pavement is better than a slip of the tongue.
SIR 21:1-28	If you have sinned, add no more. Flee sin as from a serpent's face, since its teeth are a lion's. A poor man's prayer reaches God's ears and his judgment comes quickly. Building your house with other men's money is like gathering stones against winter. Most of the chapter contrasts the wise man with the fool: a fool's inward parts are a broken vessel that holds no knowledge, and a fool laughs loudly where a clever man barely smiles.
SIR 22:1-27	The slothful man is compared to a defiled stone that everyone hisses at. Teaching a fool is gluing a potsherd or waking a deep sleeper who asks what it was. Weep for the dead seven days, the author says, but for a fool all his life, and sand, salt and a mass of iron are easier to carry than a man without understanding. Then friendship: a quarrel can be repaired, except for arrogance, disclosing a secret and a treacherous blow.
SIR 23:1-28	The chapter opens with a prayer asking God not to give him a proud look and to turn away lust and a shameless mind. Then discipline of the mouth: do not get used to swearing oaths or naming the Holy One casually. The adulterer who reasons that darkness and walls hide him is answered directly: God's eyes are ten thousand times brighter than the sun and look into secret places. An unfaithful wife's case gets the same conclusion, with consequences for her children.
SIR 24:1-34	Wisdom praises herself in the congregation. She came forth from the mouth of the Most High, covered the earth like a mist, had her throne in the pillar of cloud, until the Creator told her to make her tabernacle in Jacob. She took root in Zion, and describes herself through trees and spices: a cedar in Lebanon, a palm by the sea, cinnamon, myrrh and frankincense. All this, the author says, is the law Moses commanded, flowing out like the Pishon, Tigris and Jordan.
SIR 25:1-26	Three things the author calls beautiful: harmony among relatives, friendship between neighbours, and a wife and husband who agree. Three he hates: a haughty poor man, a lying rich man, and an old adulterer without understanding. The rest of the chapter is a sustained attack on wicked wives, saying he would rather live with a lion, blaming a woman for the beginning of sin and for death, and ending with instructions to cut her off.
SIR 26:1-29	The chapter begins by praising a good wife, who doubles her husband's days, and calls her beauty like the rising sun and her face in ripe age like a lamp on the holy candlestick. Between these come the author's fears, slander of a city, a hostile assembly and a false accusation, all worse than death, and a long series of complaints about jealous and headstrong women. At the end, three things that anger him: a soldier suffering poverty, wise men treated as refuse.
SIR 27:1-30	Sin thrusts itself in between buying and selling like a nail driven between stones. A man is tested by his reasoning, so praise nobody before you have heard him argue. Then the long section on secrets. Whoever reveals a secret will not find a friend, and the friend he loses is like a bird let out of the hand, gone beyond pursuit: a wound can be bound and reviling reconciled, but betraying a secret leaves no hope. Whoever digs a pit falls in.
SIR 28:1-26	Whoever takes vengeance will find vengeance from the Lord. The argument is put as a question: a man nourishes wrath against someone like himself and then expects atonement for his own sins. Remember your last end and stop the quarrel. Strife burns according to its fuel, and a spark can be blown into flame or spat out. Then the tongue, which has scattered nations, pulled down strong cities and driven women from their homes. More have fallen by it than by the sword.
SIR 29:1-28	On lending and dependence. Lending to a neighbour in need keeps the commandments, but many treat a loan as a windfall: the borrower kisses your hands until he has the money, then delays, complains about the times, and if he pays at all you may recover half. So help him for the commandment's sake,. The essentials of life are water, bread, clothing and a house, and a poor man's own shelter of logs beats fine food where you dare not speak.
SIR 30:1-25	The chapter opens on discipline, arguing that a man who loves his son keeps laying stripes on him, while an indulged son becomes headstrong like an unbroken horse. A well taught son continues his father, so that the father dies and yet has not died. The second half changes subject to health. Health is better than all gold, and death is better than a bitter life. Gladness of heart is a man's life, while worry brings old age early.
SIR 31:1-31	The chapter opens on money and sleeplessness, since anxiety about riches consumes the flesh and drives away sleep. He asks who has had the power to transgress and has not, and calls that man blessed. Then table manners: do not reach where your eye looks or push into the dish, and be first to stop eating. Moderate eating brings healthy sleep while an insatiable man gets colic. On wine, it is as good as life in measure, but bitterness of soul when drunk heavily.
SIR 32:1-24	Advice for a banquet and for the young. If you are made ruler of the feast, do not be exalted but take thought for the guests and take your place last. Elders should speak with sound knowledge and should not interrupt the music. A young man should speak only if needed, scarcely even when asked twice, and be as one who knows and holds his tongue. Do nothing without counsel and do not regret what is done.
SIR 33:1-31	A fool's heart is a cartwheel and his thoughts a rolling axle. The author asks why one day is better than another when all daylight comes from the same sun, and answers that God distinguished them, and does the same with people, who are clay in the potter's hand. He warns against handing over power or goods to family while still alive, and closes on servants: keep them working, but treat a servant as yourself.
SIR 34:1-26	Dreams and divination are dismissed as vain: setting your mind on dreams is catching at a shadow or chasing wind. Experience is better, and the author cites his own travels, in danger of death many times and preserved. The last section is severe about worship: a sacrifice from wrongly gotten goods is a mockery, and bringing an offering out of the goods of the poor is like killing a son before his father's eyes. Whoever deprives a hireling of his wages sheds blood.
SIR 35:1-20	Obedience is treated as sacrifice: keeping the law multiplies offerings, repaying a good turn is fine flour, giving alms is a thank offering, and departing from wickedness is itself an atoning sacrifice. Still, do not appear before the Lord empty. Do not imagine gifts can corrupt him, because he shows no favouritism and will not despise the prayer of the widow whose tears run down her cheek. The prayer of the humble pierces the clouds and will not leave until God visits.
SIR 36:1-26	The chapter opens with a national prayer asking God to have mercy on his people, send his fear on the nations, crush the heads of enemy rulers who say there is none but us, gather the tribes of Jacob and have compassion on Jerusalem. It then switches abruptly to discernment and marriage. The belly will eat any meat, and an understanding heart tastes false speeches the way the mouth tastes game. Acquiring a wife is entering on a possession, while a man without one wanders.
SIR 37:1-31	Everyone claims to be your friend, but some are friends in name only: one works alongside you for his stomach's sake. Then a careful treatment of advice. Every counsellor recommends taking counsel, but find out his interest first, since he may advise for himself and then stand nearby to watch what happens to you. He lists people not to consult: a coward about war, a merchant about exchange, a sluggard about work. Trust your own heart, which brings news better than seven watchmen.
SIR 38:1-34	Honour a physician, since the Lord created him and made medicines out of the earth; make your offering, and then give place to the doctor. On mourning: weep for the dead, do not neglect the burial, wail for a day or two lest you be spoken ill of, and then be comforted. The rest of the chapter praises craftsmen. The smith wrestling with iron, and the potter turning his wheel with his feet maintain the world, but are not sought in council.
SIR 39:1-35	The scribe seeks out the wisdom of the ancients, serves among great men, travels among foreign nations, and rises early to pray, and if the Lord wills he is filled with the spirit of understanding. All the works of the Lord are exceedingly good and nobody may ask what this is or why that is, since everything is created for its use in its season. Water, fire, iron, salt, flour, honey, milk, wine and oil are the chief necessities.
SIR 40:1-30	Great travail is created for every man from birth until burial in the earth, the mother of all things, and the fear of death troubles everyone from the man on a throne of glory to the man in a hempen frock. Then a series of comparisons: children and a city are outdone by a blameless wife, wine and music by the love of wisdom, gold and silver by counsel. It closes bluntly on begging.
SIR 41:1-24	The chapter opens on death from two sides: bitter to a man at peace in his possessions, welcome to a man needy and out of patience. There is no inquisition of life in the grave, since the sentence is the Lord's over all flesh. A good name lasts forever, worth more than a thousand treasures of gold, unlike the ungodly's, which is blotted out. The rest catalogues things to be ashamed of, by audience: a lie before a prince, theft where you are a guest, silence when someone greets you.
SIR 42:1-25	A matching list of things not to be ashamed of: the law and the covenant, exact weights and balances, instructing the unwise. Put everything you hand over in writing, by number and weight. A father's anxiety about a daughter costs him sleep at every stage of her life, followed by hostile lines about women. The second half turns to God's works: he searches out the deep and the heart, no thought escapes him, and he needed no counsellor. All things are double, one against another, nothing imperfect.
SIR 43:1-33	A hymn to the sky and the weather. The sun dries the country at noon, burning three times hotter than a man blowing a furnace; the moon marks feasts and gives the month its name; the stars keep their order without fail. Then the rainbow encircling heaven with a circle of glory, snow flying like birds, hail broken small, and ice putting a breastplate on the water. Sailors report the sea's dangers. Whatever is said falls short: the sum of our words is that he is all.
SIR 44:1-23	The praise of famous men begins. Rulers, prophets, teachers, composers of music and verse, and wealthy men living peaceably were all honoured in their generations. Some left a name and some have no memorial, as though they had never been born. Then the roll call starts. Enoch pleased the Lord and was translated. Noah was found perfect and taken in exchange for the world, leaving a remnant when the flood came. Abraham kept the law and received the oath about his seed, passed to Isaac and then to Jacob.
SIR 45:1-26	The list continues with Moses, beloved of God and men, who caused the wonders to cease by his words and was given commandments face to face in the thick darkness. Then Aaron, given an everlasting covenant and priesthood, described through his vestments: linen breeches, ephod, golden pomegranates and bells, embroidery in gold, blue and purple, and a gold crown engraved HOLINESS. When Dathan, Abiram and Korah's company opposed him they were consumed by fire. Aaron receives the firstfruits but no inheritance, since God is his portion.
SIR 46:1-20	Joshua son of Nun, valiant in war and Moses' successor, is credited with the sun going back so one day became two, and hailstones breaking violently on the enemy. With Caleb he withstood the adversary and stilled the people's murmuring, and of six hundred thousand on foot only those two came into the land. The judges whose hearts did not go astray are blessed. Then Samuel, who established a kingdom and anointed princes, and before his death protested that he had taken nobody's goods, not even a shoe.
SIR 47:1-25	Nathan prophesies under David, likened here to the fat of a peace offering: he killed the giant with a sling as a boy, destroyed the Philistines, and set singers before the altar. Solomon reigns in peace, builds the temple, and is marvelled at for his proverbs and songs, but his devotion to foreign women divides the kingdom. Rehoboam lacks understanding, and Jeroboam leads Israel into sin.
SIR 48:1-25	Elijah brings a famine, calls down fire three times, raises a dead man, brings down kings, and is taken up in a chariot of fire, appointed to turn the heart of the father to the son. Elisha is filled with his spirit and his body prophesies even after death, yet the people still do not repent until carried off. Hezekiah cuts through rock to bring in water, and when Sennacherib's officer boasts, the people pray and are delivered through Isaiah.
SIR 49:1-16	Josiah's memory is compared to sweet incense, for making godliness prevail among wicked men. Apart from David, Hezekiah and Josiah, the kings of Judah all failed, and the city was burned as Jeremiah had written, though Jeremiah himself was sanctified in the womb to root out and to build. Ezekiel saw the cherubim, and the twelve prophets are wished flourishing. Zerubbabel and Nehemiah rebuild the house and the walls. Enoch, Joseph, Shem, Seth and Adam close the list.
SIR 50:1-29	Simon son of Onias repairs the temple and fortifies the city. Coming out of the sanctuary he is described through a run of images: the morning star, the full moon, a rainbow, roses, frankincense, an olive tree, a cypress in the clouds. He puts on the robe of glory, surrounded by his kindred like palm trees around a cedar, pours the cup at the altar, and the priests sound the trumpets while the people fall on their faces. The author names himself: Jesus son of Sirach.
SIR 51:1-30	A closing thanksgiving. The author thanks God for delivering him from a slanderous tongue and forged lies; his soul drew near to death and nobody helped, but he remembered God's mercy. He recounts his search for wisdom: he asked for her before the temple while young, delighted in her like a ripening grape, and was given a tongue as reward. He invites the unlearned to lodge in the house of instruction and get her without money, since he laboured little and found much rest.
SNG 1:1-17	The song opens with the woman's voice, asking to be kissed and saying his love is better than wine. She tells the daughters of Jerusalem not to stare: her angry brothers made her keep the vineyards, so the sun darkened her and she has not kept her own. He compares her to a steed in Pharaoh's chariots and praises her dove eyes.
SNG 2:1-17	She calls herself a rose of Sharon, and he answers that she is a lily among thorns. To her he is an apple tree among the trees of the wood, whose shade she sits in. Faint with love, she asks for raisins. Then he arrives leaping like a young deer: winter is past, the figs are ripening.
SNG 3:1-11	She searches for him at night through the streets and squares of the city, and asks the watchmen whether they have seen him. Just past them she finds him and will not let go until she brings him to her mother's house. Then a procession comes up from the wilderness: Solomon's cedar carriage with a purple seat, escorted by sixty armed men.
SNG 4:1-16	He praises her from the eyes down: doves behind her veil, hair like goats coming down Mount Gilead, teeth like a washed flock where none is missing, a neck like the tower of David hung with a thousand shields. He invites her down from Lebanon. She is a locked garden of saffron, cinnamon and myrrh, and calls the winds to blow its fragrance out.
SNG 5:1-16	He has come into his garden and gathered his myrrh. Then she describes a night: asleep but her heart awake, she hears him knocking and hesitates because she has undressed. By the time she opens the door he has gone. The watchmen find her, beat her and take her cloak. Asked what makes him special, she describes him head to foot.
SNG 6:1-13	The daughters offer to help look for him, and she says he has gone down to his garden to gather lilies, then repeats that she is his and he is hers. He calls her beautiful as Tirzah and awesome as an army with banners, and asks her to turn her eyes away. Against sixty queens and eighty concubines, his dove is unique.
SNG 7:1-13	Another catalogue of praise, this one beginning at her sandals and working up: thighs like jewels, a waist like a heap of wheat set about with lilies, an ivory tower for a neck, a nose like the tower of Lebanon. Her stature is a palm tree he intends to climb. She invites him out to the fields and the vineyards.
SNG 8:1-14	She wishes he were her brother so she could kiss him in public without reproach. Then the central declaration: set me as a seal on your heart, for love is strong as death and jealousy cruel as Sheol; many waters cannot quench it, and a man who offered all his wealth for it would be scorned. She tells her brothers she is a wall.
WIS 1:1-16	The book addresses the judges of the earth, telling them to love righteousness and seek the Lord. Wisdom will not enter a soul that plans evil. Since the spirit of the Lord fills the world, no unjust word goes unheard. God did not make death; ungodly men called it to themselves and made a covenant with it.
WIS 2:1-24	The author quotes the ungodly at length. Life is short, they say, nobody returns from Hades, the breath in our nostrils is smoke and reason a spark that goes out. So enjoy it now: wine, perfumes, rosebuds crowned before they wither. Then it turns cruel: oppress the poor, spare no widow, and test the righteous man with torture and a shameful death.
WIS 3:1-19	The souls of the righteous are in God's hand. What looked to fools like ruin is peace. Their chastening was a test, gold proved in the furnace, and at their visitation they will shine and run like sparks among stubble. Two surprising blessings follow: the childless woman receives fruit, and the faithful eunuch a place in the sanctuary better than wife or children.
WIS 4:1-20	Virtue matters more than numbers of descendants. The offspring of the ungodly strike no deep root and are torn out by wind. A righteous man who dies young is at rest, because honourable old age is not measured in years: understanding is grey hair and an unspotted life is ripe old age. He was taken early so wickedness would not change his mind.
WIS 5:1-23	The righteous man stands boldly before those who afflicted him. Their speech of regret: this is the man we mocked, whose life we called madness, and now he is counted among the sons of God.
