Their own lives passed like a ship leaving no track and an arrow whose path closes behind it. Then God arms himself with jealousy, righteousness, judgment and wrath.
WIS 6:1-25	The address to rulers continues. Kings and judges are told their dominion was given by the Lord, and that judgment on the powerful is stricter: mighty men will be searched out mightily. Then the tone turns inviting. Wisdom is radiant, and makes herself known first to those who want her, so the man who rises early finds her already at his gates.
WIS 7:1-30	The speaker insists on his own ordinariness: mortal, moulded in a womb over ten months, born crying the same first wail as everyone, since no king has any other beginning. He prayed, and a spirit of wisdom came. He counted gold as sand beside her. She taught him the elements, solstices and seasons, the natures of animals and the virtues of roots.
WIS 8:1-21	Wisdom reaches from one end of the world to the other. The speaker describes seeking her from his youth and wanting her as his bride, since she lives with God. He argues her value under headings: riches, understanding, virtue and experience, since she knows the past and divines the future. Realising he could not have her unless God gave her, he prayed.
WIS 9:1-18	The prayer itself. He addresses the God who made all things by his word, then asks for wisdom, the one who sits beside God on his throne. He calls himself a servant, the son of a handmaid, weak and short-lived. He recalls being commanded to build the sanctuary, a copy of the tabernacle prepared from the beginning. A corruptible body weighs down the soul.
WIS 10:1-21	Genesis and Exodus retold with Wisdom as the actor and no names given. A man who fell away from her perished in the rage in which he killed his brother; she steered the righteous man through the flood on a poor piece of wood. She went into a dungeon with the man who had been sold, and brought the people over the Red Sea.
WIS 11:1-26	When they thirsted God gave them water out of flinty rock. The controlling idea is that the same means punished the enemy and helped Israel: the Egyptians got clotted blood instead of a river. Those who worshipped reptiles were sent a multitude of irrational creatures, since a man is punished by the very things he sins with. God ordered everything by measure, number and weight.
WIS 12:1-27	The old inhabitants of the land, who practised enchantments and child sacrifice, were destroyed by Israel's ancestors, but even then God sent hornets ahead of the army so they would perish gradually and have a place of repentance. Because he is sovereign over his own strength, he judges gently, teaching his people that the righteous must love others.
WIS 13:1-19	An argument against idolatry. People who took fire, wind, stars or water to be gods were at least captivated by beauty. A woodcutter shapes a useful vessel from good timber, burns the offcuts to cook dinner, then carves a knotty leftover into a human shape, paints it red and bolts it to a wall.
WIS 14:1-31	A man about to sail prays to a piece of wood more rotten than the ship carrying him, though a raft once carried the hope of the world. Then a history of how idols began: a grieving father made an image of a dead child, princes commanded worship of statues, and craftsmen improved the likeness until the crowd worshipped a man.
WIS 15:1-19	The author turns from idols to God. Then the potter: from the same clay he makes vessels for clean uses and vessels for the opposite, deciding himself which is which, and then makes a god out of the same clay, though he is earth himself and will hand back the borrowed soul. His heart is ashes.
WIS 16:1-29	Egypt was tormented by vermin while Israel was given quails. When Israel was bitten by serpents, it was not herb or plaster that cured them but God's word. Israel received angels' food from heaven that tasted like whatever the eater wanted, and it melted in a faint sunbeam, teaching them to rise before the sun.
WIS 17:1-21	A long treatment of the plague of darkness. The Egyptians became prisoners of darkness themselves, shut under their roofs and separated by a curtain of forgetfulness. Phantoms appeared with unsmiling faces, and no fire or star could light that night, only a self-kindled glimmering that frightened them more. Their magicians, who promised to drive terrors away, were themselves sick with fear.
WIS 18:1-25	For the holy ones there was great light, and the Egyptians, hearing their voices without seeing them, called them fortunate. Egypt had planned to kill Israel's infants, so their own children were taken. On the Passover night the children of good men sang in secret while the enemy's lament answered in discord. God's word leaped from the royal throne as a stern warrior.
WIS 19:1-22	God foreknew that after urging Israel to leave they would change their minds and pursue them, which they did while still mourning at their graves. Creation was refashioned to serve: dry land rose out of the Red Sea and the people crossed skipping like lambs. The author compares Sodom's refusal of strangers with Egypt enslaving its benefactors, and notes both were struck blind.
1KI 19:1-21	Jezebel swears to kill Elijah within a day. He runs to Beersheba and asks to die under a juniper tree. An angel wakes him twice with a cake and water, and on that food he walks forty days to Horeb. Asked what he is doing, he says Israel has killed the prophets and he alone is left. Wind, earthquake and fire pass without Yahweh in them; then a still small voice. He is sent to anoint Hazael, Jehu and Elisha, and told seven thousand have not bowed to Baal.
1KI 20:1-23	Ben Hadad besieges Samaria and demands Ahab's silver, gold, wives and children. Ahab agrees until the demand becomes a house-by-house search, and the elders tell him to refuse. Ben Hadad swears there will be no dust left to grab; Ahab answers that a man putting on armour should not boast like one removing it. A prophet promises victory through the provincial officials' young men, and two hundred and thirty-two of them rout the Syrians while Ben Hadad is drunk. Syria decides Israel's god is a god of hills.
1KI 20:24-43	Syria replaces its kings with captains to fight in the plain. At Aphek, Israel camps like two little flocks of goats against a country full of Syrians. A man of God says that because Syria called Yahweh a god of hills, the multitude will be handed over. Israel kills a hundred thousand foot soldiers, and the wall of Aphek falls on twenty-seven thousand more. Ben Hadad's servants come out in sackcloth, and Ahab calls him brother and lets him go on terms. A prophet traps Ahab into sentencing himself.
ACT 9:1-21	Saul gets letters to the Damascus synagogues so he can bring back anyone of the Way in chains. Near the city a light knocks him down and a voice asks why he is persecuting it; told it is Jesus, he is sent into the city for instructions. Blind three days, he neither eats nor drinks. Ananias, sent to him, objects that the man arrests believers, and is told Saul is a chosen vessel. Scales fall from Saul's eyes and he preaches at once.
ACT 9:22-43	Saul confounds the Damascus Jews, who plot to kill him, so his disciples lower him through the wall in a basket. In Jerusalem the disciples fear him until Barnabas vouches for him, and when the Hellenists try to kill him the brothers send him to Tarsus. Peter then tells Aeneas, paralyzed eight years, to get up and make his bed. At Joppa, Tabitha dies; the widows show the clothes she made, and Peter prays and presents her alive.
ACT 10:1-25	In Caesarea a devout centurion named Cornelius sees an angel who tells him to send to Joppa for Simon called Peter. Next day Peter, praying hungry on the roof, sees a sheet let down holding every kind of animal and a voice telling him to kill and eat. He refuses as unclean, and is told not to call unclean what God has cleansed; this happens three times. Cornelius's men arrive, the Spirit tells him to go with them, and he travels to Caesarea.
ACT 10:26-48	Peter raises Cornelius up, saying he is only a man, and explains that although a Jew does not associate with a foreigner, God has shown him not to call anyone unclean. Cornelius recounts his vision. Peter says God shows no favouritism, then summarises Jesus of Nazareth: anointed with the Spirit, doing good and healing, killed on a tree, raised on the third day, seen by chosen witnesses, appointed judge of the living and the dead. The Spirit falls on the listeners mid-sentence, and Peter has them baptized.
JER 51:1-31	God raises a destroying wind against Babylon and sends strangers to winnow her. His people are told to flee and save their own lives, because Babylon, a golden cup that made all the earth drunk, has suddenly fallen and cannot be healed. The kings of the Medes are stirred up against her walls. Babylon had been God's battle axe, used to break horses, chariots and governors alike; now she is paid for what she did in Zion.
JER 51:32-64	The fords are seized and the reeds burned, and Babylon is a threshing floor at treading time. Zion is quoted: Nebuchadnezzar swallowed me like a monster, so let my blood be on Chaldea. God answers that he will plead her cause and dry her sea and fountain. Jeremiah writes it all in a book and sends it with Seraiah, who is to read it in Babylon, tie a stone to it and throw it into the Euphrates.
JER 52:1-17	Zedekiah became king at twenty one and reigned eleven years, rebelling against Babylon. Nebuchadnezzar besieged Jerusalem from the tenth month of his ninth year into the eleventh, by which time the famine had left no bread. The wall was breached, the soldiers fled by night, and Zedekiah was overtaken near Jericho. At Riblah he watched his sons killed, was blinded, and died imprisoned in Babylon. Nebuzaradan burned the temple and palace, and deported all but the poorest.
JER 52:18-34	An inventory of what was carried off: the bronze service vessels, the gold and silver cups and lamp stands, the two pillars, the sea and the twelve bronze bulls, whose bronze was beyond weighing. Nebuzaradan also took Seraiah the chief priest, palace officials and sixty men of the city, all put to death at Riblah. Deportation totals come to four thousand six hundred. The book ends with Evilmerodach releasing Jehoiachin after thirty seven years.
1KI 21:1-29	Ahab offers to buy Naboth's vineyard, and Naboth refuses to give up his fathers' inheritance. Ahab goes home sullen. Jezebel writes letters in his name arranging a fast, Naboth in the place of honour and two scoundrels accusing him of cursing God and the king. Naboth is stoned and Ahab goes down to take the vineyard. Elijah meets him there: where dogs licked Naboth's blood they will lick Ahab's, and dogs will eat Jezebel by the rampart. God defers the disaster to his son's day.
1KI 22:1-26	After three years of peace, Jehoshaphat visits Ahab, who proposes retaking Ramoth Gilead. Jehoshaphat agrees but asks for a word from Yahweh. Four hundred prophets say go, and Zedekiah makes iron horns. Ahab names Micaiah, whom he hates for never prophesying good. Micaiah first parrots the others, then, pressed, reports Israel scattered like sheep without a shepherd, and a vision of Yahweh's court where a spirit volunteers to be a lying spirit in the mouths of Ahab's prophets. Zedekiah strikes him and Ahab has him jailed.
1KI 22:27-53	Ahab has Micaiah held on prison rations until he returns in peace, and Micaiah answers that if he returns at all, Yahweh has not spoken by him. Ahab disguises himself and sends Jehoshaphat out in royal robes; the Syrian captains, ordered to fight only the king of Israel, chase Jehoshaphat until they realise their mistake. A man draws his bow at random and hits Ahab between the joints of his armour. Propped up in his chariot, he dies at evening. The chariot is washed and dogs lick his blood.
2KI 1:1-18	Moab rebels after Ahab's death. Ahaziah falls through the lattice of his upper room and sends to ask Baal Zebub of Ekron whether he will recover. Elijah intercepts the messengers: because the king consulted a foreign god as though there were none in Israel, he will not leave his bed. Two captains and their fifties are consumed by fire from the sky; the third kneels and is spared. Elijah repeats the sentence to the king's face. Ahaziah dies sonless, and Jehoram reigns.
2KI 2:1-25	Knowing Elijah is about to be taken, Elisha refuses three times to be left behind. Elijah strikes the water with his rolled mantle and the two cross dry. Elisha asks for a double portion of his spirit, and is told he will have it if he sees the departure. A chariot of fire separates them and Elijah goes up in a whirlwind. Elisha takes the fallen mantle and parts the Jordan with it. He heals Jericho's water with salt. At Bethel he curses jeering youths, and two bears maul forty-two.
2KI 3:1-27	Jehoram of Israel removes his father's pillar of Baal but keeps the sins of Jeroboam. Mesha of Moab, who paid tribute in wool, rebels. Jehoram allies with Jehoshaphat of Judah and the king of Edom, and after seven days through the wilderness the army has no water. Elisha orders the valley dug full of trenches and promises water without rain. It arrives at morning sacrifice; the Moabites see it red and rush the camp. Besieged at Kir Hareseth, their king sacrifices his eldest son on the wall.
2KI 4:1-24	A widow of one of the prophets tells Elisha a creditor is coming for her two sons. She has one pot of oil, and he tells her to borrow every empty container she can, shut the door and pour. The oil runs until the last jar is filled, and she sells it to clear the debt. At Shunem a prominent woman feeds Elisha and builds him a roof room. Gehazi points out she has no son, and Elisha promises her one within the year. The boy dies on her knees.
2KI 4:25-44	Elisha sees the Shunammite coming and sends Gehazi to ask if all is well; she says it is, then takes hold of Elisha's feet and asks whether she ever requested a son. He sends Gehazi ahead with his staff, but laying it on the child's face does nothing. Elisha shuts himself in with the boy, prays, and stretches over him twice until the flesh warms, and the child sneezes seven times. At Gilgal a poisonous stew is made safe with meal, and twenty loaves feed a hundred men.
2KI 5:1-14	Naaman, commander of Syria's army, has leprosy. A captive Israelite girl serving his wife says the prophet in Samaria could heal him. The king of Syria sends him with a letter, silver, gold and ten changes of clothing. The king of Israel tears his clothes, taking the letter as a pretext for a quarrel, and Elisha sends word to let the man come. A messenger tells Naaman to wash seven times in the Jordan. He leaves in a rage, then relents, dips, and is restored.
2KI 5:15-27	Naaman returns and declares there is no God in all the earth but in Israel, then presses a gift on Elisha, who refuses under oath. He asks instead for two mules' load of Israelite earth, and pardon in advance for bowing in the temple of Rimmon. Elisha sends him off in peace. Gehazi runs after him, invents two young prophets, and asks for silver and clothing. He hides it and says he went nowhere. Elisha says his heart went with him, and Naaman's leprosy clings to him instead.
2KI 6:1-18	The company of prophets tells Elisha their quarters are too small and asks to build by the Jordan. A borrowed axe head flies into the water, and Elisha throws in a stick and makes the iron float. Syria is at war with Israel, and every time its king fixes a camp Elisha warns Israel away. Told the prophet is at Dothan, he surrounds the city by night. Elisha's servant panics; Elisha prays until he sees the mountain full of chariots of fire, then prays the Syrians blind.
2KI 6:19-33	Elisha tells the blinded Syrians they are at the wrong city and leads them into Samaria, where their eyes are opened. The king asks twice whether to kill them; Elisha says to feed them, and the raids stop. Later Ben Hadad besieges Samaria until a donkey's head sells for eighty pieces of silver. A woman calls to the king from the wall: two mothers agreed to eat their sons, hers was eaten, and the other has hidden her child. The king swears to have Elisha's head.
2KI 7:1-20	Elisha says that by this time tomorrow flour and barley will sell cheaply in Samaria's gate. The captain the king leans on scoffs, and is told he will see it but not eat it. Four men with leprosy at the gate reason that they will die either way and go to surrender to Syria, whose army has fled at the sound of a great host. They loot, then decide that keeping quiet on a day of good news is wrong. Prices fall, and the captain is trampled in the gate.
2KI 8:1-29	Warned by Elisha of a seven year famine, the Shunammite woman lives in Philistine territory, then returns to petition for her land. Gehazi is telling the king about her son as she arrives, and the king restores her property. In Damascus the sick Ben Hadad sends Hazael to ask whether he will recover. Elisha says to tell him he will, though God has shown he will die, then weeps over what Hazael will do to Israel. Next day Hazael smothers his master with a wet cloth.
2KI 9:1-18	Elisha sends a young prophet to Ramoth Gilead with a vial of oil, telling him to anoint Jehu privately and then run. The young man declares Jehu king over Israel and commissions him to strike down the house of Ahab, then flees. Jehu's fellow officers ask what the madman wanted; when he tells them, they spread their cloaks on the stairs and proclaim him king. He seals the city so no news reaches Jezreel, where the wounded Joram is recovering.
2KI 9:19-37	A second rider is sent and also does not come back, and the watchman recognises the furious driving as Jehu's. Joram and Ahaziah drive out and meet him on Naboth's land. Asked whether he comes in peace, Jehu answers that there is none while Jezebel's idolatry continues. Joram turns to flee and is shot through the heart, and his body is thrown onto Naboth's field. Ahaziah dies at Megiddo. At Jezreel, Jezebel paints her eyes and calls Jehu a murderer, and her eunuchs throw her down.
2KI 10:1-17	Jehu writes to the guardians of Ahab's seventy sons in Samaria, daring them to pick one and fight. They answer that they will do whatever he says. His second letter demands the seventy heads, which arrive at Jezreel in baskets and are piled at the gate. Jehu tells the people he conspired against his master, but someone else killed these. He then kills the rest of Ahab's household, its officials and priests, and forty-two of Ahaziah's brothers met on the road.
2KI 10:18-36	Jehu announces that Ahab served Baal a little but he will serve him much, and summons every priest and worshipper of Baal to a great sacrifice. The temple fills, robes are issued, and the crowd is checked to be sure no servant of Yahweh is inside. When the burnt offering ends, eighty guards kill everyone, and the temple is pulled down and made a latrine. Jehu ends Baal in Israel but keeps the calves at Bethel and Dan. Hazael begins taking land east of the Jordan.
2KI 11:1-21	When Athaliah learns her son Ahaziah is dead, she destroys the royal family. Jehosheba hides the infant Joash and his nurse in the temple for six years while Athaliah reigns. In the seventh year the priest Jehoiada shows the guard the king's son and rings him with David's spears and shields. Joash is crowned and acclaimed. Athaliah hears the noise, comes in, cries treason, and is taken out to the horses' entry and killed. The people tear down the house of Baal. Joash is seven.
2KI 12:1-21	Jehoash does right while Jehoiada instructs him, though the high places remain. He tells the priests to fund temple repairs from the dedicated money, but by his twenty-third year nothing is repaired, so he takes the collection out of their hands. Jehoiada bores a hole in a chest lid and sets it by the altar. The king's scribe and the high priest count the money and pay the builders directly. Hazael is bought off with the temple gold, and Jehoash's servants kill him.
2KI 13:1-25	Jehoahaz reigns seventeen years, does evil, and is given into the hands of Hazael until his army is fifty horsemen, ten chariots and ten thousand foot. He pleads, and Israel is granted a deliverer, but the sins of Jeroboam remain. His son Jehoash also does evil. Elisha, dying, has him shoot an arrow eastward, then strike the ground; he strikes three times and stops, and Elisha is angry, because five or six would have finished Syria. A body thrown into Elisha's tomb later revives.
2KI 14:1-29	Amaziah of Judah does right but not like David, and the high places remain. He executes his father's assassins but spares their children, citing the law that each man dies for his own sin. After killing ten thousand Edomites he challenges Jehoash of Israel, who answers with a fable of a thistle proposing marriage to a cedar and being trampled. Amaziah insists, is beaten and captured at Beth Shemesh, and loses four hundred cubits of wall and the temple treasures.
2KI 15:1-19	Azariah becomes king of Judah at sixteen and reigns fifty-two years, doing right except that the high places remain. Struck with leprosy, he lives apart while his son Jotham judges the people. In Israel the throne changes hands fast: Zechariah reigns six months and is struck down by Shallum, completing the four generations promised to Jehu; Shallum reigns one month before Menahem kills him. Menahem attacks Tiphsah and rips open its pregnant women, then pays Assyria a thousand talents of silver.
2KI 15:20-38	Menahem raises the Assyrian tribute by taxing every wealthy man fifty shekels, and Assyria withdraws. His son Pekahiah reigns two years and is killed in the palace fortress by his captain Pekah. Pekah reigns twenty years, and in his time Tiglath Pileser takes Hazor, Gilead, Galilee and all Naphtali and deports the people. Hoshea then kills Pekah and takes the throne. In Judah, Jotham reigns sixteen years and does right, though the high places remain.
2KI 16:1-20	Ahaz reigns sixteen years in Jerusalem and does not do right: he walks in the way of the kings of Israel, makes his son pass through the fire, and sacrifices at the high places. Besieged by Rezin and Pekah, he sends temple and palace gold to Tiglath Pileser, calling himself his servant and son, and Assyria takes Damascus. Visiting there, Ahaz sends home a drawing of an altar, and Urijah builds a copy. Ahaz then strips the temple's metal.
ACT 11:1-30	Back in Jerusalem, Peter is criticised for eating with uncircumcised men, and answers by retelling the vision, Cornelius's angel, and the Spirit falling on the household as it had on them at the beginning. His critics fall silent and praise God. Believers scattered by the persecution reach Antioch and preach to Greeks there. Jerusalem sends Barnabas, who fetches Saul from Tarsus; they teach for a year, and the disciples are first called Christians. Agabus predicts a famine, and the church sends relief to Judea.
ACT 12:1-25	Herod kills James the brother of John and, seeing that it pleases people, arrests Peter under four squads of soldiers. The night before the trial an angel wakes Peter, his chains fall off, and an iron gate opens by itself; Peter thinks it is a vision until the angel leaves him in the street. At the house of Mary the maid Rhoda recognises his voice and leaves him outside while she reports it. At Caesarea, hailed as a god, Herod is struck down and dies.
ACT 13:1-25	In Antioch the prophets and teachers are fasting when the Spirit sets Barnabas and Saul apart for a work. They sail to Cyprus, and at Paphos meet the sorcerer Bar Jesus, called Elymas, who tries to turn the proconsul Sergius Paulus from the faith. Saul, also called Paul, denounces him and he goes blind, and the proconsul believes. At Perga John leaves for Jerusalem. In Antioch of Pisidia, Paul begins in the synagogue with the history: the exodus, the wilderness, the judges, Saul son of Kish, then David.
ACT 13:26-52	Paul continues: the rulers in Jerusalem fulfilled the prophets by condemning Jesus, but God raised him and he was seen for many days. Quoting the promise that the Holy One would not see decay, he argues that David died and decayed while Jesus did not, and that forgiveness comes through him where the law could not justify. Almost the whole city gathers the next Sabbath; the synagogue leaders contradict him, so Paul and Barnabas turn openly to the Gentiles and go on to Iconium.
ACT 14:1-28	At Iconium a plot to stone them sends Paul and Barnabas to Lystra and Derbe. At Lystra Paul tells a man lame from birth to stand, and he leaps. The crowd calls Barnabas Jupiter and Paul Mercury, and the priest brings oxen, so the apostles tear their clothes and insist they are only men. Then Jews from Antioch persuade the crowd, and Paul is stoned and dragged out for dead, but rises and goes on to Derbe. They retrace their route, appointing elders, and report a door of faith opened.
ACT 15:1-41	Men from Judea teach at Antioch that circumcision is required for salvation, so Paul and Barnabas take the question to Jerusalem. After much debate Peter says God gave the Gentiles the Spirit, and asks why they would impose a yoke no one could bear. James, citing the prophecy about David's fallen tent, proposes requiring only abstention from idol pollution, sexual immorality, strangled meat and blood. A letter goes to Antioch and is received gladly. Later Paul and Barnabas part over John Mark, who had left them in Pamphylia.
ACT 16:1-18	At Derbe and Lystra Paul meets Timothy and circumcises him because of the Jews in the area. They deliver the Jerusalem decrees as they travel. The Spirit forbids them to speak in Asia and blocks Bithynia, so they reach Troas, where Paul sees a Macedonian begging for help. They sail to Philippi and speak on the Sabbath to women at a riverside prayer place. Lydia, a seller of purple, is baptized with her household. A slave girl with a spirit of divination follows them shouting until Paul casts it out.
ACT 16:19-40	Seeing their profit gone, the girl's owners drag Paul and Silas before the magistrates, who have them beaten and jailed with feet in stocks. At midnight, praying and singing, they feel an earthquake open every door and loosen everyone's bonds. About to kill himself, assuming they've escaped, the jailer is stopped when Paul calls out that they are all there. He asks what he must do to be saved, washes their wounds, and is baptized with his household. Paul later makes the magistrates come apologise for beating an untried Roman.
ACT 17:1-34	At Thessalonica Paul argues in the synagogue that the Christ had to suffer and rise; some believe. A mob attacks Jason's house, charging that these men say there is another king, Jesus. At Beroea people examine scripture daily, until agitators arrive and Paul is escorted to Athens. Provoked by the idols, he argues in synagogue and marketplace, and philosophers bring him to the Areopagus. He begins from an altar to an unknown god, quotes their poets, and calls for repentance before judgment by a risen man. Some mock, others believe.
ACT 18:1-28	In Corinth Paul meets Aquila and Priscilla, expelled from Rome by Claudius, and works with them as a tentmaker, reasoning in the synagogue each Sabbath. Opposed, he turns to the Gentiles and moves next door to Justus's house. Crispus the synagogue ruler believes with his household, and many Corinthians are baptized. A vision tells Paul not to be afraid, and he stays a year and a half. Gallio refuses to hear the dispute. Paul sails for Syria, shaves his head for a vow, and leaves the couple at Ephesus.
ACT 19:1-21	At Ephesus Paul finds disciples who have not heard of the Holy Spirit and were baptized only into John's baptism; he baptizes them in Jesus' name, and they speak in tongues and prophesy. He teaches daily at Tyrannus's school for two years. Cloths taken from him heal the sick. Seven sons of Sceva try the name of Jesus as a formula; the spirit says it knows Jesus and Paul but not them, and drives them out naked. Believers burn magic books worth fifty thousand pieces of silver.
ACT 19:22-41	Paul sends Timothy and Erastus into Macedonia and stays in Asia. Demetrius, a silversmith who makes shrines of Artemis, gathers the craftsmen, warning that Paul's preaching threatens their income. They shout Artemis is great, and the city rushes into the theatre, seizing Paul's companions. Paul is kept from going in by the disciples. The assembly is confused, most not knowing why they came; Alexander is shouted down as a Jew for two hours. The clerk quiets them, noting these men have robbed no temple, and dismisses them with a warning.
ACT 20:1-38	After the uproar Paul travels through Macedonia into Greece, and on learning of a plot returns to Troas. Talking until midnight, Paul is interrupted when Eutychus, asleep in a window, falls and is taken dead; Paul embraces him, says his life is in him, and talks until daybreak, and the boy is brought in alive. He sails past Ephesus for Jerusalem by Pentecost. From Miletus he summons the elders, recalls his work, and warns that wolves will come from among themselves and they will not see his face again.
ACT 21:1-22	The voyage runs by Cos, Rhodes and Patara to Tyre, where disciples tell Paul through the Spirit not to go to Jerusalem. By Ptolemais they reach Caesarea and stay with Philip the evangelist, whose four daughters prophesy. The prophet Agabus binds his own hands and feet with Paul's belt, saying its owner will be bound and handed to the Gentiles. Everyone begs Paul not to go; he answers he is ready to die, and they yield. In Jerusalem the elders raise the rumour he teaches Jews to abandon Moses.
ACT 21:23-40	The elders propose Paul join four men under a vow, purify himself and pay their expenses, so all see he keeps the law. Near the end of seven days Jews from Asia see him in the temple and rouse the crowd, claiming he teaches against the law and brought Greeks inside, drawn from seeing Trophimus with him earlier. He is dragged out, doors shut, the crowd beats him, and the commander arrives, binds him with two chains, and has him carried up the steps, unable to hear over the noise.
ACT 22:1-30	Speaking Hebrew, which quiets the crowd, Paul says he is a Jew raised in Jerusalem under Gamaliel, who persecuted the Way with letters from the high priest. He describes the light near Damascus, the voice asking why he persecutes it, and the answer: Jesus. Blinded, he is led into Damascus, where Ananias restores his sight and tells him to be baptized, adding that in a trance he was sent to the Gentiles. The crowd erupts, demanding his death. The commander scourges him but learns he was born a citizen.
ACT 23:1-35	Paul tells the council he has lived with a good conscience, and the high priest orders him struck; Paul calls him a whitewashed wall, then withdraws it on learning who he is. Noticing the council is split, he declares himself a Pharisee on trial over the resurrection, setting Pharisees and Sadducees against each other until the commander pulls him out by force. More than forty men swear off food and drink until Paul is dead. Paul's nephew warns the commander, who sends Paul to Caesarea by night under heavy guard.
ACT 24:1-27	Five days later Ananias comes with elders and the orator Tertullus, who flatters Felix, then calls Paul a plague and a ringleader of the Nazarenes. Paul answers that it was twelve days since he came to worship, no one found him disputing, and his accusers cannot prove the charges. He says he serves God after the Way, hoping in a resurrection. Felix defers the case. Hearing him later with his wife Drusilla, he is alarmed at talk of judgment. Two years on, Festus succeeds him, and Paul stays bound.
ACT 25:1-27	Three days after arriving, Festus goes to Jerusalem, where the chief priests ask him to send Paul there, planning to kill him on the road. He refuses and tells them to come to Caesarea instead. At the hearing they bring charges they cannot prove. When Festus asks whether he will be tried in Jerusalem, Paul appeals to Caesar, and Festus grants it. King Agrippa arrives, and Festus explains the case: the dispute is over their own religion and one Jesus who died and whom Paul says is alive.
ACT 26:1-32	Invited by Agrippa to speak, Paul recounts living as a strict Pharisee and says he is on trial for the hope the twelve tribes serve for. He describes imprisoning believers, the noon light brighter than the sun, the voice in Hebrew about kicking against goads, and his commission to turn people from darkness to light. He preached repentance in Damascus, Jerusalem, Judea and to the Gentiles. Festus shouts that learning has driven him mad; Agrippa asks if Paul means to make him a Christian. They agree he deserves no death.
ACT 27:1-21	Paul and other prisoners sail for Italy under centurion Julius, who lets Paul visit friends at Sidon. Contrary winds push them past Cyprus to Fair Havens. Paul warns the late-season voyage will cost cargo, ship and lives, but the centurion follows the captain, and the majority votes to try for Phoenix. A soft south wind seems right until a storm, Euroclydon, drives them off course. They secure the boat, undergird the hull, and throw cargo over. After days with no sun or stars, all hope is gone.
ACT 27:22-44	Paul tells them to take heart: an angel says he must stand before Caesar, and God grants him everyone aboard, though the ship will be lost. On the fourteenth night the sailors sense land and drop anchors. Sailors trying to escape in the boat are stopped when Paul warns the centurion. At dawn Paul has everyone eat, taking bread and giving thanks. They run aground and the stern breaks up. Soldiers want to kill the prisoners, but the centurion saves Paul by sending swimmers ashore first, the rest on planks.
ACT 28:1-31	The island is Malta, and the islanders build a fire in the rain. A viper fastens on Paul's hand; the locals call him a murderer, then a god when he shakes it off. Publius hosts them three days, and Paul heals his father of fever; the island's sick are then cured too. Three months later they sail for Rome, where believers meet them on the road. Under guard, Paul argues from Moses and the prophets all day; some believe, some do not. He stays two years, preaching without hindrance.
EXO 7:1-25	God tells Moses he will be like God to Pharaoh with Aaron as his prophet, and that he will harden Pharaoh's heart. Moses is eighty and Aaron eighty-three. Aaron throws down his rod and it becomes a serpent; the Egyptian magicians duplicate it, but Aaron's rod swallows theirs. Pharaoh is unmoved. The next morning Aaron strikes the Nile and all the water in Egypt turns to blood, killing the fish and fouling the river. The magicians duplicate that too. Pharaoh goes home while Egypt digs beside the river for water.
EXO 8:1-32	Aaron stretches out his rod and frogs cover Egypt, into beds, ovens and kneading troughs. The magicians produce frogs too. Pharaoh asks for relief and lets Moses set the time; he chooses tomorrow, and the frogs die in stinking heaps. With the pressure off, Pharaoh refuses. Aaron strikes the dust and it becomes lice; this time the magicians fail and tell Pharaoh it is God's finger. Then flies fill Egypt but not Goshen. Pharaoh offers a sacrifice inside Egypt, then a short trip, then goes back on it.
EXO 9:1-18	A pestilence is announced on Egyptian livestock in the field, with Israel's animals spared. It happens the next day; Pharaoh checks, finds not one Israelite animal dead, and still refuses. Moses then throws furnace ashes toward the sky and boils break out across Egypt, so badly that the magicians cannot stand before him. God hardens Pharaoh's heart. The next morning comes a longer message: God could have wiped Egypt out already, but has kept Pharaoh standing to display his power, and tomorrow will bring unprecedented hail.
EXO 9:19-35	Pharaoh is warned to bring livestock and workers under shelter. Moses stretches out his rod and hail falls with thunder and lightning across Egypt, striking every person, animal and plant left in the field, but not Goshen. Pharaoh admits he has sinned and that Yahweh is righteous. Moses leaves the city, spreads his hands, and the storm stops, though he says he knows Pharaoh still does not fear God. Flax and barley are destroyed; wheat and spelt survive, being late. Pharaoh hardens his heart again.
EXO 10:1-29	Pharaoh's own officials ask how long this will go on, saying Egypt is destroyed. He offers to let the men go but not the children, then has Moses thrown out. An east wind brings locusts that strip everything the hail left. Pharaoh admits sin, and a west wind drives the locusts into the Red Sea. Then three days of darkness, while Israel has light. Pharaoh offers to release everyone but the livestock. Moses says not a hoof will stay, and Pharaoh banishes him on pain of death.
EXO 11:1-10	God tells Moses one more plague is coming, after which Pharaoh will drive Israel out, and instructs the people to ask their Egyptian neighbours for silver and gold. Moses is held in high regard by Pharaoh's officials. He announces that at about midnight every firstborn in Egypt will die, from Pharaoh's heir to the servant girl's child to the livestock, and there will be a cry like no other, while not even a dog will bark at Israel. He leaves Pharaoh in hot anger.
EXO 12:1-22	God makes this month the first of the year and gives the Passover instructions. Each household takes a year-old male lamb without defect, sharing with a neighbour if it is small, and kills it at evening on the fourteenth. Blood goes on the two doorposts and the lintel; the meat is roasted whole and eaten with unleavened bread and bitter herbs, dressed to travel. Seven days of unleavened bread follow. Moses tells the elders to apply the blood with hyssop and stay indoors until morning.
EXO 12:23-51	Israel is told the blood will keep the destroyer out, and the rite explained to their children forever. At midnight every firstborn in Egypt dies, from Pharaoh's heir to the prisoner in the dungeon, and there is not a house without a death. Pharaoh summons Moses and orders them all out, asking to be blessed. The Egyptians push them to hurry, so the dough leaves unleavened, and Israel receives silver, gold and clothing. About six hundred thousand men plus families leave Rameses after four hundred and thirty years.
EXO 13:1-22	Every firstborn, human and animal, is set apart as God's. Moses tells the people to remember this day, keep seven days of unleavened bread, and hold it as a sign on the hand and between the eyes. A firstborn donkey is redeemed with a lamb or has its neck broken; every firstborn son is redeemed. Children who ask are told the story. God avoids the short Philistine road in case war makes them turn back. Moses carries Joseph's bones. Cloud leads by day, fire by night.
EXO 14:1-31	Israel camps by the sea at Pihahiroth so Pharaoh will think them trapped. He regrets releasing them and pursues with six hundred chosen chariots. The people panic, and Moses tells them to stand still and watch. The pillar moves behind them, between the camps, all night. Moses stretches out his hand, an east wind divides the sea, and Israel crosses on dry ground. The Egyptians follow, their chariot wheels come off, and the sea drowns them all. Israel sees the bodies and believes.
EXO 15:1-27	Moses and Israel sing a victory song: God threw horse and rider into the sea, sank Pharaoh's chosen captains like stone, and answered the enemy's boast about dividing plunder with a wind. The surrounding peoples, Philistia, Edom, Moab and Canaan, will hear and be terrified while Israel passes through to God's sanctuary. Miriam leads the women with tambourines and dancing. Three days later they find no water, then bitter water at Marah. Moses throws in a tree and it turns sweet. They camp at Elim, twelve springs and seventy palms.
EXO 16:1-16	Six weeks out of Egypt, the congregation complains that they should have died there, where at least there were meat pots and bread. God says he will rain bread from the sky, doubled on the sixth day, as a test. Moses and Aaron point out the grumbling is really against God. That evening quail cover the camp; in the morning a fine flaky substance is left when the dew lifts. The people ask what it is. Moses tells them to gather an omer each.
EXO 16:17-36	Measured out, everyone has exactly enough regardless of how much they gathered. Told to keep nothing overnight, some do anyway and it breeds worms and stinks. On the sixth day they gather double, and Moses explains the seventh is a Sabbath; that portion keeps. Some go out looking on the seventh anyway and find nothing, and God asks how long they will refuse his commands. They call it manna, white like coriander seed, tasting of honey wafers. They eat it forty years.
EXO 17:1-16	At Rephidim there is no water, and the people quarrel with Moses, who tells God they are nearly ready to stone him. He takes the elders, strikes a rock at Horeb, and water comes out. He names the place Massah and Meribah, after their testing of God. Then Amalek attacks. Joshua leads the fighting while Moses holds the rod up on the hill, and Aaron and Hur hold his arms steady until sunset. Moses builds an altar named Yahweh our Banner.
EXO 18:1-27	Jethro, priest of Midian, brings Moses his wife Zipporah and their sons Gershom and Eliezer to the camp. Moses tells him everything; Jethro blesses God, calls him greater than all gods, and sacrifices with Aaron and the elders. Next day he watches Moses judge every dispute alone from morning to evening and says it will wear him out. He advises appointing capable, honest men over thousands, hundreds, fifties and tens. Moses does it, and Jethro goes home.
EXO 19:1-25	Three months out of Egypt, Israel camps at Sinai. God offers a covenant: obey, and they will be his own possession, a kingdom of priests and a holy nation. The people agree. They wash their clothes and wait three days, with a boundary round the mountain and death for anyone who touches it. On the third morning: thunder, fire, smoke, a loud trumpet, the mountain shaking. Moses goes up and is sent back down to warn them again.
EXO 20:1-26	God speaks the ten commandments: no other gods, no carved images, do not misuse God's name, keep the Sabbath, honour father and mother, and no murder, adultery, theft, false testimony or coveting a neighbour's house, wife, servants or animals. Seeing the thunder and smoke, the people beg Moses to speak instead of God, and Moses tells them the fear is meant to keep them from sinning. God adds altar rules: earth or uncut stone, no tool marks, no steps.
EXO 21:1-36	Case law begins. A Hebrew servant serves six years and goes free, though a wife given by the master stays with her children; a servant who chooses to stay has his ear bored through with an awl. A daughter sold as a servant keeps her food, clothing and marital rights. Death penalties cover murder, striking or cursing a parent, and kidnapping, with a refuge for accidental killing. Injury sets the eye for eye standard. A known goring bull condemns its owner too.
EXO 22:1-31	Property law. A stolen ox is repaid fivefold and a sheep fourfold, but only double if found alive. A thief killed breaking in at night carries no bloodguilt; the same killing after sunrise does. Restitution covers grazing damage, fires, goods left for safekeeping and borrowed animals, several cases settled by an oath. Then capital clauses on sorceresses, bestiality and sacrificing to other gods, and protections: do not wrong the foreigner, the widow or the orphan, charge the poor no interest, return a cloak before sunset.
EXO 23:1-33	Rules for justice and the calendar. No false reports, no siding with a crowd, no favouritism to the poor either, no bribes, no oppressing foreigners. An enemy's stray ox goes back and his fallen donkey gets helped up. The land lies fallow every seventh year so the poor and the wild animals can eat. Three annual feasts: unleavened bread, harvest and ingathering. Do not boil a young goat in its mother's milk. An angel will lead them in, driving out the inhabitants little by little.
EXO 24:1-18	Moses, Aaron, Nadab, Abihu and seventy elders are summoned up the mountain, but only Moses may come near. Moses reports the words and the people agree to do them. He writes them down, builds an altar with twelve pillars, with young men sacrificing. Half the blood goes on the altar; he reads the covenant aloud and sprinkles the rest on the people. The seventy see the God of Israel standing on sapphire pavement, and eat and drink unharmed. Moses stays forty days in the cloud.
EXO 25:1-40	God asks for a freewill offering of gold, silver, brass, yarn, linen, acacia, oil, spices and gemstones, so a sanctuary can be built and he can dwell among them, made exactly to the pattern shown. Then specifications for the ark of acacia overlaid with gold, its carrying poles never to be removed from its rings, and a gold mercy seat with two hammered cherubim. Then the gold table for the bread of the presence, and a lampstand hammered from one talent of gold.
EXO 26:1-18	The tabernacle itself: ten linen curtains in blue, purple and scarlet worked with cherubim, joined in two sets of five with fifty loops and fifty gold clasps so the whole thing counts as one. Over it goes a tent of eleven goats' hair curtains, one more than the layer beneath, the extra doubled back over the front. Two more coverings go on top, rams' skins dyed red and sea cow hides. Then the frame: acacia boards ten cubits tall, twenty along the south side.
EXO 26:19-37	Forty silver sockets take the twenty south boards, two per board, and the same on the north; six boards close the west end with two more shaped for the corners. Five gold-overlaid acacia bars run along each side, with a middle bar passing the whole length. A veil worked with cherubim hangs on four gold pillars and divides the holy place from the most holy, where the ark goes. Table to the north, lampstand to the south, embroidered screen at the door.
EXO 27:1-21	The bronze altar is five cubits square and three high, with horns of one piece at the corners, brass pots, shovels, basins and fire pans, a bronze grating halfway up, and poles for carrying. The courtyard is a hundred cubits by fifty, walled with linen hangings on sixty pillars in brass sockets with silver hooks, and a twenty-cubit screen at the east gate. Every peg is brass. Israel supplies beaten olive oil so Aaron and his sons keep a lamp burning all night.
EXO 28:1-24	Aaron and his sons Nadab, Abihu, Eleazar and Ithamar are appointed priests, and skilled craftsmen are to make holy garments for glory and beauty: a breastplate, ephod, robe, checkered coat, turban and sash. The ephod is woven of gold, blue, purple, scarlet and linen with two shoulder straps, and carries two onyx stones engraved with the twelve tribal names, six per stone in birth order. The breastplate of judgment is square, folded double, a span each way, set with twelve named stones in four rows.
EXO 28:25-43	Chains, rings and a blue cord tie the breastplate to the ephod so it cannot swing loose, and Aaron carries the tribes' names over his heart. Into it go the Urim and the Thummim. The blue robe has a reinforced neck opening and a hem alternating gold bells with pomegranates, so Aaron can be heard going in and out and does not die. A gold plate engraved HOLY TO YAHWEH is tied to the turban. All the priests get linen undergarments, on pain of death.
EXO 29:1-23	The ordination ceremony. A young bull, two unblemished rams and a basket of unleavened bread are brought to the door of the Tent of Meeting. Aaron and his sons are washed, dressed, and Aaron is anointed with oil poured on his head. They lay hands on the bull, killed as a sin offering, its blood put on the altar horns and its carcass burned outside the camp. The first ram is burned whole. The second's blood goes on their right ear tips, thumbs and toes.
EXO 29:24-46	The fat, thigh and bread are placed in their hands and waved before God, then burned. The breast becomes Moses' portion, and the waved breast and thigh become the priests' permanent share of Israel's peace offerings. Aaron's garments pass to his successors, worn seven days on taking office. The ram is boiled and eaten at the tent door by the priests only, and anything left by morning is burned. The ordination runs seven days. After that, two lambs are offered daily, forever.
EXO 30:1-18	A gold-covered acacia incense altar, one cubit square and two high, with horns and carrying poles, goes in front of the veil. Aaron burns fragrant incense on it morning and evening when he tends the lamps, and nothing else may be offered on it. Once a year its horns get atonement blood. Then a census rule: everyone counted, twenty and over, pays half a shekel, rich and poor exactly the same, to prevent a plague. Then a bronze basin between the tent and the altar.
EXO 30:19-38	Aaron and his sons must wash hands and feet at the basin before entering the tent or approaching the altar, on pain of death. The anointing oil recipe follows: liquid myrrh, cinnamon, fragrant cane, cassia and olive oil in exact weights, blended by a perfumer, anointing the tent, the furniture, the altars, the basin and the priests. Nobody may make it or use it on an outsider. The incense is equal weights of gum resin, onycha, galbanum and frankincense, salted; copying its scent also gets someone cut off.
EXO 31:1-18	God names the craftsmen: Bezalel of Judah, filled with the Spirit of God in wisdom and every kind of workmanship, in gold, silver, brass, stonecutting and wood carving, with Oholiab of Dan alongside him and skill given to every willing worker. Between them they will make the tent, the ark, the furniture, the altars, the basin, the garments, the oil and the incense. God insists on the Sabbath as a perpetual sign, death for anyone who works, then hands Moses the two stone tablets, written with God's finger.
EXO 32:1-16	With Moses long delayed on the mountain, the people ask Aaron to make them gods to go before them. He collects their gold earrings, casts a calf, and they declare it the god that brought them out of Egypt. Aaron builds an altar and announces a feast to Yahweh; the next day they sacrifice, eat, drink and revel. God calls Israel stiff-necked and offers to destroy them and start again from Moses. Moses argues they are God's people, and the promise to Abraham stands. God relents.
EXO 32:17-35	Joshua hears the noise and takes it for war; Moses says it is singing. Seeing the calf and the dancing, he throws down the tablets and breaks them, burns the calf, grinds it to powder, scatters it on water and makes Israel drink it. Aaron blames the people and claims the calf came out of the fire by itself. Moses calls for whoever is on God's side; about three thousand are killed. Moses then seeks atonement, offering to be blotted out of God's book. God refuses.
EXO 33:1-23	God tells Moses to lead the people to the promised land with an angel ahead of them, but says he will not go among them, since they are stiff-necked and he would destroy them. The people mourn and strip off their jewellery. Moses pitches a tent outside the camp, where the cloud comes down and God speaks with him face to face. Moses presses for God's presence, and wins. He then asks to see God's glory, and is told he may see God's back but not his face.
EXO 34:1-18	Moses cuts two new stone tablets and goes back up alone at dawn, with nobody and no livestock allowed near the mountain. God descends in the cloud and proclaims his name: merciful and gracious, slow to anger, abounding in loving kindness, forgiving sin yet by no means clearing the guilty. Moses worships and asks God to go with them. God renews the covenant, promising unheard-of wonders, drives out the six nations, and forbids treaties: break their altars and pillars, cut down their poles, since his name is Jealous.
EXO 34:19-35	The covenant terms continue: every firstborn belongs to God, sons are redeemed, and nobody appears before God empty-handed. Rest on the seventh day even in ploughing and harvest. Keep the feasts, and nobody will covet the land while the men are away at them. No leaven with sacrificial blood, no Passover meat left till morning, firstfruits brought in, and no boiling a goat in its mother's milk. Moses writes the covenant over forty days, then comes down with his face shining so brightly he wears a veil.
EXO 35:1-35	Moses assembles the congregation and restates the Sabbath, adding one ban: no fire kindled anywhere on that day. He then calls for a freewill offering of gold, silver, brass, dyed yarn, linen, goats' hair, skins, acacia, oil, spices and gemstones. Everyone whose heart moves them comes, bringing brooches, earrings, signet rings and armlets. Skilled women spin the blue, purple, scarlet and linen. The rulers bring the onyx and setting stones. Moses names Bezalel and Oholiab, noting that God has also given them the ability to teach.
EXO 36:1-38	Bezalel, Oholiab and every skilled worker take the offerings and begin, and the people keep bringing more every morning. The craftsmen report there is far more than the job requires, so a proclamation tells everyone to stop. The work then follows the earlier instructions exactly: ten linen curtains with cherubim joined by fifty gold clasps, eleven goats' hair curtains joined by fifty brass ones, two outer coverings, acacia boards in silver sockets, gold-covered bars with a middle bar running the full length, the veil, and the door screen.
EXO 37:1-29	Bezalel makes the gold-covered ark with its rings and poles, and the mercy seat with two cherubim of beaten gold at its ends, wings spread, faces turned toward each other. He makes the gold table with its rim, rings, poles and dishes. The lampstand is beaten from a single talent of pure gold, base, shaft, branches, cups, buds and flowers all in one piece, with seven lamps and snuffers. He also makes the gold incense altar, the anointing oil and pure incense.
EXO 38:1-31	The bronze altar is five cubits square and three high, hollow, with horns, a grating and poles, plus pots, shovels, basins, forks and fire pans. The basin and base are cast from the bronze mirrors of the women who served at the tent door. The courtyard is hung with linen on sixty pillars in bronze sockets with silver hooks. Then an audit: twenty-nine talents of gold, a hundred talents and 1,775 shekels of silver from 603,550 men at half a shekel each, and seventy talents of brass.
EXO 39:1-21	The priestly garments are made from blue, purple and scarlet. Gold is beaten into thin plates and cut into wire, woven with the yarn and linen. The ephod has joined shoulder straps and a woven band, with two onyx stones engraved with the tribal names and set in gold. The breastplate is square and doubled, a span each way, set with twelve stones in four rows, fitted with gold chains and rings, and tied to the ephod with a blue cord so it cannot come loose.
EXO 39:22-43	The blue robe is woven in one piece with a reinforced neck, its hem alternating pomegranates and gold bells. Linen coats, turban, headbands, breeches and a sash follow for Aaron and his sons, and the gold plate reading HOLY TO YAHWEH is tied to the turban with a blue cord. Everything is then carried to Moses: the tent, coverings, ark, table, lampstand, both altars, the basin, the courtyard hangings, and the garments. Moses inspects it all, finds it done as commanded, and blesses them.
EXO 40:1-38	God tells Moses to raise the tabernacle on the first day of the first month: ark behind the veil, table and lamps, incense altar, burnt offering altar and basin outside, courtyard hung, everything anointed, Aaron and his sons dressed and anointed. Moses does all of it, and the tabernacle goes up in the second year. When it is finished the cloud covers the tent and God's glory fills it so completely that Moses cannot enter. Israel travels only when the cloud lifts.
EZK 1:1-28	By the river Chebar among the exiles, Ezekiel sees a storm wind out of the north carrying fire and glowing metal. Inside it are four living creatures, each with four faces, human, lion, ox and eagle, four wings and calf-like feet. Beside each is a wheel within a wheel, its rims full of eyes, rising when the creatures rise. Above their heads is a crystal expanse, and above that a sapphire throne with a figure like a man on it, ringed with fire and rainbow. Ezekiel falls on his face.
EZK 2:1-10	The voice calls him son of man and tells him to stand; the Spirit sets him on his feet. He is sent to Israel, a rebellious nation of stubborn, hard-faced people, and told to say Thus says the Lord Yahweh whether they listen or not, so they will know a prophet was among them. He is not to fear their words or their looks, though he lives among briers, thorns and scorpions. A hand holds out a scroll written on both sides, and its writing is lamentations, mourning and woe.
EZK 3:1-27	Ezekiel eats the scroll and finds it sweet as honey. He is sent not to foreigners, who would have listened, but to Israel, who will not, so God makes his forehead harder than flint. The Spirit lifts him to Tel Aviv among the exiles, where he sits overwhelmed seven days. Then God appoints him watchman: fail to warn the wicked and their blood is on his hands; warn them and he is clear. He is shut in his house, bound with ropes and mute except when God opens his mouth.
EZK 4:1-17	Ezekiel acts out the siege of Jerusalem. He scratches the city on a clay tile, builds siege works against it, and sets an iron pan between himself and it as a wall. Then he lies on his left side 390 days for the guilt of Israel and on his right 40 for Judah, a day per year. His food is a rationed loaf of mixed grains baked over human dung, a sign that Israel will eat unclean bread among the nations; when he objects, God allows cow dung instead.
EZK 5:1-17	Ezekiel shaves his head and beard with a sword, weighs the hair and divides it in three. A third is burned in the city, a third struck with the sword, a third scattered to the wind; a few hairs go into his robe and some of those burn too. The hair is Jerusalem, more rebellious than the nations around her. God will do what he has never done before: fathers eating sons, sons eating fathers, a third dead of plague and famine, a third by the sword, a third scattered.
EZK 6:1-14	Ezekiel faces the mountains of Israel and prophesies against them, and the hills, ravines and valleys too. God will bring a sword against the high places, break the incense altars, and lay the dead in front of the idols they worshipped, their bones scattered around the altars. A scattered remnant will survive and there remember God, loathing themselves for what they did. He is told to clap, stamp and say Alas: the distant die by plague, the near by the sword, the besieged by hunger.
EZK 7:1-27	A short, hammering oracle: the end has arrived on all four corners of the land, and God's eye will not spare. Trade becomes pointless, and the trumpet sounds but nobody goes out to fight. Sword outside, plague and famine inside. Survivors moan on the mountains like doves, hands limp and knees like water. They throw their silver into the streets and treat their gold as filth, since neither can buy food. Looking for a vision, they find none, and priests and elders have nothing left to say.
EZK 8:1-18	In the sixth year, with the elders of Judah sitting before him, a fiery figure takes Ezekiel by a lock of hair and carries him in vision to Jerusalem. God shows him four things in escalating order: the image of jealousy at the north gate; a hidden room reached by digging through a wall, where seventy elders burn incense among painted idols saying Yahweh does not see us; women weeping for Tammuz; and twenty-five men with their backs to the temple, worshipping the sun.
EZK 9:1-11	God calls the city's executioners forward. Six men arrive with weapons, and a seventh in linen carrying a writer's inkhorn. The glory moves from the cherub to the threshold, and the man in linen is sent through Jerusalem to mark the foreheads of everyone who grieves over its abominations. The six follow and kill without pity, sparing only the marked, beginning at the sanctuary with the elders. Ezekiel asks if anyone will be left. God answers that the land is full of blood.
EZK 10:1-22	Ezekiel sees the sapphire throne again above the cherubim. The man in linen is told to go in among the whirling wheels, take handfuls of burning coals from between the cherubim, and scatter them over the city; a cherub hands him the fire. He describes the wheels again, beryl, wheel within wheel, covered with eyes, each creature with four faces. Then the glory leaves the temple threshold, settles over the cherubim, and they rise and stop at the east gate.
EZK 11:1-25	The Spirit brings Ezekiel to the east gate, where twenty-five men, among them Jaazaniah and Pelatiah, say the city is the cauldron and they are the meat. Ezekiel answers that the dead they filled the streets with are the meat, and that they will be taken out of the pot and killed at the border. Pelatiah drops dead while he speaks. God promises the exiles he is their sanctuary abroad, and will replace their heart of stone with flesh.
EZK 12:1-28	Ezekiel packs an exile's baggage and carries it out in daylight, then at evening digs through the wall with his hands and goes out with his face covered. Asked what he is doing, he explains: this is the prince in Jerusalem, who will leave through a hole in the wall, be caught in God's net, and die in a Babylon he never sees. Eating and drinking in fear is a second sign. Two sayings are answered: that visions never come true, and that this one is far off.
EZK 13:1-23	An oracle against prophets who prophesy out of their own imagination. They are foxes among ruins who never went up to the gaps in the wall, and they promise peace where there is none. The image is a flimsy wall someone whitewashes: God sends a downpour, hailstones and a storm wind, and when it falls the plasterers die under it. Then women who prophesy, sewing magic bands and veils to trap people for handfuls of barley. God will tear the bands off and free those they hunted.
EZK 14:1-23	Elders of Israel come to consult God. God says they have taken idols into their hearts, and anyone who comes to a prophet in that condition will be answered according to those idols and cut off; and if the prophet answers, God deceived him, and both bear the same guilt. Then a second ruling: in a land God judges by famine, wild animals, sword or plague, even Noah, Daniel and Job would save only themselves, not a son or daughter. Jerusalem gets all four at once.
EZK 15:1-8	A short parable about wood. Vine wood is no better than any other wood in the forest; nobody builds with it, and you cannot carve a peg from it to hang a pot on. It is fuel, and once the fire has eaten both ends and charred the middle it is less useful than before. God says the people of Jerusalem are that piece of vine. They come out of one fire only for another to consume them.
EZK 16:1-33	Jerusalem's history told as an allegory. Born to an Amorite father and a Hittite mother, she was abandoned in a field until God passed by and told her to live. He raised her, washed her, dressed her in fine linen, gave her gold and a crown, and her beauty became famous. She then trusted that beauty, used his gifts to make shrines and idols, and sacrificed the children she had borne him. She took Egypt, Assyria and Chaldea as lovers, and unlike other prostitutes she paid them.
EZK 16:34-63	The sentence uses the same imagery as the crime. God gathers every lover she took, exposes her to them, and lets them pull down her shrines, strip her jewelry, stone her and burn her houses. Then his anger is spent. A proverb follows: like mother, like daughter. Samaria is her elder sister, Sodom her younger, whose sin is named as pride, plenty of food, ease and no help for the poor. Jerusalem outdid both, yet the oracle ends in restoration and an everlasting covenant.
EZK 17:1-24	Ezekiel poses a riddle. A great eagle snaps the top shoot off a cedar and plants it in a city of merchants, then sows seed that grows into a low vine turned toward him. A second eagle appears, the vine bends its roots that way, and is told the east wind will wither it. The meaning is plain: Babylon installed a royal relative under oath, who broke it by asking Egypt for horses. God will plant his own shoot on a high mountain in Israel.
EZK 18:1-32	God bans the proverb about fathers eating sour grapes and children's teeth being set on edge. Every soul is his, and the soul that sins is the one who dies. He works through three generations: a just man lives, his son who does the opposite dies, and that son's son, refusing to copy his father, lives. To the objection that this is unfair, he answers that a wicked man who turns will live. Make a new heart, he says: turn and live.
EZK 19:1-14	A funeral song for the princes of Israel, in two images. A lioness raises a cub who learns to devour men, until the nations trap him and drag him with hooks to Egypt; she raises a second, who is netted, caged and taken to Babylon. Then a vine planted by water, fruitful enough for rulers' scepters, torn up in fury and burned by fire from its own branches. No branch is left for a scepter. The text says twice that this is a lament.
EZK 20:1-26	In the seventh year the elders come to inquire, and God refuses to be inquired of. Instead Ezekiel recites their history. In Egypt he swore to bring them to a land flowing with milk and honey and told them to throw away their idols; they refused, and he acted for his own name's sake. In the wilderness he gave statutes and Sabbaths; they rebelled, and he spared them again. Their children rebelled too. He says he also gave them statutes that were not good.
EZK 20:27-49	The history continues into the land, where they found every high hill and leafy tree to sacrifice on, which is where the name Bamah comes from. God asks whether they are polluting themselves the same way, and again refuses to be consulted. Their plan of becoming like the nations will not happen: he will gather them, judge them face to face, and purge out the rebels. Told to prophesy against the forest of the south, Ezekiel complains that people call him a parable-teller.
EZK 21:1-18	Ezekiel faces Jerusalem and announces that God is drawing his sword against righteous and wicked alike, and it will not go back. He is to groan aloud with a broken heart, and when asked why, to say it is the news coming: every heart melting, every knee weak as water. Then the sword song, a blade sharpened for slaughter and polished to flash like lightning, handed to a killer. He beats his thigh and claps his hands while the sword doubles again.
EZK 21:19-32	Ezekiel marks out two roads for the king of Babylon's sword, with a signpost at the fork: one to Rabbah of the Ammonites, one to Jerusalem. At the junction the king takes omens, shaking arrows and inspecting a liver, and the lot says Jerusalem. Those who swore oaths will call the divination false, but their own guilt delivers them. The prince of Israel is told to take off his turban and crown, since the low goes up and the high comes down until the one comes whose right it is.
EZK 22:1-31	Three oracles against Jerusalem, called the bloody city. The first is a charge sheet: princes shedding blood, contempt for parents, oppression of foreigners and widows, profaned Sabbaths, extortion. The second is a smelting image, in which Israel is the dross left after the silver, gathered into the city as into a furnace. The third names each class in turn: prophets like a lion, priests who erased the line between holy and common, princes like wolves. God looked for one man to stand in the gap and found nobody.
EZK 23:1-25	An allegory of two sisters, daughters of one mother, who both prostituted themselves in Egypt. Oholah is Samaria and Oholibah is Jerusalem. Oholah pursued the Assyrians and their idols, so God handed her over to those same lovers, who killed her and took her children. Her sister watched and did worse: she chased the Assyrians, then saw Babylonians drawn in red on a wall and sent to Chaldea for them. God will bring all those lovers against her to judge her.
EZK 23:26-49	The judgment continues. She is stripped of clothes and jewels, handed to the people she hates, and left bare. She is given her sister's cup to drink, deep and wide, a cup of drunkenness and desolation, drained until she gnaws its broken pieces. God tells Ezekiel to judge both sisters and lists the charges: adultery with idols, blood on their hands, sons passed through the fire, and entering the sanctuary the same day. A mob will stone them, kill their children and burn their houses.
EZK 24:1-27	On the day Babylon reaches Jerusalem, Ezekiel records the date and tells a parable: set the pot on the fire, load in the choice cuts and boil it hard. Then the pot itself is the problem, corroded beyond cleaning, so it is set on the coals until the metal glows. Ezekiel's wife dies that evening, and he is forbidden to mourn her. God will profane the sanctuary they love, and they too will not mourn.
EZK 25:1-17	Four short oracles against neighbours who enjoyed Judah's fall. Ammon said Aha over the profaned sanctuary, so it is handed to the people of the east, with Rabbah left as a camel stable. Moab and Seir said Judah is just like every other nation, and lose their frontier cities to the same easterners. Edom took revenge, so it is made desolate from Teman to Dedan, with Israel carrying it out. The Philistines acted out of long standing spite, so the coast is cut off.
EZK 26:1-21	Tyre celebrated Jerusalem's fall as good for business, so God brings nations against her like the sea bringing up waves. Nebuchadnezzar is named: siege mounds, battering rams, hooves in the streets, her stones and timber dumped in the water. The dust is scraped off and the island left as bare rock where fishermen spread their nets. The songs and the harps stop. The princes of the sea come off their thrones, sit trembling on the ground, and sing a lament.
EZK 27:1-36	A lament for Tyre, built as a shipwreck. The city is described as a ship: cypress planks from Senir, a cedar mast, oars of Bashan oak, an embroidered Egyptian sail, rowers from Sidon and caulkers from Gebal. Then the trade register, running from Tarshish with silver and tin to the spices and gold of Sheba. Then the east wind breaks her at sea, cargo and crew go down together, and the sailors on shore throw dust on their heads.
EZK 28:1-26	Two oracles about Tyre's ruler and one about Sidon. The prince of Tyre said I am a god and grew rich by real commercial skill; God answers that he is a man, and asks whether he will still say it in front of the man killing him. The second is a lament for the king of Tyre, in Eden covered in precious stones, blameless until violence came in through his trade. He is burned to ash before watching kings. Sidon gets plague.
EZK 29:1-21	An oracle against Pharaoh, pictured as the great crocodile lying in his own river saying the Nile is mine and I made it. God will put hooks in his jaws, drag him out with the fish stuck to his scales, and leave him in the field. The charge is that Egypt was a reed staff that splintered when Israel leaned on it. Egypt will lie empty forty years, then return as the lowest of kingdoms. Nebuchadnezzar got no pay for Tyre, so God gives him Egypt as wages.
EZK 30:1-26	A wail for the day of Yahweh over Egypt: clouds, a sword, and anguish in Ethiopia. Egypt's allies fall with her, and Nebuchadnezzar is named as the instrument. God will dry the rivers, destroy the idols of Memphis and leave Egypt without a prince. A list of cities follows, from fire in Zoan to daylight withdrawing at Tehaphnehes. A dated oracle adds that one of Pharaoh's arms is already broken and unhealed, and God will break both while strengthening Babylon's.
EZK 31:1-18	Pharaoh is asked who he resembles, and is given Assyria as the answer: a cedar taller than every tree in Lebanon, watered by the deep, sheltering every bird and nation in its shade, so beautiful the trees of Eden envied it. Because its heart grew proud of its height, God handed it to the mightiest of the nations, who cut it down, and everyone living in its shade left. No well watered tree should exalt itself, since all go down to the pit.
EZK 32:1-18	A lament over Pharaoh, who thought himself a young lion but is really a sea monster thrashing in the rivers and fouling the water with his feet. God will throw a net over him, haul him onto dry land, and leave his flesh on the mountains and the valleys filled with him. When he is put out, the sky goes dark, stars, sun and moon all covered. The sword of the king of Babylon comes, Egypt's pride is ended, and afterwards the rivers run clear like oil.
EZK 32:19-32	A tour of Sheol, where the nations who terrified the living lie in ranks. Egypt is told to go down and lie with the uncircumcised. Assyria is there, graves in the far corners of the pit. Elam is there, bearing its shame. Meshech and Tubal are there with their crowds, and a note distinguishes warriors buried with their swords under their heads. Edom, the princes of the north and the Sidonians are all there. Pharaoh will look at them and be comforted by the company.
EZK 33:1-16	The watchman image is set out as a rule. When a watchman blows the trumpet at the sight of the sword, anyone who hears and ignores it is responsible for his own death; if the watchman does not blow, the victim still dies but his blood is charged to the watchman. God applies this to Ezekiel directly. Then he answers the complaint that their sins are wasting them away: he takes no pleasure in the death of the wicked, and neither past record carries forward.
EZK 33:17-33	The people again say the Lord's way is not fair, and God repeats that each is judged by his own conduct. Then a man escaped from Jerusalem arrives with the news that the city has fallen, and Ezekiel's muteness ends. Those living in the ruins argue that if Abraham inherited the land alone, they are many and it must be theirs. Finally God says people invite each other to hear Ezekiel as though he were a singer: they listen and do nothing.
EZK 34:1-15	An oracle against the shepherds of Israel, who feed themselves instead of the flock. They eat the fat, wear the wool and slaughter the fattened animals, but have not healed the sick, bandaged the injured or looked for the lost, ruling by force instead. With no shepherd the sheep scattered and were eaten. God will take the flock out of their mouths and do the job himself, gathering his sheep and feeding them on good pasture.
EZK 34:16-31	God will seek the lost, bandage the broken and strengthen the sick, but destroy the fat and the strong, feeding the flock with justice. He then turns on the sheep themselves: some eat the good grass and trample the rest, foul the water with their feet, and shove the weak aside with shoulder and horns. He will save the flock from them and set one shepherd over them, his servant David. He promises a covenant of peace, rain in season, and no more famine.
EZK 35:1-15	An oracle against Mount Seir, that is Edom. Its cities will be laid waste and its hills and valleys filled with the slain. The reasons are an unending hostility and handing Israel over to the sword at the time of their disaster. Since Edom did not hate bloodshed, blood will pursue it. Edom also said both nations and both countries would become its own, ignoring that Yahweh was there. When the whole earth rejoices, Edom will be made desolate.
LAM 1:1-22	The city that was once full of people sits alone like a widow, a princess reduced to forced labor. Her roads mourn because nobody comes to the festivals; her friends have turned enemy. Jerusalem herself speaks: she admits she rebelled, describes fire sent into her bones and a net spread for her feet, and reports her priests and elders died in the streets searching for food. She asks God to deal with her enemies as he has dealt with her.
LAM 2:1-22	The poem catalogues what the Lord himself has done to Zion. He bent his bow like an enemy, tore down her strongholds, wrecked his own tabernacle. Her king and prophets get nothing, her elders sit in sackcloth and silence. Infants faint in the streets asking their mothers for bread that is not there. Passers-by hiss at the city once called the perfection of beauty. The chapter closes with the worst of it: women eating their own children, priests and prophets killed in the sanctuary.
LAM 3:1-66	A single man speaks: walked into darkness, walled in, set up as a target for arrows, fed bitterness and gravel. His hope in God has perished, then he recalls the one thing he can still hold: God's mercies are new every morning. He argues it is good to wait quietly, that God does not afflict from the heart. He admits the nation's rebellion, then returns to weeping, remembers a pit, calling from the lowest dungeon, and the answer he heard: Don't be afraid.
LAM 4:1-22	Gold has gone dull and the sanctuary stones lie scattered at every street corner. The sons of Zion, once worth their weight in gold, are treated as clay pots. Nursing children go thirsty and no one gives the young ones bread; those raised in purple now sit on ash heaps. The poet says the starving are worse off than those killed by the sword, and reports women have boiled their own children. He blames the prophets and priests who shed innocent blood. The poem ends turning to Edom.
LAM 5:1-22	A closing prayer that simply lists the conditions. The inheritance belongs to strangers, they buy their own water and firewood, they are orphans and their mothers are widows. They beg bread from Egypt and Assyria, and get food at the risk of their lives. Women are raped, princes hanged, elders dishonored, boys carrying millstones. The music has stopped and dancing has turned to mourning. They confess they sinned, and ask why God has forgotten them. The book ends unresolved, with the fear that he has rejected them entirely.
NUM 1:1-30	A year and a fortnight after leaving Egypt, in the Tent of Meeting at Sinai, God orders a census of every male aged twenty and over who can fight, counted by family and ancestral house. One chief per tribe assists: Elizur, Shelumiel, Nahshon, Nethanel, Eliab, Elishama, Gamaliel, Abidan, Ahiezer, Pagiel, Eliasaph and Ahira. The whole congregation assembles on the first day of the second month and registers their ancestry one by one. The tallies begin: Reuben 46,500, Simeon 59,300, Gad 45,650, Judah 74,600, Issachar 54,400, and Zebulun is reached.
NUM 1:31-54	The remaining tallies: Zebulun 57,400, Ephraim 40,500, Manasseh 32,200, Benjamin 35,400, Dan 62,700, Asher 41,500, Naphtali 53,400, for a total of 603,550. The Levites are left out. Instead they are put in charge of the Tabernacle of the Testimony: they carry it, take it down, set it up and camp around it, and any outsider who comes near is put to death. The other tribes camp under their own standards, and the Levite ring keeps wrath off the congregation.
NUM 2:1-34	The camp is a square around the Tent of Meeting, each tribe under its own banner. East is Judah with Issachar and Zebulun, 186,400, marching first. South is Reuben with Simeon and Gad, 151,450, second. The Tent and the Levites travel in the middle. West is Ephraim with Manasseh and Benjamin, 108,100, third. North is Dan with Asher and Naphtali, 157,600, last. The total is again 603,550 without the Levites, and Israel camps and moves in that order.
