NUM 3:1-27	Aaron's sons were Nadab, Abihu, Eleazar and Ithamar; Nadab and Abihu died before God for offering strange fire and left no children, so Eleazar and Ithamar serve. The Levites are given to Aaron as assistants for the tent, with death for any outsider who approaches. God claims them in place of every Israelite firstborn, all of whom became his the day he struck Egypt. Levite males are counted from one month old. Gershon numbers 7,500, camps west, and handles the coverings, screens, hangings and cords.
NUM 3:28-51	Kohath numbers 8,600, camps south, and carries the ark, table, lampstand, altars and sanctuary vessels, with Eleazar as chief of the Levite chiefs. Merari numbers 6,200, camps north, and takes the boards, bars, pillars, sockets, pegs and cords. Moses and the priests camp on the east, and any outsider approaching dies. The Levite total is 22,000. Israel's firstborn males from one month old come to 22,273, so after the substitution 273 remain, redeemed at five shekels each. Moses collects 1,365 shekels for Aaron.
2KI 17:1-20	Hoshea, the last king of Israel, reigns nine years and does evil, though less than those before him. He becomes Assyria's vassal, then sends to So of Egypt and stops paying tribute. Shalmaneser imprisons him, besieges Samaria three years and takes it, deporting Israel to Halah, Gozan and the cities of the Medes. The narrator then explains why: high places in every town, pillars and Asherah poles on every hill, prophets ignored, children passed through the fire. So God removed them, leaving Judah, which kept the same practices.
2KI 17:21-41	The account continues: Israel was torn from David's house, followed Jeroboam's sin, and was carried to Assyria. The king of Assyria resettles Samaria's cities with people from Babylon, Cuthah, Avva, Hamath and Sepharvaim. Not fearing Yahweh, they are attacked by lions, so a deported priest is sent back to Bethel to teach them. Each nation still makes its own gods for the high places, and the Sepharvites burn their children to Adrammelech and Anammelech. They appoint their own priests and both fear Yahweh and serve their images.
2KI 18:1-20	Hezekiah becomes king at twenty-five and reigns twenty-nine years. He does right as David did, removes the high places, breaks the pillars and the Asherah, and destroys the bronze serpent Moses made, which was being burned incense to, calling it Nehushtan. No king of Judah is like him. Samaria falls in his sixth year. In his fourteenth, Sennacherib takes the fortified cities of Judah, and Hezekiah pays three hundred talents of silver and thirty of gold, stripping the temple doors. Assyria then sends an army to Jerusalem.
2KI 18:21-37	Rabshakeh continues: Egypt is a broken reed that pierces the hand leaning on it, and it was Hezekiah who removed Yahweh's high places. He offers two thousand horses if Judah can find riders, and claims Yahweh sent him. Asked to speak Aramaic rather than Hebrew, he answers that his message is for the men on the wall, who face eating their own filth in a siege. He shouts terms of surrender and asks which nation's gods ever delivered it from Assyria.
2KI 19:1-20	Hezekiah tears his clothes, puts on sackcloth and goes into the temple, sending his officials to Isaiah with word that this is a day of trouble, like children come to birth with no strength to deliver them. Isaiah answers that God will put a spirit in Sennacherib so he hears news, goes home and falls by the sword there. Sennacherib sends a letter repeating that no nation's gods have saved it. Hezekiah spreads the letter before Yahweh and prays that all kingdoms may know he alone is God.
2KI 19:21-37	Isaiah delivers the oracle against Sennacherib. The daughter of Zion shakes her head at him; he has defied the Holy One of Israel, boasting of felling Lebanon's cedars and drying up Egypt's rivers. But God planned it long ago, which is why fortified cities fell. For his raging, God will put a hook in his nose and a bridle in his lips and turn him back. That night the angel of Yahweh strikes a hundred and eighty-five thousand in the camp.
2KI 20:1-21	Hezekiah falls mortally ill and Isaiah tells him to set his house in order. He turns to the wall, prays that his faithfulness be remembered, and weeps. Before Isaiah has crossed the city, God sends him back: fifteen years are added and the city will be defended. A cake of figs is laid on the boil and he recovers. Asked for a sign, he chooses the harder option, and the shadow goes back ten steps. Babylon's envoys are shown everything he owns.
2KI 21:1-26	Manasseh becomes king at twelve and reigns fifty-five years, longer than anyone. He rebuilds the high places his father destroyed, raises altars to Baal, makes an Asherah as Ahab did, and builds altars to the host of heaven in both temple courts, setting the carved Asherah inside the house God had named for himself. He passes his son through the fire, consults mediums, and fills Jerusalem with innocent blood. The prophets announce that Jerusalem will be wiped like a dish turned upside down.
2KI 22:1-20	Josiah becomes king at eight and reigns thirty-one years, doing right and turning neither right nor left. In his eighteenth year he sends Shaphan the scribe to have Hilkiah pay the workmen repairing the temple. Hilkiah tells him he has found the book of the law there. Shaphan reads it to the king, who tears his clothes and sends to inquire of Yahweh. Huldah the prophetess says the disaster written in the book will come, but that Josiah will go to his grave in peace.
2KI 23:1-16	Josiah gathers the elders and all the people to the temple, reads the book of the covenant aloud, and makes a covenant to keep it. He burns the vessels made for Baal and the host of heaven in the fields of Kidron, removes the idolatrous priests, and scatters the Asherah's dust on common graves. He defiles the high places from Geba to Beersheba and Topheth in the valley of Hinnom, burns the sun chariots, and breaks Jeroboam's altar at Bethel.
2KI 23:17-37	Seeing a monument, Josiah is told it marks the man of God who foretold what he has just done, and orders his bones left undisturbed. He removes the shrines throughout Samaria and kills their priests. He orders a Passover kept as the book requires, and none like it had been kept since the days of the judges. Even so, God does not turn from his anger over Manasseh. Pharaoh Necoh kills Josiah at Megiddo and makes Jehoiakim king.
2KI 24:1-20	Nebuchadnezzar comes up and Jehoiakim serves him three years, then rebels. Raiding bands of Chaldeans, Syrians, Moabites and Ammonites are sent against Judah at Yahweh's command, because of Manasseh's sins and the innocent blood he shed. Jehoiachin succeeds his father at eighteen and reigns three months before surrendering. Nebuchadnezzar takes the temple treasures, cuts up Solomon's gold vessels, and deports ten thousand, including the princes, fighting men and craftsmen, leaving only the poorest. His uncle Mattaniah is made king as Zedekiah and later rebels.
2KI 25:1-16	In Zedekiah's ninth year Nebuchadnezzar's army encamps against Jerusalem and builds siege works. By the fourth month of the eleventh year the famine is severe and there is no bread. The wall is breached, the soldiers flee by night, and the king is overtaken near Jericho. At Riblah his sons are killed in front of him, his eyes are put out, and he is taken to Babylon. A month later Nebuzaradan burns the temple, the palace and every great house.
2KI 25:17-30	The two pillars are described, eighteen cubits high with bronze capitals worked with network and pomegranates. Nebuzaradan takes the chief priest, three keepers of the threshold, five of the king's counsellors, a scribe and sixty men to Riblah, where they are put to death. Gedaliah, made governor, tells the captains at Mizpah to serve Babylon, and is killed by Ishmael of the royal line. The people flee to Egypt. In his thirty-seventh year of captivity Jehoiachin is released and given a daily allowance for life.
DAN 1:1-21	Nebuchadnezzar besieges Jerusalem, takes Jehoiakim and some temple vessels to Shinar, and orders that promising young Israelite nobles be trained three years in Chaldean language and learning. Daniel, Hananiah, Mishael and Azariah are renamed Belteshazzar, Shadrach, Meshach and Abednego. Daniel refuses the king's food and proposes a ten day test on vegetables and water. They come out healthier, so the diet stands. God gives all four learning and gives Daniel understanding of visions. The king finds them ten times better than his magicians.
DAN 2:1-26	Nebuchadnezzar has a dream that steals his sleep and demands his magicians tell him both the dream and its meaning. They protest that only the gods could do that. Enraged, he orders every wise man in Babylon killed. Daniel asks Arioch, the captain sent to carry it out, why the decree is so urgent, then asks the king for time. He and his three friends pray, the secret is revealed to him at night, and he blesses God for removing and setting up kings.
DAN 2:27-49	Daniel says no wise man can reveal the secret, but a God in heaven does. The dream was a huge statue: gold head, silver chest, bronze belly, iron legs, feet of iron mixed with clay. A stone cut without hands smashed the feet, the image blew away like chaff, and the stone became a mountain filling the earth. Nebuchadnezzar is the head of gold, followed by three more kingdoms. God will set up a kingdom that lasts forever. The king makes Daniel ruler of Babylon.
DAN 3:1-15	Nebuchadnezzar erects a gold image sixty cubits high on the plain of Dura and summons every official in the empire to its dedication. At the sound of the orchestra, everyone must fall down and worship it or be thrown into a burning furnace at once. The crowd complies. Some Chaldeans then accuse the Jews the king appointed over the province, naming Shadrach, Meshach and Abednego. Brought in, they are offered one more chance when the music plays, and asked what god could rescue them from his hand.
DAN 3:16-30	The three answer that they need no defence: their God can rescue them, and even if he does not, they will not worship the image. Nebuchadnezzar has the furnace heated seven times hotter and has them thrown in fully clothed; the flames kill the soldiers carrying them. Then he sees four men walking loose in the fire, the fourth like a son of the gods. The officials see no burn, no singed hair. He decrees protection for their God and promotes all three.
DAN 4:1-20	Nebuchadnezzar writes an open letter describing what God did to him. Comfortable in his palace, he dreamed of an enormous tree reaching the sky, feeding everyone, sheltering animals and birds. A watcher came down from heaven and ordered it cut down, branches stripped, fruit scattered, but the stump left bound with iron and bronze in the grass, wet with dew, its mind changed to an animal's for seven times. His magicians could not interpret it, so he called Daniel, who stood silent and troubled before beginning.
DAN 4:21-37	Daniel says the tree is the king, sentenced to be driven out to live with animals, eating grass and soaked with dew for seven times until he learns the Most High rules. Daniel advises him to show mercy to the poor. Twelve months later, admiring the Babylon he built, the king hears a voice from heaven and it happens the same hour. His hair grows like eagle feathers, his nails like claws. When he looks up, his reason returns and he praises God.
DAN 5:1-14	Belshazzar throws a feast for a thousand nobles and calls for the gold and silver vessels Nebuchadnezzar took from the temple, so he, his lords, wives and concubines can drink from them while praising gods of gold, silver, bronze, iron, wood and stone. A hand appears and writes on the plaster wall by the lampstand. The king goes pale, his knees knock, and he offers purple, a gold chain and third place in the kingdom to anyone who can read it. No one can. The queen recommends Daniel.
DAN 5:15-31	Daniel tells the king to keep his gifts, then reminds him what happened to Nebuchadnezzar, driven out among wild donkeys until he learned who rules. Belshazzar knew all this and still exalted himself, drinking from the temple vessels and praising gods that cannot see or hear. The writing reads MENE, MENE, TEKEL, UPHARSIN: the kingdom is counted and ended, the king weighed and found wanting, and the kingdom divided and given to the Medes and Persians. That night Belshazzar is killed and Darius the Mede takes the kingdom.
DAN 6:1-28	Darius appoints 120 satraps under three presidents, one of them Daniel, and plans to promote him over the whole realm. Unable to find fault in his work, the officials trick the king into signing an unalterable decree that for thirty days petitions may go only to him, on pain of the lion pit. Daniel keeps praying three times a day at his window. Darius works until sunset to save him but cannot, and Daniel is thrown in. In the morning Daniel reports an angel shut the lions' mouths.
DAN 7:1-13	In Belshazzar's first year Daniel dreams of four beasts rising from the great sea: a lion with plucked eagle's wings that stands like a man, a lopsided bear told to eat much flesh, a four headed leopard, and a fourth beast with iron teeth that crushes everything. A small horn rises with human eyes and a boastful mouth. Thrones are set, the Ancient of Days sits in white, the books are opened, and the beast is burned. One like a son of man comes with the clouds.
DAN 7:14-28	The one like a son of man is given everlasting dominion over all peoples. Daniel, troubled, asks a bystander what it means and is told the four beasts are four kings, and the saints will receive the kingdom forever. He asks about the fourth beast and the boastful horn, seen making war on the saints and winning until the Ancient of Days gives judgment to them. A king arises who subdues three others and wears out the saints for a time, times and half a time.
DAN 8:1-27	By the river Ulai at Susa, Daniel sees a two horned ram charging unopposed. A male goat with one horn comes from the west without touching the ground, smashes the ram's horns and tramples it. At its strongest the great horn breaks and four grow in its place, and out of one comes a little horn that grows toward the glorious land, stops the daily offering. The answer is 2,300 evenings and mornings. Gabriel explains the ram is Media and Persia, the goat Greece.
DAN 9:1-15	In the first year of Darius, Daniel reads in Jeremiah that the desolation of Jerusalem is to last seventy years, and turns to God in prayer with fasting, sackcloth and ashes. He confesses on behalf of the whole nation: they have sinned, done wrong, rebelled and refused to listen to the prophets sent to their kings and fathers. Righteousness belongs to God and shame to them, wherever they have been scattered. The curse written in the law of Moses has been poured out.
DAN 9:16-27	Daniel asks God to turn his anger from Jerusalem, to look at the desolate sanctuary, and to act not because they deserve it but for his own mercy and name. While he is still praying, Gabriel arrives in swift flight at the evening offering, saying the order went out the moment Daniel began. He announces seventy weeks decreed to end sin and bring everlasting righteousness: after which the Anointed One is cut off, and a coming prince's people destroy the city and sanctuary, with sacrifice stopped mid-week.
EZK 36:1-18	Ezekiel prophesies to the mountains of Israel, which the surrounding nations have claimed, saying the ancient high places are ours. God speaks in jealousy against them and against Edom, and swears they will bear their own shame. Then he addresses the land: the mountains will bear fruit for an Israel about to come home, the ground will be ploughed, and the ruined cities will do better than at first. The land will no longer be called a devourer of men.
EZK 36:19-38	Scattered among the nations, the exiles profaned God's name simply by being there, since people said these are Yahweh's people and they left his land. So what he is about to do is not for their sake but for his own name's. He will gather them, sprinkle clean water to cleanse them of their idols, exchange their heart of stone for a heart of flesh, and put his Spirit in them. The wasteland will be called like the garden of Eden.
EZK 37:1-28	God sets Ezekiel down in a valley of very dry bones and asks whether they can live; he answers that God knows. Told to prophesy, he does, and bone joins bone with a rattling, then sinew, flesh and skin, but no breath. Called from the four winds, the breath comes and they stand up as a huge army. The bones are Israel, who say their hope is gone. Then two sticks, Judah and Joseph, are joined into one nation.
EZK 38:1-23	An oracle against Gog of the land of Magog, prince of Rosh, Meshech and Tubal. God will turn him around with hooks in his jaws and bring out his whole army in full armour, joined by Persia, Cush, Put and Togarmah. In the latter years Gog will come against a land living securely in unwalled villages, and will plan to plunder it. Merchants ask whether he has come for silver and livestock. Since God brings him there, his fury rises when Gog arrives: an earthquake, plague, hailstones, fire and sulphur.
EZK 39:1-14	God brings Gog down from the far north onto the mountains of Israel, knocks the bow from his left hand and the arrows from his right, and leaves him and his hordes in the open for birds and animals. Fire goes out on Magog and the coastlands. Israel burns the abandoned weapons for fuel for seven years and needs no firewood. Gog is buried in a valley east of the sea, renamed the Valley of Hamon Gog, and the burial takes seven months.
EZK 39:15-29	Searchers walk the land marking any human bone they find so the gravediggers can collect it, and a city is named Hamonah. Then Ezekiel summons the birds and animals to a sacrificial feast on the mountains, where they eat the flesh of warriors and princes and drink blood until they are full. God says the nations will finally understand that Israel went into exile for its own guilt, and that he will now bring them home and pour out his Spirit.
EZK 40:1-23	Twenty-five years into the exile, Ezekiel is set down on a high mountain in Israel where a man like bronze, holding a flax line and a measuring reed, tells him to watch closely and report everything to Israel. The man measures the outer wall, then the east gate with its steps, threshold, guardrooms, porch and palm tree carvings, then the outer court with its pavement and thirty rooms, then the north gate to the same dimensions. Gate to gate measures a hundred cubits.
EZK 40:24-49	The tour continues to the south gate, then the inner court's south, east and north gates, each matching the earlier measurements and each reached by eight steps instead of seven. At the north gate are a room for washing burnt offerings and eight tables for slaughtering, with hooks fixed around the walls. Rooms are pointed out for the priests who keep the temple and for the sons of Zadok who keep the altar. The inner court is a hundred cubits square.
EZK 41:1-26	The man measures the temple building itself: the nave forty by twenty cubits, then an inner room twenty by twenty that he identifies as the most holy place. Around the house are thirty side rooms in three storeys, resting on ledges rather than cutting into the wall, and widening at each level. The walls and doors are carved throughout with palm trees between cherubim, each cherub having a human face on one side and a lion's on the other.
EZK 42:1-20	The guide shows Ezekiel the chambers north and south of the temple yard, built in three storeys with the upper rooms set back because the galleries take space from them. He explains that these are the holy rooms where the priests eat the most holy offerings and store the grain, sin and trespass offerings, and where they must leave their ministering garments before going out to the people. He then measures the whole complex, five hundred by five hundred, walled to separate the holy from the common.
EZK 43:1-27	Ezekiel is brought to the east gate and sees the glory of God returning from the east with a voice like many waters, the same vision he saw at the Chebar. The glory enters and fills the house, and a voice calls this the place of his throne where he will live among Israel forever. Ezekiel is told to show them the plan so they are ashamed. Then the altar is measured and a seven day consecration is prescribed.
EZK 44:1-14	The outer east gate is shut, and God says it stays shut because he entered by it; only the prince may sit there to eat bread. At the north gate Ezekiel again sees the glory filling the house and falls on his face. God bans the practice of bringing in foreigners uncircumcised in heart and flesh to keep charge of his sanctuary. The Levites who went after idols keep the gates and slaughter the offerings, but never act as priests.
EZK 44:15-31	The sons of Zadok, who stayed faithful when Israel strayed, come near to minister. They wear linen inside the inner court, nothing that makes them sweat, and change out of those clothes before going out to the people. They neither shave their heads nor let their hair grow long, drink no wine on duty, and marry only virgins of Israel or a priest's widow. They teach the difference between holy and common. They get no land: God himself is their inheritance.
EZK 45:1-25	When the land is divided, a holy strip is set aside: a portion for the priests with the sanctuary at its centre, a matching portion for the Levites, land for the city, and territory on either side for the prince. The princes are told to stop dispossessing people and to keep honest weights, and the standard ephah, bath, shekel and mina are defined. The prince supplies the offerings at every feast, new moon and Sabbath.
EZK 46:1-24	The inner east gate stays shut on working days and opens on Sabbaths and new moons, when the prince worships at its threshold while the priests prepare his offerings. Worshippers entering by the north gate must leave by the south and the other way round, never doubling back. The prince may give land to his sons permanently but to a servant only until the year of release, and may not seize anyone's inheritance. Ezekiel is then shown the kitchens.
EZK 47:1-23	Water flows out from under the temple threshold and runs east. The man measures a thousand cubits four times, and the water goes from ankle deep to knee deep to waist deep to a river too deep to cross. The stream runs down to the Arabah and heals the sea, so that fish swarm and fishermen spread nets, though the marshes stay salty. The trees fruit every month. The boundaries follow, with resident foreigners inheriting alongside the tribes.
EZK 48:1-17	The land is allotted in parallel strips running east to west. From the north down: Dan, Asher, Naphtali, Manasseh, Ephraim, Reuben and Judah. Then the holy offering, twenty-five thousand wide, with the sanctuary at its centre: one section for the Zadokite priests who did not go astray, one alongside for the Levites, which may never be sold or exchanged, and a five thousand strip of common land for the city with its pasture. The city measures four thousand five hundred on each side.
EZK 48:18-35	The land beside the holy portion is farmed to feed the city's workers, who come from all the tribes. The whole offering is a twenty-five thousand square, and the land on either side of it belongs to the prince. The remaining tribes follow south of it: Benjamin, Simeon, Issachar, Zebulun and Gad, down to the boundary at Tamar and Meribath Kadesh. The city has twelve gates, three on each side, each named for a tribe, and measures eighteen thousand round. Its name from that day is Yahweh is there.
NUM 4:1-25	A second Levite census counts only men aged thirty to fifty. The Kohathites' work is the most holy things, but they may not touch or look at them: before every move Aaron and his sons wrap the ark in the veil, add sealskin and a blue cloth, and fit the poles, then cover the table, lampstand, golden altar, vessels and bronze altar in coloured cloth under sealskin. Only then may the Kohathites lift them, or they die. Eleazar is responsible for the oil, incense and anointing oil.
NUM 4:26-49	The Gershonites also carry the court hangings, the gate screen and the cords, under Ithamar's direction. The Merarites take the boards, bars, pillars, sockets, pegs and cords, each item assigned to a man by name, also under Ithamar. The counts follow: Kohath 2,750, Gershon 2,630, Merari 3,200, a total of 8,580 Levites aged thirty to fifty available for carrying and serving at the Tent of Meeting. Everything is done as God commanded through Moses, each man assigned according to his service and his load.
NUM 5:1-31	Anyone with a skin disease, a discharge, or contact with a corpse is put outside the camp. Someone who wrongs another must confess and repay in full plus a fifth, and if there is no kinsman to receive it the priest does. Then the ordeal for a suspected unfaithful wife where there is no witness: the priest loosens her hair, makes her swear, and washes the written curses into holy water for her to drink. Guilt shows in her body; innocence leaves her free to conceive.
NUM 6:1-27	The Nazirite vow, open to men and women, requires abstaining from anything from the vine down to the seeds and skins, leaving the hair uncut, and avoiding any dead body, even a parent's. A sudden death nearby voids the vow: shave on the seventh day, two birds on the eighth, start over. At the end of the term they offer a lamb, a ewe, a ram and bread, burn the shaved hair under the peace offering, and may drink wine again. The chapter closes with the priestly blessing.
NUM 7:1-29	On the day the tabernacle is anointed, the twelve tribal chiefs bring six covered wagons and twelve oxen. Moses gives two wagons to Gershon and four to Merari, but nothing to Kohath, who carry the holy things on their shoulders. Each chief then brings his dedication offering on his own day. Nahshon of Judah goes first, then Nethanel and Eliab, each bringing the same set: a silver platter of 130 shekels, a bowl of 70, a gold ladle of incense, and animals for burnt, sin and peace offerings.
NUM 7:30-61	The dedication continues, one chief a day, in the order the camps march rather than by birth. Day four is Elizur of Reuben, then Shelumiel, Eliasaph, Elishama, Gamaliel, and on the ninth day Abidan of Benjamin. Each brings exactly the same gift as the last: a silver platter of 130 shekels and a bowl of 70, both full of fine flour mixed with oil, a gold ladle of incense, and the same animals for burnt, sin and peace offerings.
NUM 7:62-89	The last three days: Ahiezer of Dan, Pagiel of Asher and Ahira of Naphtali, each bringing the identical set. Then the totals: twelve silver platters and twelve bowls weighing 2,400 shekels together, twelve gold ladles weighing 120 shekels, twelve bulls, twelve rams, twelve lambs and twelve goats for the burnt and sin offerings, and for the peace offerings twenty-four bulls, sixty rams, sixty goats and sixty lambs. The chapter ends with Moses entering the Tent of Meeting and hearing the voice speak from above the mercy seat.
NUM 8:1-26	Aaron is told to set the seven lamps so they shine forward. The Levites are then dedicated: sprinkled with the water of cleansing, shaved all over, clothes washed, and presented before the congregation, who lay their hands on them. Aaron waves them before God as an offering from Israel, and two bulls are offered. They are taken in place of Israel's firstborn so that no plague falls at the sanctuary. Their working life runs from twenty-five to fifty, after which they assist but do no service.
NUM 9:1-23	In the second year God orders the Passover kept at its appointed time, and Israel keeps it at Sinai. Some men unclean from contact with a corpse ask why they should be excluded; Moses waits for an answer. God rules that anyone unclean or away on a long journey keeps it a month later, and a resident foreigner the same way. Anyone clean and at home who skips it is cut off. The cloud then covers the tabernacle, and Israel moves only when it lifts.
NUM 10:1-36	Two silver trumpets are made of beaten work: both together summon the congregation, one summons the chiefs, and an alarm blast moves the camps out. Only Aaron's sons may blow them. On the twentieth day of the second month of the second year the cloud lifts and Israel leaves Sinai for the wilderness of Paran, marching in the set order. Moses asks Hobab the Midianite to be their guide; he declines, and Moses presses him. The ark goes three days ahead.
NUM 11:1-18	The people complain, and fire from God burns the edge of the camp until Moses prays; the place is named Taberah. Then Israel craves meat and weeps for the fish, cucumbers, melons, leeks, onions and garlic of Egypt, saying there is nothing but manna. The manna is coriander-like, ground or boiled into cakes tasting of fresh oil. Moses tells God the burden is too heavy and asks to be killed. God tells him to gather seventy elders to share it, and to promise meat tomorrow.
NUM 11:19-35	The meat will last a month, until it comes out of their nostrils. Moses objects that there are six hundred thousand men; God asks whether his hand has grown short. The seventy elders receive the Spirit and prophesy once. Eldad and Medad, who stayed in the camp, prophesy there too, and when Joshua asks Moses to stop them Moses wishes all God's people were prophets. A wind brings quail, and while the meat is still between their teeth a plague strikes Kibroth Hattaavah.
NUM 12:1-16	Miriam and Aaron speak against Moses over the Cushite woman he married, and ask whether God has spoken only through him. The narrator notes that Moses was the humblest man on earth. God summons all three and says he speaks to other prophets in visions and dreams but to Moses mouth to mouth. When the cloud lifts Miriam is leprous, white as snow. Moses cries out for her healing, and God rules she must stay outside the camp seven days. The whole nation waits for her.
NUM 13:1-33	Twelve leaders, one per tribe, are sent to scout Canaan, among them Caleb of Judah and Hoshea of Ephraim, whom Moses renames Joshua. They are told to assess the people, the cities and the soil, and to bring back fruit. They pass Hebron and cut one cluster of grapes so large two men carry it on a pole. After forty days they report milk and honey, but strong people and fortified cities. Caleb urges going up at once; the others say the land devours its inhabitants.
NUM 14:1-22	The congregation weeps all night, tells Moses and Aaron they wish they had died in Egypt, and proposes appointing a captain and going back. Joshua and Caleb tear their clothes and insist the land is good and its people have lost their protection, but the crowd threatens to stone them. God's glory appears, and he asks how long these people will despise him, offering to make Moses a greater nation instead. Moses argues from reputation, that Egypt and Canaan will say God could not finish what he started.
NUM 14:23-45	God pardons but rules that none of that generation will see the land, except Caleb, who followed fully. Everyone counted at twenty and over will die in the wilderness; the children they said would be taken captive are the ones who will enter, after forty years of wandering, a year for each of the forty days of scouting. Joshua is named alongside Caleb. The ten spies die of plague. The people then march up the mountain anyway and are driven back to Hormah by the Amalekites and Canaanites.
NUM 15:1-22	Offering measures for when they enter the land: with a lamb, a tenth of an ephah of flour with a quarter hin each of oil and wine; with a ram, two tenths and a third hin; with a bull, three tenths and half a hin, scaled by the number of animals. The rule applies equally to native Israelites and resident foreigners, stated three times. When they eat the bread of the land they give a cake from the first of the dough as a wave offering.
NUM 15:23-41	An unintentional community-wide failure is covered by one bull and one male goat, and the congregation and the foreigners among them are forgiven. An individual's unwitting sin takes a year-old female goat. But anyone who sins deliberately, native or foreigner, has blasphemed and is cut off. Then a case with no precedent: a man is found gathering sticks on the Sabbath, held until God rules, and stoned outside the camp. Israel is told to put fringes with a blue cord on their garments, to remember the commandments.
NUM 16:1-25	Korah the Levite, with Dathan, Abiram and On of Reuben and 250 leaders, confronts Moses and Aaron, arguing the whole congregation is holy and they have no right to elevate themselves. Moses proposes a test: everyone brings a censer tomorrow, and God will show whom he chooses. He asks Korah whether Levite service is not enough without the priesthood too. Dathan and Abiram refuse to come, calling Egypt the land flowing with milk and honey. God tells Moses and Aaron to stand clear so he can consume them.
NUM 16:26-50	Moses warns the congregation away from the tents of Korah, Dathan and Abiram. If these men die ordinary deaths, God has not sent him; if the ground opens, they have despised God. As he finishes, the earth splits and swallows the men and their households, closing over them, while Israel flees. Fire then consumes the 250 who offered incense. Eleazar hammers their censers into altar plating. The congregation blames Moses and Aaron for the deaths, a plague breaks out, and Aaron stops it with incense after 14,700 die.
NUM 17:1-13	To end the argument over who is chosen, God has each tribal chief bring a rod with his name, Aaron's on Levi's, all twelve laid up in the Tent of the Testimony. Next day Aaron's rod has sprouted, budded, blossomed and produced ripe almonds. Moses brings all twelve out and each man takes his own back. Aaron's is put back as a permanent sign against rebellion. The people respond in panic, saying everyone who comes near the tabernacle dies and asking whether they are all going to perish.
NUM 18:1-15	God answers that fear by assigning responsibility. Aaron and his father's house bear the guilt of the sanctuary and priesthood, and the Levites are joined as assistants who may serve the tent but must never approach the sanctuary vessels or altar, on pain of death for both. Any outsider who comes near is put to death. The priests in return receive the offerings: every meal, sin and trespass offering as holy food, the best of the oil, wine and grain, and everything devoted or that opens the womb.
NUM 18:16-32	Human firstborn are redeemed from a month old at five shekels; firstborn cattle, sheep and goats are not redeemed but sacrificed, their meat going to the priests. This is a covenant of salt forever. The priests get no land, because God himself is their portion. The Levites receive Israel's tithe as their inheritance for the tent service, and hold no land either. The Levites must then tithe their own tithe, giving the best tenth to Aaron, after which the rest is theirs to eat as wages.
NUM 19:1-22	A flawless red cow that has never been yoked is taken outside the camp and killed in front of Eleazar, who sprinkles her blood toward the tent seven times. She is burned whole, with cedar, hyssop and scarlet thrown into the fire. Everyone involved becomes unclean until evening, and the ashes are stored to make water for cleansing. Anyone touching a corpse, entering a tent where someone has died, or touching a bone or a grave is unclean seven days, and is sprinkled on the third and seventh days.
NUM 20:1-29	Israel reaches Kadesh, where Miriam dies. There is no water, and the people quarrel with Moses and Aaron. God tells him to gather the people and speak to the rock. Moses calls them rebels, asks whether they should bring water out, and strikes the rock twice. Water pours out, but God tells both men they will not bring Israel into the land. Edom refuses passage even for payment, so Israel turns aside. At Mount Hor Aaron's garments pass to Eleazar, Aaron dies, and Israel mourns him thirty days.
NUM 21:1-35	The king of Arad takes captives; Israel destroys his cities and names the place Hormah. Detouring around Edom, the people complain about the food, calling the manna disgusting, and venomous snakes are sent among them. When they confess, Moses makes a bronze serpent on a pole, and anyone bitten who looks at it lives. The march continues to Beer, where they sing to the well. Sihon refuses passage and attacks, and Israel takes his land including Heshbon. Og comes out at Edrei and is destroyed with all his people.
NUM 22:1-21	Camped on the plains of Moab, Israel alarms Balak, who says they will lick up everything like an ox eating grass. He sends elders with payment to Balaam at Pethor, asking him to curse Israel. Balaam consults God, who says not to go, because the people are blessed. Balak sends a larger delegation with promises of honour. Balaam says he cannot go beyond God's word for a house full of silver and gold, and is finally told he may go but must speak only what he is given.
NUM 22:22-41	God is angry that Balaam goes, and his angel blocks the road with a drawn sword. The donkey sees it and swerves; Balaam beats her three times, the last after she lies down in a narrow lane. God opens her mouth and she asks what she has done; Balaam says he would kill her if he had a sword. His eyes are opened, he sees the angel and falls down, and is told to go on but speak only what he is given.
NUM 23:1-30	Balaam has Balak build seven altars and offer on each, then goes off alone. God gives him words, and instead of a curse he says he cannot curse whom God has not cursed, and wishes he could die like Israel. Balak protests, so they repeat the seven altars elsewhere. The second oracle says God is not a man that he should lie. Balak asks him to at least stop blessing them, then takes him to Peor for a third attempt.
NUM 24:1-25	Seeing God intends to bless Israel, Balaam stops using enchantments and looks toward the wilderness. The Spirit comes on him, and he praises Israel's tents spread like gardens by water, and promises a king higher than Agag. Balak strikes his hands together in fury and sends him away unpaid. Before leaving, Balaam delivers final oracles: a star out of Jacob and a sceptre that strikes Moab, Edom possessed, and Amalek destroyed.
NUM 25:1-18	At Shittim the people sleep with Moabite women and join themselves to Baal Peor. God's anger burns, and Moses orders the judges to kill everyone who joined the cult. While the congregation weeps at the tent door, an Israelite brings a Midianite woman in openly. Phinehas spears them both, and the plague stops, but 24,000 have died. He is granted a covenant of peace and a lasting priesthood. The pair are named Zimri and Cozbi, and Israel is told to attack Midian.
NUM 26:1-34	After the plague a second census is taken by Moses and Eleazar, of all men twenty and over able to fight, listed by clan. Reuben comes to 43,730, and the list recalls that Dathan and Abiram of that tribe were swallowed with Korah's company, though Korah's sons did not die. Simeon is 22,200, Judah 76,500 with Er and Onan noted as dead in Canaan, and Manasseh 52,700, where Zelophehad is recorded as having no sons but five daughters.
NUM 26:35-65	The remaining tallies: Ephraim 32,500, Benjamin 45,600, Dan 64,400, Asher 53,400 with a daughter named Serah recorded, and Naphtali 45,400, for a total of 601,730. God says the land is to be divided by these numbers but allotted by lot. The Levites are counted separately, 23,000 males from a month old, and receive no inheritance. The genealogy notes Amram and Jochebed as parents of Aaron, Moses and Miriam. Not one man counted at Sinai remains alive except Caleb and Joshua.
NUM 27:1-23	The five daughters of Zelophehad stand at the tent door: their father died in his own sin, not with Korah, left no sons, and his name should not vanish. Moses brings the case to God, who rules they are right and sets an order of inheritance: daughter, then brothers, then uncles, then nearest kinsman. God tells Moses to climb Abarim and see the land, since he will be gathered to his people like Aaron. Moses asks for a successor, and Joshua is commissioned before the people.
NUM 28:1-31	A calendar of offerings. Every day, two lambs, morning and evening, each with flour, oil and drink offering. On the Sabbath, two extra lambs. At each new moon, two bulls, a ram and seven lambs, wine in proportion, and a goat for sin. Passover on the fourteenth, then seven days of unleavened bread with the same offering daily and holy assemblies on the first and seventh. At the feast of weeks, the same again with a goat for atonement.
NUM 29:1-40	The seventh month's calendar. On the first, a day of trumpet blasts with one bull, one ram, seven lambs and a goat. On the tenth, a day of self-affliction with the same offering plus atonement. On the fifteenth begins a seven-day feast whose bull count descends day by day, thirteen down to seven, always with two rams and fourteen lambs. The eighth day is a solemn assembly with one bull. All of this is on top of the continual offering, vows and freewill offerings.
NUM 30:1-16	The law of vows. A man who vows or swears must do exactly what he said. A young woman in her father's house is bound by her vow if he says nothing, but it is annulled if he forbids it that same day. The same applies to a married woman and her husband. A widow or divorced woman is bound by whatever she vows. If a husband stays silent day after day he has confirmed the vow, and if he annuls it later he bears her guilt himself.
BAR 1:1-22	Baruch son of Nerias writes in Babylon five years after the Chaldeans burned Jerusalem, and reads the book aloud to the exiled king Jechonias and all the people by the river Sud. They weep, fast and pray, then collect money and send it with the returned temple vessels to the high priest for offerings, asking prayers for Nabuchodonosor and his son Baltasar and for themselves. Enclosed is a confession for the feast: righteousness belongs to God, shame to them, since they have disobeyed since Egypt.
BAR 2:1-18	The confession says God has done exactly what he warned through Moses: plagues worse than any under heaven, a siege in which parents ate their own children, and Israel left a reproach among the nations it was scattered into. Even then nobody turned from a wicked heart. The prayer appeals to the God who brought them out of Egypt: turn your wrath away, we are few, hear us for your own name's sake, since the dead cannot praise you but a hungry, failing soul can.
BAR 2:19-35	The plea rests not on the merits of their fathers or kings but on God's own words. The prophets told them to bow to the king of Babylon and stay in the land, warning that refusal would end all mirth and marriage; they refused, the land was emptied, royal bones were turned out to lie in heat and frost, and the temple was ruined. Moses also promised that exile would teach the stiff necked people, who would be given new hearts, the land sworn to Abraham, and an everlasting covenant.
BAR 3:1-37	The prayer closes with the anguished soul asking God to forget the sins of the fathers. Then the book turns to wisdom and asks Israel why it has grown old in a foreign country among the dead: it forsook the fountain of wisdom. Foreign princes, hoarders of silver, the merchants of Merran and Teman and the giants of old all missed her and went to the grave. Nobody fetched her from heaven or across the sea. Only God, who calls the stars, knows her, and he gave her to Jacob.
BAR 4:1-37	Wisdom is identified with the law that endures: hold it and live, leave it and die, and do not give its glory to a foreign nation. Israel was sold for correction, not destruction, having sacrificed to demons and forgotten God. Jerusalem then speaks as a widow, telling her neighbours to see the captivity of her children, taken by a shameless nation of strange speech. She wears sackcloth now, but tells her children to take heart: the one who brought the plagues will deliver them, and their enemy's city will burn.
BAR 5:1-9	Jerusalem is told to take off her mourning clothes and put on the glory that comes from God, a robe of righteousness and a crown of the Everlasting, and will be called The peace of righteousness. She is to stand on the height and look east and see her children gathered from every direction at the word of the Holy One. They left on foot as prisoners and are carried back high as if on a royal throne, with mountains levelled and valleys filled to make the road plain.
BAR 6:1-36	A letter Jeremiah sends to those about to be taken to Babylon, telling them they will stay seven generations before being brought out peaceably. They will see gods of silver, gold and wood carried on shoulders, and must not fear the crowds worshipping them. The letter then argues that these images are not gods: their tongues are polished by a craftsman and cannot speak, their priests steal their gold, they cannot save themselves from rust, dust, bats or thieves, and they must be carried because they have no feet.
BAR 6:37-73	The letter keeps listing what the idols cannot do: heal the blind, help the widow or the fatherless, make or unmake a king, give rain, judge a case or save anyone from death. Even the Chaldeans dishonour them, bringing a mute man to Bel as though it could hear, and the priests hide them or flee when war or fire comes. They are the work of carpenters and goldsmiths, while sun, moon, stars, wind and fire obey God. A just man with no idols is better off.
DAN 10:1-21	After three weeks of mourning without rich food, meat or wine, Daniel is by the Tigris when he sees a man in linen with a face like lightning and eyes like torches. His companions flee without seeing it, and Daniel collapses until a hand raises him. He is told his prayer was heard on the first day, but the prince of Persia delayed the messenger twenty-one days until Michael came to help. Strengthened, he hears that the fight with Persia, and then Greece, continues.
DAN 11:1-21	The messenger sets out the future as a line of kings: three more in Persia, then a rich fourth who provokes Greece, then a mighty king whose empire breaks toward the four winds and passes to strangers rather than his heirs. The rest is the king of the south against the king of the north: a failed marriage alliance, campaigns both ways, gods and treasure carried to Egypt, a fortified city taken. One king dies over taxes, and a contemptible man gains the kingdom by flattery.
DAN 11:22-45	The contemptible king wins by deceit and broken treaties, defeats the king of the south, whose own table companions betray him, and returns home hostile to the holy covenant. Ships of Kittim turn back his second southern campaign, so he profanes the sanctuary, stops the daily offering and sets up the abomination that makes desolate. The wise instruct many and fall by sword and captivity, which refines them. He exalts himself above every god, then dies camped between the sea and the holy mountain, with no one to help him.
DAN 12:1-13	Michael arises, unequalled trouble comes, and everyone whose name is in the book is delivered. Many who sleep in the dust awake, some to everlasting life and some to everlasting contempt, and the wise shine like stars. Daniel is told to seal the book until the end. Two figures at the river ask how long, and the man in linen swears it will be a time, times and a half. Daniel does not understand; he is given 1,290 and 1,335 days and sent to rest.
DEU 1:1-22	Forty years after Egypt, on the plains of Moab, Moses begins a long address to Israel. He recalls God's order at Horeb to leave the mountain and take the land promised to Abraham, Isaac and Jacob. Unable to bear the people's disputes alone, he had them choose wise men as captains of thousands, hundreds, fifties and tens, and charged the judges to show no partiality and fear no man. They reached Kadesh Barnea, where the people asked to send scouts ahead.
DEU 1:23-46	Moses recalls the twelve scouts, who brought back fruit and a good report, and the people's refusal to go up. They complained that God hated them and that the Anakim held cities walled to the sky. His reminder of Egypt and of the fire and cloud did not persuade them. God swore that none of that generation but Caleb would see the land, told Moses he too would not enter, and named Joshua and the children instead. They attacked anyway, were chased like bees to Hormah, and wept unheard.
DEU 2:1-20	Moses recounts the years spent circling Mount Seir and God's order to turn north and pass Esau's territory without provoking it, buying food and water for money. Edom's land, and Moab's, are not Israel's to take, having been given to Esau and to Lot's descendants. He notes the Emim and Rephaim who lived there before. Thirty-eight years passed between Kadesh Barnea and crossing the brook Zered, until the whole generation of fighting men was gone. Ammon is off limits too.
DEU 2:21-37	After more notes on displaced peoples, God tells Israel to cross the Arnon and take Sihon's land. Moses sends messengers to Sihon of Heshbon asking only for passage along the highway and offering to buy food and water. Sihon refuses, his spirit hardened by God, and comes out to fight at Jahaz. Israel destroys him, his sons and every inhabited city, keeping the livestock and plunder. They take everything from Aroer to Gilead, but do not approach Ammonite land.
DEU 3:1-29	Og of Bashan attacks at Edrei and is destroyed with all sixty of his fortified cities, his people killed and the livestock taken. Moses notes Og's iron bed, nine cubits long. He assigns the conquered land east of the Jordan to Reuben, Gad and half of Manasseh, on condition their fighting men cross over armed ahead of the others. He charges Joshua not to fear, then asks to see the land himself. God refuses and tells him to climb Pisgah and look.
DEU 4:1-24	Moses urges Israel to keep the statutes without adding or subtracting, pointing to the men destroyed at Baal Peor. Obedience will make other nations call them wise, and no nation has a god so near. He warns them not to forget Horeb, where the mountain burned and they heard a voice but saw no form, and so not to make an image of anything, or worship sun, moon or stars. He repeats that he himself will die outside the land.
DEU 4:25-49	Moses predicts that once settled, Israel will make images and be scattered among the nations, left few in number, serving gods of wood and stone that cannot see or hear. Even then, if they seek God wholeheartedly they will find him, because he is merciful and keeps the covenant. He argues that no nation has heard God speak from fire and lived, so they should know there is no other God. He then sets apart Bezer, Ramoth and Golan as cities of refuge east of the Jordan.
DEU 5:1-33	Moses recites the Ten Commandments, insisting the covenant at Horeb was made with the people standing there. The list runs from no other gods and no images through the name, the Sabbath, parents, murder, adultery, theft, false testimony and coveting. Here the Sabbath is grounded in the slavery in Egypt, and extends to servants, livestock and foreigners. God spoke these from the fire and added nothing more. The people, terrified, asked Moses to listen on their behalf, and God approved.
DEU 6:1-25	Moses gives the central command: Yahweh is one, and Israel is to love him with all their heart, soul and might. These words are to be taught to children, spoken at home and on the road, bound on the hand and forehead, and written on doorposts and gates. He warns that once they live in cities they did not build and eat from vineyards they did not plant, they will be tempted to forget. When a son asks what the laws mean, the answer is the story of Egypt.
DEU 7:1-26	Israel is told to destroy the seven nations of Canaan, make no covenant with them and not intermarry, since that would draw their children to other gods, and to smash the altars, pillars, poles and images. They were chosen not for their size, being the fewest of peoples, but because God loves them and keeps his oath. Obedience brings fertility, health and freedom from Egypt's diseases. They should not fear larger nations, but the conquest will be gradual, so that wild animals do not overrun the land.
DEU 8:1-20	Moses tells Israel to remember the forty years in the wilderness, where hunger and manna taught them that man does not live by bread alone, and where their clothes did not wear out and their feet did not swell. God disciplined them as a father disciplines a son. He describes the land ahead: brooks and springs, wheat, barley, vines, figs, pomegranates, olives and honey, stones of iron and hills with copper. The warning is against claiming, once rich, that their own strength earned it.
DEU 9:1-29	Israel is about to cross and dispossess taller peoples in walled cities, but Moses insists it is not for their righteousness. They are stiff-necked, and he reviews the evidence: the golden calf while he was on the mountain forty days without food, God's offer to destroy them and start again with him, the tablets thrown down and broken, the calf burned, ground to dust and thrown into the brook. He fasted another forty days for them and prayed for Aaron.
DEU 10:1-22	Moses recounts cutting two new tablets and making an acacia ark to hold them, God rewriting the ten commandments, and Levi being set apart to carry the ark and serve, with God as its only inheritance. He notes Aaron's death at Moserah and Eleazar's succession. The requirement is stated simply: fear God, love him, walk in his ways, serve with all the heart. God takes no bribes, defends the fatherless and widow, and loves the foreigner, so Israel must love foreigners too, having been foreigners in Egypt.
DEU 11:1-32	Moses says he is speaking to the adults who saw the plagues, the Red Sea and the earth swallowing Dathan and Abiram, not to their children. He contrasts Egypt, watered by foot, with a land of hills that drinks rain, so obedience brings the early and late rains and disobedience shuts the sky. The words are again to be bound on the hand and written on doorposts. He sets before them a blessing and a curse, to be placed on Mount Gerizim and Mount Ebal.
DEU 12:1-16	The law of a single sanctuary. Israel is to destroy every high place, altar, pillar and pole where the nations worshipped, and not treat their own God that way. Sacrifices, tithes, vows and firstborn are to be brought only to the one place God chooses, and eaten there with rejoicing, the Levite included. Everyone doing what seems right in his own eyes is to end. Ordinary meat may be killed and eaten at home like game, but the blood must be poured out on the ground.
DEU 12:17-32	Tithes, firstborn and vow offerings may not be eaten at home but only at the chosen place, with the household and the Levite present, and the Levite is not to be neglected. If that place is far away, ordinary meat may be killed and eaten locally by clean and unclean alike, as with gazelle or deer. The blood is never eaten, because the blood is the life, and is poured out like water. Israel is warned not to copy the nations, who burned their children.
GEN 4:1-26	Eve bears Cain and then Abel. Abel keeps sheep, Cain farms. Both bring offerings; God respects Abel's and not Cain's, and warns Cain that sin crouches at his door. Cain lures Abel into a field and kills him. Asked where his brother is, he says he is not his brother's keeper. God curses his farming, makes him a wanderer, and marks him against reprisal. Cain settles in Nod, and his line runs down to Lamech, who boasts of killing a man. Eve bears Seth.
GEN 5:1-32	A genealogy from Adam to Noah, ten generations, each entry giving the age at fatherhood, the years lived afterward, and the total before death. Adam lives 930 years, Methuselah 969, the longest. Two entries break the pattern: Enoch walks with God for three hundred years and is taken rather than dying, and Lamech names his son Noah as a comfort against the cursed ground. The list closes with Noah at five hundred fathering Shem, Ham and Japheth.
GEN 6:1-22	God's sons take human women as wives, and the Nephilim are on the earth. God limits human life to a hundred and twenty years. Seeing that human wickedness and violence fill the earth, God regrets making humanity and resolves to destroy all flesh, but Noah finds favour. God tells Noah to build a ship of gopher wood, three hundred cubits by fifty by thirty, sealed with pitch, with three decks and a door in the side, and to bring his family, two of every creature, and food.
GEN 7:1-24	God tells Noah to enter the ship with seven pairs of every clean animal and two of the unclean, since rain will come in seven days and last forty days and nights. On the seventeenth day of the second month the fountains of the deep burst and the sky opens. The family and the animals go in and God shuts the door. The waters rise until every mountain is covered and everything on dry land dies. The flood stands for a hundred and fifty days.
GEN 8:1-22	God remembers Noah and sends a wind; the waters fall and the ship rests on the mountains of Ararat. Noah sends out a raven, then a dove that finds no footing, then the dove again, which returns with an olive leaf, then a third time, and it does not come back. The ground dries and God tells them to leave the ship and multiply. Noah builds an altar and offers burnt offerings. God resolves never again to destroy all life, and promises the seasons will not cease.
GEN 9:1-29	God blesses Noah and his sons, gives them every living thing for food but forbids eating blood, and requires a reckoning for human bloodshed, since man is made in God's image. He makes a covenant never again to flood the earth and sets the rainbow as its sign. Noah plants a vineyard, gets drunk and lies uncovered. Ham sees him and tells his brothers, who cover him walking backwards. Waking, Noah curses Canaan to servitude and blesses Shem and Japheth. Noah dies at nine hundred and fifty.
GEN 10:1-32	A table of the nations descended from Noah's three sons. Japheth's line spreads to the coastlands. Ham's includes Cush, Egypt, Put and Canaan, and Cush fathers Nimrod, a mighty hunter whose kingdom begins at Babel and who builds Nineveh and other Assyrian cities; Canaan's descendants are the peoples of Canaan, with their borders described. Shem's line runs through Arpachshad to Eber, whose son Peleg is named for the earth being divided. From these families the nations spread after the flood.
GEN 11:1-32	The whole earth speaks one language. Settling in Shinar, the people make bricks and set out to build a city and a tower reaching the sky so they will not be scattered. God comes down, sees that nothing will be beyond them, and confuses their speech, so they stop building and scatter; the place is called Babel. A genealogy then runs from Shem to Terah, who fathers Abram, Nahor and Haran. Sarai is barren. Terah takes the family from Ur toward Canaan, settles in Haran, and dies there.
GEN 12:1-20	God tells Abram to leave his country for a land he will be shown, promising to make him a great nation through whom all families of the earth will be blessed. Abram, seventy-five, sets out from Haran with Sarai and Lot, reaches Canaan, and builds altars at Shechem and near Bethel. Famine drives him to Egypt, where he tells Sarai to pass as his sister. Pharaoh takes her into his house and enriches Abram. Plagues follow, Pharaoh works out why, and sends them all away.
GEN 13:1-18	Abram comes up from Egypt rich in livestock, silver and gold, and returns to his altar between Bethel and Ai. He and Lot have too many herds for the land to carry them both, and their herdsmen quarrel, so Abram offers Lot the first choice of territory. Lot picks the well-watered Jordan plain and settles near Sodom, whose people are wicked. God tells Abram to look in every direction: all of it will belong to his offspring, who will be as countless as dust. Abram settles at Hebron.
GEN 14:1-24	Four kings under Chedorlaomer put down a revolt by five kings of the plain, defeating Sodom and Gomorrah in the tar-pit valley of Siddim and carrying off the goods and Lot. Hearing of it, Abram arms three hundred and eighteen men of his household, attacks by night, pursues past Damascus and recovers everyone and everything. Melchizedek, king of Salem and priest of God Most High, brings bread and wine and blesses him, and Abram gives him a tenth. He refuses to keep anything of Sodom's.
GEN 15:1-21	God tells Abram not to fear, and Abram objects that he is childless and his servant Eliezer stands to inherit. God says the heir will be his own child and tells him to count the stars; Abram believes, and it is credited as righteousness. Asked for assurance about the land, God has him cut three animals in half. Abram drives off the birds, then in a dark, terrified sleep hears that his descendants will be enslaved four hundred years. A furnace and torch pass between the pieces.
GEN 16:1-16	Childless, Sarai gives Abram her Egyptian servant Hagar as a wife in the hope of getting children through her. Hagar conceives and looks down on Sarai, who blames Abram; he tells her to do as she likes, and she treats Hagar so harshly that Hagar runs into the wilderness. An angel finds her at a spring, tells her to go back, and promises her a son called Ishmael, who will live at odds with everyone. She names God as the one who sees. Ishmael is born when Abram is eighty-six.
GEN 17:1-27	At ninety-nine Abram is renamed Abraham, father of a multitude, and promised nations, kings and all Canaan as an everlasting possession. The covenant sign is circumcision of every male at eight days old, household slaves included. Sarai becomes Sarah and is promised a son. Abraham laughs at the idea of a child at a hundred and asks for Ishmael instead; God insists on Isaac, born within the year, while blessing Ishmael with twelve princes. Abraham circumcises his whole household that day.
GEN 18:1-33	Three men appear at Mamre in the heat of the day, and Abraham rushes to serve them cakes, curds, milk and a dressed calf. They say Sarah will have a son within the year; she laughs to herself in the tent, is called out on it and denies it. As they set off toward Sodom, God decides not to hide his plans from Abraham. Abraham then bargains him down: he will spare the city for fifty righteous, then forty-five, forty, thirty, twenty, and finally ten.
GEN 19:1-17	Two angels reach Sodom and Lot presses them to stay with him. The men of the city surround it and demand he bring them out to be raped. Lot offers his two virgin daughters instead; the crowd turns on him and moves to break down the door. The angels pull him inside and strike the men blind, then tell Lot to gather his family and leave. His sons-in-law think he is joking, and at dawn the angels take the four by the hand and lead them out.
GEN 19:18-38	Lot begs to shelter in the nearby small town rather than the mountains, and is allowed it; the place is called Zoar. As the sun rises, sulphur and fire fall on Sodom and Gomorrah and destroy the cities and the whole plain. Lot's wife looks back and becomes a pillar of salt. Abraham sees the smoke rising. Afraid to stay in Zoar, Lot moves into a cave with his daughters, who get him drunk on successive nights and conceive by him. Their sons are Moab and Ben Ammi.
GEN 20:1-18	Living as a foreigner in Gerar, Abraham again calls Sarah his sister, and King Abimelech takes her. God warns Abimelech in a dream that he is a dead man, though he has not touched her, and tells him to return her and ask her husband to pray. Abimelech confronts Abraham, who says he feared for his life and that Sarah is his half-sister. Abimelech returns her with livestock, servants and a thousand pieces of silver. Abraham prays, and the closed wombs of Abimelech's household open.
GEN 21:1-34	Sarah bears Isaac in Abraham's hundredth year and says God has made her laugh. At the weaning feast she sees Ishmael mocking and demands that Hagar and her son be sent away. Abraham is distressed, but God tells him to do it. Hagar's water runs out in the wilderness and she leaves the boy under a shrub; God hears him and shows her a well. Ishmael grows up an archer in Paran. Abraham and Abimelech swear a treaty over a disputed well at Beersheba.
GEN 22:1-24	God tests Abraham, telling him to offer Isaac as a burnt offering. Abraham sets out with wood and a knife. Isaac carries the wood and asks where the lamb is; his father says God will provide it. Abraham builds the altar, binds Isaac and raises the knife, and an angel stops him. He finds a ram caught in a thicket and offers it instead, naming the place Yahweh Will Provide. The promise of offspring is sworn again. A note lists Nahor's children, including Rebekah's father Bethuel.
GEN 23:1-20	Sarah dies at a hundred and twenty-seven in Hebron, and Abraham mourns her. Owning no land, he asks the Hittites for a burial place. They offer him the best of their tombs free, and Ephron offers the field and cave of Machpelah as a gift. Abraham insists on paying, and Ephron names four hundred shekels of silver, which Abraham weighs out in front of the whole assembly at the gate. The field and its cave are deeded to him, and he buries Sarah there.
GEN 24:1-32	Old and blessed, Abraham makes his senior servant swear not to take a Canaanite wife for Isaac but to fetch one from his own relatives, and not to take Isaac back there. The servant travels to the city of Nahor with ten camels and waits at the well, asking God for a sign: the right woman will offer to water his camels. Rebekah, granddaughter of Abraham's brother, arrives and does exactly that. He gives her a gold ring and bracelets and worships. Her brother Laban brings him home.
GEN 24:33-67	The servant refuses to eat until he has told his errand. He recounts Abraham's wealth, the oath, his prayer at the well and how Rebekah answered it, then asks for a decision. Laban and Bethuel agree it is from God. He hands out silver, gold and clothing. Asked to stay ten days, he insists on leaving at once, and Rebekah says she will go. She sees Isaac walking in a field at evening and veils herself. Isaac marries her and is comforted after his mother's death.
GEN 25:1-34	Abraham takes another wife, Keturah, and has six more sons, but leaves everything to Isaac and sends the others east with gifts. He dies at a hundred and seventy-five, and Isaac and Ishmael bury him beside Sarah. Ishmael's twelve sons are listed. Rebekah conceives after Isaac prays, and is told the elder of her twins will serve the younger. Esau is born red and hairy, Jacob gripping his heel. Esau later sells his birthright for lentil stew.
GEN 26:1-35	Famine sends Isaac to Gerar, where God tells him to stay put and repeats Abraham's promises. Afraid, he calls Rebekah his sister until Abimelech sees them together and orders that nobody touch either of them. Isaac's harvest is a hundredfold and the Philistines envy him, stopping up his father's wells. He redigs them, and moves on twice after quarrels over water before finding a well nobody contests. At Beersheba he builds an altar and swears a treaty with Abimelech. Esau marries two Hittite women, to his parents' grief.
GEN 27:1-26	Old and blind, Isaac asks Esau to hunt venison so he can bless him before he dies. Rebekah overhears and sends Jacob in with goat meat instead, his hands and neck covered in goatskins and Esau's clothes on his back. He claims to be Esau. Isaac questions the speed of the hunt, then feels his hands and says the voice is Jacob's but the hands are Esau's. He eats and asks for a kiss.
GEN 27:27-46	Smelling Esau's clothes, Isaac blesses Jacob with grain, wine and mastery over his brothers. Esau arrives moments later with his own meal, and Isaac trembles as he realises what has happened, but says the blessing stands. Esau cries out bitterly and asks for one of his own; Isaac can only promise him a life by the sword, serving his brother until he breaks the yoke. Esau decides to kill Jacob once their father is mourned. Rebekah hears, and sends Jacob to her brother Laban in Haran until the anger passes.
GEN 28:1-22	Isaac sends Jacob to Paddan Aram to find a wife among Laban's daughters rather than in Canaan. Esau, seeing that Canaanite wives displease his father, marries Ishmael's daughter Mahalath. On the road Jacob sleeps with a stone for a pillow and dreams of a stairway between earth and heaven with angels on it, and God above promising him the land and offspring. He wakes afraid, calls the place Bethel, sets the stone up as a pillar, and vows a tenth of everything.
GEN 29:1-35	Jacob reaches a well near Haran, meets Rachel, rolls the stone away for her flock and weeps as he tells her who he is. Laban takes him in, and Jacob agrees to seven years' work for Rachel. On the wedding night Laban substitutes Leah, defending it as local custom, and Jacob must serve another seven years for Rachel, whom he loves more. Leah, unloved, bears Reuben, Simeon, Levi and Judah, while Rachel remains childless.
GEN 30:1-25	Rachel, still childless, demands children of Jacob, who answers that he is not God. She gives him her servant Bilhah, who bears Dan and Naphtali; Leah gives her servant Zilpah, who bears Gad and Asher. Reuben finds mandrakes, and Rachel trades a night with Jacob for them, after which Leah bears Issachar, then Zebulun, then a daughter, Dinah. At last God opens Rachel's womb and she bears Joseph. Jacob then asks Laban to let him go home.
GEN 30:26-43	Jacob asks for his wives and children and leave to go, but Laban, who knows he has prospered because of him, asks him to name his wages. Jacob takes as his pay only the speckled, spotted and black animals, and Laban separates those out and puts three days' distance between the flocks. Jacob then peels streaks into fresh rods and sets them in the watering troughs where the flocks breed, working the stronger animals into his own share and leaving Laban the feeble. He grows very rich.
GEN 31:1-29	Hearing Laban's sons complain and seeing Laban's face turn against him, Jacob is told by God to go home. He tells Rachel and Leah how their father changed his wages ten times and how God kept giving him the flocks. They agree that nothing is left for them there. While Laban is shearing, Rachel steals his household gods and the family slips away toward Gilead. Laban pursues for seven days, warned in a dream to say nothing to Jacob.
GEN 31:30-55	Laban demands to know why his gods were stolen. Jacob, who does not know Rachel took them, invites a search on pain of death; she has hidden them in her camel saddle and stays seated, pleading her period. Nothing is found. Jacob rounds on Laban with twenty years of grievance: losses he absorbed, nights without sleep, wages changed ten times. Laban proposes a covenant instead. They raise a heap of stones as a witness that neither will cross it to harm the other, eat together, and part in the morning.
GEN 32:1-32	Angels meet Jacob on the road, and he sends word ahead to Esau. The messengers come back saying Esau is coming with four hundred men. Frightened, Jacob splits his people into two companies so one can escape, prays for deliverance, and sends ahead a graded procession of livestock as a gift. That night he sends his family over the Jabbok and is left alone, wrestling a man until daybreak. His hip is strained, but he refuses to let go without a blessing and is renamed Israel.
GEN 33:1-20	Esau approaches with his four hundred men. Jacob arranges the women and children by rank, goes ahead and bows to the ground seven times, and Esau runs to embrace him; they weep. Esau refuses the gift until Jacob presses it on him, saying that seeing his face was like seeing the face of God. Offered an escort, Jacob pleads the pace of the children and the nursing flocks. Esau returns to Seir; Jacob settles at Succoth, then buys land at Shechem.
GEN 34:1-31	Shechem son of Hamor rapes Dinah, then wants to marry her. Jacob's sons answer with deceit: they will agree to intermarriage only if every male in the city is circumcised, and the men submit. On the third day, while the city is still sore, Simeon and Levi kill every male in it and take Dinah back, while the rest plunder the town. Jacob says they have made him hated; they answer that their sister was treated as a prostitute.
GEN 35:1-29	Told to go up to Bethel, Jacob has his household surrender their foreign gods and earrings, which he buries under an oak, and they travel unpursued. He builds an altar there. Rebekah's nurse Deborah dies and is buried nearby. God appears again, confirms the name Israel, and repeats the promise of nations, kings and land. On the road to Ephrath, Rachel dies bearing Benjamin and is buried there. Reuben sleeps with Bilhah. The twelve sons are listed, and Isaac dies at a hundred and eighty.
GEN 36:1-43	A register of Esau's descendants, who are the Edomites. His wives and sons are named, and he moves to the hill country of Seir because the land cannot support both brothers' herds. The chiefs descended from each of his sons are listed, then the sons and chiefs of Seir the Horite, including the Anah who found the hot springs while pasturing donkeys. Eight kings who reigned in Edom before Israel had any king are named in succession, and a final list of Edomite chiefs closes the chapter.
GEN 37:1-36	Seventeen-year-old Joseph, his father's favourite and owner of a coloured coat, tells his brothers two dreams in which their sheaves, then the sun, moon and eleven stars, bow to him. Even his father rebukes him. Sent to check on them at Dothan, he is seized; Reuben talks them out of killing him and they throw him into a dry pit. On Judah's suggestion they sell him to traders for twenty pieces of silver. They dip his coat in goat's blood, and Jacob refuses all comfort.
GEN 38:1-30	Judah marries a Canaanite woman and has three sons. Er marries Tamar and dies; Onan refuses to father a child for his brother and dies too; Judah sends Tamar home a widow rather than give her Shelah. Widowed himself, he goes to the sheep shearing, and Tamar veils herself at the roadside. Taking her for a prostitute, he pays with his signet, cord and staff. Told she is pregnant, he orders her burned, until she produces the pledge and he admits she is more in the right.
GEN 39:1-23	Potiphar, captain of Pharaoh's guard, buys Joseph, and everything he touches prospers, so his master puts the whole household in his hands and stops watching it. Potiphar's wife propositions him repeatedly and he refuses, calling it a sin against God. Catching him alone, she grabs his garment; he runs, leaving it behind, and she uses it to accuse him of assault. Potiphar has him jailed with the king's prisoners. There too he prospers, and the warden puts every prisoner under his charge and stops checking on him.
GEN 40:1-23	Pharaoh's chief cup bearer and chief baker are jailed and put in Joseph's charge. Both dream on the same night and find nobody to interpret. Joseph says interpretations belong to God and listens: three vine branches mean three days until the cup bearer is restored, and three baskets of bread the birds eat mean three days until the baker is hanged. He asks the cup bearer to mention him to Pharaoh. On his birthday Pharaoh restores one man and hangs the other. The cup bearer forgets him.
GEN 41:1-30	Two years later Pharaoh dreams of seven fat cattle eaten by seven thin ones, then seven good heads of grain swallowed by seven withered ones, and no magician can explain it. The cup bearer finally remembers Joseph, who is brought from the dungeon, shaved and dressed. Joseph says the answer belongs to God, and that both dreams mean one thing: seven years of great plenty across Egypt, followed by seven years of famine so severe that the plenty will be forgotten.
GEN 41:31-57	Joseph explains that the dream came twice because God has fixed it, and advises Pharaoh to appoint a wise administrator and store a fifth of the harvest through the good years. Pharaoh gives him his signet ring, fine linen, a gold chain and authority over all Egypt, renames him Zaphenath-Paneah and marries him to Asenath. At thirty he tours the country and stockpiles grain beyond counting. Two sons, Manasseh and Ephraim, are born. When the famine comes, Egypt alone has bread, and every country comes to Joseph to buy.
GEN 42:1-21	Hearing there is grain in Egypt, Jacob sends ten of his sons to buy, keeping Benjamin at home. They bow before the governor without recognising him. Joseph knows them, speaks harshly and accuses them of spying. They protest that they are twelve brothers, the youngest at home and one gone. He holds them three days, then offers a test: one brother stays behind, the rest carry grain home and return with Benjamin. Among themselves they call it punishment.
GEN 42:22-38	Reuben reminds them that he warned against harming the boy. Joseph, who has been using an interpreter, understands every word, turns away and weeps, then binds Simeon in front of them. He orders their sacks filled with grain, their money returned inside, and provisions for the road. One brother finds his silver at the lodging place and they are terrified. At home they report everything to Jacob, and all the money turns up in the sacks. Jacob refuses to let Benjamin go, despite Reuben pledging his own sons.
GEN 43:1-34	With the grain gone, Jacob tells his sons to buy more, and Judah insists they cannot go without Benjamin, offering himself as surety. Jacob relents, sending gifts of balm, honey, spices, nuts and double money. Fearing arrest over the returned silver, the brothers explain themselves to the steward, who reassures them and brings out Simeon. Joseph asks after their father, sees Benjamin, and leaves the room to weep. He seats them by age, and gives Benjamin five times as much as anyone else.
GEN 44:1-34	Joseph has their sacks filled and his own silver cup hidden in Benjamin's. Once they have left the city his steward overtakes them and accuses them of theft. Confident, they agree that whoever has it should die; the search runs from oldest to youngest and the cup is in Benjamin's sack. They return and fall on the ground, and Joseph says only the guilty man will be his slave. Judah pleads at length and offers himself in Benjamin's place.
GEN 45:1-28	Joseph clears the room and tells his brothers who he is, weeping aloud. They are too terrified to answer. He tells them not to blame themselves, since God sent him ahead to preserve life, with five years of famine still to come. They are to fetch their father at once and settle in Goshen. He embraces Benjamin and then all of them. Pharaoh, pleased, sends wagons. Loaded with gifts, they carry the news home, and Jacob revives when he sees the wagons.
GEN 46:1-34	At Beersheba, Jacob sacrifices and God tells him in a night vision not to fear going down to Egypt, where he will become a great nation and Joseph will close his eyes. The household is listed son by son: thirty-three from Leah, sixteen from Zilpah, fourteen from Rachel, seven from Bilhah, seventy in all. Judah goes ahead to Goshen, where Joseph meets his father and weeps on his neck. Joseph coaches them to say they are herdsmen, since Egyptians despise shepherds.
GEN 47:1-17	Joseph tells Pharaoh his family has arrived and presents five of his brothers, who say they are shepherds and ask to live in Goshen. Pharaoh grants them the best of the land and offers work managing his own livestock. Jacob is brought in, blesses Pharaoh, and says his hundred and thirty years have been few and hard.
