As the famine bites, Joseph collects all the money in Egypt and Canaan for grain, and when that runs out he takes livestock in payment.
GEN 47:18-31	The next year the Egyptians have nothing left but their bodies and their fields, and ask to sell both for bread. Joseph buys all the land for Pharaoh, moves the population into the cities, and leaves only the priests' land untouched. He hands out seed and sets a permanent rule that a fifth of the harvest goes to Pharaoh. Israel prospers in Goshen. Jacob lives seventeen years in Egypt, reaching a hundred and forty-seven, and makes Joseph swear not to bury him there but in his fathers' burial place.
GEN 48:1-22	Told his father is ill, Joseph brings Manasseh and Ephraim. Jacob recalls God's promise at Luz and claims the two boys as his own sons, equal to Reuben and Simeon, then mentions Rachel's death and burial near Ephrath. Nearly blind, he embraces them, and crosses his hands so that his right rests on the younger, Ephraim. Joseph tries to correct him and is refused: Manasseh will be great, but Ephraim greater. Jacob blesses them both, says God will bring the family back to Canaan, and gives Joseph an extra portion.
GEN 49:1-33	Jacob gathers his sons and speaks over each in turn. Reuben loses his standing for defiling his father's bed; Simeon and Levi are cursed for their violence and will be scattered. Judah gets the lion, the scepter and the wine. Zebulun gets the coast, Issachar the burdened donkey, Dan the snake in the path. Joseph is a fruitful vine, shot at but unbroken, and Benjamin a ravenous wolf. Jacob then charges them to bury him at Machpelah, and dies.
GEN 50:1-26	Joseph weeps over his father and has him embalmed, which takes forty days, and Egypt mourns seventy. With Pharaoh's leave he takes a great procession to Canaan, mourning seven days at the threshing floor of Atad, and buries Jacob at Machpelah. Back in Egypt the brothers fear reprisal and offer themselves as slaves. Joseph weeps and tells them that what they meant for evil God meant for good. He lives to a hundred and ten and makes them swear to carry his bones out of Egypt.
LEV 1:1-17	God speaks to Moses from the Tent of Meeting and sets out the burnt offering. Cattle must be an unblemished male, presented at the tent door, with the offerer laying a hand on its head and killing it while the priests handle the blood. The carcass is flayed, cut up, the innards and legs washed, and the whole thing burned. Sheep and goats follow the same pattern, killed on the north side. A bird offering is a dove or pigeon, its head wrung off.
LEV 2:1-16	Rules for the grain offering. Fine flour with oil and frankincense, of which the priest burns a handful as a memorial portion and Aaron's sons keep the rest as something most holy. Baked versions are permitted: unleavened cakes from the oven, wafers, griddle bread or pan bread, all of fine flour with oil. Yeast and honey are forbidden on the altar, salt is required on every offering, and a first fruits offering is fresh grain parched and bruised, with oil and frankincense.
LEV 3:1-17	The peace offering. It may be from the herd or the flock, male or female, but unblemished, killed at the tent door with a hand laid on its head while the priests sprinkle the blood around the altar. What burns is the fat: the fat over the innards, the two kidneys with their fat, and the covering of the liver, with a lamb's whole tail fat added. The same procedure covers cattle, sheep and goats. A standing rule closes the chapter: never eat fat or blood.
LEV 4:1-19	Rules for unintentional sin. If the anointed priest sins, he offers a young bull, sprinkling its blood seven times before the veil, daubing the incense altar's horns and pouring the rest at the base of the burnt offering altar; the fat is burned and the carcass taken outside the camp and burned where the ashes go. If the whole congregation sins in ignorance, the elders lay hands on a bull and the priest handles the blood the same way.
LEV 4:20-35	The congregation's bull is treated like the priest's and burned outside the camp, and the people are forgiven. A ruler who sins unwittingly brings a male goat, its blood put on the horns of the burnt offering altar and the rest poured at the base, its fat burned. A commoner brings a female goat, or else a female lamb, handled by the same procedure. In each case the priest makes atonement and the person is forgiven.
LEV 5:1-19	Cases requiring a guilt offering: staying silent as a witness under oath, touching something unclean without realising, or swearing a rash oath. On discovering it, the person confesses and brings a female sheep or goat. Those who cannot afford one bring two doves or pigeons, one for sin and one for burnt offering; those who cannot afford birds bring a tenth of an ephah of flour without oil or frankincense. Wrongs against holy things require an unblemished ram, plus restitution with a fifth added.
LEV 6:1-30	Fraud against a neighbour, robbery, a false oath or a kept deposit require full restitution plus a fifth, then an unblemished ram as a guilt offering. The burnt offering stays on the altar overnight and the fire must never go out; the priest clears the ashes in linen and carries them outside the camp. Grain offerings are eaten unleavened in the courtyard by Aaron's male descendants, but a priest's own grain offering is burned entirely. Sin offering meat is eaten in a holy place.
LEV 7:1-19	The guilt offering follows the sin offering's law: killed where the burnt offering is, its fat burned, the meat eaten by the priests in a holy place. The hide of a burnt offering and every baked grain offering go to the officiating priest. A peace offering given in thanksgiving comes with leavened and unleavened cakes and must be eaten the same day; one given for a vow may be eaten the next day, but anything left on the third is burned. Meat that touches something unclean is burned.
LEV 7:20-38	Anyone unclean who eats peace offering meat is cut off from the people. Fat and blood are forbidden entirely, whether from cattle, sheep, goats or birds, though fat from an animal found dead may be used for other purposes. The offerer brings the fat and breast himself; the breast is waved and goes to Aaron's family, and the right thigh goes to the priest who handled the blood. These portions are theirs permanently. A summary closes the section, naming the laws given to Moses at Sinai.
LEV 8:1-21	With the congregation assembled at the tent door, Moses ordains Aaron and his sons. He washes them, dresses Aaron in the coat, sash, robe, ephod and breastplate with the Urim and Thummim, and sets the turban with its gold plate on his head. He anoints the tabernacle and altar, sprinkling it seven times, then pours oil on Aaron's head and dresses his sons. A bull is offered for sin, its blood daubed on the altar horns, then a ram is offered whole as a burnt offering.
LEV 8:22-36	The ram of consecration is killed and its blood put on the right ear, thumb and big toe of Aaron and each son. The fat, the right thigh and three kinds of bread are placed in their hands and waved before God, then burned; Moses takes the breast as his own portion. He sprinkles oil and altar blood over the men and their garments. They boil and eat the meat at the tent door, burn the leftovers, and stay there seven days on pain of death.
LEV 9:1-24	On the eighth day Moses has Aaron begin his ministry. Aaron offers a calf for his own sin and a ram as a burnt offering, then the people's goat, calf and lamb, a grain offering, and a bull and ram as peace offerings, waving the breast and thigh. He blesses the people, and after he and Moses come out of the Tent of Meeting the glory of God appears. Fire comes out and consumes the offering, and the people shout and fall on their faces.
LEV 10:1-20	Nadab and Abihu offer unauthorised fire before God and are killed by fire. Moses tells Aaron this is what God's holiness means, and Aaron says nothing. Cousins carry the bodies out of the camp, and the surviving priests are forbidden to mourn. God bans wine for priests on duty and charges them to distinguish holy from common. Moses is angry that the sin offering goat was burned rather than eaten, and accepts Aaron's answer that eating it that day would not have been right.
LEV 11:1-28	Clean and unclean creatures. Land animals must both have split hooves and chew the cud, which rules out the camel, the cony, the hare and the pig. Water creatures must have fins and scales. A list of birds is banned, from eagles and vultures to the stork, hoopoe and bat. Flying insects are out except hopping ones: locusts, katydids, crickets and grasshoppers. Touching a forbidden carcass makes a person unclean until evening, and carrying one requires washing clothes as well.
LEV 11:29-47	Eight small creatures are listed unclean, among them the weasel, the rat, the gecko and the chameleon. If one dies on a utensil the object must be washed, an earthen vessel broken, and an oven smashed; a spring stays clean, and dry seed stays clean but wet seed does not. Touching the carcass of an otherwise edible animal also brings uncleanness until evening. Anything that crawls is forbidden as food. The reason given is that God is holy, so Israel is to be holy too.
LEV 12:1-8	Purification after childbirth. A woman who bears a son is unclean seven days, the boy is circumcised on the eighth, and she waits another thirty-three days before touching anything holy or entering the sanctuary. For a daughter both periods double, to two weeks and sixty-six days. At the end she brings a year-old lamb as a burnt offering and a pigeon or dove as a sin offering, and the priest makes atonement. If she cannot afford a lamb, two birds will do.
LEV 13:1-30	Diagnostic rules for skin disease. A swelling, scab or bright spot is brought to a priest, who looks for whitened hair and a sore deeper than the skin; uncertain cases are isolated seven days and re-examined. Chronic disease with raw flesh is unclean at once, while a condition that has turned entirely white is declared clean, and raw flesh reappearing makes it unclean again. Similar tests apply to spots in a healed boil or burn, and to sores on the head or beard.
LEV 13:31-59	An itch on the head or beard is isolated and the surrounding hair shaved, and black hair growing back means healing. Dull white patches are a harmless rash, and baldness itself is clean, though a reddish-white sore on a bald scalp is not. A person declared leprous wears torn clothes, leaves his hair loose, covers his lip, calls out "Unclean" and lives outside the camp. The same procedure covers mould in wool, linen or leather: isolate it, wash it, watch whether it spreads, cut out or burn the affected part.
LEV 14:1-25	The ritual for a healed leper. The priest inspects him outside the camp, then takes two birds, cedar wood, scarlet yarn and hyssop; one bird is killed over running water, the other dipped in its blood and released, and the man is sprinkled seven times. He washes, shaves and bathes, waits seven days, shaves again, and on the eighth day brings two male lambs, a ewe lamb, flour and oil. Blood and then oil go on his right ear, thumb and big toe.
LEV 14:26-57	The reduced ritual runs the same way: oil sprinkled seven times, dabbed on ear, thumb and toe over the blood, and two birds offered. Then comes mould in a house. The owner reports it, the priest empties and inspects the house and shuts it seven days; spreading mould means removing the stones, scraping and replastering, and if it returns the house is demolished and carted outside the city. A clean house is purified with the same two birds, cedar, scarlet and hyssop.
LEV 15:1-33	Rules about bodily discharges. A man with a discharge makes unclean any bed, seat or saddle he uses, and anyone touching him, his spit or his things must wash and wait until evening; earthenware he touches is broken. After it stops he counts seven days, bathes, and on the eighth brings two birds. Semen emission and intercourse both require bathing. A woman is unclean seven days during her period, with the same rules for what she lies on, and a prolonged discharge is treated the same way.
LEV 16:1-17	After the death of Aaron's two sons, God restricts entry to the Most Holy Place. Aaron bathes, dresses in plain linen, and brings a bull for his own sin, a ram, and two goats. Lots decide which goat is for God and which is sent away. He offers the bull, carries a censer of coals and incense inside the veil so the cloud covers the mercy seat, and sprinkles the bull's blood seven times. The people's goat is killed and its blood used the same way.
LEV 16:18-34	Aaron then puts blood from both animals on the horns of the altar and sprinkles it seven times to cleanse it. He lays both hands on the live goat, confesses over it all Israel's sins, and has a man lead it into the wilderness. He leaves the linen garments in the tent, bathes, changes, and offers the burnt offerings. The men who released the goat and burned the carcasses must wash. The tenth day of the seventh month is fixed as a permanent day of self-denial and rest.
LEV 17:1-16	All slaughter of cattle, sheep or goats must be brought to the Tent of Meeting, and anyone who kills an animal elsewhere is treated as having shed blood and is cut off. The point is to end sacrifices in the open field and offerings to goat idols. Eating blood of any kind is forbidden, because the life is in the blood and the blood is what makes atonement. A hunter must pour out an animal's blood and cover it with earth.
LEV 18:1-30	Israel is told not to copy the practices of Egypt or Canaan. A long list of forbidden sexual relationships follows, covering mother, stepmother, sister and half-sister, granddaughter, aunt, daughter-in-law, sister-in-law, a woman and her daughter, and a wife's sister while the wife lives. Also forbidden are sex during menstruation, adultery, sacrificing children to Molech, sex between men, and sex with animals. The reason given is that these things defiled the previous inhabitants until the land vomited them out.
LEV 19:1-37	A mixed collection of laws under the heading that Israel must be holy because God is holy. It covers respect for parents, Sabbath, avoiding idols, eating peace offerings within two days, leaving field corners for the poor, honest dealing, prompt wages, fair judgment, not cursing the deaf or tripping the blind, not slandering or bearing a grudge, and loving your neighbour as yourself. Then mixed breeding and mixed cloth, unharvested fruit trees, bans on divination, tattoos and mediums, respect for the old, and honest weights.
LEV 20:1-27	Penalties for the offences already named. Giving a child to Molech means death by stoning, and a community that looks away is implicated. Consulting mediums, cursing a parent, adultery, sex with a father's wife or a daughter-in-law, sex between men, and taking both a woman and her mother all carry death. Sex with an animal means death for both. Other cases bring being cut off, or childlessness. Israel is told to keep these laws so the land will not vomit them out, and to be holy.
NUM 31:1-23	Yahweh tells Moses to avenge Israel on Midian, after which he will die. Twelve thousand men, a thousand per tribe, go out with Phinehas and the sanctuary trumpets. They kill every Midianite male, the five kings, and Balaam son of Beor, burn the towns, and bring back the women, children and livestock. Moses is angry the women were spared and orders the boys and every woman who has known a man killed. The soldiers stay outside the camp seven days, and metal plunder is purified by fire.
NUM 31:24-54	The soldiers wash their clothes on the seventh day and return to camp. Moses, Eleazar and the family heads count the plunder and halve it between the fighters and the congregation. One in five hundred of the soldiers' half goes to Eleazar, one in fifty of the people's half to the Levites. The totals are 675,000 sheep, 72,000 cattle, 61,000 donkeys and 32,000 captive women. The officers report not one Israelite missing and bring 16,750 shekels of gold.
NUM 32:1-20	Reuben and Gad, who own huge herds, see that Jazer and Gilead are good grazing land and ask Moses to give them that territory instead of crossing the Jordan. Moses accuses them of discouraging Israel exactly as the spies did at Kadesh Barnea, which cost that generation the land and forty years of wandering. They answer that they will build sheepfolds and cities for their families, then go armed ahead of Israel and not return home until every tribe has its inheritance. Moses begins to set terms.
NUM 32:21-42	Moses sets the condition: every armed man must cross the Jordan and fight until the land is subdued, after which they may return and hold Gilead. If they refuse, they have sinned, and their sin will find them out. Gad and Reuben agree, and Moses puts Eleazar, Joshua and the tribal heads in charge of enforcing it. He assigns Gad, Reuben and half of Manasseh the kingdoms of Sihon and Og. The two tribes rebuild and rename the cities, and Machir, Jair and Nobah take towns in Gilead.
NUM 33:1-56	Moses records the whole itinerary out of Egypt: some forty camps from Rameses, through the sea, Marah, Elim with its twelve springs and seventy palms, Sinai, Kadesh and Mount Hor, where Aaron died at a hundred and twenty-three in the fortieth year, ending in the plains of Moab. Yahweh then orders Israel, on crossing the Jordan, to drive out the inhabitants, destroy their idols and high places, and divide the land by lot. Any inhabitants left will be thorns in their sides.
NUM 34:1-29	Yahweh dictates the borders of Canaan: south from the wilderness of Zin along Edom, west the great sea, north from the sea to Mount Hor and Hazar Enan, east down past Chinnereth to the Jordan and the Salt Sea. Moses notes this is for the nine and a half tribes, since Reuben, Gad and half of Manasseh already hold land east of the Jordan. Eleazar and Joshua will divide it, with one named prince from each remaining tribe, Caleb first.
NUM 35:1-17	The Levites, who get no tribal territory, are to receive forty-eight cities with pasture measured two thousand cubits out on each side. Six of those are cities of refuge, three east of the Jordan and three in Canaan, open to Israelites and foreigners alike, where anyone who kills a person accidentally can flee the avenger of blood until the assembly judges the case. A killer who used an iron tool or a stone is a murderer and is put to death.
NUM 35:18-34	The law continues: killing with a wooden weapon, or out of hatred, ambush or hostility, makes a man a murderer, and the avenger of blood executes him. If the death was sudden and without enmity, the assembly judges and returns him to his city of refuge, where he must stay until the high priest dies. If he leaves early, the avenger may kill him without guilt. Conviction needs more than one witness, and no ransom may be taken for a murderer.
NUM 36:1-13	The heads of Gilead's family raise a problem with the inheritance of Zelophehad's daughters: if they marry outside the tribe, their land goes with them and Manasseh loses it at the jubilee. Moses rules that they are right. The daughters may marry whom they choose, provided it is within their father's tribe, and the same applies to any heiress, so no inheritance moves between tribes. Mahlah, Tirzah, Hoglah, Milcah and Noah marry their cousins, and Numbers ends in the plains of Moab.
DEU 13:1-18	Three cases of enticement to other gods. A prophet whose sign comes true but who urges worship of other gods is a test, and is to be put to death. A brother, child, spouse or closest friend who suggests it in private must not be pitied or hidden but stoned, the hearer's hand first. A whole city led astray is to be investigated carefully, then destroyed with its livestock, its plunder burned in the street, and left a ruin that is never rebuilt.
DEU 14:1-29	Israel is not to gash itself or shave its head for the dead. Food laws follow: cloven-hoofed cud-chewers may be eaten, but not camel, hare, rabbit or pig; fish need fins and scales; birds of prey, the bat, winged insects and anything found dead are forbidden. A kid is not to be boiled in its mother's milk. Tithes are eaten at the chosen place, or sold for money if the journey is long, and every third year stored locally for the Levite, foreigner, orphan and widow.
DEU 15:1-23	Every seventh year debts between Israelites are cancelled, though a foreigner's may still be collected. Lenders are warned not to refuse a loan because the year of release is near, since the poor will never cease out of the land. A Hebrew slave goes free in the seventh year and must be sent off supplied from flock, threshing floor and winepress. One who chooses to stay has his ear pierced to the door with an awl. Firstborn animals are dedicated, but a defective one is eaten at home.
DEU 16:1-22	The three annual festivals. Passover is kept in Abib at the chosen place only, at sunset, with unleavened bread for seven days and no meat left overnight. Weeks is counted seven weeks from the first sickle and kept with a freewill offering. Tents lasts seven days after the harvest. All three include servants, Levites, foreigners, orphans and widows, and every male appears three times a year, never empty-handed. Judges are appointed who take no bribes, since a bribe blinds the wise.
DEU 17:1-20	No defective animal may be sacrificed. Anyone who worships other gods or the sun, moon or stars is stoned after diligent inquiry, on the word of two or three witnesses and never one, with the witnesses casting first. Cases too hard for local judges go to the priests and the judge at the chosen place, whose verdict is binding on pain of death. If Israel asks for a king, he must be an Israelite, must not accumulate horses, wives or silver, and must write out and read this law daily.
DEU 18:1-22	The Levites hold no land and live on the offerings, receiving the shoulder, cheeks and inner parts of sacrifices, plus first fruits and the first fleece. A Levite who moves to the chosen place shares equally. Israel must not imitate the nations: child sacrifice, divination, sorcery, charms, mediums or consulting the dead. God will raise up a prophet like Moses, since the people asked at Horeb not to hear the voice again. A prophet who speaks presumptuously dies, and the test is whether his word comes true.
DEU 19:1-21	Three cities of refuge are to be set apart, with roads prepared, and three more if the territory grows. The example given is an axe head flying off its handle and killing a companion. A man who killed out of hatred and ambush is handed over by the elders to the avenger of blood. Boundary markers may not be moved. No one is convicted on one witness, and a witness proved false suffers what he intended, life for life, eye for eye, tooth for tooth.
DEU 20:1-20	Rules for war. A priest addresses the army, and officers send home anyone with a new house, a new vineyard or a fiancee, and anyone who is afraid, so his fear does not spread. Distant cities are offered peace first, and if they refuse, the men are killed and the rest taken as plunder. The cities of the seven Canaanite nations are to be destroyed entirely, so their practices are not learned. Fruit trees may not be cut down during a siege.
DEU 21:1-23	An unsolved killing is expiated by the nearest city's elders breaking a heifer's neck in an unploughed valley and washing their hands over it, declaring their innocence. A captive woman a soldier wishes to marry shaves her head, trims her nails and mourns her parents a month first, and if he rejects her she goes free and may not be sold. A firstborn son's double portion holds even if his mother is the unloved wife. A rebellious son is stoned, and an executed body is buried the same day.
DEU 22:1-30	A lost ox, sheep, donkey or garment must be returned, and a fallen animal helped up. Clothing of the other sex is forbidden, as are mixed seed, ploughing with ox and donkey together, and wool woven with linen. Cloaks get fringes, roofs get railings, and a mother bird must be let go. Sexual offences follow: a false accusation against a bride costs a hundred shekels, adultery is capital for both, and a betrothed woman forced in open country is held guiltless while the man dies.
DEU 23:1-25	Rules on who may enter the assembly: not the emasculated, not those of a forbidden union, and not Ammonites or Moabites to the tenth generation, because they refused bread and water and hired Balaam. Edomites and Egyptians may enter in the third generation. The camp must be kept clean, with a latrine outside and a trowel among the weapons. An escaped slave must not be handed back, no interest may be charged to an Israelite, and vows must be paid.
DEU 24:1-22	Divorce requires a written bill, and a woman who has remarried may not return to her first husband. A newly married man is exempt from war and business for a year. A millstone may not be taken as a pledge, kidnapping is capital, and a poor man's cloak taken in pledge must be returned by sunset. Wages are paid the same day. Children are not executed for their fathers' sins. Forgotten sheaves, olives and grapes are left for the foreigner, the orphan and the widow.
DEU 25:1-19	A flogging is capped at forty stripes so the man is not degraded, and an ox is not muzzled while treading grain. If a man dies childless, his brother marries the widow and her first son carries the dead man's name. A brother who refuses is summoned before the elders, has his sandal pulled off and is spat at. A woman who seizes a fighting man's genitals loses her hand. Weights and measures must be honest, and Amalek, who struck the stragglers, is to be blotted out.
DEU 26:1-19	On entering the land, the first fruits are brought in a basket to the priest with a recital: my father was a wandering Syrian, we went to Egypt, were oppressed, cried out, and were brought here with signs and wonders. The offering is set down, and the household, the Levite and the foreigner rejoice. In the third year the tithe goes to the Levite, foreigner, orphan and widow, with a declaration that it was handled properly. Israel and God each declare the other their own.
DEU 27:1-26	Moses orders that on crossing the Jordan, great plastered stones be set up on Mount Ebal with the law written plainly on them, and an altar of uncut stones built there for offerings and a meal. Six tribes stand on Gerizim for the blessing, six on Ebal for the curse. The Levites shout twelve curses, against secret idols, dishonouring parents, moving landmarks, misleading the blind, denying justice, incest, bestiality, secret murder and bribes to kill, and the people answer amen to each.
DEU 28:1-25	Obedience brings blessing, listed in pairs: in the city and in the field, in body, ground and livestock, in basket and kneading trough, coming in and going out. Enemies flee seven ways, rain comes in season, Israel lends and does not borrow, and is the head and not the tail. Disobedience reverses every item. Then the curses proper begin: pestilence, consumption, fever, blight and mildew, a sky of brass and earth of iron, rain of dust, and defeat before enemies.
DEU 28:26-50	The curses continue: unburied bodies eaten by birds, incurable boils and tumours, madness and blindness, groping at noon. Everything built or planted is taken by others, wife, house, vineyard, ox, donkey and sheep, and children are carried off while their parents watch. Locusts and worms eat the crops. The foreigner rises while Israel sinks to the tail. Israel and its king are carried to an unknown nation and serve their enemies in hunger under an iron yoke.
DEU 28:51-68	The final curses describe a siege: the walls Israel trusted come down, and famine drives parents to eat their own children in secret. Plagues of long duration follow, including all the diseases of Egypt, and a nation once numerous as the stars is left few. They are uprooted and scattered from one end of the earth to the other, with trembling hearts and no rest, longing in the morning for evening and in the evening for morning. The last verse returns them to Egypt in ships, unsold.
DEU 29:1-29	Moses makes a second covenant in Moab, in addition to the one at Horeb. He recalls Egypt, the forty years in which clothes and sandals did not wear out, and the defeat of Sihon and Og. Everyone stands to enter the oath: leaders, wives, children, and the foreigners who cut wood and draw water, along with those not present. Anyone who secretly turns to other gods will be blotted out, and the land left sulphur and salt like Sodom. The secret things belong to God.
DEU 30:1-20	Moses looks past the exile: when the scattered people remember and return with all their heart, God will gather them from the ends of the heavens, bring them back, circumcise their hearts, and put the curses on their enemies. He insists the command is not remote, not in heaven or across the sea, but in their mouth and heart. He sets before them life and death, blessing and curse, calls heaven and earth as witnesses, and tells them to choose life.
DEU 31:1-16	At a hundred and twenty, Moses tells Israel he cannot cross the Jordan, but God and Joshua will go ahead of them. He charges Joshua publicly to be strong and courageous. He writes the law, gives it to the Levitical priests and the elders, and commands that it be read aloud to the whole assembly, women, children and foreigners included, every seventh year at the feast of tents. God tells him he is about to die and summons Joshua to the Tent, appearing in a pillar of cloud.
DEU 31:17-30	God predicts that once fat and settled, Israel will turn to other gods, and he will hide his face until they ask whether these evils came because God is not among them. He tells Moses to write a song and teach it as a witness. Moses writes it, commissions Joshua, finishes the book of the law, and has the Levites place it beside the ark as a witness. He says plainly that they rebelled while he lived and will corrupt themselves after his death, then recites the song.
EST 1:1-22	King Ahasuerus, ruler of a hundred and twenty-seven provinces, holds a hundred and eighty days of display for his officials, then a seven-day feast in the palace garden. Queen Vashti feasts separately with the women. On the seventh day the king, merry with wine, sends seven eunuchs to fetch her so the guests can admire her beauty. She refuses. His adviser Memucan warns that other wives will copy her, and an irrevocable decree bars her from the king's presence and gives her position away. Letters go out across the empire.
EST 2:1-23	The king's servants gather beautiful young women so he can choose a new queen. Among them is Esther, an orphaned Jew raised by her cousin Mordecai, who tells her to hide her people. Hegai the keeper of the women favours her, and after twelve months of treatments she pleases the king most and is crowned in Vashti's place. Mordecai, at the palace gate, learns of a plot by two eunuchs, Bigthan and Teresh, reports it through Esther, and they are hanged.
EST 3:1-15	Ahasuerus promotes Haman the Agagite above all his officials and orders everyone to bow. Mordecai will not, and on learning he is a Jew, Haman resolves to destroy every Jew in the empire. Lots are cast and Adar, the twelfth month, comes up. He sells the king a story about a scattered people with their own laws, plus ten thousand talents of silver, and receives the signet ring. Couriers carry the decree out while Susa is left bewildered.
EST 4:1-17	Mordecai puts on sackcloth and ashes and wails in the city, and Jews fast across the provinces. Esther sends clothes, which he refuses, then sends a eunuch to ask why. Back comes the decree and a demand that she plead with the king. She answers that entering uncalled means death and he has not summoned her in thirty days. Mordecai says she will not escape either, and suggests this may be why she is queen. She orders a three-day fast: if she dies, she dies.
EST 5:1-14	On the third day Esther enters the inner court in her royal robes. The king holds out the golden scepter and offers her half the kingdom; she asks only that he and Haman come to a banquet, and there invites them back the next day. Haman leaves pleased until he passes Mordecai, who will not rise. At home he boasts of his wealth and his invitations, then complains that Mordecai spoils it, and his wife Zeresh tells him to build a gallows fifty cubits high.
EST 6:1-14	Sleepless, the king has the chronicles read aloud and hears that Mordecai exposed the assassination plot and was never rewarded. Haman, waiting outside to ask permission to hang Mordecai, is asked what should be done for a man the king wishes to honour. Assuming he is the man, he proposes royal robes, the king's horse and a herald, and is ordered to do all of it for Mordecai. He leads the horse through the square, then goes home to be told he will fall.
EST 7:1-10	At the second banquet the king again offers Esther whatever she asks. She asks for her own life and the lives of her people, sold for destruction. Asked who is responsible, she names Haman. The king storms out into the garden, and Haman throws himself on Esther's couch to beg. Returning, the king takes this as an assault on the queen, and Haman's face is covered. The eunuch Harbonah mentions the fifty-cubit gallows standing at Haman's house, and the king has Haman hanged on it.
EST 8:1-17	The king gives Haman's estate to Esther and his signet ring to Mordecai. Esther falls at the king's feet begging him to undo Haman's decree, and since a sealed order cannot be revoked he tells her and Mordecai to write what they wish in his name. Letters grant the Jews in all hundred and twenty-seven provinces the right to defend themselves on the thirteenth of Adar. Mordecai appears in blue and white, Susa cheers, and many become Jews out of fear.
EST 9:1-17	On the thirteenth of Adar, the day meant for their destruction, the Jews assemble and strike at those who meant to harm them, with officials assisting because Mordecai is now feared. Five hundred die in the citadel of Susa, Haman's ten named sons among them, and no plunder is taken. The king asks Esther what else she wants; she asks for another day in Susa and the sons' bodies hanged. Three hundred more die there, seventy-five thousand in the provinces.
EST 9:18-32	Susa's Jews fought two days and rested on the fifteenth, while those in the unwalled towns rested on the fourteenth, so both days are kept. Mordecai writes to every province making the fourteenth and fifteenth of Adar annual days of feasting, gifts of food and gifts to the poor. The name Purim comes from the lot Haman cast. The Jews bind themselves and their descendants to it, and Esther and Mordecai confirm it in a second letter.
EST 10:1-3	A short closing note. Ahasuerus imposes tribute across the land and the coastal islands. His achievements and the full account of Mordecai's greatness are said to be recorded in the chronicles of the kings of Media and Persia. Mordecai stands second to the king himself, holds high standing among the Jews, is respected by his own people, and works for their welfare and for peace for those who come after him.
JOS 1:1-18	After Moses dies, God tells Joshua to cross the Jordan and take the land, promising that nobody will withstand him. He is repeatedly told to be strong and courageous and to keep the whole law without deviating. Joshua orders provisions for a crossing in three days, and reminds Reuben, Gad and half of Manasseh that their fighting men must cross armed ahead of the others. They promise to obey him as they obeyed Moses.
JOS 2:1-24	Joshua sends two spies into Jericho, and they lodge with a prostitute named Rahab. When the king's men come looking, she hides them under flax on the roof and sends the search toward the Jordan fords. She says she knows the land is theirs, having heard of the Red Sea and of Sihon and Og, and asks that her family be spared. They agree, provided she keeps her household inside the house marked by a scarlet cord.
JOS 3:1-17	The camp moves to the Jordan. After three days the officers tell the people to follow the ark at two thousand cubits' distance, and Joshua tells them to consecrate themselves. God says he will begin to raise Joshua's standing, and instructs the priests carrying the ark to stop at the water's edge. When their feet touch the river, in full flood at harvest, the water piles up at Adam near Zarethan. The priests stand in the riverbed while the whole nation crosses on dry ground.
JOS 4:1-24	Twelve men, one from each tribe, take twelve stones from where the priests stood, and Joshua also sets up twelve stones in the riverbed itself. The stones are to prompt children in future to ask what they mean. Reuben, Gad and half of Manasseh cross armed at the head of about forty thousand fighting men. When the priests come up out of the Jordan the water returns and floods its banks. The people camp at Gilgal, where the stones are set up.
JOS 5:1-15	News of the dried-up Jordan leaves the Amorite and Canaanite kings without courage. God tells Joshua to circumcise the nation, since the men who left Egypt died in the wilderness and their sons were never circumcised. It is done with flint knives, and the camp waits while they heal; the place is named Gilgal. They keep the Passover on the plains of Jericho, eat produce of the land, and the manna stops. Near Jericho Joshua meets a man with a drawn sword who says he commands God's army.
JOS 6:1-27	Jericho is shut tight. God tells Joshua to march the army around it once a day for six days, with seven priests carrying ram's horn trumpets before the ark, then seven times on the seventh day. The people march in silence, and on the seventh day they shout at Joshua's word, the wall falls flat and they take the city. Everything in it is destroyed and burned, with the metal put into the treasury. The two spies fetch Rahab and her family out alive, as promised.
JOS 7:1-26	Achan of Judah takes plunder that was to be destroyed. Told Ai is weakly held, Joshua sends only three thousand men, who are routed with thirty-six dead. He tears his clothes and lies before the ark until God tells him to get up: Israel has broken the covenant and will not stand until the offence is removed. Lots narrow it to Achan, who admits burying a Babylonian robe, silver and gold in his tent. He and his household are stoned and burned in the valley of Achor.
JOS 8:1-18	God tells Joshua to attack Ai, this time keeping the livestock and goods, and to set an ambush behind the city. Joshua sends thirty thousand men out by night to hide west of Ai, with a further five thousand in ambush, and camps the main force north across a valley. The plan is to feign the earlier rout. The king of Ai takes the bait, and every man in Ai and Bethel leaves the city open. God tells Joshua to stretch out his javelin, and he does.
JOS 8:19-35	The ambush runs into Ai and sets it on fire. The men of Ai, seeing the smoke, are trapped between two forces and cut down, twelve thousand killed and none escaping. Joshua holds out the javelin until it is done. The livestock and goods are taken and the city left a permanent ruin. The king is hanged until sundown, then buried under stones. Joshua builds an altar on Mount Ebal, writes a copy of the law on stones, and reads it to the assembly.
JOS 9:1-27	The kings of the region unite against Israel, but Gibeon tries something else. Its people arrive at Gilgal in worn clothes and patched sandals with dry, mouldy bread, claiming to have travelled from a distant country because of God's reputation, and ask for a treaty. The Israelites inspect the provisions but do not consult God, and Joshua swears a covenant to let them live. Three days later the truth comes out, and the oath holds. He spares them and makes them woodcutters and water carriers.
JOS 10:1-23	Alarmed by Gibeon's treaty with Israel, Adoni-Zedek of Jerusalem brings the kings of Hebron, Jarmuth, Lachish and Eglon against it. Gibeon appeals to Joshua, who marches all night from Gilgal. God throws the Amorites into confusion, and more die from hailstones than by the sword. Joshua calls on the sun to stand still over Gibeon and the moon over Aijalon, and the day is held. The five kings hide in a cave at Makkedah, and Joshua seals it with stones until the pursuit is over.
JOS 10:24-43	Joshua has his commanders put their feet on the necks of the five kings, then kills them and hangs the bodies until sunset before sealing them in the cave. He takes Makkedah, Libnah, Lachish, Eglon, Hebron and Debir in turn, killing every inhabitant and each king, and destroys the king of Gezer's relief force. The account concludes that he struck the whole region from Kadesh Barnea to Gaza and left nobody alive, because God fought for Israel.
JOS 11:1-23	Jabin of Hazor gathers a northern coalition with horses and chariots as numerous as sand, camped at the waters of Merom. God tells Joshua not to fear and to hamstring the horses and burn the chariots. Joshua attacks suddenly, drives them as far as Sidon, and leaves no survivors. Hazor alone is burned; the other cities are left standing and their livestock and goods taken. No city made peace except Gibeon. He clears the Anakim from the hill country, and the divided land has rest from war.
JOS 12:1-24	A summary of conquests. East of the Jordan, Moses defeated Sihon king of Heshbon, whose territory ran from the Arnon to the Jabbok, and Og king of Bashan, the last of the Rephaim, and gave that land to Reuben, Gad and half of Manasseh. West of the Jordan, Joshua's conquests from Baal Gad to Mount Halak are set out as a roll call of defeated kings, one apiece, beginning with Jericho and Ai and running through Jerusalem, Hebron, Lachish, Megiddo, Hazor and Tirzah. The total is thirty-one kings.
JOS 13:1-33	Joshua is old, and much land is still unconquered: Philistine territory, the Geshurites, Sidonian country, Lebanon and the land of the Gebalites. God tells him to allocate it anyway and promises to drive out the inhabitants. The eastern allotments Moses made are restated. Reuben gets the plain around Heshbon, and Balaam is noted among the dead there. Gad gets Jazer, Gilead and the Jordan valley, and half of Manasseh gets Bashan. Levi receives no land, only the offerings.
JOS 14:1-15	Eleazar, Joshua and the tribal heads divide Canaan by lot among the nine and a half tribes, the rest having been settled east of the Jordan, and the Levites get only towns and pasture. Caleb then comes forward: he was forty when he scouted the land and reported honestly while the others frightened the people, and Moses swore the ground he walked would be his. Now eighty-five and still fit for war, he asks for the Anakim's hill country. Joshua gives him Hebron.
JOS 15:1-14	Judah's allotment is traced by its borders: south from the end of the Salt Sea past Zin and Kadesh Barnea to the brook of Egypt, east along the Salt Sea, north through the valley of Achor past Jerusalem and Hinnom out to Ekron, and west to the Mediterranean. Caleb receives Hebron inside Judah's territory and drives out Anak's three sons, Sheshai, Ahiman and Talmai.
JOS 15:15-63	Caleb offers his daughter Achsah to whoever takes Debir, and his relative Othniel does it; she then asks her father for springs to go with her dry southern land, and gets the upper and lower springs. The rest of the chapter lists Judah's towns by region: the far south, the lowland, the Philistine coast, the hill country, and six in the wilderness including En Gedi. Judah could not drive the Jebusites out of Jerusalem, and they still live there.
JOS 16:1-10	The allotment for Joseph's descendants starts at the Jordan by Jericho and runs up through the hill country to Bethel, then west past Beth Horon and Gezer to the sea. Ephraim's own border is described in detail, from Ataroth Addar and upper Beth Horon westward to the brook of Kanah and the sea, and eastward back down to the Jordan, along with towns set aside for Ephraim inside Manasseh's territory. Ephraim did not drive the Canaanites out of Gezer; they remain there as forced labour.
JOS 17:1-18	Manasseh's allotment. Machir's line already holds Gilead and Bashan, and the daughters of Zelophehad, who had no brothers, come to Eleazar and Joshua and are given land alongside their father's kinsmen, making ten portions west of the Jordan. Manasseh's people could not expel the Canaanites and later put them to forced labour. Joseph's descendants complain that one allotment is too small, and Joshua tells them to clear the forest and take the valleys despite the iron chariots.
JOS 18:1-28	The assembly gathers at Shiloh and sets up the Tent of Meeting, with seven tribes still unsettled. Joshua asks how long they will delay, and has three men from each tribe survey the remaining land and write it up in seven portions. He casts lots for them at Shiloh. Benjamin's lot falls between Judah and Joseph, its border traced from the Jordan past Jericho and Bethel and back down to the Salt Sea. Its towns include Jericho, Gibeon and Jerusalem.
JOS 19:1-51	The remaining lots. Simeon gets thirteen towns and four more inside Judah's territory, which was larger than Judah needed. Zebulun gets twelve towns, Issachar sixteen around Jezreel, Asher twenty-two reaching to Sidon and Tyre, and Naphtali nineteen fortified towns including Hazor. Dan gets Zorah, Aijalon, Ekron and the coast near Joppa, and later takes Leshem by force and renames it Dan. With the division finished, Israel gives Joshua the town he asked for, Timnath Serah, and he settles there.
JOS 20:1-9	God tells Joshua to designate the cities of refuge promised through Moses. Someone who kills accidentally may flee to one, state his case at the gate, and be taken in; the elders are not to hand him over to the avenger of blood. He stays until he has stood trial and until the serving high priest dies, after which he may go home. Six cities are set apart, three each side of the Jordan, and resident foreigners may use them too.
JOS 21:1-20	The Levite family heads ask Eleazar and Joshua for the towns and pasture promised through Moses. Lots assign thirteen towns to Aaron's descendants from Judah, Simeon and Benjamin, ten to the rest of the Kohathites, thirteen to the Gershonites, and twelve to the Merarites. The priestly towns are then named, beginning with Hebron, which is also a city of refuge, though its outlying fields stay with Caleb.
JOS 21:21-45	The rest of the Levite towns are listed by clan: Shechem, Gezer and Beth Horon in Ephraim for the Kohathites; Golan, Kishion and Kedesh in Galilee among the Gershonites' thirteen; Jokneam, Bezer, Ramoth in Gilead and Heshbon among the Merarites' twelve. Four of them, Shechem, Golan, Kedesh and Ramoth, are cities of refuge. Forty-eight towns in all. The chapter closes by stating that God gave Israel all the land he had sworn, and that no promise failed.
JOS 22:1-18	Joshua commends Reuben, Gad and half of Manasseh for staying with their brothers throughout the campaign, and sends them home across the Jordan with a charge to keep the law and a large share of plunder. On reaching the Jordan they build a large altar there. Hearing of it, the rest of Israel assembles at Shiloh ready for war and sends Phinehas with ten tribal chiefs to Gilead. The delegation accuses them of rebellion, invoking the sin at Peor and warning that God's anger will fall on the whole nation.
JOS 22:19-34	The eastern tribes swear the altar was never meant for sacrifice. They built it out of fear that later generations west of the Jordan would tell their children they had no share in God, so it stands as a witness, a copy of the real altar rather than a rival. Phinehas and the chiefs are satisfied, talk of war ends, and the eastern tribes name the altar A Witness Between Us that Yahweh is God.
JOS 23:1-16	Old and with the fighting over, Joshua summons Israel's leaders. He reminds them that God fought for them, that the remaining nations have already been allotted to them, and that one of them can chase a thousand. He tells them to keep the whole law of Moses without deviating and not to serve the surviving nations' gods. Intermarry, and those nations become a snare, a whip on their sides and thorns in their eyes. Every promised good has come; so will the promised ruin.
JOS 24:1-16	At Shechem Joshua recites Israel's history as God's own words: Terah served other gods, Abraham was brought from beyond the river, Egypt was plagued and the sea closed over the pursuing chariots, Balaam was made to bless rather than curse, and the land came complete with towns and vineyards they never planted. He tells them to serve God sincerely and discard the old gods, and says that he and his household will. The people refuse to forsake him.
JOS 24:17-33	The people recount the exodus and the driving out of the Amorites as their reason for choosing God. Joshua warns them they cannot serve him lightly, since he is holy and jealous and will not overlook their sins. They insist twice, so he makes them witnesses against themselves, tells them to get rid of their foreign gods, and cuts a covenant at Shechem, setting up a great stone as a witness. Joshua dies at a hundred and ten, and Joseph's bones are buried at Shechem.
LEV 21:1-24	Rules for priests. A priest may not make himself unclean for the dead except his closest relatives, and may not shave his head or cut his flesh in mourning. He may not marry a prostitute or a divorced woman, and a priest's daughter who turns to prostitution is burned. The high priest is stricter: no mourning at all, no leaving the sanctuary, and marriage only to a virgin of his own people. A descendant of Aaron with a defect may eat the holy food but may not officiate.
LEV 22:1-33	Priests must keep clear of the holy offerings while unclean, whether from disease, discharge or contact with a corpse, and may eat again only after bathing and sunset. Outsiders, hired workers and a priest's daughter married outside the family may not eat the holy food, though bought slaves and a widowed daughter who has come home may. Eating it accidentally requires restitution plus a fifth. Sacrifices must be unblemished males, at least eight days old, and a thanksgiving offering must be eaten the same day.
LEV 23:1-21	The calendar of appointed festivals. Every seventh day is a Sabbath of complete rest. Passover falls on the evening of the fourteenth day of the first month, followed by seven days of unleavened bread with holy assemblies on the first and last. When the harvest begins, the first sheaf is waved before God, and no new grain is eaten until then. Fifty days later comes a new grain offering of two leavened loaves, with lambs, a bull, two rams, a goat and a holy assembly.
LEV 23:22-44	Field corners and gleanings are again left for the poor and the foreigner. The first day of the seventh month is a day of rest marked by trumpet blasts. The tenth is the Day of Atonement, a Sabbath of self-denial from evening to evening, with anyone working or refusing to deny himself cut off. The fifteenth begins the seven-day Feast of Tents, with assemblies on the first and eighth days. The people take palm and willow branches and fruit and live in temporary shelters for a week.
LEV 24:1-23	Israel supplies pure olive oil for the lamps that burn continually before the veil, and twelve cakes of flour are set in two rows with frankincense on the gold table each Sabbath, eaten by the priests in a holy place. Then a case: the son of an Israelite woman and an Egyptian father, quarrelling in the camp, curses the Name. He is held in custody until God's ruling comes: death by stoning. The law that follows sets out life for life, applied equally to foreigner and native.
LEV 25:1-29	The land keeps a Sabbath. After six years of sowing and pruning, the seventh year is left uncultivated, and whatever grows on its own feeds the household, the workers, the foreigner and the livestock. After seven such cycles, the fiftieth year is a jubilee, announced by trumpet on the Day of Atonement: liberty is proclaimed, and everyone returns to their property. Land is priced by the harvests left until the jubilee, cannot be sold permanently since it belongs to God, and a poor man's relative may redeem it.
LEV 25:30-55	A house in a walled city not redeemed within a year stays with the buyer permanently, while village houses count as farmland and return at the jubilee, and Levites may always redeem theirs. A poor Israelite is supported without interest or profit on food. One who sells himself is treated as a hired worker, not a slave, and released with his children at the jubilee. Slaves may be bought from surrounding nations and held permanently, but not fellow Israelites, redeemed at a price prorated to the jubilee.
LEV 26:1-25	No idols, no sacred pillars, keep the Sabbaths. Obedience brings rain in season, harvests running into each other, safety, sleep without fear, dangerous animals removed, and enemies routed by a handful of men, with God's presence walking among the people. Disobedience brings escalating punishment, each seven times worse than the last: wasting disease and fever, crops eaten by enemies, defeat by those who hate them, a sky like iron and soil like bronze, wild animals taking their children and livestock, then the sword, siege and plague.
LEV 26:26-46	The punishments continue: bread rationed by weight and never enough, hunger reaching the point of eating their own children, the high places destroyed, corpses thrown on the idols, cities and sanctuaries laid waste, and the people scattered while the land at last enjoys the Sabbaths it was denied. Survivors will be so afraid a blown leaf sends them running. But if they confess and humble themselves, God says he will remember the covenant with Jacob, Isaac and Abraham, and not utterly reject them even in exile.
LEV 27:1-17	A schedule of valuations for things vowed to God. A person is assessed by age and sex, from fifty shekels for a man in his prime down to three for an infant girl, the priest setting a lower figure for anyone who cannot pay. A sacrificial animal becomes holy and cannot be swapped; an unclean one is valued and bought back with a fifth added. A house is likewise valued, with a fifth added to redeem it, and a field by how much barley seed it takes.
LEV 27:18-34	A field dedicated after the jubilee is valued only for the years remaining. Redeeming it costs a fifth extra; leaving it unredeemed or selling it means it passes permanently to the priests at the jubilee. A bought field returns to its original owner then. All valuations use the sanctuary shekel of twenty gerahs. Firstborn animals cannot be dedicated, since they already belong to God, and anything irrevocably devoted may never be redeemed. A tenth of crops and livestock is holy, redeemable only with a fifth added.
PSA 1:1-6	A short poem contrasting two kinds of life. The person who avoids the advice, company and mockery of the wicked and instead dwells on God's law day and night is compared to a tree planted by streams, fruitful in season and never withering. The wicked are chaff blown away by the wind, unable to stand in judgment or among the righteous. The closing line states the difference: God knows the way of the righteous, while the way of the wicked comes to nothing.
PSA 2:1-12	A psalm about rebellion against God and his anointed king. Nations conspire and rulers plot to throw off their bonds, and the response from heaven is laughter, then anger. God declares he has installed his king on Zion, and the king recites the decree naming him God's son and promising him the nations as an inheritance. The psalm ends by advising the kings of the earth to serve with fear and take refuge in him.
PSA 3:1-8	A prayer under attack. The writer complains that his enemies have multiplied and that many are saying God will not help him, then calls God a shield around him and the one who lifts his head. He notes that he can lie down and sleep and still wake up, and that he will not fear tens of thousands surrounding him. He asks God to rise and save him, and ends by saying salvation belongs to God.
PSA 4:1-8	An evening prayer for relief. The writer asks God to answer and have mercy, then addresses those turning his honour into shame and chasing lies, telling them God hears the godly. He advises them to be in awe rather than sin, to examine themselves in bed and be quiet, and to trust God. Against the many who ask who will show them any good, he says God has put more gladness in his heart than a good harvest brings, and that he can lie down and sleep in safety.
PSA 5:1-12	A morning prayer. The writer lays his requests out at daybreak and waits, on the grounds that God takes no pleasure in wickedness and cannot live with evil, hating liars and the bloodthirsty. He asks to be led straight because of his enemies, whose mouths have no truth in them and whose throats are open graves, and asks that they be held guilty and fall by their own schemes. He closes asking that those who take refuge in God rejoice, since God surrounds the righteous with favour like a shield.
PSA 6:1-10	A plea from illness and exhaustion. The writer asks not to be disciplined in anger but healed, saying his bones are troubled and his whole self in anguish, and asks how long this will go on. He argues that the dead cannot praise God from Sheol. He describes flooding his bed with tears every night and his eyes wasting with grief. Then the tone shifts: God has heard his weeping and accepted his prayer, so he tells the wrongdoers to leave and expects his enemies to be shamed.
PSA 7:1-17	A prayer for vindication. Pursued and afraid of being torn apart like prey, the writer offers to accept defeat and disgrace if he is actually guilty of what he is accused of. He asks God to rise up and judge, appealing to his own integrity, and describes God as a righteous judge with a sharpened sword and flaming arrows ready. The central image is the trap that rebounds: the enemy digs a pit and falls into it.
PSA 8:1-9	A short hymn of praise, opening and closing with the same line about the majesty of God's name in all the earth. Strength is established even from the mouths of infants, silencing the enemy. Looking at the moon and stars as the work of God's fingers, the writer asks why God should give any thought to human beings at all, then answers that he made them a little lower than the angels, crowned them with glory and put everything under their feet: flocks, herds, wild animals, birds and fish.
RUT 1:1-22	Famine drives Elimelech of Bethlehem to Moab with Naomi and their two sons. He dies, the sons marry Moabite women, Orpah and Ruth, and ten years later both sons die. Hearing there is food in Judah, Naomi starts home and urges her daughters-in-law back to their mothers. Orpah goes. Ruth refuses, saying she will go where Naomi goes and take her people and her God. They reach Bethlehem at the barley harvest, and Naomi tells the town to call her Mara.
RUT 2:1-23	Ruth asks leave to glean and happens into a field belonging to Boaz, a wealthy relative of Elimelech. Boaz learns she came back with Naomi and tells her to stay in his field, drink his water and keep near his women, having ordered the men not to touch her. He credits her with leaving home for her mother-in-law's sake, shares his meal, and tells the reapers to leave extra. She carries home an ephah of barley, and Naomi names Boaz a near kinsman.
RUT 3:1-18	Naomi tells Ruth to wash and dress, go to the threshing floor where Boaz is winnowing, wait until he has eaten and lain down, then uncover his feet and lie there. At midnight Boaz wakes to find a woman at his feet, and she asks him to spread his garment over her as a near kinsman. He blesses her and agrees, but says a nearer kinsman has first claim. She leaves before dawn with six measures of barley.
RUT 4:1-22	Boaz sits at the town gate, stops the nearer kinsman and gathers ten elders as witnesses. He offers the man Naomi's field, and the man agrees until he hears Ruth comes with it, then hands over the right by taking off his sandal. Boaz declares he has bought Elimelech's property and taken Ruth as his wife to keep the dead man's name alive. Ruth bears a son, Obed, the father of Jesse, the father of David.
1CH 10:1-14	The Philistines rout Israel on Mount Gilboa, killing Jonathan, Abinadab and Malchishua. Cornered by archers, Saul asks his armor bearer to kill him; the man refuses, so Saul falls on his own sword, and the bearer does the same. The Philistines strip the bodies, fasten Saul's head in Dagon's temple and announce the news to their idols. Men of Jabesh Gilead recover the bodies, bury the bones under an oak and fast seven days. Saul died for his unfaithfulness, for not keeping Yahweh's word, and for consulting a medium instead.
1CH 11:1-18	All Israel gathers to David at Hebron, makes a covenant with him and anoints him king, as Yahweh had said through Samuel. He takes Jerusalem, also called Jebus, after its people say he cannot enter; the first to strike the Jebusites is promised command, and Joab goes up first. David builds up the city and grows greater. His chief warriors are named: Jashobeam, who killed three hundred at once, and Eleazar, who held a barley field alone. Three of the thirty break through Philistine lines for water from Bethlehem's well.
1CH 11:19-47	David refuses to drink the water, calling it the blood of men who risked their lives, and pours it out to Yahweh. Abishai killed three hundred and captained the three without being counted among them. Benaiah killed two sons of Ariel of Moab, killed a lion in a pit on a snowy day, and killed an Egyptian five cubits tall with the man's own spear; David set him over his guard. The chapter then lists the mighty men by name and home town, Uriah the Hittite among them.
1CH 12:1-20	Men join David at Ziklag while he is a fugitive from Saul: Benjaminites of Saul's own tribe who could sling and shoot with either hand. Gadites come to his wilderness stronghold, faces like lions and swift as gazelles, the least of them equal to a hundred, and cross the Jordan at flood. When men of Benjamin and Judah arrive, David asks whether they come to help or betray; the Spirit comes on Amasai, who declares for him, and David makes them captains. Manassites join him on the road to Ziklag.
1CH 12:21-40	The men who came to David at Hebron are counted by tribe: six thousand eight hundred from Judah, seven thousand one hundred from Simeon, four thousand six hundred Levites with Jehoiada and the young Zadok, and three thousand from Benjamin, most of that tribe having stayed with Saul's house until then. Issachar sends two hundred chiefs who understood the times. From east of the Jordan come a hundred twenty thousand. They spend three days eating and drinking, supplies brought in on donkeys, camels, mules and oxen.
1CH 13:1-14	David consults the commanders and the assembly about bringing back the ark, noting it was neglected in Saul's day, and they agree. All Israel gathers from Egypt's brook to Hamath, and the ark is carried from Kiriath Jearim on a new cart driven by Uzza and Ahio, with singing. At Chidon's threshing floor the oxen stumble, Uzza reaches out to steady the ark, and God strikes him dead. David, displeased and afraid, names the place Perez Uzza and leaves the ark with Obed-Edom, whose house is blessed for three months.
1CH 14:1-17	Hiram of Tyre sends cedar, masons and carpenters to build David a house, and David understands his kingdom is established. He takes more wives in Jerusalem, and thirteen more children are named. Hearing he is king, the Philistines raid Rephaim; David asks God, is told to go up, and defeats them at Baal Perazim, burning the gods they abandon. On a second raid God tells him to circle to the mulberry trees and attack at the sound of marching overhead. He does, and drives them from Gibeon to Gezer.
1CH 15:1-29	David prepares a place and pitches a tent for the ark, then rules that only Levites may carry it, since Yahweh chose them. He gathers Aaron's sons and six Levite families, tells them to sanctify themselves, and says the earlier disaster came from not carrying it as the ordinance required. They bear it on their shoulders with poles as Moses commanded. Singers and players are appointed, Chenaniah over the singing. Seven bulls and seven rams are sacrificed. Michal watches David dancing from a window and despises him.
1CH 16:1-43	The ark is set in its tent, offerings are made, and David blesses the people and gives every man and woman bread, meat and a raisin cake. He appoints Levites under Asaph to minister before the ark, and gives them a psalm of thanks: call on his name, remember the covenant with Abraham, the gods of the peoples are idols, let the sea roar and the trees sing. The people say Amen. Asaph stays with the ark, Zadok is left at Gibeon's tabernacle for daily offerings, and everyone goes home.
1CH 17:1-27	David tells Nathan it is wrong that he lives in cedar while the ark sits in a tent, and Nathan tells him to do as he wishes. That night God tells Nathan otherwise: he has lived in tents since Egypt and never asked any judge of Israel for cedar. Instead Yahweh will build David a house; a son of his will build the temple, throne established forever, God as his father. David sits before Yahweh, asks who he is to be brought this far, and prays the promise stands.
1CO 1:1-31	Paul thanks God for the Corinthians' gifts, then names the problem: Chloe's household reports factions, people claiming Paul, Apollos, Cephas or Christ. He asks whether Christ is divided, and notes he baptised almost none of them. The cross looks like foolishness, since Jews want signs and Greeks want wisdom, but God's foolishness outmatches human wisdom. Few of them were wise, powerful or well born: God chose the lowly and despised so nobody could boast.
1CO 2:1-16	Paul recalls arriving in Corinth without polished speech or clever argument, in weakness and fear, determined to talk about nothing but Christ crucified, so that their faith would rest on God's power rather than human persuasion. The wisdom he does teach is a hidden wisdom, prepared before the world and unknown to its rulers, who would not have crucified the Lord of glory had they recognised him. Only God's Spirit knows God's mind, and only those with that Spirit can discern such things.
1CO 3:1-23	Paul says the Corinthians are still infants, fed milk rather than solid food, and their jealousy and factions prove it. He and Apollos are only workers: one planted, one watered, God gave the growth. Switching to construction, he says he laid the foundation, which is Christ, and warns that whatever is built on it will be tested by fire, so that gold survives and straw does not, though the builder himself is saved. They are God's temple. Nobody should boast in human leaders, since everything already belongs to them.
1CO 4:1-21	Paul describes himself and Apollos as servants and stewards who answer to the Lord, not to the Corinthians, and asks them to postpone judgment until Christ comes. He then contrasts their self-satisfaction with the apostles' condition: hungry, thirsty, beaten, homeless, working with their hands, treated as the world's rubbish and made a spectacle. He says he writes as their father rather than to shame them, has sent Timothy ahead, and will come himself soon, asking whether they would prefer he arrive with a rod or with gentleness.
1CO 5:1-13	Paul reports a man living with his father's wife, something even the Gentiles do not tolerate, and he is angrier that the church is proud rather than grieving. Though absent, he has already judged the case and tells them to hand the man over to Satan so his spirit may be saved. A little yeast leavens the whole lump, so the old yeast must go. He clarifies his earlier letter: the ban on association concerns members of the church, not outsiders.
1CO 6:1-20	Paul is appalled that believers sue each other in pagan courts, when people who will judge the world and angels ought to settle small matters themselves. Better to be wronged, he says, than to sue a brother. He lists those who will not inherit the kingdom, then reminds the Corinthians some of them were such people, and were washed and justified. Against the slogan that all things are lawful, he argues the body belongs to the Lord, and their bodies are temples of the Holy Spirit.
1CO 7:1-22	Answering a written question, Paul says celibacy is good but marriage is the remedy for lack of self-control, and husband and wife each have authority over the other's body. He wishes everyone were single like him, but says it is better to marry than to burn. Married couples should not separate, and a believer married to an unbeliever should stay if the unbeliever is willing, though a departing unbeliever releases them. Each person should remain in the condition in which they were called.
1CO 7:23-40	On virgins Paul says he has no command from the Lord, only his own judgment: given the present distress, people should stay as they are. Marrying is not a sin, but it brings trouble, and the time is short, so those who have wives should live as though they had none, since this world is passing away. The unmarried are free to attend to the Lord undistracted. A father who gives his virgin daughter in marriage does well; not giving her does better.
1CO 8:1-13	The question is meat sacrificed to idols. Paul grants their argument that idols are nothing and there is one God, but notes that knowledge puffs up while love builds up. Food itself does not make anyone better or worse before God. The danger is that a believer with a weak conscience, seeing someone eating in an idol's temple, may be drawn into eating as if to an idol and be destroyed. Paul says he would give up meat permanently rather than trip up a brother.
1CO 9:1-27	Paul defends his refusal to be paid for preaching. He argues he has every right to support, citing soldiers, vinedressers, shepherds, the law against muzzling a threshing ox, and temple servants living from the altar. Yet he has never used the right, because preaching free of charge is his only boast. He makes himself a Jew to Jews and weak to the weak in order to win people, and compares himself to an athlete training under discipline for an imperishable crown.
1CO 10:1-33	Paul uses Israel in the wilderness as a warning: all of them ate the same spiritual food, yet most fell, through idolatry, immorality, testing God, and grumbling. Anyone confident of standing should watch out, though God always provides a way of escape. Returning to idol meat, he argues that sharing the cup and loaf makes one body, and that pagan sacrifices are offered to demons, so one cannot drink both cups. Market meat is fine; if a host names it as idol food, refrain for the other person's conscience.
1CO 11:1-34	Paul addresses head coverings, arguing from an order of headship and from creation that a man should pray uncovered and a woman covered. He then turns to the Lord's supper, which he says they are not really eating: they divide by wealth, some going hungry while others get drunk. He repeats the words of Jesus over the bread and cup on the night he was betrayed, warns that eating unworthily brings judgment, and tells them to wait for one another.
1CO 12:1-31	On spiritual gifts, Paul says nobody can call Jesus Lord except by the Spirit, and the varieties of gifts, service and working all come from one Spirit, distributed as the Spirit chooses. He develops the body image at length, with the foot and ear insisting they do not belong and the eye telling the hand it is unnecessary, arguing that the weaker and less honourable parts get the greater honour, so all suffer and rejoice together. He lists roles in the church and points ahead to a better way.
1CO 13:1-13	Paul says that tongues without love are only noise, and that prophecy, complete knowledge, mountain-moving faith, giving away everything and even being burned all count for nothing without it. He describes love as patient, kind, not envious or boastful, not easily provoked, keeping no record of wrong, bearing and enduring everything. Prophecy, tongues and knowledge will all end, because they are partial, as childhood is put away and a dim reflection gives way to seeing face to face. Faith, hope and love remain, and love is the greatest.
1CO 14:1-40	Paul ranks prophecy above tongues because prophecy builds up the whole assembly while tongues without interpretation edify only the speaker. He compares unclear speech to an instrument with no distinct notes, and says he would rather speak five intelligible words in church than ten thousand unintelligible ones. An outsider walking into a room where everyone speaks in tongues will think them mad. He then sets rules: two or three speakers at a time, in turn, with an interpreter, prophets yielding to one another, and everything done decently and in order.
1CO 15:1-29	Paul restates the gospel he received: Christ died for our sins, was buried, was raised on the third day, and appeared to Cephas, the twelve, more than five hundred at once, James, all the apostles, and last of all Paul himself, whom he calls the least, since he persecuted the church. He then confronts those denying the resurrection of the dead: if the dead are not raised then Christ is not raised, the preaching is empty, the faith is empty, and the dead have perished. Christ is the first fruits.
1CO 15:30-58	Paul asks why he would risk his life daily, including fighting beasts at Ephesus, if the dead are not raised. To the question of what kind of body rises, he answers with a seed that must die, noting that flesh differs from flesh and star from star in glory. The body is sown perishable and raised imperishable, natural then spiritual, bearing first the image of dust and then the heavenly. At the last trumpet the dead will rise and the living be changed, and death will be swallowed up.
1CO 16:1-24	Paul gives instructions for the collection for Jerusalem: each person setting aside money weekly so nothing has to be gathered when he arrives, with approved delegates carrying the gift. He plans to come through Macedonia and perhaps winter in Corinth, but will stay at Ephesus until Pentecost because a great door has opened and there are many opponents. He asks them to receive Timothy without contempt, notes Apollos declined to come now, commends Stephanas, Fortunatus and Achaicus, and sends greetings from Aquila and Priscilla before signing in his own hand.
1JN 1:1-10	The letter opens without a greeting, insisting instead on firsthand contact: what was heard, seen and touched from the beginning is now reported so readers can share the same fellowship with the Father and the Son. The message is simple: God is light, no darkness in him. Claiming fellowship while walking in darkness is a lie. Two more claims are ruled out: saying we have no sin deceives ourselves, and saying we have not sinned makes God a liar. Confession brings forgiveness.
1JN 2:1-29	John writes so they will not sin, but names Jesus as advocate and atoning sacrifice if they do. The test of knowing God is keeping his commandments: whoever hates his brother is in the dark, while whoever loves him stays in the light. He addresses children, fathers and young men in turn, then warns against loving the world, which is passing away. He calls this the final hour, noting many antichrists have already gone out from among them, and names the liar as anyone denying that Jesus is the Christ.
1JN 3:1-24	The chapter marvels that they are called children of God, unrecognized by the world, and says that when he appears they will be like him. Sin is defined as lawlessness, and the Son came to destroy the devil's work. Cain is the counterexample, killing his brother because his own deeds were evil. Hating a brother is called murder, and love is defined by Christ laying down his life. A concrete test follows: seeing a brother in need and closing your heart. Love should be in deed rather than words.
1JN 4:1-21	John tells them to test the spirits, since many false prophets are out in the world, and gives the test: whether a spirit confesses that Jesus Christ has come in the flesh. The rest of the chapter is about love. Love comes from God, so whoever loves has been born of God, and God is love. This was shown by God sending his Son as the atoning sacrifice, before anyone loved him. Since no one has seen God, mutual love is where he becomes visible. Perfect love casts out fear.
1JN 5:1-21	Belief that Jesus is the Christ marks birth from God, and loving God means keeping commandments that are not burdensome, since faith is the victory that overcomes the world. John points to three witnesses that agree: the Spirit, the water and the blood, and says God's testimony is that eternal life is in his Son. He states his purpose: so they may know they have eternal life. Prayer according to God's will is heard, though he distinguishes a sin leading to death. The letter ends abruptly: keep yourselves from idols.
1PE 1:1-25	Peter writes to scattered believers across five provinces, calling them foreigners in the Dispersion. He blesses God for a new birth into a living hope and an inheritance kept in heaven. Their griefs are trials that test faith the way fire tests gold. The prophets searched for the time this salvation pointed to, and even angels want to look into it. He tells them to be sober and holy, since they were ransomed not with silver or gold but with the blood of a lamb without blemish.
1PE 2:1-25	Peter tells them to crave the word like newborns wanting milk, and describes Christ as a living stone rejected by builders but chosen by God, with believers built into a spiritual house and a holy priesthood. They are a chosen race and a holy nation, once no people and now God's. As foreigners they should behave well in front of outsiders and submit to the emperor and governors. Slaves are told to endure even harsh masters, following Christ, who did not curse back and bore sins on the tree.
1PE 3:1-22	Household instructions come first: wives are to win unbelieving husbands by conduct rather than argument, adorning the hidden person of the heart instead of braided hair and gold, and husbands are to honor their wives as joint heirs so their prayers are not hindered. Everyone should be like-minded and bless rather than repay insult. They should always be ready to explain their hope, with humility. Christ suffered once for sins, preached to the spirits in prison, and Noah's eight rescued through water are compared to baptism.
1PE 4:1-19	Since Christ suffered in the flesh, Peter says, arm yourselves with the same intention and stop living for human desires; enough time was spent on drunkenness, orgies and idolatry, and former companions find the change strange. Because the end of all things is near, they should be sober in prayer, love each other earnestly, be hospitable without grumbling, and use their gifts to serve. He addresses their fiery trial directly: suffering as a Christian is no disgrace, but suffering as a thief or a meddler is.
1PE 5:1-14	Peter closes by addressing the elders as a fellow elder, telling them to shepherd the flock willingly rather than for gain, and by example rather than domineering, with a crown of glory promised when the chief Shepherd appears. Younger members should defer, and everyone should clothe themselves in humility and cast their worries on God. The devil is described prowling like a roaring lion. He signs off through Silvanus, sends greetings from Babylon and Mark, and asks for a kiss.
