1TH 1:1-10	Paul, Silvanus and Timothy greet the Thessalonians and give thanks for their work of faith, labour of love and perseverance of hope. Paul says the gospel came to them not in words only but in power and the Holy Spirit, and that they became imitators of the apostles and of the Lord, receiving the word in affliction with joy. Their example has spread through Macedonia and Achaia and beyond, so others report how the Thessalonians turned from idols to serve the living God and wait for his Son from heaven.
1TH 2:1-20	Paul recalls coming to them after being shamefully treated at Philippi, and insists his preaching involved no deception, flattery or greed, and that he sought no honour from them though he could have claimed apostolic authority. He was gentle as a nursing mother and worked night and day so as not to burden anyone, exhorting each of them as a father does. He notes they suffered from their own countrymen as the churches in Judea did, and calls them his crown and joy.
1TH 3:1-13	Unable to bear the separation any longer, Paul stayed behind at Athens and sent Timothy to strengthen them, worried the afflictions he had warned them about might have shaken them and his labour proved wasted. Timothy has now returned with good news of their faith and love and their longing to see him, which comforted Paul in his own distress. He prays night and day to see them, and prays their love will increase so their hearts are blameless at the Lord's coming.
1TH 4:1-18	Paul urges them to please God more and more, naming sexual purity as God's will, with each person controlling his own body. On brotherly love he says they need no instruction, but should aim at a quiet life, mind their own business and work with their hands so outsiders respect them. He then addresses grief over those who have died: since Jesus rose, God will bring them with him. The Lord will descend with a shout, a trumpet and the archangel's voice, the dead in Christ rising first.
1TH 5:1-28	Paul says the day of the Lord comes like a thief in the night, sudden destruction arriving just as people say peace and safety, but the Thessalonians are children of light and should stay awake and sober, wearing faith and love as a breastplate and hope of salvation as a helmet. He asks them to respect their leaders, admonish the disorderly, encourage the fainthearted and never repay evil with evil. Then a run of short commands: rejoice always, pray constantly, give thanks, do not quench the Spirit, test everything.
1TI 1:1-20	Paul writes to Timothy, whom he left at Ephesus to stop certain men teaching a different doctrine and occupying themselves with myths and endless genealogies. The point of the instruction is love from a pure heart. The law, he says, is good when used properly, and was made for the lawless, whom he lists. He thanks Christ for appointing him despite having been a blasphemer and persecutor, calls himself the chief of sinners, and hands Timothy the charge, naming Hymenaeus and Alexander as men who made shipwreck of the faith.
1TI 2:1-15	Paul asks that prayers be made for everyone, including kings, so believers can live quiet and godly lives, since God wants all people saved and there is one mediator, Christ Jesus, who gave himself as a ransom for all. He wants men to pray with lifted hands, without anger or argument, and women to dress modestly rather than relying on braided hair, gold and pearls, and to be known for good works. He then directs that a woman learn quietly and not teach or hold authority over a man.
1TI 3:1-16	Paul sets out qualifications for church office. An overseer must be above reproach, married once, temperate, sensible, hospitable, able to teach, not a drinker or violent or greedy, gentle, managing his own household well, not a recent convert, and well regarded by outsiders. Deacons likewise must be dignified, not double-tongued or greedy, tested before serving, with their wives dignified and trustworthy. He closes by quoting a hymn about God revealed in the flesh.
1TI 4:1-16	Paul warns that some will fall away, following liars who forbid marriage and certain foods, and answers that everything God created is good if received with thanksgiving. Timothy should refuse profane fables and train himself in godliness, since physical training has limited value while godliness has value for this life and the next. Nobody should look down on his youth; instead he should set an example in speech, conduct, love, faith and purity, and not neglect the gift given him by prophecy and the elders' laying on of hands.
1TI 5:1-25	Paul tells Timothy to treat older men as fathers, younger as brothers, older women as mothers and younger as sisters. On widows he separates those genuinely alone from those with families who should support them, sets sixty as the age for enrolment, and advises younger widows to marry rather than drift into idleness and gossip. Elders who lead well deserve double honour. Accusations need two or three witnesses, ordination should not be hurried, and he advises a little wine for Timothy's stomach.
1TI 6:1-21	Slaves should honour their masters, including believing ones, so the teaching is not slandered. Anyone teaching otherwise is conceited and supposes godliness is a way to make money. Paul answers that godliness with contentment is the real gain, since we bring nothing in and take nothing out, and that the love of money is a root of all kinds of evil. He charges Timothy to flee these things and fight the good fight, then tells the rich to hope in God rather than their wealth and to be generous.
2CO 1:1-24	Paul blesses God as the Father of mercies who comforts the afflicted so they can comfort others. He describes a crisis in Asia so severe he despaired of life and felt under a death sentence, which taught him to rely on God who raises the dead, and he thanks the Corinthians for their prayers. He then defends his travel plans, denying he changed his route out of fickleness, since God's promises in Christ are all Yes rather than yes and no. He says he stayed away to spare them.
2CO 2:1-17	Paul explains why he did not visit: he did not want another painful trip, and the harsh letter he sent came out of anguish and tears, meant to show his love rather than to hurt them. The offender has been punished enough, and Paul urges them to forgive and comfort him before grief swallows him, forgiving him himself so Satan gains no advantage. He describes his restlessness at Troas when Titus failed to appear, then thanks God for a fragrance that is life to some and death to others.
2CO 3:1-18	Asked for letters of recommendation, Paul says the Corinthians themselves are his letter, written not in ink but by the Spirit, on hearts rather than stone tablets. He contrasts two ministries: the letter kills, the Spirit gives life. If the covenant carved on stone came with such glory that Israel could not look at Moses' face, the ministry of the Spirit has far more, and its glory does not fade. Moses veiled his face; a veil still lies over the old covenant, removed when a person turns to the Lord.
2CO 4:1-18	Paul says he does not lose heart and has renounced underhanded methods and any tampering with God's word. He preaches Christ rather than himself, and the treasure is carried in clay pots so the power is plainly God's. He lists his condition in pairs: pressed but not crushed, perplexed but not despairing, struck down but not destroyed. The outer self decays while the inner is renewed daily, and present affliction is light beside eternal glory.
2CO 5:1-21	Paul compares the body to a tent that will be dissolved and replaced by an eternal building from God, and says believers walk by faith rather than sight, preferring to be at home with the Lord. All must appear before Christ's judgment seat. Christ's love compels him, since one died for all, so those who live should live for him. Anyone in Christ is a new creation, and God has committed to them the ministry of reconciliation, making them ambassadors who beg others to be reconciled.
2CO 6:1-18	Paul urges them not to receive God's grace in vain, since now is the day of salvation, and defends his ministry with what he has endured: beatings, imprisonments, riots, sleeplessness and fasting, along with purity and sincere love. He describes his position in paradoxes: treated as a deceiver yet true, dying yet alive, poor yet making many rich. He tells them his heart is wide open and asks the same of them, then warns against being yoked with unbelievers, since they are the temple of the living God.
2CO 7:1-16	Paul asks them again to open their hearts, insisting he has wronged nobody. He recalls arriving in Macedonia with no rest, harassed outside and afraid inside, until Titus came with news of their longing and zeal for him. His harsh letter caused grief, which he does not regret, because it was godly sorrow that produced repentance rather than the sorrow of the world, which produces death. He says he wrote to test their care for him, and reports that his boasting about them to Titus proved true.
2CO 8:1-24	Paul holds up the churches of Macedonia, who gave beyond their means out of deep poverty and begged for the privilege. He asks the Corinthians, who excel in everything else, to excel in giving too, not as a command but as a test of sincerity, citing Christ who became poor to make them rich. They began a year ago and should now finish; the aim is equality, abundance supplying lack. He is sending Titus with two other brothers, protecting himself from any accusation about handling the money.
2CO 9:1-15	Paul says he hardly needs to write about the collection, since Achaia has been ready for a year and their zeal stirred up the Macedonians, but he is sending brothers ahead so the gift is prepared as generosity rather than something squeezed out at the last minute. Sparse sowing means a sparse harvest. Each should give as he has decided, without reluctance, since God loves a cheerful giver. The result is not only relief for the poor but thanksgiving to God, and he ends thanking God for his indescribable gift.
2CO 10:1-18	Paul answers the charge that he is bold in letters and feeble in person. He says his warfare is not fought with worldly weapons but demolishes strongholds and takes every thought captive to Christ. He quotes his critics directly: his letters are weighty, his presence weak, his speech contemptible, and warns he will act in person as he writes when absent. He refuses to compare himself with people who measure themselves by themselves, insists he stays within the territory God assigned him, and says approval comes from the Lord's commendation.
2CO 11:1-33	Paul asks them to bear with a little foolishness. He compares himself to a father who betrothed them to Christ as a pure bride and fears they are being seduced as Eve was by the serpent. He notes bitterly they tolerate a different gospel, and that his refusal of payment is held against him. He calls his rivals false apostles, since Satan masquerades as an angel of light. Then he boasts as they do: floggings, a stoning, three shipwrecks, hunger, cold, and his escape from Damascus in a basket.
2CO 12:1-21	Paul describes a man caught up fourteen years ago to the third heaven and Paradise, who heard words he is not permitted to repeat, and declines to boast about it. To keep him from pride he was given a thorn in the flesh, a messenger of Satan, and three times asked for its removal, receiving instead the answer that grace is sufficient and power perfected in weakness. He argues he performed the signs of an apostle among them, and his only offence was refusing their money.
2CO 13:1-14	Paul says he is coming a third time and will not spare them, invoking the rule about two or three witnesses. Since they demand proof that Christ speaks through him, he tells them to examine themselves instead. He would rather appear weak while they are strong, and says he writes in advance so he will not have to be severe in person, since his authority was given for building up rather than tearing down. He closes with instructions to live in peace and a benediction naming grace, love and fellowship.
2PE 1:1-21	Peter opens by saying God's power has supplied everything needed for godliness, then gives a chain of qualities to add to faith: moral excellence, knowledge, self-control, perseverance, godliness, brotherly affection and love. Anyone lacking them is short-sighted and has forgotten being cleansed. He explains why he keeps repeating himself: he knows he will soon put off his tent, and wants them to remember after he is gone. The message is not invented, since he was on the holy mountain and heard the voice call Jesus his beloved Son.
2PE 2:1-22	A chapter against false teachers who smuggle in destructive teachings for profit. Peter stacks up precedents for their punishment: the angels who sinned and were thrown into Tartarus, the flood that spared only Noah and seven others, and Sodom and Gomorrah reduced to ashes while Lot was rescued. He describes the teachers as unreasoning animals, eyes full of adultery, following Balaam, who was stopped by a talking donkey. They are wells without water, promising freedom while enslaved themselves, and he ends with a dog returning to its vomit.
2PE 3:1-18	Peter writes a second time answering mockers asking where the promised coming is, since everything carries on as always. He replies they deliberately forget the flood, and the present world is stored up for fire. With the Lord a day is like a thousand years; his slowness is patience, meant to allow repentance. The day will arrive like a thief, heavens dissolving in fire, followed by a new heaven and earth where righteousness lives. He mentions Paul's letters, hard to understand and often twisted, and closes urging growth in grace.
2TH 1:1-12	Paul, Silvanus and Timothy thank God that the Thessalonians' faith is growing and their love increasing, and say they boast about their endurance under persecution. Paul treats their suffering as evidence of God's righteous judgment: it is just for God to repay those who afflict them and give the afflicted relief when the Lord Jesus is revealed from heaven with mighty angels in flaming fire, punishing those who do not know God with eternal destruction. He prays God will count them worthy of their calling.
2TH 2:1-17	Paul tells them not to be shaken by any spirit, word or forged letter claiming the day of the Lord has already arrived. First must come the rebellion and the revealing of the man of sin, who opposes every god and seats himself in God's temple claiming to be God. Something restrains him for now; when that is removed he will appear with Satanic power and lying signs, and the Lord will destroy him with the breath of his mouth. He tells them to stand firm.
2TH 3:1-18	Paul asks for prayer that the word spread quickly and that he be rescued from unreasonable and evil men. He commands them to keep away from any brother living in idleness, pointing to his own example of working night and day and paying for his food rather than burdening them, though he had the right to be supported. He repeats the rule he gave in person: anyone unwilling to work should not eat. Some are not working but meddling; such a person should be shamed but treated as a brother.
2TI 1:1-18	Paul writes to Timothy as his beloved child, remembering his tears and the faith he inherited from his grandmother Lois and mother Eunice, and tells him to stir up the gift given through the laying on of hands, since God gave a spirit of power, love and self-control rather than fear. Timothy should not be ashamed of the gospel or of Paul's imprisonment, but guard the pattern of sound words. Paul notes everyone in Asia deserted him, and asks mercy for Onesiphorus, who searched Rome until he found him.
2TI 2:1-26	Paul tells Timothy to pass on what he has heard to faithful people who can teach others, and offers three pictures: a soldier who avoids distractions, an athlete who competes by the rules, a farmer who gets the first share. He warns against arguing about words, comparing empty talk to gangrene and naming Hymenaeus and Philetus, who say the resurrection has already happened. In a large house some vessels are gold and some clay. The Lord's servant should not quarrel but correct opponents gently.
2TI 3:1-17	Paul describes the last days with a long list of vices: lovers of self and money, boastful, ungrateful, without self-control, lovers of pleasure rather than God, keeping the form of godliness while denying its power. Some prey on vulnerable households and oppose the truth as Jannes and Jambres opposed Moses, but their folly will become obvious. Timothy has followed Paul's teaching and knows his persecutions at Antioch, Iconium and Lystra. He has known the Scriptures from infancy, and all Scripture is God-breathed and useful.
2TI 4:1-22	Paul charges Timothy to preach the word in season and out, because a time is coming when people with itching ears will collect teachers who suit their own desires. His own departure is at hand: he has fought the good fight, finished the course and kept the faith. Then personal notes: Demas deserted him for the world, only Luke is with him, and he asks Timothy to bring Mark, his cloak and the books. Nobody supported him at his first defence, but the Lord stood by him.
BEL 1:1-20	Under Cyrus, Daniel is the king's favorite, and the king asks why he will not worship Bel, whose idol consumes twelve measures of flour, forty sheep and six firkins of wine daily. Daniel says the thing is clay inside and bronze outside and has never eaten anything. The king tests it with his seventy priests, on pain of death for whoever is wrong. The priests, who have a secret entrance under the table, are unconcerned. Daniel has ashes scattered across the floor first, and the door is sealed.
BEL 1:21-42	Footprints in the ashes convict the priests, who show the king their hidden doors and are executed, and Daniel destroys Bel and his temple. The king then points to a dragon the Babylonians worship as alive. Daniel kills it with no weapon, feeding it boiled pitch, fat and hair until it bursts. The populace forces the king to hand him over, and he spends six days in a den of hungry lions. An angel brings Habakkuk with a meal, and on the seventh day he is found alive.
COL 1:1-29	Paul thanks God for the Colossians' faith, reported by Epaphras, and prays they will grow in the knowledge of God's will. He describes Christ as the image of the invisible God and firstborn of all creation, through whom everything was made and in whom all things hold together, head of the church and first from the dead, reconciling all things by the blood of his cross. Once enemies, they are now reconciled. Paul says he serves the mystery now revealed: Christ in you, the hope of glory.
COL 2:1-23	Paul tells the Colossians how hard he struggles for them and for Laodicea, people who have never met him, and warns against being talked out of their faith by philosophy or human tradition, since the fullness of deity dwells in Christ bodily. Buried and raised with him in baptism, they had the record of debts against them nailed to the cross, and the powers were stripped and paraded. So nobody should judge them over food, festivals or Sabbaths, or rob them with angel worship and self-imposed severity.
COL 3:1-25	Since they were raised with Christ, Paul tells them to set their minds on things above, where Christ is seated. They should put to death immorality, evil desire and greed, and put away anger, slander and lying, having taken off the old self and put on the new, in which there is no Greek or Jew, slave or free. Instead they should put on compassion, kindness, humility and patience, forgive one another as Christ forgave them, and let peace rule. Then instructions follow for wives, husbands, children, fathers and slaves.
COL 4:1-18	Masters are told to treat their slaves justly, since they have a Master in heaven. Paul asks for steady prayer, including for a door to open for his message, and tells them to be wise with outsiders and keep their speech gracious. Tychicus and Onesimus will bring news. Greetings come from Aristarchus, Mark, Epaphras, Luke the physician and Demas. He asks that the letter be read in Laodicea and their letter in Colossae, sends a message to Archippus, and signs in his own hand, asking them to remember his chains.
DEU 32:1-26	Moses recites his song. He calls heaven and earth to listen, praises God as the Rock whose ways are just, and accuses Israel of being a crooked generation that repaid its father badly. He recounts how God found Jacob in the wilderness, carried him as an eagle carries its young, and fed him honey, oil and the finest wheat. Grown fat, Israel kicked, abandoned the Rock and sacrificed to demons. God vows to hide his face, spend his arrows and scatter them.
DEU 32:27-52	God holds back total destruction only so that enemies will not claim the credit. He declares that vengeance is his, that he alone kills and makes alive, and that no other god can rescue from his hand. Moses and Joshua finish reciting the song, and Moses tells Israel these words are their life. That same day God tells Moses to climb Mount Nebo, see Canaan and die there, barred from entering because of Meribah.
DEU 33:1-29	Before his death Moses blesses the tribes. God comes from Sinai with ten thousands of holy ones. Reuben is asked to live and not dwindle, Judah to be helped against his adversaries, Levi to keep the Urim and Thummim and teach the law, Benjamin to dwell in safety, Joseph to receive the richest land, and Gad, Dan and Naphtali are likened to lions or given territory. Moses ends by calling Israel happy, a people saved by Yahweh, with the everlasting arms underneath.
DEU 34:1-12	Moses climbs Nebo to the top of Pisgah, and God shows him the whole land, from Gilead to Dan, Naphtali, Ephraim, Manasseh, Judah and the valley of Jericho, then repeats that he will not cross into it. Moses dies in Moab at a hundred and twenty, his eyesight and strength intact, and is buried in a grave no one has ever found. Israel mourns thirty days. Joshua takes over, and no prophet like Moses has arisen since.
EPH 1:1-23	Paul blesses God for choosing believers before the foundation of the world, predestining them for adoption, redeeming them by Christ's blood and revealing his plan to sum up all things in Christ. Believers were sealed with the Holy Spirit as a pledge of their inheritance. He then prays that they be given wisdom and revelation, with the eyes of their hearts enlightened, so they grasp the power that raised Christ and seated him above every rule and authority, head over all things for the church.
EPH 2:1-22	Paul says his readers were dead in their sins, following the prince of the power of the air, until God made them alive with Christ out of mercy. They are saved by grace through faith, a gift rather than a reward, so nobody can boast, made instead for good works. He then tells the Gentiles that they were once outside Israel's covenants and without hope, but Christ broke down the dividing wall, making one new humanity out of two. They are fellow citizens, a temple built on Christ the cornerstone.
EPH 3:1-21	Writing as a prisoner for the Gentiles, Paul describes the mystery revealed to him: that Gentiles are fellow heirs and fellow members of the same body. He calls himself the least of all the saints, given the task of preaching the unsearchable riches of Christ and making God's wisdom known even to the powers in the heavenly places. He asks them not to lose heart over his sufferings, then prays that they be strengthened inwardly, rooted in love, able to grasp its width, length, height and depth.
EPH 4:1-32	Paul urges them to live worthily of their calling with humility and patience, keeping the unity of the Spirit, and lists what they share: one body, one Spirit, one hope, one Lord, one faith, one baptism, one God. Christ gave apostles, prophets, evangelists, shepherds and teachers to equip the saints, so the church grows up instead of being blown about by every wind of doctrine. They should put off the old self and put on the new: no lying, no anger carried past sunset, no stealing, but kindness and forgiveness.
EPH 5:1-33	Believers should imitate God and walk in love, avoiding immorality, greed and coarse talk, since such people have no inheritance in the kingdom. Once darkness, they are now light, and should expose deeds of darkness rather than share them. Paul tells them to use the time well since the days are evil, and to be filled with the Spirit rather than drunk, singing psalms and giving thanks. Then household instruction: wives subject to husbands, husbands loving their wives as Christ loved the church and gave himself for it.
EPH 6:1-24	Children should obey their parents, the first commandment with a promise attached, and fathers should not provoke them. Slaves should serve sincerely rather than only when watched, and masters should stop threatening, since both answer to the same impartial Master. Paul then lists the armour of God for a fight against spiritual powers, not flesh and blood, from the belt of truth to the sword of the Spirit. He asks prayer for boldness as an imprisoned ambassador, and says Tychicus will bring news.
GAL 1:1-24	Paul skips the usual thanksgiving and says he is astonished that the Galatians are so quickly deserting the gospel for a different one, which is no gospel at all. Anyone preaching another message should be cursed, even an angel from heaven. He insists he did not receive his gospel from any human being but by revelation, and gives his history: he persecuted the church violently, then went to Arabia rather than to Jerusalem, and only after three years met Peter for fifteen days, seeing no other apostle except James.
GAL 2:1-21	Fourteen years later Paul went to Jerusalem with Barnabas and Titus, presenting his gospel privately to the leaders. Titus, a Greek, was not required to be circumcised despite pressure from people he calls false brothers. James, Cephas and John recognised his mission to the Gentiles and asked only that he remember the poor. He reports confronting Peter publicly at Antioch, because Peter had eaten with Gentiles until visitors came from James and then withdrew, taking even Barnabas with him. Paul argues nobody is justified by works of law.
GAL 3:1-29	Paul calls the Galatians foolish and asks whether they got the Spirit by law or by faith. Abraham believed and was counted righteous, so those of faith are his children, while those relying on law are under its curse, a curse Christ absorbed by hanging on a tree. A promise made to Abraham cannot be annulled by a law arriving four hundred and thirty years later. The law was a guardian until Christ came. Now all are one in Christ: no Jew or Greek, slave or free, male or female.
GAL 4:1-31	Paul compares life before Christ to an heir who is still a minor under guardians, and says God sent his Son so they could be adopted as children who cry Abba, Father, no longer slaves but heirs. He is dismayed that they are turning back to observing days and seasons. He recalls preaching to them during an illness, when they received him as an angel, and asks whether telling the truth made him their enemy. He then reads Hagar and Sarah as an allegory of two covenants.
GAL 5:1-26	Paul tells them to stand firm in freedom, warning that anyone accepting circumcision must keep the entire law and has cut himself off from grace, since what counts is faith working through love. They were running well until someone interfered, and he wishes his opponents would cut themselves off. Freedom is not licence for the flesh but service to one another, since the whole law is fulfilled in loving your neighbour. He lists the works of the flesh, from immorality to drunkenness, against the fruit of the Spirit.
GAL 6:1-18	Paul asks the spiritual to restore a person caught in a fault gently, watching themselves, and to carry each other's burdens while also bearing their own. Whatever a person sows they will reap, so they should not tire of doing good. He then takes up the pen himself, noting his large handwriting, and says those pushing circumcision want to avoid persecution and to boast about Galatian flesh, without keeping the law themselves. He boasts only in the cross, and points to the marks branded on his body.
HEB 1:1-14	The letter opens by contrasting how God once spoke through prophets with how he has now spoken through his Son, heir of all things, through whom the worlds were made, the radiance of God's glory, who purified sins and sat down at God's right hand. The writer then argues from scripture that the Son outranks the angels: no angel was ever called Son, angels are told to worship him, and the Son is addressed as God with an everlasting throne. Angels are only serving spirits.
HEB 2:1-18	Since the message came through the Son rather than through angels, the writer warns against drifting away: if the law delivered through angels carried penalties, neglecting this greater salvation is worse. He quotes the psalm about man being crowned with glory and applies it to Jesus, who tasted death for everyone and was made perfect through suffering. Because the children share flesh and blood, he shared it too, to destroy the devil's power over death and free those enslaved by fear of it.
HEB 3:1-19	Jesus is compared with Moses: both were faithful, but Moses was a servant in the house while Christ is a Son over it, and the builder deserves more honour than the house. The readers are that house if they hold their confidence to the end. The writer then quotes the psalm warning against hardening hearts as in the wilderness rebellion, where God was displeased with that generation for forty years and swore they would not enter his rest. Unbelief, he concludes, is what kept them out.
HEB 4:1-16	The promise of rest still stands, the writer argues, since the earlier generation failed to enter through unbelief, and since David speaks of a today long after Joshua, meaning Joshua's conquest was not the rest in question. A Sabbath rest remains for God's people, who should be diligent to enter it. God's word is living and sharper than a two-edged sword, dividing soul and spirit. Because their high priest passed through the heavens and was tempted without sin, they can approach the throne of grace boldly.
JAS 1:1-27	James tells the twelve tribes to count trials as joy, since testing produces endurance. Anyone lacking wisdom should ask God without doubting, since a doubter is a wave in the wind. The rich fade like a flower in scorching wind. Temptation comes not from God but from one's own desire, which conceives sin and then death. He tells them to be quick to hear, slow to anger, and doers rather than hearers who forget their own face in a mirror. Real religion is caring for orphans and widows.
JAS 2:1-26	James opens against favouritism, imagining a man with a gold ring seated well while a poor man is told to stand, and notes God chose the poor to be rich in faith. Loving your neighbour is the royal law, and breaking it at one point makes a person guilty of it all. Faith without works is dead: telling a hungry person to go be warmed feeds nobody, and even demons believe God is one. He cites Abraham offering Isaac and Rahab hiding the messengers as faith completed by action.
JAS 3:1-18	A warning to would-be teachers, who face heavier judgment, then a chapter about the tongue. Small things steer big ones: a bit controls a horse, a small rudder a ship. The tongue is small but boasts greatly, a fire that can burn a forest, untameable even though people have tamed every kind of animal. James objects that blessing and cursing come from the same mouth, when no spring gives both fresh and salt water. He contrasts earthly wisdom, jealous and ambitious, with wisdom from above, pure, peaceable and merciful.
JAS 4:1-17	James traces quarrels back to cravings: they want, do not get, and fight, and when they do ask their motives are wrong. He calls friendship with the world hostility toward God, and tells them to submit, resist the devil, draw near, and turn their laughter into mourning. He forbids speaking against one another, since judging a brother means setting oneself above the law. Then he mocks confident business plans, saying life is a vapour, and ends noting that knowing the good and not doing it is sin.
JAS 5:1-20	James tells the rich to weep, describing corroded gold, moth-eaten clothes, and the withheld wages of harvesters crying out to the Lord. The others should be patient like a farmer waiting for the rain, and not grumble, since the judge is at the door; the prophets and Job are examples of endurance. He forbids oaths, telling them to let yes be yes. The sick should call the elders to pray and anoint with oil, and confess to one another, since Elijah's prayer stopped and restarted the rain.
JDT 1:1-16	The book opens with a war between two kings: Nebuchadnezzar, ruling the Assyrians from Nineveh, and Arphaxad of the Medes, who has fortified Ecbatana with enormous stone walls and towers. Nebuchadnezzar summons the nations to join him, and they refuse and send his messengers home in disgrace. He swears vengeance. In his seventeenth year he defeats Arphaxad anyway, sacks Ecbatana, kills the king with darts in the mountains of Ragau, and feasts for a hundred and twenty days.
JDT 2:1-28	In his eighteenth year Nebuchadnezzar decides to punish everyone who ignored his summons. He gives Holofernes a hundred and twenty thousand infantry and twelve thousand cavalry, orders him to demand earth and water from the west, and to spare surrendering nations while destroying the rest. Holofernes marches out with a horde compared to locusts, and burns his way through Put, Lud, Mesopotamia and Cilicia, torching the fields of Damascus at harvest, until the coastal cities are terrified.
JDT 3:1-10	The coastal peoples send envoys offering complete surrender: land, crops, flocks, cities and inhabitants, for Holofernes to use as he pleases. He garrisons the towns and conscripts their best men, and is welcomed with garlands, dancing and drums. He then demolishes their shrines, since he is authorized to destroy every local god so all nations worship Nebuchadnezzar alone. He camps a month near Esdraelon, opposite Judea, to gather supplies.
JDT 4:1-15	Hearing what Holofernes has done to other nations' temples, the Israelites panic, especially for Jerusalem, since they have only recently returned from captivity. They fortify the hilltop villages and stockpile the harvest. Joakim the high priest writes to Bethulia to hold the mountain passes, narrow enough for two men to block. Everyone puts on sackcloth, including children, servants and livestock, and throws ashes on their heads. The Lord hears them.
JDT 5:1-24	Holofernes demands to know who these hill people are and why they alone did not come out to meet him. Achior, leader of the Ammonites, summarizes Israel's history: the Chaldeans, Egypt, the plagues, the Red Sea, the conquest, exile, and their recent return to Jerusalem. His advice is to check whether they have sinned, and if not, to pass by rather than be humiliated. The council calls for his death.
JDT 6:1-21	Holofernes rejects the advice with the question who is God but Nebuchadnezzar, and predicts mountains drunk with Israelite blood. Achior is handed over to the Israelites to die with them. Guards take him to the springs below Bethulia, where slingers drive them off, so they leave him tied at the foot of the slope. The men of Bethulia untie him and bring him to their rulers. He repeats Holofernes's words, and the people worship and pray all night.
JDT 7:1-17	Holofernes moves the whole army against Bethulia, a hundred and seventy thousand infantry and twelve thousand cavalry. His Edomite and Moabite allies propose a siege rather than a battle: hold the water source at the foot of the mountain, since all Bethulia's water comes from there, and let thirst finish the city. He agrees, and troops occupy the springs.
JDT 7:18-32	Bethulia is completely surrounded for thirty-four days, and every water vessel runs dry. Children, women and young men collapse from thirst in the streets. The population turns on Ozias, blaming him for not making peace, and demands the city be surrendered, arguing slavery beats watching their children die. Ozias asks them to hold out five more days for God to show mercy, and promises to do as they say if nothing comes.
JDT 8:1-17	Judith is introduced with a long genealogy. Her husband Manasses died of sunstroke on the harvest, leaving her wealthy, and she has been a widow three years and four months, wearing sackcloth and fasting except on Sabbaths and feasts. Hearing that Ozias has promised to surrender the city in five days, she summons the elders and tells them the promise was wrong: setting a deadline for God is putting themselves in his place.
JDT 8:18-36	Judith continues her rebuke: this generation worships no idols, unlike the fathers who were handed over for it, and if Bethulia falls, all Judea falls with it. She calls their situation a test like Abraham's, Isaac's and Jacob's. Ozias grants she has always been wise but says the thirsty people forced the oath, and asks her to pray for rain. She answers that she will do something remembered for generations, and refuses to say what.
JDT 9:1-14	At the hour of the evening incense, Judith prostrates herself in ashes and prays. She invokes her ancestor Simeon, avenged by a sword for a violated sister, and affirms God plans events before they happen. She describes the Assyrians trusting in horses and spears without knowing the Lord breaks battles, and asks that their arrogance be broken by the hand of a woman, her lips and deceit the weapon. God, she says, is the helper of the oppressed.
JDT 10:1-23	Judith washes, anoints herself, braids her hair, and dresses in the tiara and festival clothes she wore while her husband lived, to catch the eyes of every man who sees her. She packs her own kosher food and leaves through the gate with her maid, watched by astonished elders. The Assyrian patrol stops her; she claims she is fleeing her doomed people and can show Holofernes how to take the hill country unopposed. A hundred men escort her through a staring crowd, and she bows before him.
JDT 11:1-23	Holofernes asks why she fled, and Judith builds her lie on Achior's argument: Israel cannot be defeated unless they sin, and she claims the starving people of Bethulia are about to eat forbidden food. She offers to pray in the valley each night and report the moment the sin is committed, after which he can march to Jerusalem unopposed. Holofernes and his officers are delighted, and he promises her a place in the king's house.
JDT 12:1-20	Offered the general's own food, Judith refuses and eats only what she brought, and is allowed out each night to pray and wash at the spring. On the fourth day Holofernes throws a private banquet and sends the eunuch Bagoas to fetch her, admitting it would be a disgrace to let her go untouched. She agrees, dresses up, and reclines beside him. He drinks more wine than on any day of his life.
JDT 13:1-20	Bagoas closes the tent, leaving Judith alone with Holofernes drunk on his bed. She prays for strength, takes his own scimitar from the bedrail, and strikes his neck twice, cutting off the head. She puts it in her food bag and walks out with her maid as if going to pray, straight through the camp to Bethulia. She calls to the watchmen and shows the head, saying he did not defile her. Ozias blesses her above all women.
JDT 14:1-19	Judith gives the plan: hang the head on the wall, and march out at dawn as if to attack, so the Assyrians rouse their commander and find him missing. First she has Achior brought in; he faints at the sight of the head, then bows to her, and is circumcised and joins Israel. At sunrise the head goes up. Bagoas goes to wake his master and finds the headless body, and shouts that one woman has disgraced the king.
JDT 15:1-13	Panic empties the camp, and the Israelites chase the scattering soldiers down. Ozias sends word to Jerusalem, the hill country, Gilead and Galilee, who join the pursuit past Damascus. Bethulia loots the abandoned camp for thirty days and grows rich. Joakim the high priest comes from Jerusalem to bless Judith as the glory of Israel, and gives her Holofernes's tent and furniture. The women of Israel dance for her, and she leads the procession in olive garlands.
JDT 16:1-25	Judith leads a victory song, insisting the enemy commander fell not to warriors or giants but to a widow's beauty: her sandal caught his eye, and the scimitar went through his neck. It praises God as creator whose voice nothing resists, and warns the nations who attack Israel. In Jerusalem she dedicates Holofernes's goods and canopy to the Lord. She refuses remarriage, frees her maid, and dies at a hundred and five.
MAN 1:1-15	A short penitential prayer put in the mouth of King Manasseh, addressing the God of Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, who made heaven and earth, whose anger toward sinners is unendurable but whose mercy cannot be measured. The speaker argues repentance was appointed not for the righteous patriarchs but for sinners like himself. He says his sins outnumber the sands of the sea, that he is bowed under iron bands, and that he set up abominations. He bows the knee of his heart and asks not to be destroyed.
MRK 1:1-45	Mark opens with no birth story. John the Baptist appears in the wilderness, baptizing crowds and announcing someone mightier. Jesus is baptized, sees the Spirit descend and hears a voice call him beloved Son, then forty days tempted by Satan. After John's arrest he preaches in Galilee, calls Simon, Andrew, James and John from their nets, drives out an unclean spirit in the Capernaum synagogue, heals Simon's mother in law and a whole town at the door, and cleanses a leper who then publicizes it.
MRK 2:1-28	Four men carry a paralytic to a crowded house and dig through the roof to lower him in. Jesus forgives his sins, which the scribes call blasphemy, then tells him to walk. He calls Levi and eats with tax collectors and sinners, saying a physician is for the sick. Asked why his disciples do not fast, he answers that groomsmen cannot fast while the bridegroom is present, and adds sayings about unshrunk cloth and new wine. Challenged over grain plucked on the Sabbath, he cites David eating the show bread.
MRK 3:1-35	Watched in the synagogue, Jesus asks whether it is lawful to do good on the Sabbath, gets silence, and heals the hand in anger at their hard hearts. The Pharisees and Herodians plot to kill him. Crowds press in so hard he needs a boat offshore. He appoints twelve, named in full. His family come to seize him as insane, and scribes say he casts out demons by Beelzebul. He answers that a divided kingdom cannot stand, warns about blaspheming the Spirit, and calls whoever does God's will his family.
MRK 4:1-41	Teaching from a boat, Jesus tells the parable of the sower: seed lost to path, rock and thorns, and good soil yielding thirtyfold to a hundredfold. Privately: the seed is the word, the thorns are cares and riches. He adds sayings about a lamp not hidden and the measure you use, then two parables: seed growing while the farmer sleeps, and the mustard seed outgrowing everything. That evening a storm nearly swamps the boat while he sleeps; he rebukes the wind and the disciples ask who he is.
MRK 5:1-43	Across the lake a man living among the tombs, uncontainable by chains, meets Jesus. The spirits name themselves Legion and ask to enter two thousand pigs, which stampede into the sea. The locals ask him to leave; the healed man is sent to tell Decapolis. Back across the water Jairus begs for his dying daughter. On the way a woman with twelve years of bleeding touches his clothes and is healed. Word comes that the girl has died; he takes three disciples in and tells her to get up.
MRK 6:1-27	Teaching in his home town, Jesus is dismissed as the carpenter whose family everyone knows, and does little there because of their unbelief. He sends the twelve out two by two with a staff and nothing else. Herod hears the reports and concludes John the Baptist has risen. The narrative doubles back: Herod had imprisoned John for condemning his marriage to Herodias, and at his birthday feast Herodias's daughter danced, was promised anything, and asked for John's head on a platter. Herod sends a soldier to the prison.
MRK 6:28-56	John's head is delivered on a platter and his disciples bury the body. The apostles return and report, and Jesus takes them away to rest, but crowds run ahead and arrive first. Seeing them as sheep without a shepherd, he teaches, then feeds five thousand from five loaves and two fish, twelve baskets left over. He sends the disciples ahead and prays alone, then walks out on the water in the fourth watch. They think he is a ghost. At Gennesaret the sick are laid out to touch his clothes.
MRK 7:1-37	Pharisees from Jerusalem object that his disciples eat with unwashed hands. Jesus quotes Isaiah and gives an example: a man who declares his property Corban can withhold support from his parents, setting aside God's command for tradition. He tells the crowd that nothing entering a person defiles him, and privately lists what comes out of the heart instead. Near Tyre a Syrophoenician woman argues back about dogs eating crumbs, and her daughter is freed. He takes a deaf man aside, touches his ears and tongue and says Ephphatha.
MRK 8:1-38	Four thousand have been with him three days, and seven loaves and a few fish feed them all, leaving seven baskets. Pharisees demand a sign from heaven and are refused. In the boat he warns about the yeast of the Pharisees and Herod, and when the disciples worry about bread he asks whether they still do not understand. At Bethsaida a blind man first sees men like walking trees, then clearly. Peter calls him the Christ; when Jesus predicts his death Peter rebukes him and is called Satan.
MRK 9:1-26	Six days later Jesus takes Peter, James and John up a mountain and is transfigured, with Moses and Elijah talking to him. Peter offers three tents because he does not know what to say. A cloud covers them and a voice names him beloved Son. Coming down, he tells them to say nothing until the resurrection. At the bottom a father explains his son's convulsions and the disciples' failure. He says he believes and asks help for his unbelief; the spirit is driven out, leaving the boy apparently dead.
MRK 9:27-50	Jesus lifts the boy up, and tells the disciples this kind needs prayer. He predicts again that he will be killed and rise, which they neither understand nor dare question. Caught arguing about who is greatest, they are told the first must be last, and he sets a child among them. Told of an outsider casting out demons, he says whoever is not against them is for them. He warns about making little ones stumble, with the millstone and the severed hand, foot and eye, then ends on salt.
MRK 10:1-29	Asked whether divorce is lawful, Jesus points past Moses to creation: the two become one flesh, and no one should separate what God joined. Remarriage, he adds, is adultery either way. When the disciples turn children away he is indignant: the kingdom belongs to such as these. A man asks how to inherit eternal life, claims to have kept them all, and is told to sell everything; he leaves grieving. Jesus says a camel through a needle's eye is easier, and Peter points out that they left everything.
MRK 10:30-52	Jesus finishes the promise of a hundredfold return with persecutions, and adds that the first will be last. On the road up to Jerusalem he spells out that he will be handed over, mocked, spat on, flogged and killed, and rise on the third day. James and John ask for the seats at his right and left. The other ten are angry, and he tells them greatness among them means being a servant. Leaving Jericho, blind Bartimaeus shouts him down, is called over, and receives his sight.
MRK 11:1-33	Two disciples fetch a colt as instructed, and Jesus rides into Jerusalem over cloaks and branches while crowds shout Hosanna. He looks around the temple and leaves. Next morning he finds a fig tree with leaves but no fruit and curses it, then clears the temple, quoting house of prayer against den of robbers. The chief priests look for a way to kill him. The tree is withered by morning, prompting teaching on faith and prayer. Asked by what authority he acts, he asks about John's baptism, and they refuse.
MRK 12:1-23	Jesus tells a parable about tenants who beat and kill each servant sent for the owner's share, then kill his son to take the inheritance. The owner will destroy them and give the vineyard away. His hearers understand it is aimed at them but fear the crowd. Pharisees and Herodians try to trap him over the tax; he asks whose image is on the denarius and says render to Caesar what is Caesar's. Sadducees then pose a woman married to seven brothers in turn and ask whose wife she is.
MRK 12:24-44	Jesus tells the Sadducees they know neither the scriptures nor God's power: the risen do not marry, and God told Moses he is God of Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, so God of the living. A scribe asks which commandment is greatest and hears the Shema plus love of neighbour; he is not far from the kingdom. Jesus then asks how the Christ can be David's son, warns against scribes who devour widows' houses, and points out a widow putting in two small coins, all she had.
MRK 13:1-37	A disciple admires the temple stones; not one will be left standing. On the Mount of Olives four ask when. He warns of impostors, wars, earthquakes and famines as birth pains, arrests, and families betraying each other. When the abomination of desolation appears, Judea should flee without stopping for a cloak. False christs will do signs. Then the sun and moon go dark, stars fall, and the Son of Man comes to gather the chosen. The fig tree shows summer is near, but no one knows the hour: keep watch.
MRK 14:1-34	Two days before Passover the priests look for a quiet way to arrest him. At Bethany a woman breaks a jar of costly nard over his head; the grumbling about waste is answered: she has anointed him for burial. Judas goes to the priests for money. Two disciples prepare the upper room. At table he says one of those dipping with him will betray him, then gives bread and cup as his body and blood. On the Mount of Olives he predicts they will all scatter, and Peter's three denials.
MRK 14:35-72	In Gethsemane Jesus asks that the cup be taken, adding not what he wants but what God wants, and three times finds the disciples asleep. Judas arrives with an armed crowd and identifies him with a kiss; a bystander cuts off a servant's ear and everyone runs. Before the council the witnesses contradict each other, so the high priest asks whether he is the Christ; his answer is taken as blasphemy and he is condemned, spat on and struck. In the courtyard Peter denies him three times and weeps.
MRK 15:1-47	Before Pilate, Jesus answers the question about being king, then stops answering. The priests steer the crowd to choose Barabbas and demand crucifixion. Soldiers dress him in purple with a crown of thorns and mock him. Simon of Cyrene carries the cross to Golgotha. He is crucified between two robbers, his clothes divided by lot, mocked by passers by. Darkness covers the land from noon; he cries out and dies, the temple veil tears, and the centurion calls him Son of God. Joseph of Arimathaea buries him.
MRK 16:1-20	After the Sabbath the women bring spices and worry about the stone, which they find rolled back. Inside, a young man in white tells them Jesus has risen and that the disciples and Peter should go to Galilee. They flee and say nothing out of fear. The rest reports appearances: first to Mary Magdalene, who is not believed, then to two walking into the country, also disbelieved, then to the eleven at table, whom he rebukes. He commissions them to preach everywhere, lists signs, and is taken up.
NAM 1:1-15	The book opens with a vision about Nineveh, describing Yahweh as jealous and avenging, slow to anger but certain not to leave the guilty unpunished. He travels in whirlwind and storm, dries up sea and rivers, withers Bashan, Carmel and Lebanon, and makes the mountains quake. He is also called good, a stronghold for those who take refuge in him. Nineveh gets a flood and a grave; Judah is told to keep its feasts because the yoke is broken.
PHM 1:1-25	Paul writes from prison to Philemon and the church meeting in his house, praising his love and the way he has refreshed the saints. He then makes his request: though he could command, he asks instead, on behalf of Onesimus, Philemon's slave, now Paul's own child and being sent back. He asks that Onesimus be received as Paul himself would be, no longer a slave but a beloved brother, offers to cover any debt in his own hand, and asks for a guest room to be prepared.
PHP 1:1-30	Paul thanks God for the Philippians' partnership from the first day and prays their love will grow in knowledge and discernment. He reports that his imprisonment has advanced the gospel, becoming known throughout the palace guard. Some preach Christ out of rivalry to add to his troubles, but he is glad either way as long as Christ is proclaimed. He is torn between dying to be with Christ and staying for their sake, expects to remain, and tells them to expect to suffer as he does.
PHP 2:1-30	Paul asks them to be of one mind, counting others better than themselves, and points to Christ, who did not grasp at equality with God but emptied himself, took the form of a servant and obeyed to the point of death on a cross, and was given the name at which every knee bows. They should work out their salvation without grumbling. He plans to send Timothy, calling him uniquely trustworthy, and is sending back Epaphroditus, who nearly died and whom they should honour.
PHP 3:1-21	Paul warns against the dogs and the false circumcision, then lists what he could boast about: circumcised on the eighth day, of Benjamin, a Pharisee, blameless under the law, a persecutor out of zeal. All of it he now counts as loss next to knowing Christ. He denies having arrived, describing himself as forgetting what is behind and straining toward the prize. He weeps over enemies of the cross whose god is the belly, and says their citizenship is in heaven, where a Saviour will transform their bodies.
PHP 4:1-23	Paul urges Euodia and Syntyche to agree, and asks a colleague to help these women who laboured with him. He tells the Philippians to rejoice always, to be anxious about nothing but pray about everything, and promises the peace of God will guard their hearts. They should fix their minds on whatever is true, honourable, pure and lovely. He thanks them for their gift, noting they alone supported him after he left Macedonia, and says he has learned to be content whether full or hungry.
PS2 1:1-7	Seven verses spoken by David, telling his own story in miniature. He was the smallest of his brothers, keeping the sheep, and made himself an instrument and tuned a lute. Asking who could report him to God, he answers that the Lord hears for himself. God sent an angel to take him from the flock and anoint him, passing over his taller brothers. The psalm ends with the Philistine, who cursed him by his idols, and David drawing the man's own sword to behead him.
PSA 9:1-20	Thanksgiving for victory. The singer promises to praise Yahweh wholeheartedly because his enemies have stumbled and perished and their cities are forgotten. Yahweh reigns forever on a throne set up for judgment, and he is a stronghold for the oppressed and does not forget the cry of the afflicted. The psalm then turns to petition: have mercy, lift me from the gates of death. The nations have fallen into pits they dug themselves. It closes asking God to remind the nations they are only men.
PSA 10:1-18	A complaint that God seems far away while the wicked prosper. The wicked man boasts, has no room for God in his thoughts, and tells himself he will never be shaken and will never be called to account. He is described as a lion in ambush, murdering the innocent from hiding and dragging the helpless into his net. The psalmist calls on God to arise, break the arm of the wicked, and judge for the fatherless and the oppressed, insisting Yahweh does see and is king forever.
PSA 11:1-7	A short psalm of confidence answering advice to run. Others tell the singer to flee to the mountains like a bird because the wicked have their arrows on the string to shoot at the upright in the dark. He refuses, saying Yahweh is in his holy temple and on his throne, watching and testing everyone. The wicked will get blazing coals, fire, sulfur and scorching wind as their cup, while the upright will see God's face.
PSA 12:1-8	A plea for help in a society of liars. The faithful have disappeared, and everyone flatters his neighbor with a double heart while boasting that their own tongues will win and no one is lord over them. God answers directly, promising to arise because of the groaning of the needy and to set the oppressed in safety. Yahweh's words are contrasted with theirs: silver refined seven times. It ends noting the wicked strut about while worthless things are honored.
PSA 13:1-6	Six verses of impatience. The psalmist asks how long four times over: how long will God forget him, hide his face, leave him carrying sorrow every day and his enemy on top. He asks for an answer and for light in his eyes so he does not sleep in death, and so his enemy cannot claim victory. The last two verses turn without explanation to trust in God's loving kindness and a promise to sing, because God has been good to him.
PSA 14:1-7	The fool says in his heart there is no God. God looks down from heaven for anyone who understands or seeks him and finds no one: all have turned aside and become corrupt, not one does good. The wrongdoers who eat up God's people like bread are said to be seized with great fear, because God is with the righteous. It closes wishing salvation for Israel would come out of Zion, so that Jacob might rejoice.
PSA 15:1-5	A short entrance psalm in question and answer form. Who may live on God's holy hill? The one who walks blamelessly and speaks truth, who does not slander, harm a friend or insult a neighbor, who despises the vile and honors those who fear Yahweh, who keeps an oath even when it costs him, and who neither lends at interest nor takes a bribe against the innocent. Such a person will never be shaken.
PSA 16:1-11	A psalm of contentment and trust. The singer takes refuge in God, says he has no good apart from him, and refuses to pour out drink offerings or even name other gods. Using the language of land allotment, he says his portion and cup are assigned by Yahweh and the boundary lines fell in pleasant places. He blesses God for counsel given in the night, and ends confident he will not be abandoned to Sheol but shown the path of life.
PSA 17:1-15	A prayer for vindication from someone who insists he has nothing to hide. He asks God to hear a plea that comes from honest lips, and points out that God has already tested him in the night and found nothing. He asks to be kept as the apple of God's eye and hidden under the shadow of his wings from enemies who surround him like a lion crouched for prey. Their reward is in this life; his hope is to wake and see God's face.
PSA 18:1-50	A long royal thanksgiving for rescue. The singer piles up titles for God as rock, fortress and shield, then describes being caught in the cords of death and crying out. The answer is a storm: the earth shakes, smoke and fire come from God's nostrils and mouth, he rides a cherub through thick darkness, then reaches down and pulls the singer out of deep waters. The rest credits God with his military success and his enemies' surrender.
PSA 19:1-14	Two halves joined by a comparison. First the heavens: they speak without words in a voice that carries over the whole earth, and the sun runs its circuit like a bridegroom or an athlete, missing nothing with its heat. Then the law, called perfect, sure, right, pure and true, and worth more than fine gold and sweeter than honey. The psalm ends personally, asking forgiveness for hidden faults, protection from deliberate sins, and that his words and thoughts be acceptable.
PSA 20:1-9	A blessing sung over the king before battle. The congregation asks that Yahweh answer him in trouble, send help from the sanctuary in Zion, accept his sacrifices and grant what he has asked, and promises to raise banners when he wins. The speaker then declares confidence that God saves his anointed. The famous line follows: some trust in chariots and some in horses, but we trust the name of Yahweh. It closes with a plea for the king to be answered.
PSA 21:1-13	A companion to the previous psalm, sung after the request was granted. The king rejoices because God gave him his heart's desire, met him with blessings, set a crown of fine gold on his head and granted the long life he asked for. The second half turns on his enemies: God's hand will find them, they will burn like a furnace, their descendants will be cut off, and their plots against him cannot succeed. It ends calling on Yahweh to be exalted.
PSA 22:1-31	The psalm opens with the cry asking why God has forsaken him and does not answer day or night. The sufferer calls himself a worm, mocked and taunted by onlookers who tell him to let God rescue him. He describes being encircled by bulls, lions and dogs, poured out like water with bones out of joint, hands and feet pierced, his clothing divided by lot. Then it turns: God did hear, and the singer vows praise in the assembly, ending with all the nations and generations still unborn worshipping.
PSA 23:1-6	Six verses built on one image and then a second. Yahweh is the shepherd, so the singer lacks nothing: he is made to lie down in green pastures, led beside still waters, restored, and guided along right paths. Walking through the valley of the shadow of death, he fears nothing, because the shepherd's rod and staff are there. The image shifts to a host who sets a table in front of his enemies, anoints his head with oil and fills his cup past the brim.
PSA 24:1-10	The psalm opens by claiming the whole earth and everyone in it for Yahweh, who founded it on the seas. It then asks who may climb his hill and stand in his holy place, and answers: the one with clean hands and a pure heart who has not sworn deceitfully. The last section is a call and response at the city gates, telling the everlasting doors to lift up their heads for the King of glory, identified as Yahweh of Armies.
PSA 25:1-22	A prayer alternating between requests for teaching and requests for forgiveness. The singer lifts up his soul, asks not to be shamed, and repeatedly asks to be shown God's ways and guided in his truth. He asks God to forget the sins of his youth and to pardon an iniquity he admits is great. He describes God as instructing sinners and guiding the humble, then returns to his own state: lonely, afflicted, hated by many enemies. It ends asking redemption for all Israel.
PSA 26:1-12	A protest of innocence. The psalmist invites God to examine, test and try his heart, arguing he has walked in integrity, avoided the company of deceitful men and hypocrites, and refused to sit with evildoers. He speaks of washing his hands in innocence and walking around the altar to give thanks, and says he loves the house where God's glory dwells. He asks not to be swept away with the bloodthirsty and the bribe takers, and ends promising to bless Yahweh publicly.
PSA 27:1-14	Confidence first, then petition. With Yahweh as his light and salvation, the singer says he fears no army encamped against him, and names his one request: to live in Yahweh's house all his days and see his beauty. In trouble God will hide him and set him on a rock. The tone then shifts to pleading not to be abandoned, since false witnesses breathing cruelty have risen against him. He ends telling himself to wait for Yahweh and take courage.
PSA 28:1-9	The psalmist calls out to his rock and begs not to be met with silence, which would leave him among those going down to the pit. He asks not to be dragged off with the wicked, men who speak peace to their neighbors while planning mischief, and asks that they be paid back exactly according to their deeds. Midway the mood changes: God has heard, and the singer calls him his strength and shield. It closes asking God to shepherd his people.
PSA 29:1-11	A short hymn about a thunderstorm. It begins by calling on the heavenly beings to give Yahweh glory, then repeats the phrase Yahweh's voice seven times, describing thunder over the waters, cedars of Lebanon splintering, mountains skipping like calves, lightning, the wilderness of Kadesh shaking, and forests stripped bare. In his temple everything answers Glory. The psalm ends with Yahweh enthroned as king forever, giving strength to his people and blessing them with peace.
PSA 30:1-12	Thanksgiving after recovery from illness. The singer says he cried out and was healed, brought up from Sheol and kept from the pit. He admits that in his prosperity he had told himself he would never be shaken, and that when God hid his face he was thrown into confusion. He records the argument he used in prayer: dust cannot praise you. The psalm's summary lines are that weeping may last the night but joy comes in the morning, and that his mourning was turned into dancing.
PSA 31:1-24	A long prayer for rescue that keeps circling back to trust. The singer asks God to be a strong rock and pull him out of a hidden net, and commits his spirit into God's hand. He describes wasting away with grief, avoided in the street, forgotten like a dead man and like broken pottery, while enemies conspire against him. He insists his times are in God's hand, admits he panicked, and tells the faithful to take courage.
PSA 32:1-11	A psalm about confession. It opens by calling blessed the person whose sin is forgiven and not counted against him. The singer describes what silence cost him: bones wasting away, groaning all day, God's hand heavy on him day and night, his strength dried up as in summer heat. When he acknowledged his sin instead of hiding it, it was forgiven. He then advises others to pray while God may be found, and not to be like a horse or mule that has to be steered by bit and bridle.
PSA 33:1-22	A hymn calling the righteous to sing a new song with lyre and ten stringed harp. It praises God's word, by which the heavens were made and the seas gathered and stored, and notes that he spoke and it stood firm. God frustrates the plans of nations while his own counsel lasts forever, and from heaven he watches everyone he has made. No king is saved by the size of his army and no horse can deliver anyone. The psalm ends waiting on Yahweh as help and shield.
PSA 34:1-22	A psalm of thanksgiving that turns into instruction. The singer blesses Yahweh at all times, invites others to magnify him together, and reports that he sought God and was delivered from all his fears; this poor man cried and was heard. He tells listeners to taste and see that Yahweh is good, then teaches the fear of Yahweh: keep your tongue from lies, turn from evil, seek peace and pursue it. God is near the broken hearted, watches over the righteous, and sets his face against evildoers.
PSA 35:1-28	A prayer asking God to take up arms in a lawsuit and a fight. The singer wants God to seize shield and spear, and asks that his pursuers be chaff before the wind on a dark and slippery road, caught in the net they hid. He complains of false witnesses who repay good with evil: when they were sick he fasted and mourned for them, and they gloated at his misfortune. He asks for vindication and promises public thanks.
PSA 36:1-12	A contrast in two halves. The first describes the wicked man, who has no fear of God before his eyes, flatters himself too much to recognize his own sin, and lies awake on his bed plotting. The second turns to God's loving kindness, which reaches to the heavens, righteousness like mountains and judgments like the great deep, with people sheltering under the shadow of his wings and drinking from the river of his pleasures. The psalm ends asking not to be trampled by pride.
PSA 37:1-40	An extended answer to the complaint that the wicked prosper. Its advice is repeated in many forms: do not fret, do not envy, trust and do good, delight in Yahweh, commit your way to him, rest and wait patiently. The reason given each time is that the wicked wither like grass and vanish like smoke, while the humble inherit the land. Better a little that the righteous has than the wealth of many wicked. The speaker adds his own observation: he has grown old and never seen the righteous forsaken.
PSA 38:1-22	A prayer from someone who reads his illness as God's discipline. He asks not to be rebuked in wrath, describing God's arrows in him and a hand pressing hard, and links his rotting wounds and aching bones directly to his own sin, which he calls a burden too heavy to carry. His friends and relatives keep their distance while his enemies lay snares and spread rumours. He answers nothing, like a deaf and mute man, and confesses his sin, ending with a plea for God to hurry.
PSA 39:1-13	A meditation on how short life is. The singer resolves to keep his mouth bridled in front of the wicked, but the silence makes his heart burn until he finally speaks. What comes out is a request to be shown how few his days are: a lifetime measured in hand widths, every man a breath, walking like a shadow and hoarding what someone else will collect. He asks for relief from God's blow and, since he is only a passing stranger, to be spared long enough to recover.
PSA 40:1-17	Thanksgiving first, then a fresh emergency. The singer waited patiently and was lifted out of a pit of miry clay, set on a rock and given a new song. He says God did not want sacrifice and offering but an open ear, and declares that he delights to do God's will and has proclaimed it publicly rather than sealing his lips. Then the tone turns: countless troubles surround him and his own sins outnumber the hairs on his head. He asks God to hurry, calling himself poor and needy.
PSA 41:1-13	A psalm from a sickbed. It opens by blessing whoever considers the poor, promising God will sustain such a person through illness. The singer asks for healing and admits he has sinned. His enemies are asking when he will die, and visitors come to his bedside speaking falsely and then spread what they saw, saying an evil disease has him and he will not get up again. The sharpest line is that his own trusted friend, who shared his bread, has turned on him.
PSA 61:1-8	A short prayer from a man calling out from the end of the earth with an overwhelmed heart. He asks to be led to a rock higher than himself, recalls that God has been a strong tower against the enemy, and says he will shelter under God's wings and live in his tent. He notes that God has heard his vows and given him the heritage of those who fear him, prays for the king's life to be prolonged for generations, and promises daily praise.
PSA 62:1-12	The psalmist declares that his soul waits in silence for God alone, his rock and fortress, and says twice that he will not be shaken. His attackers mean to push him down like a leaning wall, blessing him aloud while cursing inwardly. He urges the people to trust God and pour out their hearts to him, warns that low and high alike weigh less than a breath, and says not to set the heart on riches.
PSA 63:1-11	Written from a dry and waterless land, the psalm describes a soul thirsting for God as the body thirsts for water. The psalmist recalls seeing God's power in the sanctuary, says his loving kindness is better than life itself, and expects to be satisfied as if by rich food. He remembers God on his bed through the night watches. Those trying to kill him will fall to the sword and become food for jackals.
PSA 64:1-10	The psalmist asks God to hide him from a conspiracy of the wicked, who sharpen their tongues like swords and aim their words like arrows to shoot at innocent men from ambush. They encourage one another, lay snares in secret and congratulate themselves on a perfect plan, assuming nobody will see. God will shoot back, striking them suddenly with an arrow, and their own tongues will be their ruin. Onlookers will shake their heads, and everyone will consider what God has done, while the upright take refuge in him.
PSA 65:1-13	A psalm of praise and harvest. Praise waits for God in Zion, where vows are kept and prayer is heard; he atones for transgressions and blesses those he brings near. He forms the mountains and stills the roaring seas and the turmoil of the nations. The second half describes rain and crops: God waters the earth, drenches its furrows, softens it with showers and crowns the year with bounty, until the pastures fill with flocks.
PSA 42:1-11	A psalm of homesickness for the temple. The singer compares his thirst for God to a deer panting for water and says his tears have been his food while people keep asking him where his God is. He remembers leading the festival crowd to God's house, which makes the present worse. From the far north, near the Jordan and Hermon, he says deep calls to deep and God's waves have gone over him. Twice he tells his own soul to hope.
PSA 43:1-5	Five verses continuing the previous psalm's mood. The singer asks God to plead his case against a godless nation and rescue him from deceitful men, then asks why he has been rejected and left mourning under enemy oppression. He requests that God send out his light and truth to lead him back to the holy hill and the altar, where he will praise God on the harp. It ends with the same refrain, asking his soul why it despairs and telling it to hope in God.
PSA 44:1-26	A national complaint after a military defeat. It begins with what the fathers reported: God drove out nations and planted Israel, and the land was won by his right hand rather than by anyone's sword. Then it turns on the present. God no longer marches with the armies, and the people are routed, scattered among the nations, sold for nothing and mocked. They insist they have not forgotten him, and demand to know why he is asleep.
PSA 45:1-17	A wedding song for the king. The poet says his tongue is like a skilled writer's pen, then addresses the king directly: strap on your sword, ride out for truth and righteousness, your arrows are sharp and the nations fall. His throne is called everlasting and his robes smell of myrrh, aloes and cassia. The second half addresses the bride, telling her to forget her father's house and honor her lord, and describes her led in gold and embroidery with her companions into the palace.
PSA 46:1-11	A short psalm of confidence in God as refuge. The singer says he will not fear even if the earth gives way and the mountains fall into a roaring sea. A river makes the city of God glad, and because God is inside her she will not be moved: nations raged, but he raised his voice and the earth melted. He is then shown making wars cease everywhere, breaking bows and spears and burning chariots. God speaks once: be still and know that I am God.
PSA 47:1-9	A brief enthronement hymn calling all the nations to clap and shout. Yahweh Most High is named great king over all the earth, the one who subdues peoples and chooses Israel's inheritance. God is described going up with a shout and the sound of a trumpet, which sets off a run of commands to sing praises. He reigns over the nations from his holy throne, and the psalm closes with the princes of the peoples gathered and the shields of the earth belonging to him.
PSA 48:1-14	A hymn about Jerusalem. Mount Zion is called beautiful in elevation and the joy of the whole earth, the city of the great King, with God himself proving to be a refuge in her citadels. A group of kings assembled and marched past, then took one look, panicked and fled in pains like a woman in labour. The psalm ends inviting the hearer to walk around Zion, count her towers and tell the next generation.
PSA 49:1-20	A wisdom psalm addressed to everyone, rich and poor alike, offering a riddle set to the harp. Its point is that money cannot buy anyone out of death: no payment is ever enough to ransom a life. Wise men die and so do fools, and both leave their wealth to someone else, however many estates they have named after themselves. Death is called the shepherd of that flock. Twice the psalm compares a rich man without understanding to animals that perish, and says his glory will not follow him down.
PSA 50:1-23	God arrives in fire and storm to hold court over his own people. His first complaint is about sacrifice: he does not need a bull from anyone's stall, since every animal in the forest and the cattle on a thousand hills are already his, and he would not mention it if he were hungry. What he wants is thanksgiving, kept vows and being called on in trouble. He then turns on the wicked, who recite his covenant while keeping company with thieves and adulterers and slandering their own brothers.
PSA 66:1-20	The psalm calls the whole earth to shout to God and to come and see his deeds, recalling the sea turned into dry land. It then describes the testing: God refined them like silver, brought them into prison, laid burdens on their backs, let men ride over their heads, and brought them through fire and water to a place of abundance. The psalmist promises burnt offerings of fat animals, rams, bulls and goats, and tells those who fear God that he prayed and was heard.
PSA 67:1-7	A short blessing psalm. The psalmist asks God to be merciful, to bless them and make his face shine on them, so that his way and his salvation may be known among all nations. Twice it repeats the call for all the peoples to praise God, and it asks the nations to be glad because God judges the peoples with equity. It closes by noting that the earth has yielded its increase and that God will bless them.
PSA 68:1-35	A long processional psalm. It opens asking God to arise and scatter his enemies like smoke, and names him father of the fatherless, defender of widows, and the one who brings prisoners out singing. It recalls the march through the wilderness with Sinai trembling, kings fleeing and plunder divided. A temple procession follows with singers, musicians and tambourines, and princes of Benjamin, Judah, Zebulun and Naphtali. Kings bring gifts, and Egypt and Ethiopia turn to God.
PSA 69:1-36	The psalmist is drowning: the waters are at his neck and he sinks in deep mire with no foothold, weary with crying. He is hated without cause, has become a stranger to his own brothers, is sung about by drunkards, and is given gall for food and vinegar for thirst. He asks that his enemies' eyes be darkened, their homes left desolate and their names blotted out of the book of life. He ends promising praise.
PSA 70:1-5	Five verses of urgent request. The psalmist asks God to hurry and deliver him, and to come quickly to help. He asks that those who seek his life be turned back in disgrace, especially those who say "Aha! Aha!", and that those who seek God rejoice and keep saying "Let God be exalted." He ends by calling himself poor and needy, naming God as his help and deliverer, and asking him again not to delay.
PSA 71:1-24	The prayer of an old man. He says he has relied on God since the womb and asks not to be rejected in his old age when his strength fails.
