His enemies are saying God has forsaken him and that nobody will rescue him, so he asks for their disgrace and for God to stay near. He wants to live long enough to declare God's strength to the next generation, recalls the bitter troubles God has shown him, and ends praising God with harp and lyre.
PSA 72:1-20	A prayer for the king. It asks God to give him justice and righteousness so he judges the poor fairly and breaks the oppressor, and describes his rule coming down like rain on mown grass. His dominion runs from sea to sea, with the kings of Tarshish, Sheba and Seba bringing tribute. He rescues the needy who have no helper. A closing note records that the prayers of David, son of Jesse, end here.
PSA 73:1-28	The psalmist admits his feet nearly slipped because he envied the prosperity of the wicked, who die without struggle, wear pride like a chain and ask how God could know anything. He felt he had kept his heart clean for nothing. Trying to understand it was too painful until he entered God's sanctuary and considered their end: they stand in slippery places and are swept away like a dream on waking.
PSA 74:1-23	A lament after the sanctuary has been destroyed. The psalmist asks why God has rejected his own flock, and describes enemies roaring in the assembly, hacking the carved work with axes and hammers, and burning the sanctuary to the ground. There are no signs and no prophet left, and nobody knows how long. He recalls God dividing the sea and breaking the heads of Leviathan, then asks him to remember the mockery and rise.
PSA 75:1-10	The psalm gives thanks that God's name is near, then switches to God's own voice: he chooses the appointed time to judge, and he holds the pillars of the quaking earth. He tells the arrogant not to boast and the wicked not to lift up their horn. Exaltation comes from no direction on the compass, only from God, who puts one down and lifts another. In his hand is a cup of foaming spiced wine that the wicked drink to the dregs.
PSA 76:1-12	A short psalm about God as warrior. He is known in Judah, with his dwelling in Salem and Zion, and it was there he broke the flaming arrows, the shield and the sword. Valiant men lie plundered and asleep, and at his rebuke both chariot and horse fall into a dead sleep. The earth fell silent when he pronounced judgment from heaven to save the afflicted. The psalm ends telling people to keep their vows and bring presents.
PSA 77:1-20	The psalmist cries out in trouble and refuses to be comforted, kept awake with his eyelids held open and too troubled to speak. He asks whether God has rejected them forever and forgotten to be gracious. He then decides to appeal to the years of God's right hand and to remember his older deeds. The rest recalls the exodus: the waters writhing at the sight of God, thunder and lightning, and a path through the sea with footsteps nobody saw.
PSA 78:1-35	The psalmist announces a parable of old sayings that must be told to the next generation so they do not become stubborn like their fathers. Ephraim forgot God's deeds in Egypt: the sea split, the cloud by day and the fire by night, water brought out of rock. Even so they tested God by demanding food, asking whether he could set a table in the wilderness. He sent manna, then birds as thick as sand, and struck them while the food was still in their mouths.
PSA 78:36-72	The people flatter God with their mouths while their hearts are elsewhere, and he forgives repeatedly, remembering they are only flesh. The psalm recounts the plagues of Egypt, then how God led his people like sheep, drove out the nations and settled the tribes, only to be provoked by high places and carved images. He abandoned Shiloh and gave the people to the sword. Then he woke like a man roused by wine, chose Judah and Zion, and took David from the sheepfolds.
PSA 79:1-13	A lament over a sacked Jerusalem. The nations have defiled the temple and left the city in heaps, and the bodies of God's servants were left for birds and animals with nobody to bury them. The people have become a joke to their neighbours. The psalmist asks how long God will be angry, asks him to pour his wrath on the nations that do not know him, and asks that the sins of their forefathers not be counted against them.
PSA 80:1-19	A prayer addressed to the Shepherd of Israel, three times repeating the refrain asking God to turn them again and make his face shine so they may be saved. The people have been fed with the bread of tears. The central image is a vine brought out of Egypt and planted after the nations were driven out, which covered the mountains and reached the sea. The psalmist asks why its walls are broken so passers by pluck it and a boar ravages it.
PSA 81:1-16	The psalm opens by calling for tambourine, lyre, harp and trumpet at the new moon, since the festival is a statute of God. God then speaks: he lifted the burden from their shoulders, answered them in the secret place of thunder and tested them at Meribah. He tells them to have no foreign god and to open their mouths wide so he can fill them, but says his people wanted none of him, so he let them follow their own stubborn hearts.
PSA 82:1-8	A short courtroom psalm. God stands in the great assembly and judges among the gods, asking how long they will judge unjustly and show partiality to the wicked. He orders them to defend the weak, the poor and the fatherless, and to rescue the needy from the hand of the wicked. They neither know nor understand and walk about in darkness while the foundations of the earth shake. Though they are gods, he says, they will die like men.
PSA 83:1-18	The psalmist asks God not to stay silent, because his enemies have conspired with one mind to wipe out Israel so its name is remembered no more. Ten allies are named, from Edom and Moab to Philistia, Tyre and Assyria. He asks God to treat them as he treated Midian, Sisera and Jabin at the river Kishon. He asks that they be made like tumbleweed and chaff, so that they know Yahweh alone is Most High over all the earth.
PSA 84:1-12	A pilgrim psalm. The psalmist's soul faints for the courts of Yahweh, and he notes that the sparrow and the swallow have found nests near God's altars. He calls blessed those who set their hearts on pilgrimage, who turn the valley of Weeping into a place of springs and go from strength to strength until they appear before God in Zion. A day in God's courts is better than a thousand elsewhere, and he would rather be a doorkeeper there.
PSA 85:1-13	The psalm begins by recalling that God restored the fortunes of Jacob, forgave the people's iniquity and turned from his anger. It then asks him to do it again: to stop being angry forever and to revive them so they can rejoice. The psalmist says he will listen for God to speak peace to his people, provided they do not return to folly. It ends with mercy and truth meeting, righteousness and peace kissing, and the land yielding its increase.
PSA 86:1-17	A prayer from someone calling himself poor and needy. He asks to be heard and preserved, says he calls on God all day long, and describes God as good, ready to forgive and abundant in loving kindness. He says no god compares to God and that all nations will come and worship him. He asks to be taught God's way and given an undivided heart. Proud and violent men have risen against him, so he asks for a visible sign of God's goodness.
PSA 87:1-7	A seven-verse psalm about Zion. God's foundation is in the holy mountains, and he loves the gates of Zion more than all the other dwellings of Jacob. The psalm imagines a register of peoples in which Rahab, Babylon, Philistia, Tyre and Ethiopia are recorded as acknowledging God, each entry reading that this one was born there. Of Zion it will be said that this one and that one were born in her. It ends with singers saying all their springs are in her.
PSA 88:1-18	The bleakest psalm in the collection, and it does not resolve. The psalmist has cried day and night; his life draws near to Sheol and he counts himself among those going down into the pit, set apart among the dead whom God remembers no more. He says God has taken his friends away and made him an abomination to them. He asks whether the dead can praise God, then asks why God hides his face. It ends with his companions in darkness.
PSA 89:1-52	The long psalm of the broken covenant. It opens praising God's faithfulness and quoting his oath to David: his offspring established forever and his throne built up to all generations. God's speech promises David victory, sonship and an enduring throne, with punishment for disobedient descendants but no withdrawal of loving kindness. At verse thirty-eight the psalm turns: God has renounced the covenant, thrown the crown in the dust and let passers by rob the king. The psalmist asks how long, and where the former loving kindness went.
PSA 90:1-17	A psalm about how brief human life is. God has been their dwelling place for all generations, and existed before the mountains were born. A thousand years in his sight are like yesterday, while people are swept away in their sleep and sprout like grass that withers by evening. Their secret sins are set in the light of his presence. Life runs seventy years, or eighty with strength, and even that is labour and sorrow. He asks to be taught to count his days.
PSA 91:1-16	A psalm of protection. Whoever lives in the secret place of the Most High is delivered from the fowler's snare and from deadly pestilence, covered with God's feathers and sheltered under his wings. The reader is told not to fear the terror by night or the arrow by day: a thousand may fall alongside him without touching him. God puts his angels in charge of him so he does not strike his foot on a stone. God speaks at the end, promising to answer and deliver him.
PSA 92:1-15	A psalm for giving thanks, calling for God's loving kindness to be proclaimed in the morning and his faithfulness at night, with lute, harp and lyre. The psalmist says God's thoughts are very deep, something a senseless man does not grasp: the wicked spring up like grass, but will be destroyed forever. His own horn has been raised like a wild ox's. The righteous flourish like a palm and grow like a cedar, planted in God's house and still bearing fruit in old age.
PSA 93:1-5	Five verses declaring that Yahweh reigns. He is clothed with majesty and armed with strength, and the world he established cannot be moved. His throne was established long ago and he himself is from everlasting. The floods lift up their voice and their waves, but Yahweh on high is mightier than the many waters and the breakers of the sea. The psalm closes saying his statutes stand firm and that holiness adorns his house forever.
PSA 94:1-23	The psalmist calls on God as the one to whom vengeance belongs and asks how long the wicked will triumph. They break God's people in pieces, kill the widow, the alien and the fatherless, and say God will not see. He answers with a question: the one who made the ear and the eye surely hears and sees. He calls blessed the man God disciplines, and recalls that when his foot was slipping God's loving kindness held him up.
PSA 95:1-11	An invitation to worship that turns into a warning. It calls people to sing and shout to the rock of their salvation, since Yahweh is a great King above all gods, holding the deep places of the earth and the mountain heights. They are told to kneel before their Maker, because they are the sheep in his care. Then God speaks: do not harden your heart as at Meribah and Massah. He swore in his wrath that that generation would not enter his rest.
PSA 96:1-13	A call for the whole earth to sing a new song to Yahweh, proclaim his salvation daily and declare his glory among the nations. The gods of the peoples are only idols, while Yahweh made the heavens. The families of nations are told to bring an offering into his courts and to worship him in holy array. The heavens, earth, sea, fields and the trees of the woods are all called to rejoice, because he comes to judge the world with equity.
PSA 97:1-12	Another psalm announcing that Yahweh reigns, calling the earth and the many islands to be glad. Clouds and darkness surround him, righteousness and justice are the foundation of his throne, fire burns up his adversaries and the mountains melt like wax at his presence. Those who serve carved images are shamed, and even the gods are told to worship him. The psalm ends telling those who love God to hate evil, since light is sown for the righteous.
PSA 98:1-9	A short psalm calling for a new song because God has done marvellous things, working salvation with his right hand and his holy arm. He has shown his righteousness in the sight of the nations and remembered his loving kindness to Israel, so that all the ends of the earth have seen it. The whole earth is told to make a joyful noise with harp, trumpets and the ram's horn. The sea, the rivers and the mountains are called to join in.
PSA 99:1-9	The psalm declares that Yahweh reigns, enthroned among the cherubim, and calls the peoples to tremble. He is great in Zion and high above all peoples, and three times the psalm says he is holy. His strength loves justice, and he executes justice in Jacob. It recalls Moses, Aaron and Samuel calling on him: he answered from the pillar of cloud, and forgave them while still taking vengeance for what they did.
PSA 100:1-5	Five verses of straightforward praise. All lands are told to shout for joy to Yahweh, serve him with gladness and come before him singing. They are to know that Yahweh is God, that he made them and that they are his people and the sheep of his pasture. They are told to enter his gates with thanksgiving and his courts with praise, giving thanks and blessing his name, because Yahweh is good and his loving kindness endures forever, his faithfulness to all generations.
PSA 101:1-8	A ruler's declaration of policy. The psalmist promises to sing of loving kindness and justice and to live a blameless life, walking with a blameless heart inside his own house. He will set no vile thing before his eyes and will have nothing to do with faithless men or perverse hearts. He will silence anyone who secretly slanders a neighbour, while keeping his eyes on the faithful, who may serve him. Morning by morning he will cut off the wicked from Yahweh's city.
PSA 102:1-28	A prayer of the afflicted. His days vanish like smoke, his bones burn like a torch, and he forgets to eat. He compares himself to a pelican of the wilderness and a lone sparrow on a housetop, mocked all day by enemies who use his name as a curse. The psalm then turns to Zion: God will arise and pity her, hear the groans of prisoners and free the condemned. The heavens will wear out like a garment while God stays the same.
PSA 103:1-22	The psalmist tells his own soul to praise God and not forget his benefits: forgiveness of sins, healing of diseases, redemption from destruction, and a youth renewed like the eagle's. God executes justice for the oppressed and made his ways known to Moses. He is merciful and slow to anger, and has not repaid them according to their sins. He has removed their transgressions as far as east is from west, and remembers that people are dust and their days are grass.
PSA 104:1-35	A psalm of creation. God is clothed in light, stretches the heavens like a curtain, makes the clouds his chariot and walks on the wings of the wind. He set the earth's foundations and a boundary the waters may not cross. Springs water the animals, grass grows for livestock and plants for people. The cedars, storks and wild goats all have their places, and the moon and sun mark the seasons. The sea holds leviathan, formed to play there. All of them wait for God to feed them.
PSA 105:1-45	A retelling of Israel's history as thanksgiving. God remembered his covenant with Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, promising Canaan when they were few and foreigners. He called a famine and sent Joseph ahead, sold as a slave with his feet in shackles, until the king made him lord of his house. Israel grew strong in Egypt, so God sent Moses and Aaron and the plagues followed. He brought the people out with silver and gold, fed them in the desert, and gave them the lands of the nations.
PSA 106:1-48	The same history told as confession. The psalmist admits that they and their fathers have sinned: rebelling at the Red Sea, craving food in the desert, envying Moses, and making the calf at Horeb, exchanging their glory for the image of a bull. Moses stood in the breach to stop their destruction. They despised the pleasant land, joined themselves to Baal Peor, mixed with the nations and sacrificed their own children. God gave them to their enemies, rescued them repeatedly, and remembered his covenant.
PSA 107:1-43	A thanksgiving psalm built on four stories of rescue, each ending with the same call to praise. Wanderers in the desert, hungry and with no city, cried out and were led by a straight way to one. Prisoners sitting in darkness cried out, and God broke their chains. Fools made ill by their own disobedience cried out and were healed. Sailors caught in a storm cried out, and God stilled the waves and brought them to harbour. He turns rivers into deserts and deserts into pools.
PSA 108:1-13	The psalmist declares his heart is steadfast, wakes harp and lyre and says he will wake up the dawn, promising to thank God among the nations. He asks God to save with his right hand. God then speaks from his sanctuary, claiming Shechem, Succoth, Gilead and Manasseh, with Ephraim as his helmet and Judah his scepter, Moab his wash pot and Edom the target of his sandal. The psalm asks who will bring them into the fortified city, and says the help of man is worthless.
PSA 109:1-31	The psalmist asks God not to stay silent while lying and hateful people surround him and repay his love with hostility. He then curses one enemy at length: let his days be few and another take his office, let his children be fatherless, let creditors seize everything, let his name be blotted out. The reason given is that the man never showed kindness and persecuted the poor. The psalmist calls himself poor and needy, fading like an evening shadow, and asks for help.
PSA 110:1-7	A short royal psalm. Yahweh tells the psalmist's Lord to sit at his right hand until his enemies are made his footstool, and says he will send out the rod of his strength from Zion. His people offer themselves willingly in holy array, and he has the dew of his youth out of the womb of the morning. Yahweh swears, without changing his mind, that he is a priest forever in the order of Melchizedek.
PSA 111:1-10	A short psalm of thanks in the congregation. God's works are great and worth pondering, his righteousness endures forever, and he has made his wonderful works remembered. He is gracious and merciful, has given food to those who fear him, and always remembers his covenant, giving his people the heritage of the nations. His precepts are sure and established forever. The psalm ends with the line that the fear of Yahweh is the beginning of wisdom.
PSA 112:1-10	A companion psalm describing the man who fears God and delights in his commandments. His offspring will be mighty and his household will hold wealth and riches, while his righteousness endures forever. Light dawns in darkness for the upright, and it goes well with the man who deals graciously and lends. He will never be shaken, unafraid of bad news because his heart is steadfast. He has given freely to the poor. The wicked will see it, gnash their teeth and melt away.
PSA 113:1-9	A short call to praise. The servants of Yahweh are told to praise his name from now on and forever, and from the rising of the sun to its setting. He is high above all nations, seated on high yet stooping down to see what happens in heaven and on earth. He raises the poor out of the dust and lifts the needy from the ash heap to seat them with princes, and he settles the barren woman as a joyful mother of children.
PSA 114:1-8	An eight-verse psalm about the exodus. When Israel went out of Egypt from a people of foreign language, Judah became God's sanctuary and Israel his dominion. The sea saw it and fled, the Jordan was driven back, and the mountains skipped like rams. The psalm then questions them directly, asking the sea why it fled and the hills why they skipped. It calls the earth to tremble before the God of Jacob, who turned rock into a pool and flint into a spring.
PSA 115:1-18	The psalm asks that glory go to God's name rather than to them, and answers the nations who ask where their God is by saying he is in the heavens and does whatever he pleases. It then mocks idols of silver and gold, listing what they cannot do: mouths that do not speak, eyes that do not see, feet that do not walk. Those who make them become like them. Israel is told to trust in him as help and shield.
PSA 116:1-19	The psalmist says he loves Yahweh because God listened to him. The cords of death had surrounded him and the pains of Sheol had taken hold, so he called out and was saved. He tells his soul to return to its rest, since God has delivered him from death, his eyes from tears and his feet from falling. He recalls saying in haste that all men are liars. He will take the cup of salvation and pay his vows before all the people.
PSA 117:1-2	The shortest psalm, only two verses. It calls all the nations to praise Yahweh and all the peoples to extol him. The reason given is that his loving kindness toward them is great and his faithfulness endures forever. It closes with a call to praise.
PSA 118:1-29	A processional thanksgiving. It opens with Israel, the house of Aaron and all who fear God each repeating that his loving kindness endures forever. The psalmist called out in distress and was answered, and says it is better to take refuge in God than to trust in princes. Nations surrounded him like bees and were quenched like burning thorns. He asks for the gates of righteousness to be opened so he can enter and give thanks. The rejected stone has become the cornerstone.
PSA 119:1-60	The first third of the longest psalm, a meditation on God's law using a rotating set of words: statutes, precepts, testimonies, commandments, word. It opens by calling blameless those who walk in God's law, and asks how a young man stays pure: by living according to God's word, hidden in his heart so he will not sin. He calls himself a stranger on the earth, weary with sorrow, and asks that his heart be turned from selfish gain. Though princes slander him, he keeps meditating on God's statutes.
PSA 119:61-116	The middle third. The psalmist is bound by the ropes of the wicked but rises at midnight to give thanks, and admits his suffering did him good: before he was afflicted he went astray, but the affliction taught him. He calls the law worth more than thousands of pieces of gold and silver, compares himself to a wineskin shrivelled in the smoke, and calls God's word a lamp to his feet. Enemies dig pits and lay snares, and he says he nearly was wiped from the earth.
PSA 119:117-176	The final third. God rejects those who stray and puts the wicked away like dross, and the psalmist's flesh trembles at his judgments. His eyes fail looking for salvation, tears run down his face over people who do not keep the law, and his zeal wears him out. He calls himself small and despised, rises before dawn to cry for help, and keeps watch through the night to meditate. He closes by saying he has gone astray like a lost sheep and asking God to come and seek his servant.
PSA 120:1-7	A short psalm of complaint. The psalmist cried out in distress and was answered, and asks to be delivered from lying lips and a deceitful tongue: sharp arrows of the mighty and coals of juniper. He complains about where he lives, in Meshech and among the tents of Kedar, saying his soul has dwelt too long with someone who hates peace. He ends by saying he is for peace, but when he speaks, they are for war.
PSA 121:1-8	Eight verses about being kept safe. The psalmist lifts his eyes to the hills and asks where his help comes from, then answers: from Yahweh, who made heaven and earth and does not sleep. Yahweh is his keeper and his shade at his right hand, so the sun will not harm him by day nor the moon by night. God will keep him from all evil and keep his going out and his coming in, from now on and forever.
PSA 122:1-9	A pilgrim's psalm about Jerusalem. The psalmist was glad when people said they should go to Yahweh's house, and now their feet stand inside the city's gates, a city built compactly together where the tribes go up to give thanks and the thrones of David's house are set for judgment. He calls for prayer for the peace of Jerusalem, saying those who love her will prosper. For the sake of his brothers and of God's house, he will seek the city's good.
PSA 123:1-4	A four-verse plea for mercy. The psalmist lifts his eyes to the one who sits in the heavens and compares his waiting to servants watching their master's hand and a maid watching her mistress's hand: their eyes stay on God until he shows mercy. He then asks twice for mercy, saying they have endured much contempt, and describes their souls as filled with the scoffing of those who are at ease and the contempt of the proud.
PSA 124:1-8	A psalm of narrow escape, built around a repeated condition: if Yahweh had not been on their side when people rose up against them, they would have been swallowed alive, and the waters and the proud waters would have gone over them. The psalmist blesses God for not handing them over as prey to their teeth, and says their soul escaped like a bird out of a fowler's snare, with the snare broken. He ends saying their help is in the name of Yahweh, who made heaven and earth.
PSA 125:1-5	Five verses about security. Those who trust in Yahweh are like Mount Zion, unmovable and lasting forever, and as the mountains surround Jerusalem, so God surrounds his people. The scepter of wickedness will not remain over the land allotted to the righteous, so they will not put their own hands to evil. He asks God to do good to the upright in heart, while those who turn to crooked ways will be led away with the workers of iniquity. It ends with peace on Israel.
PSA 126:1-6	A short psalm about return. When God brought back those who returned to Zion, they felt like people dreaming; their mouths filled with laughter and their tongues with singing, and the nations said God had done great things for them. The psalmist agrees, then asks God to restore their fortunes again, like streams in the Negev. He ends with the promise that those who sow in tears will reap in joy, and that the one who goes out weeping carrying seed will come back with joy, carrying his sheaves.
PSA 127:1-5	Five verses on effort and its limits. Unless Yahweh builds the house, the builders labour in vain, and unless he watches the city, the watchman guards it in vain. Rising early and staying up late for the bread of toil is pointless, since God gives sleep to those he loves. The psalm turns to children, a heritage from Yahweh and a warrior's arrows, and says the man with a full quiver of them will not be put to shame speaking with his enemies in the gate.
PSA 128:1-6	A short blessing on the person who fears God and walks in his ways. He will eat the produce of his own labour and it will go well with him. His wife will be like a fruitful vine in his house, his children like olive plants around his table. The psalm asks that Yahweh bless him out of Zion so he sees the good of Jerusalem all his life and lives to see his children's children. It closes with peace on Israel.
PSA 129:1-8	Eight verses looking back on long affliction. Israel says it has been afflicted many times from its youth, yet the attackers have not prevailed. The psalmist describes his back as ground plowers plowed into long furrows, and says Yahweh has cut apart the cords of the wicked. He asks that all who hate Zion wither like grass on a housetop: no reaper fills his hand with it, nobody binds it into sheaves, and no passer by blesses it.
PSA 130:1-8	A short psalm crying out from the depths. The psalmist asks God to hear him, then observes that if God kept a record of sins nobody could stand, but there is forgiveness with him, which is why he is feared. He waits for Yahweh and hopes in his word, his soul longing for the Lord more than watchmen long for the morning. He tells Israel to hope in Yahweh, because with him there is loving kindness and abundant redemption.
PSA 131:1-3	The shortest psalm after 117, only three verses. The psalmist says his heart is not haughty and his eyes are not lofty, and that he does not concern himself with great matters or things too wonderful for him. He says he has stilled and quieted his soul, comparing it twice to a weaned child with its mother. The last verse turns outward and tells Israel to hope in Yahweh from now on and forever.
PSA 132:1-18	A psalm about David and the ark. It recalls David's vow not to enter his house or sleep until he found a dwelling place for the Mighty One of Jacob, and asks God to arise into that resting place along with the ark. God swears to David that his descendants will sit on his throne if they keep the covenant, and declares he has chosen Zion forever, where he will bless her provision, satisfy her poor, clothe her priests with salvation, and make David's horn bud.
PSA 133:1-3	Three verses on unity. The psalmist says how good and pleasant it is for brothers to live together in unity, then gives two comparisons. The first is precious oil poured on the head, running down onto the beard, specifically Aaron's beard, and down to the edge of his robes. The second is the dew of Hermon coming down on the hills of Zion. It is there, he says, that Yahweh gives his blessing, even life forever.
PSA 134:1-3	The last of the pilgrim psalms, and only three verses long. It calls on all the servants of Yahweh who stand by night in his house to praise him and to lift up their hands in the sanctuary. The final verse turns the blessing back on them, asking that Yahweh, who made heaven and earth, bless them out of Zion.
PSA 135:1-21	A call for the servants who stand in God's house to praise him, because he is good and has chosen Jacob as his own possession. He is above all gods, doing as he pleases in heaven, earth and the seas, controlling clouds, lightning and wind. The psalm recalls Egypt's firstborn struck, the signs against Pharaoh, and the defeat of Sihon and Og, whose land became Israel's heritage. It then mocks idols of silver and gold that cannot speak, see or hear, and calls Israel, Aaron and Levi to praise.
PSA 136:1-26	A responsive psalm where every one of the twenty-six verses ends with the same line, that his loving kindness endures forever. The first half thanks him for creation: the heavens, the earth spread above the waters, sun, moon and stars set to rule day and night. The second half runs through the exodus and conquest: Egypt's firstborn struck, the Red Sea divided, Pharaoh's army overthrown, Sihon and Og killed and their land given as an inheritance. It ends with God remembering them in their low estate and feeding every creature.
PSA 137:1-9	An exile's psalm. The people sat down and wept by the rivers of Babylon when they remembered Zion, hanging their harps on the willows because their captors demanded songs of Zion for entertainment. The psalmist swears that if he forgets Jerusalem his right hand should forget its skill and his tongue stick to the roof of his mouth. He asks God to remember what Edom said, calling for the city to be razed, and ends with a curse on Babylon, calling happy whoever dashes her little ones against the rock.
PSA 138:1-8	The psalmist gives thanks with his whole heart and sings before the gods, bowing toward the holy temple for God's loving kindness and truth, since he has exalted his name and word above all. On the day he called, God answered and strengthened his soul. All the kings of the earth will give thanks, because though God is high he looks after the lowly and knows the proud from a distance. Walking in the middle of trouble, he trusts God to revive him and save him with his right hand.
PSA 139:1-24	A psalm about being fully known. God has searched him, knowing when he sits and rises and every word before he speaks it. He asks where he could go to escape God: heaven, Sheol, the far sea, even darkness, which shines like day to God. God knit him together in his mother's womb and wrote his days in a book before any existed. He asks God to kill the wicked, says he hates those who hate God, and closes by asking to be searched and led in the everlasting way.
PSA 140:1-13	A prayer for deliverance from violent men who plot mischief and gather for war, tongues sharpened like a serpent's, viper's poison under their lips. They have hidden a snare and set traps to trip his feet. The psalmist calls God the strength of his salvation who covered his head in battle, and asks that the wicked's own desires be denied, their own lips' mischief cover them, and burning coals and miry pits swallow them. He says God will maintain the cause of the afflicted.
PSA 141:1-10	A short evening prayer. The psalmist asks God to receive his prayer like incense and his lifted hands like the evening sacrifice, and to set a watch before his mouth so his heart does not turn to evil or share in the delicacies of wicked men. He says a rebuke from a righteous man is a kindness, like oil on the head, and should not be refused. He keeps his eyes on God and asks that the wicked fall into their own nets while he passes by.
PSA 142:1-7	A short cry for help. The psalmist pours out his complaint before God, saying that when his spirit was overwhelmed God knew the route he was taking, on which a snare had been hidden. He looks to his right and finds nobody concerned for him, no refuge, nobody who cares for his soul. He calls God his refuge and portion in the land of the living, and asks to be delivered from persecutors stronger than himself and brought out of prison to give thanks.
PSA 143:1-12	The psalmist asks God to hear him and not enter into judgment with him, since no living person is righteous in his sight. The enemy has struck him down and made him live in dark places like the long dead, and his spirit is overwhelmed. He remembers the days of old and meditates on God's works, hands spread out, soul thirsty as parched land. He asks to hear God's loving kindness in the morning, to be taught God's way, and that his enemies be cut off.
PSA 144:1-15	The psalmist blesses God as his rock, who trains his hands for war and is his fortress, deliverer and shield. He asks what man is, that God cares for him, since man is like a breath and his days a passing shadow. He asks God to part the heavens and come down, touch the mountains so they smoke, and send lightning and arrows to scatter the enemy and rescue him from foreigners who speak deceit. It closes on prosperity: healthy children, full barns, thriving livestock, no outcry in the streets.
PSA 145:1-21	A psalm of praise arranged as steady declaration. The psalmist will exalt God as King and praise him every day forever, since God's greatness is unsearchable. God is gracious, merciful, slow to anger and of great loving kindness, with tender mercies over all his works. He upholds all who fall and raises those who are bowed down, opens his hand and satisfies every living thing. He is near to all who call on him in truth, saves those who fear him, and destroys the wicked.
ROM 9:1-33	Paul grieves for his own people and would accept being cut off himself if it helped them. Descent from Abraham never guaranteed inclusion: Isaac was chosen over Ishmael, Jacob over Esau before birth. God told Moses he shows mercy as he chooses, and hardened Pharaoh to display his power. To the charge that this is unfair, Paul answers that a potter shapes one lump into different vessels. Hosea and Isaiah are quoted on a remnant: Israel pursued righteousness by works and tripped on the stone; Gentiles reached it by faith.
ROM 10:1-21	Paul says Israel has real zeal but lacks knowledge, seeking a righteousness of its own instead of God's. Christ is the fulfillment of the law for everyone who believes. Righteousness by faith needs no journey to heaven or the abyss: the word is near, in mouth and heart. Confessing Jesus as Lord and believing in the resurrection saves, Jew or Greek. He traces the chain from calling to believing to hearing to preaching, closing with Isaiah's God stretching out his hands all day to a people who will not listen.
ROM 11:1-36	Paul denies God has rejected Israel, citing himself as an Israelite and Elijah's seven thousand who never bowed to Baal. A remnant remains by grace; the rest were hardened. Their stumbling brought salvation to the Gentiles, meant to provoke jealousy. He compares Israel to an olive tree with branches broken off and wild branches grafted in, warning the Gentiles not to boast: the God who cut off natural branches can cut them off too, and graft the old ones back. The hardening is partial. He ends praising God's unsearchable judgments.
ROM 12:1-21	The argument turns to practice. Paul asks his readers to present their bodies as a living sacrifice and to be transformed by renewed minds rather than shaped by the world. Nobody should overrate himself, since the church is one body with different functions, and he lists the gifts: prophecy, service, teaching, exhortation, giving, leading, showing mercy. A rapid run of instructions follows: sincere love, hospitality, blessing persecutors, weeping with those who weep, refusing revenge because vengeance belongs to God, and feeding a hungry enemy. Overcome evil with good.
ROM 13:1-14	Paul tells his readers to submit to governing authorities, arguing all authority comes from God and rulers punish wrongdoing rather than good work. Taxes, customs, respect and honour should all be paid. The only lasting debt is love, fulfilling the whole law: the commandments against adultery, murder, theft and coveting reduce to loving your neighbour as yourself. He invokes the hour, saying the night is nearly over. They should wake up, drop the deeds of darkness, put on the armour of light, and make no provision for the flesh.
ROM 14:1-26	Paul addresses a dispute over food and holy days. One believer eats anything, another only vegetables; one keeps a special day, another treats all days alike. Neither should despise or judge the other, since each answers to God, not to fellow believers. Nothing is unclean in itself, but it is wrong to eat in a way that trips up someone else, because the kingdom is righteousness, peace and joy rather than eating and drinking. He closes with a doxology to the God who can establish them.
ROM 15:1-33	Paul tells the strong to carry the weak rather than please themselves, since Christ did not please himself either, and stacks up quotations showing Gentiles praising God alongside Israel. He explains his own work: a priestly ministry to the Gentiles, preached from Jerusalem to Illyricum, deliberately where nobody had preached before. He plans to visit Rome on his way to Spain, but first must take a collection from Macedonia and Achaia to the poor believers in Jerusalem, and asks them to pray he survives the hostility waiting in Judea.
ROM 16:1-25	Paul commends Phoebe, a servant of the church at Cenchreae, and greets by name more than two dozen people in Rome, among them Prisca and Aquila, who risked their lives for him, and Andronicus and Junia, fellow prisoners noted among the apostles. Several are women he credits with hard work. He warns them to avoid people who cause divisions by smooth flattery, promises God will soon crush Satan under their feet, and passes on greetings from Timothy, Gaius, Erastus the city treasurer, and Tertius, who wrote the letter down.
S3Y 1:1-29	The three men walk about in the furnace praising God, and Azarias prays aloud. He concedes that everything that has happened was just, since the people sinned, and they have been handed to a lawless king. With no temple, prophet or sacrifice left, he offers a contrite heart instead. The king's men keep stoking the fire until the flame shoots forty-nine cubits and kills the Chaldeans standing nearby, and an angel turns the furnace into a cool damp wind.
S3Y 1:30-68	Unharmed, the three sing a long litany of blessing, beginning with God himself in his holy temple and on his throne, then calling on the whole of creation: heavens and angels, sun, moon and stars, showers and dew, winds, fire, frost and snow, mountains, seas, whales, birds and animals. It turns to people: Israel, the priests, the humble of heart, and finally the three men themselves, who thank God for pulling them out of the fire.
SUS 1:1-33	In Babylon, Susanna, the beautiful and devout wife of the wealthy Joakim, walks daily in her husband's garden. Two elders appointed as judges watch her, admit their lust to each other, and arrange to catch her alone. When she sends her maids for oil and shuts the doors to wash, they demand she sleep with them, threatening to testify they found her with a young man. She reasons either way is ruin, chooses not to sin, and screams. They shout too, and their story shames the household.
SUS 1:34-64	The two elders testify that a young man lay with her in the garden and escaped, too strong for them. The assembly believes them because they are judges, and condemns her to death. Susanna prays aloud to the God who knows secrets. God stirs up a young man named Daniel, who demands the case be reopened. He separates the two witnesses and asks each which tree it happened under; one says a mastic, the other a holm, and they are executed instead.
TIT 1:1-16	Paul reminds Titus that he was left in Crete to put things in order and appoint elders in every city, and names the requirements: blameless, married once, with believing children, not arrogant, violent or greedy, able to teach sound doctrine and refute those who contradict it. He warns about deceivers, especially from the circumcision party, who upset households for money, and quotes a Cretan prophet calling Cretans liars and gluttons. Such people, Paul says, profess to know God while denying him by their deeds.
TIT 2:1-15	Paul gives Titus instructions by group. Older men should be temperate and sound in faith; older women reverent and not heavy drinkers, teaching the young wives to love their husbands and children and manage their homes; younger men sober minded, with Titus himself an example of integrity so opponents have nothing to accuse. Slaves should be trustworthy and not steal, so the teaching is adorned. He grounds all of it in the appearing of God's grace, which trains people to renounce worldly desires while awaiting the blessed hope.
TIT 3:1-15	Titus should remind them to obey rulers, be ready for good work, slander nobody and be gentle, since they too were once foolish and enslaved to their desires. God saved them not by their own righteous works but through mercy, by the washing of regeneration and renewal in the Holy Spirit, making them heirs of eternal life. Titus should insist on good works but avoid foolish controversies and legal disputes, and drop a divisive person after two warnings. Paul asks him to come to Nicopolis.
TOB 1:1-22	Tobit introduces himself: a man of Naphtali deported to Nineveh, who alone kept going to Jerusalem with his tithes while the rest sacrificed to Baal. In exile he refused Gentile food and became purveyor to King Enemessar, leaving ten talents of silver with Gabael in Media. Under Sennacherib he secretly buried Israelites the king had killed, was denounced, and lost everything but his wife Anna and son Tobias. After Sennacherib's murder, his nephew Achiacharus brings him back.
TOB 2:1-14	Home for Pentecost, Tobit sends Tobias to find a poor relative to share the meal. Tobias returns saying a fellow Israelite has been strangled and dumped in the marketplace. Tobit hides the body until sunset, then buries it, and the neighbors mock him for it. Sleeping outside with his face uncovered, he is blinded by sparrow droppings. He accuses Anna of stealing a kid she was given, and she asks what his charity has come to.
TOB 3:1-17	Tobit prays, admitting the sins of his fathers, and asks God to let him die rather than keep hearing false reproach. The same day in Ecbatana, Sarah is taunted by her father's maids: she has had seven husbands, each killed by the demon Asmodaeus before the marriage was consummated. She considers hanging herself, but prays instead to be released. Both prayers are heard, and Raphael is sent to heal Tobit's eyes, bind Asmodaeus, and give Sarah to Tobias.
TOB 4:1-21	Remembering the silver left with Gabael, Tobit calls Tobias in for a deathbed lecture: bury him, honor his mother, keep God's commandments, and give alms in proportion to whatever he has, since almsgiving delivers from death. He insists on marrying within the family, and adds practical rules: pay a worker the same day, avoid drunkenness, do to no one what you hate. Only at the end does he mention the ten talents at Rages.
TOB 5:1-22	Tobias points out he does not know Gabael, so Tobit sends him to hire a guide. He finds Raphael, an angel, without knowing it, who says he has lodged with Gabael. Tobit interviews him; he gives his name as Azarias, a kinsman, and Tobit settles on a drachma a day. They set off, with the dog following. Anna weeps that her son has been sent away for money, and Tobit assures her a good angel will travel with him.
TOB 6:1-17	Camping by the Tigris, Tobias goes to wash and a fish tries to swallow him. He hauls it out and keeps the heart, liver and gall. Raphael explains that burning the heart and liver drives off a demon, and the gall cures white films on the eyes. Nearing Ecbatana he proposes Tobias marry Sarah, whose inheritance is rightfully his. Tobias objects that seven husbands have died in her bride chamber. Raphael tells him to burn the fish parts and pray, and Tobias falls in love.
TOB 7:1-18	At Raguel's house, Sarah meets them at the door and Raguel remarks how much the young man resembles his cousin Tobit. On learning Tobias is Tobit's son he embraces him and weeps, again at the news of the blindness. A ram is killed for the meal, but Tobias asks Raphael to raise the marriage at once. Raguel agrees, admitting seven husbands died in the night, and Tobias refuses to eat until the covenant is made. Sarah is given to him, and a contract is written and sealed.
TOB 8:1-21	Brought in to Sarah after supper, Tobias burns the fish's heart and liver on the incense ashes; the demon flees to upper Egypt and Raphael binds him. Tobias asks Sarah to pray with him, blessing God for making Eve as a helper for Adam, saying he takes her in truth rather than lust. Meanwhile Raguel digs a grave in the dark in case there is another death, then sends a maid at dawn, who finds them both alive. He fills in the grave and holds a fourteen-day feast.
TOB 9:1-6	A short chapter of errands. Because Raguel has made him swear to stay for the full wedding feast, Tobias sends Raphael on to Rages with a servant and two camels to collect the money from Gabael and bring him back for the celebration, explaining that his father is counting the days and will grieve if he is late. Raphael lodges with Gabael, hands over the receipt, and receives the sealed bags of silver. The two of them set out early and reach the feast, and Tobias blesses his wife.
TOB 10:1-12	Tobit counts the days past the expected return and wonders whether Gabael has died. Anna concludes their son is dead and mourns, watching the road all day and weeping all night for fourteen days. Tobias asks Raguel to release him; Raguel gives in, hands over Sarah and half his property, and blesses them. He tells Sarah to honor her new parents, and Edna asks Tobias not to grieve her daughter.
TOB 11:1-19	Nearing Nineveh, Raphael suggests they run ahead to prepare the house, and tells Tobias to bring the fish gall. Anna sees them coming and says she is ready to die now that she has seen her son. Tobit stumbles toward the door and Tobias rubs the gall on his eyes; the white films sting and peel away. He blesses God and goes out to welcome Sarah, to the amazement of onlookers. The wedding feast continues another seven days.
TOB 12:1-22	Tobit tells Tobias to pay his guide well, and Tobias offers half of everything they brought back. The stranger urges them to praise God publicly, since almsgiving delivers from death, then reveals himself: he is Raphael, one of the seven angels who present the prayers of the saints. He explains he never really ate or drank, tells them to write everything in a book, and vanishes.
TOB 13:1-18	A psalm of praise written by Tobit. He blesses the God who scourges and shows mercy, and tells the scattered Israelites to give thanks among the Gentiles, since their exile is discipline and God will gather them again if they turn back wholeheartedly. He then addresses Jerusalem directly, promising the tabernacle rebuilt, gifts from far nations, and walls of sapphires, emeralds and gold, her streets paved with precious stones and shouting Hallelujah.
TOB 14:1-15	The book closes with a deathbed speech. Tobit went blind at fifty-eight and was healed eight years later. Grown very old, he tells Tobias to leave Nineveh, since Jonah's prophecy against the city will come true, and predicts Jerusalem's desolation, rebuilding, and the nations turning to God. He asks to be buried with Anna, cites Achiacharus and Aman as proof almsgiving delivers, and dies at a hundred and fifty-eight. Tobias moves to Ecbatana and hears of Nineveh's fall before his own death.
PSA 146:1-10	The psalmist vows to praise Yahweh as long as he lives, and warns against trusting princes, whose thoughts perish on the day their breath departs. Happy instead is the one whose help is the God of Jacob, maker of heaven, earth and sea, who keeps justice for the oppressed, feeds the hungry, frees prisoners, opens the eyes of the blind, and upholds foreigners, the fatherless and widows. Yahweh reigns forever.
PSA 147:1-20	Praise is called good, pleasant and fitting. Yahweh rebuilds Jerusalem and gathers Israel's outcasts, heals the brokenhearted, and counts the stars and calls them all by name. He takes no pleasure in the strength of a horse, but in those who hope in his loving kindness. He sends rain and grass, feeds livestock and young ravens, gives snow like wool and hail like pebbles, then melts them with a word. His statutes were shown to Israel alone.
PSA 148:1-14	A call for the whole creation to praise Yahweh, working downward from heaven to earth. Angels, sun, moon, stars and the waters above the heavens praise him because he commanded and they were created. Then sea creatures, lightning, hail, snow, wind, mountains, fruit trees, cedars, wild animals, livestock, birds, kings, princes, young men, maidens, old men and children. His name alone is exalted, and he has lifted up the horn of his people.
PSA 149:1-9	Israel is called to sing Yahweh a new song in the assembly of the saints, to rejoice in its maker and King, and to praise him with dance, tambourine and harp. Yahweh takes pleasure in his people and crowns the humble with salvation. The saints sing for joy on their beds, with the high praises of God in their mouths and a two-edged sword in their hand, to bind kings with chains and carry out the written judgment.
PSA 150:1-6	The last psalm is six verses of praise and nothing else. Praise God in his sanctuary and in his heavens, for his acts of power and his excellent greatness, with the sounding of the trumpet, harp and lyre, tambourine and dancing, stringed instruments and flute, loud cymbals and resounding cymbals. The closing line widens the summons past Israel and past instruments: let everything that has breath praise Yah.
HEB 5:1-14	A high priest is chosen from among men to offer gifts and sacrifices, and can deal gently with the ignorant because he shares their weakness. Nobody takes the office himself: Aaron was called, and so was Christ, named a priest after the order of Melchizedek. Christ prayed with strong crying and tears, learned obedience through suffering, and became the author of eternal salvation. The writer then breaks off to say his readers have grown dull, need milk rather than solid food, and ought to be teachers by now.
HEB 6:1-20	The writer urges his readers past elementary teaching: repentance, faith, baptisms, laying on of hands, resurrection, judgment. He warns that those once enlightened who then fall away cannot be renewed to repentance; land that drinks rain and grows thorns is burned. Still, he expects better of them, since God will not forget the service they showed the saints. God's promise to Abraham was sworn by himself, there being no one greater, so hope is an anchor of the soul reaching behind the veil, where Jesus has already gone.
HEB 7:1-28	Melchizedek met Abraham returning from battle, blessed him and received a tenth of the plunder. His name means king of righteousness, his city means peace, and he appears with no genealogy and no recorded beginning or end. Since the greater blesses the lesser, and Levi was still unborn in Abraham, the writer argues this priesthood outranks Aaron's. A change of priesthood requires a change of law. Jesus comes from Judah, holds his office by oath and endless life, and offered himself once for all.
HEB 8:1-13	The main point, the writer says, is this high priest, seated at the right hand of the throne in heaven, serving a tabernacle the Lord pitched rather than men. Earthly priests serve a copy and shadow; Moses was told to build to the pattern shown him on the mountain. He then quotes Jeremiah's new covenant at length: laws written on the heart, everyone knowing God, sins remembered no more. Calling a covenant new, he notes, makes the first one old and near vanishing.
HEB 9:1-28	The furniture of the first sanctuary is listed: lamp stand, table and show bread in the Holy Place, and behind the second veil the ark holding the manna, Aaron's rod and the tablets. Priests served the outer room continually; the high priest entered the inner one once a year with blood. Christ, the argument runs, entered heaven itself once with his own blood. A will takes effect only at a death, and under the law almost nothing is cleansed without blood. He will appear a second time.
HOS 1:1-11	Hosea is told to marry a prostitute, because the land itself has been unfaithful. He marries Gomer, and their three children are named as sentences against Israel: Jezreel, for the blood Yahweh will avenge on the house of Jehu; Lo-Ruhamah, meaning no more mercy for Israel, though Judah will be spared; and Lo-Ammi, meaning "not my people." The chapter then reverses itself, promising Israel will be as countless as sand and called sons of the living God, reunited with Judah under one head.
HOS 2:1-23	The marriage becomes the accusation. Israel is the wife who chased lovers for bread, wool, oil and wine, never realising Yahweh supplied all of it. He threatens to strip her bare, end her feasts and new moons, and lay waste the vines she called her wages. Then the tone turns: he will lead her into the wilderness and speak tenderly, give her the valley of Achor as a door of hope, and betroth her forever in righteousness and faithfulness. Lo-Ammi is undone: "You are my people."
HOS 3:1-5	Hosea is told to go and love an adulterous woman again, as a picture of how Yahweh loves Israel while it turns to other gods and raisin cakes. He buys her for fifteen pieces of silver and a homer and a half of barley, and requires that she stay with him and be with no one else, promising the same in return. Israel likewise will live a long stretch with no king, sacrifice or idol, then return and seek Yahweh and David their king.
HOS 4:1-19	Yahweh brings a formal charge against the land: no truth, no goodness, no knowledge of God, only cursing, lying, murder, theft and adultery, until the land mourns and even the fish die. The priests get the worst of it, since they feed on the people's sin, and the verdict is "like people, like priest." Idols consulted, sacrifices on every hilltop, drinking and prostitution that take away understanding. Israel is a stubborn heifer, and Ephraim is so joined to idols that the order is simply to leave him alone.
HOS 5:1-15	Priests, people and palace are all summoned to hear judgement. Ephraim is known to Yahweh and cannot hide; their deeds themselves prevent them turning back, and when they bring flocks and herds to seek him they will not find him, because he has withdrawn. Trumpets sound at Gibeah and Ramah. Yahweh calls himself a moth to Ephraim and rot to Judah, then a lion who tears and carries off. Ephraim turns to Assyria for a cure it cannot give.
1CH 18:1-17	David defeats the Philistines and takes Gath, subdues Moab, and beats Hadadezer of Zobah as far as the Euphrates, hamstringing the chariot horses. Twenty-two thousand Syrians of Damascus fall, and Syria pays tribute. The brass taken from Hadadezer's cities is noted as the metal Solomon later used for the bronze sea and pillars. Tou of Hamath sends gifts by his son Hadoram, and David dedicates all the plunder to Yahweh. Abishai defeats Edom. A list of David's officials closes the chapter.
1CH 19:1-19	Nahash of Ammon dies and David sends envoys to console his son Hanun. Hanun's advisers call them spies, so he shaves the men and cuts their clothes off at the buttocks; David tells them to wait at Jericho until their beards grow. Ammon hires thirty-two thousand chariots from Mesopotamia and Syria. Facing enemies front and behind, Joab splits the army with Abishai and both forces rout their opponents. David later crosses the Jordan and defeats the Syrian reinforcements, killing their commander Shophach.
1CH 20:1-8	In the spring Joab takes the army out, ravages Ammon and besieges Rabbah while David remains in Jerusalem. David comes for the end, takes the Ammonite king's crown of a talent of gold, plunders the city and puts its people to forced labour with saws and axes. Three later fights with the Philistines follow: Sibbecai kills Sippai, Elhanan kills Lahmi the brother of Goliath, and Jonathan the son of David's brother kills a giant with six fingers on each hand and six toes on each foot.
1CH 21:1-30	Satan incites David to number Israel. Joab objects, obeys under protest, and leaves out Levi and Benjamin; the total is over a million and a half swordsmen. God is displeased, and Gad offers David three punishments. David chooses to fall into God's hand, and seventy thousand die of plague. Seeing the angel with a drawn sword over Jerusalem, David buys the threshing floor of Ornan for six hundred shekels of gold, refusing a gift, and builds an altar. Fire answers from heaven and the plague stops.
1CH 22:1-19	David declares Ornan's threshing floor the site of God's house and begins stockpiling for it: dressed stone cut by resident foreigners, iron, brass beyond weighing, and cedar from Tyre and Sidon. He tells Solomon that he was forbidden to build because he had shed much blood, and that a son of peace would build instead. He hands over a hundred thousand talents of gold and a million of silver, urges Solomon to keep the law, and orders the princes of Israel to help him.
1CH 23:1-32	Old and near death, David makes Solomon king and organises the Levites. Thirty-eight thousand are counted from age thirty: twenty-four thousand for temple work, six thousand officers and judges, four thousand gatekeepers, four thousand musicians. The clans of Gershon, Kohath and Merari are listed, including Eleazar son of Mahli, who left only daughters, married by their cousins. Because the tabernacle no longer has to be carried, the age of service is lowered to twenty. Their duties are set: assist the priests, prepare offerings, and praise morning and evening.
1CH 24:1-31	The priests are organised into twenty-four courses. Aaron's sons Nadab and Abihu died childless, so the line runs through Eleazar and Ithamar, with sixteen chief houses from Eleazar and eight from Ithamar. Zadok and Ahimelech help David divide them, and the order is settled by lot so that no house has precedence, the scribe Shemaiah recording the results before the king. The twenty-four names are listed in order, from Jehoiarib first to Maaziah last. The remaining Levites cast lots the same way.
1CH 25:1-31	David and the army captains set apart the sons of Asaph, Heman and Jeduthun to prophesy with harps, lyres and cymbals. Heman, the king's seer, had fourteen sons and three daughters. Two hundred and eighty-eight trained musicians are counted in all. They cast lots for their turns of duty, small and great alike, teacher along with student, and the result is twenty-four groups of twelve, listed in order from Joseph to Romamti-Ezer.
1CH 26:1-32	The gatekeepers are divided by family and assigned gates by lot: east, north, south, west and the storehouse, with watchman set opposite watchman. Obed-Edom's line numbers sixty-two, the text noting that God blessed him. Hosah made a son who was not the firstborn his chief. Shebuel, descended from Moses, oversees the treasuries, and Shelomoth keeps the dedicated spoil given by Samuel, Saul, Abner and Joab for repairing the house. Other Levites serve as officers and judges outside Jerusalem.
1CH 27:1-34	Israel's militia is arranged in twelve divisions of twenty-four thousand men, each serving one month of the year, with its commander named. Then the tribal chiefs are listed. A note records that David did not count those under twenty, and that Joab began a census but never finished it, wrath having fallen on Israel, so the figure was left out of the royal records. Finally the stewards of the king's estates, herds, vineyards and camels, and his counsellors, including Ahithophel and Hushai the king's friend.
1CH 28:1-21	David assembles Israel's officials and explains that he wanted to build the temple but was refused, since he was a man of war who had shed blood. God chose Solomon to build it, and his kingdom will stand if he keeps the commandments. David charges his son to serve God wholeheartedly, then hands over the plans: the porch, rooms, treasuries, courts, the weights of gold and silver for every vessel and lampstand, and the golden cherubim over the ark, saying the pattern came in writing from God's hand.
1CH 29:1-14	David tells the assembly that Solomon is young and the work is huge, and that beyond the materials already stockpiled he is giving his own treasure: three thousand talents of gold from Ophir and seven thousand of refined silver. He asks who else will give. The leaders respond with gold, silver, brass, iron and precious stones handed to Jehiel. The people rejoice at their own willingness, and David blesses God before them all, saying that greatness, power and riches are God's and that everything given came from God's hand first.
1CH 29:15-30	David's prayer continues, calling the people strangers whose days on earth are a shadow, and asking God to keep this willing spirit in them and to give Solomon a whole heart to build the house. The assembly blesses God and bows. The next day they sacrifice a thousand bulls, a thousand rams and a thousand lambs, and make Solomon king a second time, anointing Zadok as priest. Solomon prospers and all Israel obeys him. David dies old and rich after forty years, seven in Hebron and thirty-three in Jerusalem.
1ES 1:1-29	The book opens with Josiah's Passover. He sets the priests in their courses, tells the Levites they need no longer carry the ark, and gives thirty thousand lambs and three thousand calves from his own property, with officials adding thousands more. The Levites roast and serve the offerings, and the singers and gatekeepers stay at their posts. No such Passover since Samuel. Josiah then rides out against Pharaoh at Carchemish, ignoring Jeremiah's word, and meets him at Megiddo.
1ES 1:30-58	Wounded, Josiah asks to be carried out and dies. Jeremiah laments him, and the mourning becomes a permanent ordinance. Joachaz reigns three months before Egypt deposes him and installs Joakim, who is later chained and taken to Babylon with the holy vessels. His son lasts three months and ten days. Zedekiah breaks his sworn oath and ignores Jeremiah, the leaders defile the temple, and Babylon burns the house, breaks the walls, and deports the survivors for seventy Sabbath years.
1ES 2:1-30	Cyrus, stirred by God, proclaims that the Lord of Israel has made him king of the world and charged him to build a house at Jerusalem, and that anyone of that people may go, helped by their neighbours with gold and cattle. He brings out the vessels Nebuchadnezzar had set among his idols, five thousand four hundred and sixty-nine in all, and delivers them to Sanabassar. Later, officials in Samaria write to Artaxerxes calling Jerusalem rebellious, and the work is halted by force.
1ES 3:1-24	Darius holds a great feast for his empire, then wakes to find three bodyguards proposing a contest: each writes what he thinks strongest, and the winner wears purple, drinks from gold, and is called the king's cousin. They seal their answers under his pillow. The first writes wine, the second the king, the third women, but above all truth. Called to argue, the first says wine levels king and orphan, erases debt and memory, and turns friends to swords.
1ES 4:1-34	The second argues for the king: one man commands armies to kill and be killed, farmers to hand over their harvest, and cities built or levelled, and he sleeps while others keep watch. Zerubbabel argues for women: they bear kings and the men who plant the vineyards, and men leave father and country for a wife. He describes seeing the concubine Apame take the crown off the king's head and strike him, while he flattered her to be reconciled. Then he turns to truth.
1ES 4:35-63	Zerubbabel argues that the earth is great, heaven high and the sun swift, but greater is the one who made them, and truth is stronger than all. Wine, kings, women and all people are unrighteous and perish, but truth lives and conquers. The assembly shouts that truth is great. Offered anything, Zerubbabel asks only that Darius keep his vow to rebuild Jerusalem and return the vessels. The king kisses him and writes letters for safe passage, cedar, tax exemption, and yearly grants.
1ES 5:1-29	The heads of families are chosen to go up by tribe, with wives, children, servants and cattle, and Darius sends a thousand horsemen to escort them safely to Jerusalem. The leaders are named, Zerubbabel and Jeshua at their head, and the families are listed by ancestor and town, from Phoros at two thousand one hundred seventy-two down through Bethlehem and Anathoth. Four priestly houses follow, numbering over four thousand, then seventy-four Levites and a hundred and twenty-eight singers.
1ES 5:30-50	The temple servants and sons of Solomon's servants are listed, three hundred and seventy-two in all. Some who came up could not show their descent, and priests not found in the register, including one who married Augia and took her father's name, were barred from office until a high priest with Urim and Thummim should arise. Israel from twelve years old upward numbers 42,360, with servants, singers and livestock counted. In the seventh month they gather and set up the altar.
1ES 5:51-73	They keep the feast of tabernacles and the regular offerings although no temple yet stands, paying masons and carpenters and sending goods to Tyre for cedar floated to Joppa. In the second month of the second year the foundation is laid, with Levites overseeing the work, priests with trumpets, and Asaph's sons with cymbals. The elders who had seen the former house weep so loudly the trumpets cannot be heard. Neighbours who offer to help are refused, and they obstruct the work for two years.
1ES 6:1-19	In Darius' second year Haggai and Zechariah prophesy, and Zerubbabel and Jeshua restart the building. Sisinnes, governor of Syria and Phoenicia, arrives to ask by whose order they build, but they are not stopped while the question goes to the king. Their letter reports great new stonework going up fast, and quotes the elders' answer: they are servants of the God who made heaven and earth, rebuilding what Nebuchadnezzar burned, under an order of Cyrus who returned the vessels.
1ES 6:20-34	The letter notes that Sanabassarus laid the foundation and the work has dragged on ever since, and asks that the royal archives be searched. The scroll turns up at Ekbatana in Media, recording Cyrus' order with the temple's dimensions and the charge on the royal treasury. Darius orders the governors not to meddle, to fund the work and daily sacrifices from provincial tribute so prayers may be made for the king, and rules that violators be hanged on a beam from their own house.
1ES 7:1-15	Sisinnes and Sathrabuzanes carry out the order carefully, and the work prospers while Haggai and Zechariah prophesy. The house is finished on the twenty-third of Adar in Darius' sixth year, by the commandment of God and the consent of Cyrus, Darius and Artaxerxes. At the dedication they offer a hundred bulls, two hundred rams, four hundred lambs and twelve goats for the twelve tribes. Passover follows, kept by all who had separated themselves from the surrounding peoples, with seven days of unleavened bread.
1ES 8:1-29	Under Artaxerxes, Ezra, traced through sixteen generations to Aaron, goes up from Babylon as a scribe skilled in the law, taking priests, Levites, singers and temple servants with him. The king's commission lets any willing Jew go, sends gifts vowed by the king and his counsellors, orders the treasurers of Syria and Phoenicia to supply up to a hundred talents of silver, exempts temple staff from tax, and tells Ezra to appoint judges and teach those who do not know the law.
1ES 8:30-65	Ezra lists the family heads who came with him. Camped three days at the river Theras, he finds no priests or Levites, and sends eleven men to Iddo at Casiphia to ask for ministers; they return with eighteen of Mahli's line, twenty more, and two hundred and twenty temple servants. He vows a fast rather than ask the king for an escort, having said God protects those who seek him. He weighs out silver and gold to twelve priests, weighed again in Jerusalem.
1ES 8:66-96	The offerings made and the king's orders delivered, the leading men tell Ezra that Israel, priests and Levites have not separated from the surrounding peoples, and the rulers led the way. He tears his clothes and holy garment, pulls out his hair, and sits until the evening sacrifice, then prays that sins have reached to heaven, that mercy left them a root, and that they cannot stand before God. A great crowd gathers weeping, and Jechonias proposes an oath to put away the foreign wives and children.
1ES 9:1-28	Ezra withdraws to a chamber and neither eats nor drinks, mourning. A proclamation orders everyone of the captivity to Jerusalem within days, on pain of losing their cattle and being expelled from the assembly. They gather trembling in foul weather, and agree to Ezra's charge, but ask that commissioners handle the matter over time since the crowd is large and the rain heavy. Four men take it on, the inquiry runs three months, and the priests involved are named, beginning with four of Jeshua's line.
1ES 9:29-55	The register of those with foreign wives continues through several families, and the text states they put them away with their children. On the new moon of the seventh month the assembly gathers before the temple porch and asks Ezra to bring the law. He reads from a wooden platform, with thirteen men flanking him, from morning until midday, and the Levites explain it. Told the day is holy, the people stop weeping and go to eat, drink and send portions to those who have nothing.
1MA 6:1-32	Antiochus tries to plunder a rich temple at Elymais and is driven off. Retreating toward Babylon he hears his armies in Judah are routed and the abomination pulled down. He falls sick with grief, tells his Friends he is dying for what he did at Jerusalem, hands Philip his diadem to raise his son, and dies. Lysias crowns the boy Eupator instead. Petitioned by the citadel garrison, the new king marches on Bethsura with a hundred thousand foot and thirty-two elephants.
1MA 6:33-63	The elephants are shown the blood of grapes and mulberries to stir them, each given a thousand armoured men and a wooden tower of fighters. Six hundred of the king's men fall. Eleazar Avaran, believing the king rides the tallest beast, stabs it from below and dies beneath it. The Jews withdraw. Bethsura surrenders for want of food, and the sanctuary starves too. Hearing Philip is seizing power, Lysias makes peace granting the Jews their own laws, then pulls the wall down anyway.
1MA 7:1-24	Demetrius, son of Seleucus, leaves Rome and takes a city by the sea; the army seizes Antiochus and Lysias, and he has them killed without looking at them. Alcimus, wanting the high priesthood, accuses Judas before him. Demetrius sends Bacchides with Alcimus and an army, offering peace deceitfully; Judas will not listen. The Hasidaeans trust Alcimus because he is a priest of Aaron's line, and sixty of them are killed in one day. Bacchides kills more and throws them into the great pit, leaves troops with Alcimus, and goes home.
1MA 7:25-50	Alcimus goes back to the king with fresh accusations, and Demetrius sends Nicanor to destroy the people. Nicanor's peaceful approach conceals a plan to seize Judas, which Judas discovers. They fight at Capharsalama, where five hundred of Nicanor's men fall. Nicanor mocks the priests and swears to burn the house unless Judas is handed over. On the thirteenth of Adar the armies meet at Adasa. Nicanor falls first, his army throws down its arms, and the villages close in until none are left.
1MA 8:1-15	Judas hears reports of the Romans: their conquest of the Gauls, their seizure of the silver and gold mines of Spain, their defeat of Philip and Perseus, and their capture of Antiochus the Great, who came with a hundred and twenty elephants, was taken alive, and had to pay tribute, give hostages and surrender India, Media and Lydia. They crushed the Greeks, they make and depose kings, and none of them wears a crown or purple. Three hundred and twenty men sit in their senate house daily.
1MA 8:16-32	The report adds that Rome puts one man in charge each year, with no envy among them. Judas sends Eupolemus and Jason to Rome to make a treaty. The senate agrees and writes it on tablets of brass: each side will aid the other in war and supply no food, arms, money or ships to the other's enemies, and either party may amend the terms. Rome also writes to Demetrius, asking why he oppresses their friends the Jews, and threatening war.
1MA 9:1-24	Demetrius sends Bacchides and Alcimus into Judah again. Judas camps at Elasa with three thousand chosen men against twenty thousand foot and two thousand horse; most slip away, leaving eight hundred. He refuses to flee, saying he will not leave a reproach against his glory. The battle runs from morning to evening; Judas breaks the right wing and pursues it, but the left wing closes behind him and he falls. Jonathan and Simon bury him at Modin, and Israel mourns.
1MA 9:25-50	Bacchides puts the ungodly in charge of the country and hunts down Judas's friends. They choose Jonathan as leader in his brother's place, and Bacchides seeks to kill him, so Jonathan withdraws to the wilderness. The children of Jambri seize his brother John and his baggage, so Jonathan and Simon ambush a Jambri wedding party and turn the marriage into mourning. Cornered at the Jordan, Jonathan strikes at Bacchides, who turns away, and the company swims the river.
1MA 9:51-73	Bacchides garrisons the fortresses and holds the sons of the chief men hostage. Alcimus orders the wall of the inner court pulled down, undoing the work of the prophets, and mid-demolition he is struck with a palsy, loses his speech, and dies in torment. Bacchides goes home and the land rests two years. The lawless call him back; Jonathan raids the countryside while Simon burns the siege engines at Bethbasi. Beaten, Bacchides makes peace, returns the captives, and never comes back.
1MA 10:1-32	Alexander Epiphanes takes Ptolemais and claims the throne, so Demetrius writes to Jonathan first, authorising him to raise an army and releasing the hostages in the citadel. Jonathan rebuilds the walls of Jerusalem, and the foreigners abandon Bacchides' strongholds. Alexander then appoints Jonathan high priest and King's Friend, sending a purple robe and a crown of gold; Jonathan puts on the holy garments at the feast of tabernacles. Demetrius answers with a longer offer still, cancelling tributes, salt duties and crown taxes, freeing Jerusalem, and yielding the citadel.
1MA 10:33-58	Demetrius's offer continues: captives freed, feasts and sabbaths exempt from exaction, thirty thousand Jews enrolled and paid as royal troops under their own officers and laws, three Samaritan districts annexed to Judea, Ptolemais given to the sanctuary, and building costs met from royal revenue. Jonathan and the people give it no credence, remembering the harm he had done, and stay with Alexander. The two kings join battle; Alexander's army flees, but Demetrius falls that day. Alexander then marries Ptolemy's daughter Cleopatra at Ptolemais with great pomp.
1MA 10:59-89	Alexander summons Jonathan to Ptolemais, clothes him in purple and seats him beside himself; his accusers flee, and he is made a Chief Friend and governor. Demetrius's son comes from Crete, and his general Apollonius taunts Jonathan to come down from the mountains into the plain. Jonathan takes Joppa, then meets Apollonius at Azotus, where hidden horsemen surround him and throw darts all day until they tire. Simon breaks the phalanx; about eight thousand die, and Jonathan is given Ekron.
1MA 11:1-26	Ptolemy of Egypt enters Syria with peaceful words and garrisons every city he passes. At Azotus he is shown the burned temple of Dagon and the dead as a complaint against Jonathan, and says nothing. He then takes his daughter back from Alexander, gives her to Demetrius, and wears the diadems of both Egypt and Asia. Alexander is routed, flees to Arabia, and Zabdiel the Arabian sends his head to Ptolemy, who dies three days later. Demetrius reigns; Jonathan is summoned to Ptolemais and honoured.
1MA 11:27-48	Demetrius confirms Jonathan's high priesthood and, for three hundred talents, frees Judea and the three Samaritan districts from tribute, tolls and crown taxes, in a letter posted on the holy mount. Feeling secure, he dismisses his own forces, who hate him for it. Tryphon begins working to put Alexander's young son Antiochus on the throne. Jonathan asks Demetrius to clear the citadel; Demetrius asks for troops instead, and Jonathan's three thousand men save him when a hundred and twenty thousand citizens of Antioch rise to kill him.
1MA 11:49-74	The city surrenders, and the Jews return to Jerusalem with spoil and honour. Demetrius, secure again, breaks every promise he made to Jonathan. Tryphon returns with the young Antiochus, wins over the troops Demetrius dismissed, routs him, and takes Antioch and the elephants. The boy king confirms Jonathan and makes Simon captain of the coast. Jonathan burns the suburbs of Gaza until it yields. At Hazor his army is ambushed and flees but for two captains; Jonathan prays and rallies to win.
1MA 12:1-28	Jonathan sends envoys to Rome to renew the friendship, and writes to the Spartans too, citing an old letter claiming the Spartans and the Jews are kindred, both of the stock of Abraham.
