His letter says the Jews need nothing, having the holy books, but do not wish to become estranged, and that they remember the Spartans in their sacrifices. Demetrius's princes return with a larger army; Jonathan meets them at Hamath, learns from spies of a planned night attack, and keeps his men armed and watching.
1MA 12:29-53	The enemy slip away in the night leaving their fires burning, and Jonathan does not know until morning; he pursues, fails to catch them, and raids the Zabadaean Arabs instead. Simon takes Joppa before Demetrius's men can. They raise the walls of Jerusalem and build a mound to cut the citadel off from the city. Tryphon, wanting the crown, greets Jonathan warmly at Bethshan, persuades him to send his army home and come to Ptolemais with a thousand men, then shuts the gates and seizes him. Israel mourns.
1MA 13:1-29	Simon calls Jerusalem together, says his whole family has died for Israel and he is left alone, and promises to avenge the nation; the people make him leader in place of Judas and Jonathan. He finishes the walls and takes Joppa. Tryphon demands a hundred talents and two of Jonathan's sons for his release; Simon knows it is deceit but pays anyway. Tryphon keeps Jonathan, is turned back by a snowfall, and kills him at Bascama. Simon buries him at Modin.
1MA 13:30-53	Tryphon murders the young king Antiochus and takes the crown himself. Simon sends Demetrius a golden crown and a palm branch; Demetrius grants immunity and confirms the strongholds. The Gentile yoke is lifted and contracts are dated to the first year of Simon. He takes Gazara by siege engine and cleanses it of idols. The starving citadel surrenders, and Simon enters it with palm branches, harps and hymns, making the day an annual feast. His son John is made commander.
1MA 14:1-27	Demetrius goes into Media for reinforcements and is taken alive and imprisoned. Under Simon the land has rest: Joppa becomes a harbour, the fields yield, old men sit talking in the streets, and every man sits under his vine and fig tree with none to make him afraid. Rome and Sparta, hearing Jonathan is dead and Simon has succeeded him, write on tablets of brass to renew the alliance. Simon sends Numenius to Rome with a gold shield of a thousand pound weight.
1MA 14:28-49	The decree is read at a great assembly at Asaramel. It recites what Mattathias's sons did and how Simon armed the nation at his own expense and fortified Bethsura, Joppa and Gazara. The people make Simon leader and high priest forever, until a faithful prophet arises, with charge of the sanctuary, the country, the arms and the strongholds. No one may contradict him, convene an assembly without him, or wear purple. It is engraved on brass in the sanctuary, with copies in the treasury.
1MA 15:1-22	Antiochus, son of Demetrius, writes from the isles to Simon, confirming every remission earlier kings granted, releasing all debts, leaving Jerusalem and the sanctuary free, and letting him keep his arms, strongholds and his own coinage. He lands, the armies desert to him, and he besieges Tryphon at Dor by land and sea with a hundred and twenty thousand men. Numenius returns from Rome with a letter telling the kings and countries not to harm the Jews and to hand fugitives over to Simon.
1MA 15:23-41	The Roman letter is copied to Sparta, Delos, Rhodes, Cyprus, Cyrene and other places. Simon sends Antiochus two thousand picked men with silver and gold; Antiochus refuses them and repudiates every agreement, sending Athenobius to demand Joppa, Gazara and the citadel, or a thousand talents. Athenobius sees Simon's gold and silver plate and is amazed. Simon answers that they hold only their fathers' inheritance, and offers a hundred talents for the two towns. Athenobius leaves in a rage. Cendebaeus is made coastal commander and raids Judea.
1MA 16:1-24	John brings word of Cendebaeus's raids, and Simon, now old, hands the war to his sons Judas and John with twenty thousand men. At the brook the troops hesitate, so John crosses first and they follow; Cendebaeus is routed and about two thousand fall. Ptolemy son of Abubus, Simon's son in law and captain of Jericho, invites Simon and two sons to a banquet at Dok and kills them there. Men sent to kill John at Gazara arrive too late; he is warned and kills them first.
2CH 1:1-17	Solomon, established as king, leads the assembly to the high place at Gibeon, where the Tent of Meeting and the bronze altar Bezalel made still stand, and offers a thousand burnt offerings. That night God tells him to ask for anything. Solomon asks for wisdom and knowledge to govern so large a people. Because he did not ask for wealth, honour or long life, God grants wisdom and adds riches beyond any king before or after. Solomon returns to Jerusalem and accumulates chariots, horses and silver.
2CH 2:1-18	Solomon determines to build a house for God and a palace for himself, and musters a workforce of seventy thousand carriers, eighty thousand quarrymen and three thousand six hundred overseers. He writes to Huram of Tyre asking for timber and a master craftsman, admitting heaven itself cannot contain God and the house can only be a place to burn incense. Huram replies in writing, blesses Israel's God, and sends Huram-abi, whose mother was of Dan, promising timber floated by sea to Joppa. The labourers are resident foreigners, counted at 153,600.
2CH 3:1-17	Solomon begins building on Mount Moriah, where God appeared to David, on Ornan's threshing floor, in his reign's fourth year. The house measures sixty cubits by twenty, with a porch as wide as the house. Cypress ceilings and walls are overlaid with gold from Parvaim and carved with cherubim, the most holy place alone taking six hundred talents; the nails weigh fifty shekels of gold. Two carved cherubim stand with wings spanning twenty cubits. A veil of blue, purple and crimson hangs, the two pillars named Jachin and Boaz.
2CH 4:1-22	The furnishings are made: a bronze altar twenty cubits square, and a cast metal sea ten cubits across resting on twelve oxen facing outward in four directions, holding three thousand baths. Ten basins serve for washing offerings, while the sea is for the priests. Ten gold lampstands and ten tables stand in the temple, with courts and bronze-plated doors outside. Huram casts the bronze work in the clay ground between Succoth and Zeredah, in quantities too great to weigh, and Solomon makes the golden altar, vessels and doors.
2CH 5:1-14	With the work finished, Solomon stores David's dedicated silver and gold in the treasuries, then gathers the elders and tribal heads at the seventh-month feast to bring up the ark from Zion. The Levites carry it into the most holy place beneath the cherubim's wings, its poles long enough to be seen from in front of the sanctuary. The ark holds only the two tablets from Horeb. As trumpeters and singers sound as one, praising God's goodness, a cloud fills the house so the priests cannot stand to minister.
2CH 6:1-22	Solomon addresses the assembly, recalling that God chose Jerusalem and chose David, that David wanted to build the house but was told his son would do it, and that the promise is now kept, the ark set in place. He stands on a bronze platform in the court, kneels before all Israel and spreads his hands toward heaven. His prayer acknowledges that the highest heavens cannot contain God, let alone this house, and asks that God's eyes stay open toward it and prayers made here be heard.
2CH 6:23-42	Solomon lists the cases he wants heard: disputes settled by oath, defeat in war, drought, famine, plague, blight and locusts, and any private grief a person brings. He asks that the foreigner who comes from far off for God's name be answered too, so all peoples learn it. He asks that armies sent out be heard, and that exiles who repent in a distant land and pray toward this place be forgiven, since no one is without sin. He closes by asking God to enter his resting place.
2CH 7:1-22	Fire falls from heaven and consumes the offerings, and God's glory so fills the house the priests cannot enter. The people bow on the pavement and give thanks. Solomon sacrifices twenty-two thousand cattle and a hundred twenty thousand sheep, consecrating the court's middle since the bronze altar cannot hold it all, and keeps a seven-day feast. God appears to him by night, accepts the house, and promises that if his people humble themselves and pray he will heal their land, but warns other gods will make this temple a byword.
2CH 8:1-18	Twenty years on, with both houses finished, Solomon rebuilds the cities Huram had given him and settles Israelites in them. He takes Hamath Zobah, builds Tadmor and store cities, fortifies both Beth Horons, and conscripts the surviving Canaanites as forced labour while making no Israelite a slave. Pharaoh's daughter is moved out of David's house because the ark has made it holy. Solomon keeps the appointed feasts and the divisions of priests and Levites as David commanded. Huram's sailors bring him four hundred fifty talents of gold from Ophir.
2CH 9:1-31	The queen of Sheba arrives with a caravan to test Solomon with hard questions, and he answers all of them. Seeing his wisdom, his palace, his table and his officials, she says the half was not told her, and gives a hundred and twenty talents of gold and unmatched spices. Solomon's annual gold income is six hundred and sixty-six talents. He makes gold shields, an ivory throne with twelve lions on six steps, and receives tribute from every king. He reigns forty years and Rehoboam succeeds him.
2CH 10:1-19	All Israel gathers at Shechem to make Rehoboam king and asks him to lighten the burden Solomon laid on them. He asks for three days, and the elders who served Solomon advise kindness. Rehoboam rejects their advice for that of the young men he grew up with and answers roughly: his little finger is thicker than his father's waist, and he will use scorpions where his father used whips. The northern tribes walk out, stone the officer in charge of forced labour, and Rehoboam flees to Jerusalem.
2CH 11:1-23	Rehoboam masses a hundred and eighty thousand men to retake the north, but the prophet Shemaiah tells him not to fight his brothers, and the army goes home. He fortifies fifteen cities across Judah and Benjamin and stocks them with food, oil, wine and weapons. The priests and Levites abandon their pasture lands and come to him, because Jeroboam has thrown them out and appointed his own priests for the calves and goat idols. Rehoboam takes eighteen wives and sixty concubines, favouring Maacah, whose son Abijah he names heir.
2CH 12:1-16	Once secure, Rehoboam abandons the law and all Israel with him. In his fifth year Shishak of Egypt invades with twelve hundred chariots and troops from Libya, Sukkiim and Ethiopia, taking the fortified cities of Judah. Shemaiah tells the king and princes that God has left them to Shishak, and they humble themselves, saying God is righteous. Their punishment is reduced to servitude rather than destruction. Shishak strips the temple and palace treasuries, including Solomon's gold shields, which Rehoboam replaces with bronze ones. He reigns seventeen years.
2CH 13:1-22	Abijah of Judah faces Jeroboam with four hundred thousand men against eight hundred thousand. From Mount Zemaraim he addresses the northern army: the kingdom was given to David by a covenant of salt, Jeroboam rebelled against his own master, and the north drove out Aaron's priests to ordain anyone who brought a bull and seven rams for gods that are no gods. Ambushed front and rear, Judah cries out, the priests sound trumpets, and Israel is routed, five hundred thousand killed. Abijah takes Bethel and other towns; Jeroboam never recovers.
2CH 14:1-15	Asa succeeds Abijah and the land is quiet ten years. He removes the foreign altars, high places, pillars and Asherah poles, orders Judah to seek God, and uses the peace to build walled cities with towers and gates. His army numbers three hundred thousand from Judah and two hundred eighty thousand from Benjamin. Zerah the Ethiopian invades with a million men and three hundred chariots. Asa prays that God alone can help the weak against the mighty, and the Ethiopians are routed and pursued to Gerar, with great plunder taken.
2CH 15:1-19	The Spirit comes on Azariah son of Oded, who tells Asa that God is with him while he is with God, and that Israel long went without a teaching priest until distress drove them back. Encouraged, Asa purges the idols and repairs the altar. He gathers Judah, Benjamin and defectors from Ephraim, Manasseh and Simeon, sacrifices seven hundred cattle and seven thousand sheep, and they covenant with shouting and trumpets to seek God on pain of death. He deposes his grandmother Maacah and burns her Asherah image at the Kidron.
2CH 16:1-14	In his thirty-sixth year Asa faces Baasha fortifying Ramah to blockade Judah. Rather than pray, he empties the temple and palace treasuries to buy Ben Hadad of Syria out of his treaty with Israel. Syria raids the north, Baasha withdraws, and Asa carts Ramah's stone away to build Geba and Mizpah. Hanani the seer says he has forfeited a greater victory by relying on Syria, and will now have wars. Asa jails him and oppresses others, then, diseased in his feet, consults only physicians and dies in his forty-first year.
2CH 17:1-19	Jehoshaphat succeeds Asa, garrisons the fortified cities of Judah and the towns Asa took from Ephraim, and refuses the Baals. In his third year he sends five princes, nine Levites and two priests through the cities of Judah to teach, carrying the book of the law. Surrounding kingdoms fear him and make no war; Philistines pay tribute and Arabians bring seven thousand seven hundred rams and as many goats. He builds fortresses and store cities, and musters over a million men, one commander noted for volunteering himself.
2CH 18:1-16	Jehoshaphat, rich and honoured, allies himself with Ahab, who feasts him and persuades him to join an attack on Ramoth Gilead. Jehoshaphat asks that God be consulted first. Ahab's four hundred prophets all promise victory, and Zedekiah makes iron horns to act it out. Jehoshaphat asks whether there is another prophet, and Ahab names Micaiah, whom he says never prophesies anything good about him. Micaiah first echoes the others, then, pressed for the truth, says he saw Israel scattered on the mountains like sheep without a shepherd.
2CH 18:17-34	Micaiah describes seeing God on his throne asking who will entice Ahab to fall at Ramoth Gilead, and a spirit volunteering to be a lying spirit in his prophets' mouths. Zedekiah strikes him, and Ahab jails him on bread and water until he returns safely, which Micaiah says will not happen. Ahab enters battle disguised while Jehoshaphat wears his robes, and Syrian chariots, ordered to target only Israel's king, chase Jehoshaphat until they see their mistake. A random arrow finds the gap in Ahab's armour, and he dies at sunset.
2CH 19:1-11	Returning safely to Jerusalem, Jehoshaphat is met by Jehu son of Hanani, who rebukes him for helping the wicked and loving those who hate God, though he grants that good has been found in him for removing the Asherah poles. Jehoshaphat tours the country from Beersheba to Ephraim bringing people back to God, and appoints judges in every fortified city, warning them that they judge for God, who takes no bribes and shows no partiality. In Jerusalem he sets priests, Levites and family heads over religious and royal cases.
2CH 20:1-19	Moab, Ammon and others march on Judah and are reported already at En Gedi. Alarmed, Jehoshaphat proclaims a national fast and prays in the temple court, reminding God that a cry from this house would be heard, and admitting Judah has no strength and does not know what to do. The whole nation stands there with wives and children. The Spirit comes on the Levite Jahaziel, who says the battle is God's, not theirs: go down tomorrow, stand still and watch. They bow, and the Levites praise loudly.
2CH 20:20-37	Marching out to Tekoa, Jehoshaphat tells Judah to believe God and his prophets, and puts singers in front of the army praising God's enduring love. As the singing starts, the invaders turn on each other, Ammon and Moab destroying Seir and then one another, and Judah finds only corpses. Gathering the plunder takes three days. They bless God in a valley they name Beracah and return with music. Later Jehoshaphat partners with Ahaziah of Israel to build ships, and a prophet says his works are destroyed; the ships are wrecked.
2CH 21:1-20	Jehoshaphat leaves his other sons wealth and fortified cities, but the kingdom to Jehoram, who secures it by killing every one of his brothers. Married to Ahab's daughter, he builds high places and leads Judah astray. Edom and Libnah revolt. A letter arrives from Elijah predicting plague on his family and a bowel disease that will kill him slowly. Philistines and Arabians carry off his wives and sons, leaving only the youngest. After two years his bowels fall out. No funeral fire is made for him, and nobody misses him.
2CH 22:1-12	Jerusalem crowns Ahaziah, the only surviving son, whose mother Athaliah, Omri's daughter, advises him badly, as does the rest of Ahab's house. He joins Joram of Israel against Hazael at Ramoth Gilead, then visits the wounded Joram at Jezreel, and is there when Jehu arrives executing judgment on Ahab's family. Jehu's men kill Ahaziah's relatives, then find him hiding in Samaria and kill him, though they bury him for Jehoshaphat's sake. Athaliah destroys the royal line, but Jehoshabeath hides the infant Joash in the temple six years.
2CH 23:1-21	In the seventh year the priest Jehoiada moves. He binds five captains by covenant, gathers Levites and family heads from every city, and stations them in thirds at the temple thresholds, the palace and the foundation gate, arming them from David's own weapons stored in the house. Joash is crowned, given the testimony and anointed. Athaliah hears the noise, cries treason, and is taken outside and killed at the horse gate rather than in the temple. The people tear down the Baal temple and kill his priest Mattan.
2CH 24:1-27	Joash is seven at his accession and does right while Jehoiada lives. He orders the temple repaired, and when the Levites are slow, a chest is set at the gate for Moses' tax; the people fill it repeatedly, and masons restore the house, the surplus becoming vessels. Jehoiada dies at a hundred and thirty and is buried among the kings. Joash then listens to the princes, returns to idols, and has Jehoiada's son Zechariah stoned. A small Syrian force defeats Judah, and his own servants kill him in bed.
2CH 25:1-14	Amaziah does right but not wholeheartedly. He executes his father's assassins but spares their children, citing Moses' law that each dies for his own sin. Mustering three hundred thousand men, he hires a hundred thousand Israelites too, for a hundred talents of silver, then dismisses them when a prophet says God is not with Israel; told he loses the money, he hears God can give more. The dismissed troops raid Judah in fury. Amaziah kills ten thousand Edomites, throws ten thousand more off a cliff, and brings home their gods.
2CH 25:15-28	A prophet asks Amaziah why he consults gods that could not save their own people, and the king threatens to have him struck down. Emboldened by Edom, he challenges Joash of Israel, who replies with a fable about a thistle proposing marriage to a cedar and being trampled by a passing animal. Amaziah attacks anyway and is captured at Beth Shemesh. Joash breaks four hundred cubits of Jerusalem's wall, empties the temple and palace treasuries, and takes hostages. Years later a conspiracy chases Amaziah to Lachish and kills him there.
2CH 26:1-23	Uzziah becomes king at sixteen and prospers while he seeks God under Zechariah's instruction. He breaks the walls of Gath, Jabneh and Ashdod, takes tribute from Ammon, builds towers and cisterns, keeps farmers and vineyards because he loved the soil, arms an army of over three hundred thousand, and installs engines on the walls for shooting arrows and stones. Strong, he grows proud and enters the temple to burn incense. Eighty priests confront him; leprosy breaks out on his forehead, and he lives isolated until his death.
2CH 27:1-9	Jotham reigns sixteen years and does right as his father had done, with the pointed exception that he does not enter the temple. The people, however, remain corrupt. He builds the upper gate of the temple, extends the wall of Ophel, and puts cities in the hill country and forts and towers in the forests. He defeats the Ammonites, who pay him a hundred talents of silver and ten thousand cors each of wheat and barley for three years running. He grew strong because he ordered his ways before God.
2CH 28:1-27	Ahaz makes images for the Baals and burns his own children in the valley of Hinnom. Syria carries off captives, and Pekah of Israel kills a hundred twenty thousand Judeans in a day and takes two hundred thousand women and children. The prophet Oded stops the army at Samaria, and four Ephraimite leaders clothe, feed and carry the captives back to Jericho. Ahaz appeals to Assyria, which takes his temple treasure and gives no help. He sacrifices to Damascus's gods, breaks up the temple vessels, and shuts its doors.
2CH 29:1-18	Hezekiah becomes king at twenty-five and in the first month of his first year reopens and repairs the temple doors his father had shut. He assembles the priests and Levites and tells them their fathers turned their backs on the sanctuary, put out the lamps and stopped the offerings, which is why Judah has been made a horror and its families taken captive. He intends to make a covenant. Fourteen named Levites gather their brothers and clean the house, carrying the refuse to the Kidron. The work takes sixteen days.
2CH 29:19-36	The Levites report that even the vessels Ahaz discarded are restored and set before the altar. Hezekiah gathers the princes and offers seven bulls, rams, lambs and goats as a sin offering for the kingdom, atonement made for all Israel. The Levites take up David's instruments and the priests the trumpets, and the song begins with the burnt offering. The assembly brings seventy bulls, a hundred rams, two hundred lambs, plus six hundred cattle and three thousand sheep. Too few priests are consecrated, so the Levites help flay the animals.
2CH 30:1-27	Hezekiah writes to Israel and Judah, including Ephraim and Manasseh, calling them to keep Passover in Jerusalem in the second month, since too few priests were ready in the first. His couriers are mocked across the north, though some from Asher, Manasseh and Zebulun come. The people destroy Jerusalem's incense altars, and the Levites slaughter the lambs for those who are unclean. Hezekiah prays that God pardon everyone who seeks him though not purified, and God heals them. The feast runs a second week, joy unmatched since Solomon.
2CH 31:1-21	After the feast the people fan out to smash pillars, Asherah poles and high places throughout Judah, Benjamin, Ephraim and Manasseh. Hezekiah restores the priestly and Levitical divisions, funds the daily offerings from his own property, and orders Jerusalem to give the priests their portion. The tithes of grain, wine, oil, honey and livestock pile up in heaps from the third month to the seventh, so storerooms are prepared and Conaniah put in charge. Distribution is organised by genealogy, reaching the priests' villages and children from three years upward.
2CH 32:1-17	Sennacherib of Assyria invades and camps against Judah's fortified cities. Hezekiah has the springs outside Jerusalem stopped so the Assyrians find no water, rebuilds the broken wall with a second wall outside it, strengthens the Millo and makes weapons. He tells the assembled people that Assyria has an arm of flesh while they have God to fight for them. Sennacherib, then at Lachish, sends envoys and letters arguing that no nation's god has saved anyone from him, and that Hezekiah removing the high places has weakened Judah's own worship.
2CH 32:18-33	The envoys shout in Hebrew to the people on the wall, talking about Jerusalem's God as though he were made by hands. Hezekiah and the prophet Isaiah pray, and an angel destroys the Assyrian commanders and troops; Sennacherib goes home shamed and is killed by his own sons in his god's temple. Gifts pour into Jerusalem. Hezekiah falls mortally ill, is given a sign, then grows proud, and only humbling himself averts wrath. He diverts the Gihon spring westward and is left to himself when Babylon's envoys arrive.
2CH 33:1-25	Manasseh becomes king at twelve and reigns fifty-five years, rebuilding the high places, raising altars to the Baals and the host of heaven in the temple courts, burning his children in Hinnom, and practising sorcery. Assyria's captains take him in fetters to Babylon, where he humbles himself and prays, and God restores him to Jerusalem. He removes the idols, rebuilds the altar and orders Judah to serve God, though the people still sacrifice at high places. His son Amon repeats his sins unrepentant and is murdered by his own servants.
2CH 34:1-17	Josiah is eight at his accession. At sixteen he begins seeking God, and at twenty he purges Judah of high places, Asherah poles and images, grinding them to dust, scattering it on the graves of those who sacrificed to them, and burning the priests' bones on their altars, the purge reaching north to Naphtali. In his eighteenth year he sends officials to repair the temple with money the Levites collected from Judah and the north. While the money is brought out, Hilkiah finds the book of the law.
2CH 34:18-33	Shaphan reads the book aloud and Josiah tears his clothes, sending his officials to inquire of God about the curses in it. They go to the prophetess Huldah, who confirms that disaster will come on the place and will not be quenched, but tells Josiah that because his heart was tender and he wept, he will be gathered to his grave in peace and will not see it. Josiah assembles everyone, reads the covenant aloud himself, and pledges to keep it. Judah serves God for the rest of his life.
2CH 35:1-27	Josiah keeps Passover on the fourteenth of the first month, restores the priests to their offices, and tells the Levites to set the ark in the house since carrying it is no longer their burden. He provides thirty thousand lambs and goats and three thousand bulls from his own property, the officials adding thousands more. The Levites flay the animals and prepare portions for the priests, busy until night. No Passover like it since Samuel. Josiah later intercepts Neco at Megiddo, ignores his warning, and dies of an arrow wound.
2CH 36:1-23	Jehoahaz reigns three months before Egypt deposes him and installs his brother Jehoiakim, who is later bound in fetters by Nebuchadnezzar, some temple vessels going to Babylon. Jehoiachin lasts three months and ten days, then Zedekiah rebels despite his oath and refuses to humble himself before Jeremiah. The priests and people defile the temple and mock the prophets until there is no remedy. Babylon kills, plunders, burns the house and the walls, and deports the survivors for seventy Sabbath years. Then Cyrus of Persia proclaims that the house be rebuilt.
2JN 1:1-13	The elder writes to a chosen lady and her children, glad that some of them are walking in truth, and asks them to keep the old commandment to love one another. His real concern is a group of deceivers now circulating who do not confess that Jesus Christ came in the flesh; he calls this the Antichrist. Anyone arriving without that teaching is not to be taken into the house or greeted. He has more to say but prefers to come in person rather than use paper and ink.
2MA 1:1-18	Two letters from the Jews of Jerusalem to those in Egypt. The first prays for them, recalls the troubles that began when Jason revolted, and urges them to keep the feast of the month Chislev. The second, addressed to Aristobulus, reports the death of Antiochus: he entered the temple of Nanaea in Persia pretending to marry the goddess to take her treasure as a dowry, and the priests shut him in and cut him and his companions to pieces. They write so Egypt keeps the purification too.
2MA 1:19-36	The letter explains the festival's origin. Before the exile the priests hid the altar fire in a dry well. Generations later Nehemiah sent their descendants to look, and they found not fire but thick water. Poured over the sacrifice, it blazed up when the sun came out. The priests sang, and the leftover water set great stones alight. The Persian king enclosed the site, and they called the substance Nephthar, meaning cleansing, though most call it Nephthai.
2MA 2:1-17	The records add that Jeremiah told the exiles to take some of the fire, warning them not to be led astray by images of gold and silver. Warned by God, he hid the tabernacle, the ark and the altar of incense in a chamber in the rock, and told those who tried to mark the way that the place would stay unknown until God gathers his people. The letter recalls fire falling for Moses and Solomon, notes Nehemiah's library and Judas's collected writings, and offers to lend them.
2MA 2:18-32	The letters end in hope of being gathered to the holy place. The author then introduces his own work: the deeds of Judas Maccabaeus and his brothers, the purification of the temple, the wars against Antiochus Epiphanes and his son Eupator, and the manifestations from heaven, all condensed from the five books of Jason of Cyrene into one. He says he has aimed at brevity so readers will enjoy and remember it, compares abridging to decorating a house another man built, and admits the work cost him sweat and sleeplessness.
2MA 3:1-19	While Onias is high priest, Jerusalem is at peace and even the kings honour the temple, Seleucus paying for the sacrifices from his own revenues. Simon, the temple guardian, quarrels with Onias over the city market and tells the governor Apollonius that the treasury holds untold money. The king sends his chancellor Heliodorus to seize it. Onias explains the deposits belong to widows and orphans and to Hyrcanus son of Tobias, but Heliodorus insists. The city despairs, priests prostrate themselves, and women in sackcloth fill the streets.
2MA 3:20-40	As Heliodorus reaches the treasury, a horse with a terrible rider in golden armour rushes at him and strikes him with its forefeet, and two young men scourge him without stopping. He collapses and is carried out on a litter. His friends beg Onias to pray for him, and Onias, afraid the king will suspect treachery, offers a sacrifice. The young men reappear and tell Heliodorus he lives for Onias's sake, and must proclaim God's majesty. He does, and tells the king to send his enemies to Jerusalem.
2MA 4:1-25	Simon slanders Onias as the author of the trouble, murders follow, and Onias appeals to the king. When Seleucus dies and Antiochus Epiphanes succeeds, Jason buys the high priesthood from his brother Onias for three hundred and sixty talents, plus more to build a Greek gymnasium and register Jerusalem as citizens of Antioch. Priests abandon the altar for the wrestling ground. Three years later Menelaus outbids Jason by three hundred talents and takes the office.
2MA 4:26-50	Jason, who supplanted his brother, is supplanted in turn and flees to the Ammonites. Menelaus pays the king nothing, and is summoned. He bribes the deputy Andronicus with stolen temple vessels; when Onias exposes this from sanctuary at Daphne, Andronicus lures him out with oaths and kills him. Antiochus weeps, strips Andronicus of his purple and executes him where the murder was done. Lysimachus robs the temple until a crowd kills him by the treasury. Menelaus buys his acquittal, and his accusers are put to death.
2MA 5:1-14	During Antiochus's second invasion of Egypt, horsemen in gold-worked robes appear in the sky over Jerusalem for nearly forty days, charging and wheeling in battle array. On a false rumour that the king is dead, Jason attacks the city with a thousand men, slaughters his own countrymen, fails to regain the office, and flees again; he dies an exile, unmourned and unburied. Antiochus, thinking Judea in revolt, storms the city and orders three days of killing: eighty thousand destroyed, and as many sold as killed.
2MA 5:15-27	With Menelaus as his guide, Antiochus enters the temple and carries off the sacred vessels and the gifts other kings had dedicated, eighteen hundred talents in all. The narrator says God turned his eye away for a time because of the nation's sins. Antiochus leaves Philip and Andronicus in charge, then sends Apollonius with twenty-two thousand men, who waits for the Sabbath and kills those who come out to watch. Judas withdraws to the mountains with nine others.
2MA 6:1-17	An old man of Athens is sent to force the Jews from their law and rename the temple for Jupiter Olympius. Nobody may keep the Sabbath, keep a feast, or admit to being a Jew. They are compelled to eat sacrificial meat monthly and wear ivy in procession for Bacchus. Two women who circumcised their children are thrown from the wall with their babies. Others hiding in caves to keep the Sabbath are burned alive. The author asks readers not to be discouraged: this is chastening, not destruction.
2MA 6:18-31	Eleazar, a leading scribe of ninety, is forced to eat swine's flesh. He spits it out and goes of his own accord to the instrument of torture. Old friends among the officials offer to substitute lawful meat so he can appear to comply, but he refuses. Dying under the scourge, he says he could have been delivered but gladly suffers in body out of fear of God. His death is left as an example of nobility.
2MA 7:1-21	Seven brothers and their mother are seized and scourged to make them eat swine's flesh. The eldest speaks for them and refuses; the king has him mutilated and fried alive in a pan while the rest watch. The second is scalped, saying the King of the world will raise them to eternal life. The third holds out his tongue and hands, saying heaven gave them and heaven will give them back. The others die the same way, and their mother watches all seven die in one day.
2MA 7:22-42	The mother tells her sons she did not give them their spirit or life, and the Creator will give both back. Antiochus, believing himself mocked, offers the youngest wealth and office to abandon his fathers' customs, and asks the mother to persuade him. She agrees, then tells her son in their own language to look at heaven and earth, know God made them out of nothing, and not fear the butcher. The boy refuses, says the king will not escape God, and dies. The mother dies last.
2MA 8:1-19	Judas gathers about six thousand men from the villages and prays for the oppressed people, the profaned sanctuary and the murdered infants. He raids by night, burning towns, and his courage is talked of everywhere. Philip appeals to Ptolemy, who sends Nicanor with twenty thousand men, planning to pay Rome's tribute by selling Jewish captives at ninety slaves for a talent. The fearful desert; the rest sell what they have and pray. Judas tells his men the enemy trust arms while they trust God.
2MA 8:20-36	Judas recalls a battle where six thousand overcame a hundred and twenty thousand, divides his army into four bands, reads the sacred book aloud, and gives the watchword The Help of God. Over nine thousand of Nicanor's men die and the rest flee; the Jews take the money the slave dealers had brought, and stop early for the Sabbath. Afterwards they share the spoil with the maimed, widows and orphans. Twenty thousand more of Timotheus's forces fall. Nicanor slips away to Antioch in disguise.
2MA 9:1-13	Antiochus, driven out of Persepolis after trying to rob a temple, hears at Ecbatana what happened to Nicanor and Timotheus. He orders his charioteer to hurry, swearing to make Jerusalem a Jewish graveyard. As he speaks, an incurable pain seizes his bowels. He will not stop, falls from the speeding chariot and is racked in every limb; worms swarm out of him, and the stench drives off his own army. Broken at last, he says a mortal should not be arrogant, and begins to make vows.
2MA 9:14-29	Antiochus vows to declare Jerusalem free, make the Jews equal to citizens of Athens, adorn the sanctuary at his own expense, and become a Jew himself, publishing God's power everywhere. When the pain does not stop he writes to the Jews as fellow citizens, wishing them well, explaining his illness, and appointing his son king, asking them to keep goodwill toward them both. He dies wretchedly in a strange land, and his foster brother Philip takes the body home, then flees to Ptolemy Philometor in Egypt.
2MA 10:1-19	Judas recovers the temple and the city, pulls down the alien altars in the marketplace, cleanses the sanctuary and builds a new altar, striking fire out of stones. Sacrifice resumes after two years on the twenty-fifth of Chislev, the day it was profaned, and they keep eight days like the feast of tabernacles with palms and hymns. Antiochus Eupator succeeds and appoints Lysias chancellor. Ptolemy Macron, accused of favouring the Jews, poisons himself. Gorgias hires mercenaries, and Judas storms the Idumaean strongholds, killing twenty thousand.
2MA 10:20-38	Simon's men take seventy thousand drachmas to let some of the besieged escape; Judas has them killed as traitors and takes the towers, destroying more than twenty thousand. Timotheus returns with foreign troops. The Jews pray in sackcloth, and in the battle five horsemen with golden bridles appear from heaven, shielding Judas with their own armour while shooting arrows and thunderbolts at the enemy. Twenty thousand five hundred foot and six hundred horse fall. Timotheus flees to Gazara, is found hiding in a cistern, and is killed.
2MA 11:1-18	Lysias, displeased, brings eighty thousand foot, all his cavalry and eighty elephants, meaning to settle Greeks in Jerusalem and sell the high priesthood every year. He presses Bethsuron hard. The Jews pray for a good angel, and a horseman in white with golden weapons appears at their head. Eleven thousand foot and sixteen hundred horse are killed and the rest flee; Lysias escapes in shameful flight. Concluding the Hebrews cannot be overcome, he opens terms acknowledging all their rights, and Judas accepts.
2MA 11:19-38	Four letters. Lysias promises to keep working for their good. Antiochus writes to Lysias that the Jews may have their temple back and live by their ancestral customs. He writes to the nation that any who return home by the thirtieth of Xanthicus have his friendship and their own food and laws, and adds that Menelaus is sent to encourage them. The Roman envoys consent, and ask for a delegation quickly.
2MA 12:1-22	After the treaty the Jews return to farming, but the local governors will not leave them alone. The men of Joppa invite their Jewish neighbours onto boats, take them out to sea and drown two hundred. Judas burns the harbour and boats by night, then does the same at Jamnia. An Arab force attacks and, beaten, asks for friendship and is granted it. Caspin, whose people jeer from the walls, is stormed with great slaughter. Timotheus's garrison loses ten thousand, and his army flees before Judas's vanguard.
2MA 12:23-45	Thirty thousand die in the pursuit. Timotheus, captured, talks his way free by pointing out he holds the relatives of many of his captors. Judas takes Carnion and Ephron, and thanks Scythopolis, who had treated their Jews well. After Pentecost he fights Gorgias, who escapes when a Thracian horseman disables the man dragging him off. Collecting their own dead, they find idols of Jamnia under every tunic, and conclude that is why they fell. Judas raises two thousand drachmas for a sin offering, looking to the resurrection.
2MA 13:1-26	Antiochus Eupator and Lysias march with a hundred and ten thousand foot, twenty-two elephants and three hundred scythed chariots. Menelaus joins them hoping for the government, and is instead sent to Beroea and thrown into a tower of ashes, dying without a grave. After three days of fasting, Judas raids the camp by night with the watchword Victory is God's, kills two thousand and brings down the chief elephant. The king fails at Bethsura, and news of Philip's revolt at Antioch makes the king swear peace and withdraw.
2MA 14:1-25	Demetrius son of Seleucus lands at Tripolis and takes the country. Alcimus, a former high priest who had defiled himself, tells the royal council that Judas will never let the kingdom be at peace. Demetrius appoints Nicanor, formerly master of the elephants, governor of Judea, with orders to kill Judas and install Alcimus. Nicanor, hearing how the Jews fight, prefers terms, and the two confer in state. He keeps Judas beside him, grows fond of him, and urges him to marry and settle, which Judas does.
2MA 14:26-46	Alcimus tells Demetrius the treaty has made the king's enemy Nicanor's successor. Ordered to send Judas to Antioch in chains, Nicanor turns harsh; Judas notices and hides. Nicanor goes to the temple, demands the priests hand him over, and swears otherwise he will level the place and raise a temple to Bacchus. Informed against Razis, an elder called Father of the Jews, he sends soldiers to take him. Surrounded, Razis falls on his sword, then throws himself from the wall, and dies calling on the Lord of life.
2MA 15:1-19	Nicanor plans to attack on the Sabbath. Told the day is hallowed, he asks whether a Sovereign in heaven commanded it and declares himself a sovereign on earth, but does not carry the plan out.  Judas encourages his men from the law and the prophets and tells them a dream: Onias the high priest praying for the people, and beside him Jeremiah, who hands Judas a golden sword as a gift from God. They resolve to attack at once.
2MA 15:20-39	As the elephants are posted and the cavalry drawn up, Judas prays that God send a good angel as he did against Sennacherib. Nicanor advances with trumpets, Judas with prayers, and thirty-five thousand of the enemy fall. Nicanor is found dead in his armour. Judas has his head and hand cut off and hung from the citadel. The thirteenth of Adar is decreed a yearly memorial. The author ends, comparing his style to wine mixed with water.
3JN 1:1-14	The elder writes to Gaius, praising him for supporting travelling brothers who set out for the Name and took nothing from the Gentiles, and asking him to send them on well. The complaint is about Diotrephes, who loves to be first, refuses to accept the elder's authority, spreads malicious talk, will not receive the brothers, and expels anyone who does. Demetrius, by contrast, is well spoken of by everyone. The elder hopes to come and speak face to face rather than write more.
3MA 1:1-29	Philopater marches to Raphia against Antiochus. A plot to kill him in his tent fails because Dositheus, a Jew who had abandoned his country's laws, hides him and puts another man in his place. Arsinoe rallies the ranks in tears, promising two minas of gold apiece, and Antiochus's men are beaten. Ptolemy then tours the cities, comes to Jerusalem, sacrifices, and insists on entering the sanctuary. Told only the high priest may go in, once a year, he refuses to accept it. The city fills with weeping crowds.
3MA 2:1-17	Simon the high priest kneels and prays. He addresses God as creator and judge of the proud, recalling the giants drowned by the flood, the Sodomites covered with fire and brimstone, and Pharaoh rolled over by the sea while Israel crossed safely. He recalls that God chose this city and promised to hear prayer offered in this house. He confesses that their many sins have brought them low, and asks that the holy place not be dishonoured by an insolent man, nor the people chastised by the profanity of others.
3MA 2:18-33	The prayer ends asking God to blot out their sins. God hears it, shakes the king like a reed and throws him to the pavement, paralysed and unable to speak. His guards carry him out. He recovers without repenting and leaves with threats. In Egypt he erects a pillar barring any who will not sacrifice from their own temple, ordering Jews registered among the common people and marked with the ivy leaf of Dionysus, and resisters killed. Most pay money to avoid registration.
3MA 3:1-16	The king's rage spreads from Alexandria to the Jews of the countryside, whom he orders collected and killed. Rumours circulate that the Jews keep others from the ordinances of the law; their separate worship and food make them look unsociable and hostile, though they had been loyal and well regarded. Greeks in the city sympathise but cannot help. The king then writes to his commanders, opening with his own good health, his successful Asian campaign, and his gifts to the temples, including that at Jerusalem.
3MA 3:17-30	The letter continues: the Jews received him in appearance only, forbade him the temple, and alone among nations lift their heads against kings. He offered them Alexandrian citizenship and admission to the rites, and they spurned it. Fearing treachery, he orders all Jews, with wives and children, sent to him in iron chains for a cruel death. Anyone sheltering a Jew is to be tortured to death with his household; informers receive the victim's property, freedom and a crown.
3MA 4:1-21	The decree is greeted with public rejoicing, and the Jews are driven from every town: old men stumbling along, brides dragged out unveiled, their husbands in halters instead of crowns. They are chained below decks, shipped to Schedia, and penned in the hippodrome in view of everyone entering the city. The whole nation is to be registered by name for execution, but after forty days the registrars report the task impossible: too many Jews, and the paper and pens run out. The narrator calls this providence.
AMO 1:1-15	Amos, a herdsman of Tekoa, speaks in the days of Uzziah and Jeroboam, two years before the earthquake. Yahweh roars from Zion and Carmel withers. A series of oracles follows, each opening with three transgressions and then four: Damascus for threshing Gilead with iron, Gaza and Tyre for deporting whole communities to Edom, Edom for pursuing his brother with the sword, and Ammon for ripping open the pregnant women of Gilead. Each is promised fire on its palaces.
AMO 2:1-16	The circuit continues with Moab, which burned the bones of Edom's king into lime, and Judah, which rejected Yahweh's law. Then it reaches Israel: selling the righteous for silver and the needy for a pair of shoes, trampling the poor, a man and his father using the same girl, lying beside altars on clothes taken in pledge, drinking the wine of those they fined. God recalls the exodus and the prophets they silenced. The bravest of the mighty will flee away naked.
AMO 3:1-15	Because God chose Israel alone of all the families of the earth, he will punish it for all its sins. A run of rhetorical questions insists nothing happens without a cause: no lion roars without prey, no bird falls where no snare is set, and God does nothing without revealing it to the prophets. Samaria hoards plunder in its palaces. What is rescued will be like two legs or a piece of an ear pulled from a lion's mouth, and the altars of Bethel and the ivory houses are struck.
AMO 4:1-13	The women of Samaria are addressed as cows of Bashan who crush the needy and tell their husbands to bring drinks; they will be taken away with hooks. Israel is told, sarcastically, to go to Bethel and sin. God then lists what he sent: hunger, withheld rain, blight and locusts, plague and sword, and an overthrow like Sodom, each followed by the same refrain, yet you have not returned to me. Israel is told to prepare to meet its God.
AMO 5:1-27	Amos raises a lamentation for Israel as a fallen virgin who will not rise, with a city that sent out a thousand left with a hundred. He tells them to seek Yahweh and live rather than Bethel or Gilgal, and to establish justice in the courts. They have built stone houses they will not live in. The day of Yahweh is darkness: a man flees a lion and meets a bear. God rejects their feasts and songs and asks instead for justice rolling like rivers.
AMO 6:1-14	Woe is pronounced on those at ease in Zion and secure in Samaria, who lie on beds of ivory, eat lambs from the flock, invent instruments like David, drink wine from bowls and use the best oils, but are not grieved over the ruin of Joseph. They will go into captivity first. God swears he detests Jacob's pride and will hand the city over; even ten men in one house will die. They boast of horns taken by their own strength, and a nation is raised against them.
AMO 7:1-17	Amos is shown locusts and then judgment by fire, and both times he pleads that Jacob is small, and both times Yahweh relents. The third vision is a plumb line set in the middle of Israel, and this one stands. Amaziah, priest of Bethel, reports Amos to Jeroboam and tells him to go earn his bread prophesying in Judah. Amos answers that he was no prophet but a herdsman and a farmer of sycamore figs, then delivers a sentence on Amaziah's own family.
AMO 8:1-14	Amos is shown a basket of summer fruit, and God says the end has come for Israel. The merchants are impatient for the new moon and the Sabbath to be over so they can sell again, shrinking the measure, enlarging the price, using false balances, buying the poor for a pair of shoes and selling the sweepings with the wheat. God will darken the sun at noon, turn feasts into mourning, and send a famine of hearing his words, which people will search for and not find.
AMO 9:1-15	Amos sees the Lord beside the altar ordering the temple struck so it falls on everyone's heads, and says that no hiding place works: Sheol, heaven, the top of Carmel, the bottom of the sea, or captivity. God reminds Israel that he also brought the Philistines from Caphtor and the Syrians from Kir. He will sift the house of Israel like grain in a sieve without losing a kernel. The book ends with David's fallen tent rebuilt, wine dripping from the mountains, and the people replanted for good.
ESG 1:1-5	The Greek text opens two years before the Hebrew one. Mordecai, a Benjaminite serving in the palace at Susa, dreams of thunder, earthquake and two great serpents rousing every nation against the nation of the just, until a little spring becomes a river and light rises. He keeps the dream and puzzles over it. He then overhears two royal eunuchs plotting against Ahasuerus, reports them, and they are executed. Haman is honoured and begins to seek harm against Mordecai over the affair.
ESG 1:6-22	The banquet is described in detail: linen and purple on marble pillars, gold and silver couches on emerald and pearl, and a jewelled cup valued at thirty thousand talents. Drinking is by the king's wish rather than fixed rule. Vashti holds her own feast for the women. On the seventh day the king, merry, sends seven chamberlains to bring her crowned before the guests, and she refuses. His advisers warn every wife in the empire will hear of it, and Memucan proposes replacing her by decree.
ESG 2:1-23	With the king's anger settled, his servants propose gathering beautiful young women from every province. Mordecai, a captive from Jerusalem, has raised his orphaned cousin Esther as his own. Brought to Hegai the keeper of the women, she pleases him and receives her portion and seven maids, but reveals nothing of her family as Mordecai instructed. After twelve months of purification, six with myrrh and six with spices, she goes in, and the king crowns her. Mordecai uncovers a plot by two bodyguards, and the record is written.
ESG 3:1-15	The king promotes Haman above all his friends and orders everyone to bow. Mordecai refuses and explains he is a Jew. Enraged, Haman decides to destroy every Jew in the empire and casts lots for the day. He tells the king of a scattered people whose laws differ and who disobey, offering ten thousand talents of silver. The king gives him his ring and tells him to keep the money. The decree orders every Jew killed with wives and children. The king and Haman drink; the city is troubled.
ESG 4:1-16	Mordecai tears his clothes, puts on sackcloth and ashes, and cries through the city that a people who have done no wrong is to be destroyed. He stops at the gate, since sackcloth may not enter. Esther sends clothes, which he refuses, then sends Hathach, who brings back the decree and Mordecai's charge to plead with the king. She replies that anyone entering uncalled dies unless the sceptre is held out, and she has not been summoned in thirty days. Mordecai answers she may have been made queen for this.
ESG 4:17-17	Mordecai prays, insisting his refusal to bow was not pride and that he would gladly have kissed Haman's feet to save Israel, but would not set a man's glory above God's. He asks that mourning be turned to gladness. Esther strips off her royal dress, fills her hair with ashes, and prays that God help someone who has no other helper. She confesses Israel honoured other gods, asks for persuasive speech before the lion, and says she has not eaten at Haman's table nor drunk the wine of libations.
ESG 5:1-14	On the third day Esther exchanges mourning clothes for royal dress and goes in, leaning on one maid while another carries her train, cheerful and afraid. The king looks up in anger and she faints. God turns his mood to gentleness; he takes her in his arms, calls her his sister, and lays the golden sceptre on her neck. She faints again, then asks only that he and Haman come to a banquet, then a second. Haman, elated, sees Mordecai and orders a fifty-cubit gallows.
ESG 6:1-14	That night the Lord takes sleep from the king, who has the daily registers read and finds Mordecai's report of a plot unrewarded. Haman arrives intending to ask for Mordecai's hanging, and is asked instead what should be done for a man the king wishes to honour. Assuming himself, he proposes the royal robe, the king's horse and a proclamation, and is ordered to do it all for Mordecai. He goes home covered, and his wife tells him he cannot stand against a man the living God is with.
ESG 7:1-10	At the second banquet the king again offers half his kingdom, and Esther asks for her own life and her people's, saying they have been sold for destruction, pillage and slavery. Asked who dared, she names Haman. The king walks out into the garden, and Haman throws himself on the couch to beg the queen; returning, the king takes it as an assault on his wife. The chamberlain Bugathan mentions the fifty-cubit gallows Haman built for the man who saved the king's life, and Haman is hanged on it.
ESG 8:1-12	The king gives Esther everything that belonged to Haman, learns Mordecai is her kinsman, and gives him the ring taken from Haman. Esther falls at his feet and begs that Haman's letters be reversed, asking how she could watch her people destroyed. He answers that what is sealed with his ring cannot be revoked, so they may write whatever they wish in his name. Scribes write to the hundred and twenty-seven provinces, permitting the Jews to defend themselves on the thirteenth of Adar.
ESG 8:13-17	The counter-decree is given in full. It complains that men raised up by royal kindness turn on their benefactors, names Haman a Macedonian and an alien to Persian blood who aimed to transfer the empire to Macedon, and states the Jews are not criminals but live by the justest laws. Provinces are told to ignore Haman's letter, let the Jews defend themselves, and keep the day as a festival, on pain of fire and spear. Mordecai goes out in royal purple and a golden crown.
ESG 9:1-32	On the thirteenth of Adar the Jews' enemies fall, since officials fear Mordecai. Five hundred die in Susa, including Haman's ten sons, named one by one. Told the number, the king asks what else Esther wants; she asks for one more day and that the sons' bodies be hung, and three hundred more die. Fifteen thousand fall in the provinces, with no plunder taken. They rest on the fourteenth, and Mordecai writes to establish the fourteenth and fifteenth as days of feasting and gifts, called Phrurae after the lots.
ESG 10:1-3	The king levies a tax by land and sea, and his greatness is recorded in the Persian and Median annals. Mordecai serves as viceroy, honoured by the Jews and loved by his people. He then interprets his opening dream: the little spring that became a river is Esther, whom the king made queen; the two serpents are himself and Haman; the massed nations are those who combined against the Jews. A closing note names the Egyptian year and the men who brought this letter of Purim to Egypt.
EZR 1:1-11	In Cyrus of Persia's first year, to fulfil Jeremiah's word, God stirs him to proclaim that he has been charged to build a house in Jerusalem, and that any of God's people may go up to do it, with their neighbours supplying silver, gold, goods and animals. The heads of Judah and Benjamin, the priests and the Levites whose spirits God stirred prepare to go. Cyrus has the temple vessels Nebuchadnezzar took brought out and counted to Sheshbazzar, five thousand four hundred pieces in all.
EZR 2:1-44	A register of those who returned from Babylon with Zerubbabel, Jeshua and ten other leaders, each going back to his own town. The families are listed by ancestor, from Parosh with two thousand one hundred and seventy-two down to Gibbar with ninety-five, then by place of origin: Bethlehem, Netophah, Anathoth, Bethel and Ai, Jericho and others. Four priestly houses follow, together well over four thousand men, and then the Levites, who number only seventy-four, with a hundred and twenty-eight singers of Asaph and a hundred and thirty-nine gatekeepers.
EZR 2:45-70	The list of temple servants and descendants of Solomon's servants runs to three hundred and ninety-two. Some who came up from Tel Melah and elsewhere could not prove their Israelite descent, and three priestly families, including the sons of Barzillai who took their name from the family they married into, were struck from the priesthood until a priest with Urim and Thummim could rule. The assembly totals 42,360, with servants, singers, horses, mules, camels and donkeys counted. Family heads give gold, silver and priests' garments toward rebuilding.
EZR 3:1-13	In the seventh month the people gather as one at Jerusalem. Jeshua and Zerubbabel rebuild the altar on its base, afraid of the surrounding peoples, and begin morning and evening offerings, keeping the feast of tents, though no foundation is laid yet. They pay masons and carpenters and send food and oil to Sidon and Tyre for cedar shipped to Joppa. In year two the foundation is laid, priests trumpeting, Levites praising. The old men who saw the first house weep so loudly it cannot be told from the shouting.
EZR 4:1-24	Judah's adversaries offer to help build, claiming they have sacrificed to the same God since Esarhaddon settled them there, and are refused. They then discourage the builders and hire counsellors against them for years. Under Artaxerxes, Rehum and Shimshai write to the king warning that a rebuilt Jerusalem will stop paying tribute and that the records show it as a rebellious city. The king searches the archives, confirms it, and orders the work halted. The officials go and stop it by force, and building ceases until Darius' second year.
EZR 5:1-17	Stirred by the prophets Haggai and Zechariah, Zerubbabel and Jeshua restart the building. Tattenai, governor beyond the River, arrives and asks who authorised it and demands the builders' names, but does not stop the work while the matter goes to Darius. His letter to the king reports the great stones and timber and the speed of the work, and quotes the elders' answer: they are servants of the God of heaven, rebuilding what Nebuchadnezzar destroyed, under a decree of Cyrus. He asks the king to search the archives.
EZR 6:1-22	The search turns up the scroll, not in Babylon but at Achmetha in Media, recording Cyrus' decree with the temple's dimensions and the order that costs come from the royal treasury. Darius tells Tattenai to keep away, fund the work from provincial tribute, and supply animals, wheat, salt, wine and oil daily, adding that anyone altering the order will be impaled on a beam from his own house. The temple is finished in Darius' sixth year, dedicated with hundreds of animals and twelve tribal goats, and Passover is kept.
EZR 7:1-28	Ezra, traced back sixteen generations to Aaron, is a scribe skilled in the law of Moses, and goes up from Babylon in Artaxerxes' seventh year, the journey taking four months. He had set his heart to study the law, do it and teach it. The king's letter lets any Israelite go with him, sends silver and gold for offerings, orders the treasurers beyond the River to supply up to a hundred talents of silver, exempts temple staff from tax, and authorises him to appoint judges. Ezra blesses God for it.
EZR 8:1-21	Ezra lists the family heads who returned with him, about fifteen hundred men in all, from the sons of Phinehas, Ithamar and David through Parosh, Pahathmoab, Adin, Elam and the rest. Camped three days at the river Ahava, he reviews the company and finds not one Levite among them. He sends eleven leading men to Iddo at Casiphia to ask for ministers, and they bring back Sherebiah with eighteen, Hashabiah and Jeshaiah with twenty, and two hundred twenty temple servants. Ezra then proclaims a fast to seek a safe road.
EZR 8:22-36	Ezra explains he was ashamed to ask the king for troops, having told him God protects those who seek him, so they fasted instead. He weighs out to twelve chief priests six hundred fifty talents of silver, a hundred talents of gold, gold bowls and two vessels of bright brass, telling them both they and the vessels are holy and must be weighed again on arrival. They leave on the twelfth day, are kept from ambush, and after three days in Jerusalem the treasure is weighed in and recorded.
EZR 9:1-15	The princes tell Ezra that Israelites, priests and Levites alike have married women of the surrounding peoples, the leaders leading the way. Ezra tears his clothes, pulls hair from his head and beard, and sits appalled until the evening offering. Then he kneels and prays, confessing that guilt has piled up since their fathers' days, that exile and plunder were the result, and that God has punished them less than they deserved by leaving a remnant. He ends by saying no one can stand before God in this guilt.
EZR 10:1-44	A large crowd gathers weeping as Ezra prays, and Shecaniah proposes a covenant to send away the foreign wives and their children. Ezra makes the leaders swear to it, then fasts. A proclamation summons everyone to Jerusalem within three days on pain of losing their property. They assemble in the ninth month, trembling in heavy rain, and ask for commissioners to handle it city by city rather than in one sitting. Four men object. The inquiry runs three months, and the men involved, priests among them, are listed by name.
HAB 1:1-17	Habakkuk asks how long he must cry out about violence with no answer, since the law is paralysed and justice comes out perverted. God replies that he is raising up the Chaldeans, a bitter and hasty nation whose horses are swifter than leopards, who laugh at kings and take every stronghold with an earthen ramp. This makes the prophet's question worse, and he asks it again: how can God, whose eyes are too pure to look on evil, watch the wicked swallow the more righteous and stay silent.
HAB 2:1-20	Habakkuk takes his post on the ramparts to wait for an answer. God tells him to write the vision plainly on tablets so a runner can read it, and says that though it takes time it will not delay, and that the righteous will live by his faith. Then five woes against the plunderer: enriching himself by extortion, building his nest on high, founding a city with blood, getting his neighbour drunk to look at him, and praying to a piece of gold-plated wood with no breath in it.
HAB 3:1-19	The last chapter is a prayer set to music. Habakkuk describes God coming from Teman and Mount Paran, his splendour like sunrise, plague going ahead of him, the ancient mountains crumbling and the nations trembling. He asks whether the anger was really against the rivers and the sea, and pictures God riding chariots of salvation, with the sun and moon standing still. The prophet says his body trembles, then ends by saying that even with no figs, no vines, no flocks and no herds, he will rejoice in Yahweh.
HAG 1:1-15	In the second year of Darius, Haggai brings God's word to Zerubbabel the governor and Joshua the high priest. The people are saying the time to rebuild Yahweh's house has not come, and God asks whether it is time for them to live in panelled houses while his lies in ruins. Consider your ways, he tells them: they sow much and bring in little, and earn wages to put into a bag with holes. A drought was called for this reason. They obey, and work on the house begins.
HAG 2:1-23	A month later Haggai asks who remembers the former temple's glory, and tells Zerubbabel, Joshua and the people to be strong and work, since God will shake all nations and fill this house, and its later glory will be greater. He then puts a legal question to the priests: holiness does not spread by touch, but uncleanness does, and so is this people. Poor harvests are dated to before the foundation was laid, and blessing begins from today. Zerubbabel is told he will be made like a signet ring.
HEB 10:1-39	Repeated sacrifices, the author argues, only remind people of sins yearly, since bull and goat blood cannot remove them. Quoting a psalm about a body prepared rather than offerings desired, he says Christ made one offering and sat down. Because the way in is open, the readers should draw near, hold their confession, provoke one another to love, and keep meeting. Then a warning: deliberate sin after knowing the truth leaves no sacrifice. He reminds them they once endured public abuse and the plundering of their property.
HEB 11:1-17	Faith is defined as assurance of things hoped for and proof of things not seen, then illustrated by a roll call. By faith the universe was framed by God's word. Abel offered a better sacrifice and still speaks though dead. Enoch was taken without dying. Noah built a ship over an unseen warning. Abraham left home without knowing where he was going and lived in tents, looking for a city with foundations. Sarah conceived past age. Abraham, tested, offered up Isaac.
HEB 11:18-40	Abraham reasoned God could raise the dead, and got Isaac back figuratively. The list continues: Isaac, Jacob blessing Joseph's sons while leaning on his staff, Joseph giving orders about his bones, Moses hidden three months and later refusing to be called Pharaoh's daughter's son, Passover, the Red Sea, Jericho, Rahab. The author says time would fail him, names Gideon, Barak, Samson, Jephthah, David and Samuel, then turns to those tortured, stoned and sawn apart. None received the promise.
HEB 12:1-29	Surrounded by a cloud of witnesses, the readers are told to drop every weight and run the race, looking to Jesus, who endured the cross. Their hardship is fatherly discipline, unpleasant now but yielding righteousness later, and a child without discipline is illegitimate. They should pursue peace, watch for a root of bitterness, and avoid being like Esau, who sold his birthright for a meal and could not get the blessing back. They have come not to the burning mountain but to Mount Zion.
HEB 13:1-25	Closing instructions: keep loving one another, show hospitality since some have entertained angels unawares, remember prisoners, honour marriage, and stay free of the love of money. Imitate the faith of past leaders. Avoid strange teachings about food. As sin offerings were burned outside the camp, Jesus suffered outside the gate, so they should go out to him. Obey the leaders, pray for the writer, greetings from the Italians, and news that Timothy has been freed.
HOS 6:1-11	The people propose returning to Yahweh, expecting healing after two days and revival on the third, and comparing his coming to the spring rain. God answers that their love is a morning cloud, dew that disappears early, and that he has cut them with the words of the prophets. He wants mercy rather than sacrifice and knowledge of God rather than burnt offerings. Gilead is stained with blood and priests murder on the road toward Shechem.
HOS 7:1-16	Whenever God moves to heal Israel, Ephraim's crime is uncovered instead: thieves inside, raiders outside, and a court that delights the king with its wickedness. Their passion is an oven the baker leaves untended overnight and finds blazing by morning; they devour their own judges and all their kings have fallen. Ephraim is a cake not turned over, going gray without noticing, an easily deceived dove calling to Egypt and Assyria. God will net them like birds.
HOS 8:1-14	A trumpet sounds as something like an eagle comes over Yahweh's house. Israel has set up kings without God's approval and made idols from its own silver and gold, and Samaria's calf, which a workman made, will be broken to pieces. They sow the wind and reap the whirlwind. Israel has gone to Assyria like a lone wild donkey and hired lovers; its many altars only multiply sin, and its sacrifices are not accepted. Fire is promised on the cities.
HOS 9:1-17	Israel is told not to celebrate like the other nations. The threshing floor and wine press will not feed them, and instead of Yahweh's land they will eat unclean food in Assyria and be buried in Memphis. Their sacrifices will be like the bread of mourners. The prophet is considered a fool and the inspired man insane. God recalls finding Israel like grapes in the wilderness before Baal Peor, and promises no births, no fruit, and wandering among the nations.
HOS 10:1-15	Israel is a luxuriant vine that used its prosperity to multiply altars and adorn sacred stones, and those altars will be demolished. The people say they have no king and do not fear Yahweh. Samaria's calf will be carried to Assyria as a present, and its king floats away like a twig on water. People will beg the hills to fall on them. Israel is told to sow righteousness and break up its fallow ground, but it has plowed wickedness instead.
HOS 11:1-12	God remembers Israel as a child he loved and called out of Egypt, taught to walk and held by the arms, though they did not know who healed them and sacrificed to the Baals instead. Assyria, not Egypt, will be their king, and the sword will fall on their cities. Then God asks how he can give Ephraim up or make him like Admah and Zeboiim, says his compassion is aroused, and promises not to come in wrath.
HOS 12:1-14	Ephraim feeds on wind, making a covenant with Assyria while oil is carried to Egypt. God also has a controversy with Judah, and recalls Jacob, who took his brother by the heel in the womb, struggled with the angel and wept, and met God at Bethel. The people are told to keep kindness and justice and wait for God. Ephraim is a merchant with dishonest scales who claims his wealth proves him innocent; God says he will make them dwell in tents again.
HOS 13:1-16	Ephraim once commanded trembling, but became guilty through Baal and died. They keep making molten images of silver and will vanish like morning mist, dew, chaff and smoke. God, who knew them in the wilderness, says that once filled they forgot him, and he will meet them as a lion, a leopard, and a bear robbed of her cubs. Their king cannot save them. The chapter ends with Samaria bearing her guilt: the people fall by the sword, infants and pregnant women included.
HOS 14:1-9	The book closes with an invitation to return to Yahweh, bringing words rather than sacrifices and admitting that Assyria cannot save and that the work of their hands is not God. God promises to heal their waywardness and love them freely. He will be like dew to Israel, who will blossom like a lily, take root like Lebanon, and carry the beauty of an olive tree. A closing line says the wise understand these ways while the rebellious stumble in them.
JOL 1:1-20	Joel tells the elders to remember an unprecedented disaster and pass it on: four waves of locusts, each eating what the last one left. Drunkards are told to weep because the wine is gone, priests to lie all night in sackcloth because grain and drink offerings have stopped, and farmers to wail over lost wheat and barley. Vine, fig, pomegranate, palm and apple are all withered. A fast is called. Animals groan for pasture and the brooks have dried up.
JOL 2:1-32	A trumpet sounds in Zion for the day of Yahweh. The locusts become an army: like horses, running like warriors, climbing walls, entering windows like thieves, with fire ahead and desolation behind, and the sun and moon darkened. God then calls for a return with fasting and weeping, telling them to tear their hearts and not their clothes. He answers with grain, wine and oil, and promises to restore the years the locusts ate, and to pour out his Spirit on all flesh.
JOL 3:1-21	God says he will gather all nations to the valley of Jehoshaphat and judge them for scattering his people and casting lots for them, selling a boy for a prostitute and a girl for wine. Tyre, Sidon and Philistia are charged with looting the temple treasures and selling Judah's children to the Greeks. Nations are told to beat plowshares into swords. The valley of decision fills, the sun goes dark, and the book ends with mountains dripping sweet wine.
JON 1:1-17	Told to go and preach against Nineveh, Jonah instead pays for passage to Tarshish. Yahweh sends a storm; the sailors throw the cargo overboard and pray to their own gods while Jonah sleeps below deck. The captain wakes him, lots are cast, and the lot falls on Jonah, who admits he is fleeing Yahweh and tells them to throw him into the sea. They try to row to land first, then do it, and the sea calms. A great fish swallows him for three days and nights.
JON 2:1-10	From inside the fish Jonah prays. He describes being thrown into the deep with the waves passing over him, weeds wrapped around his head, and the earth barring him in, and says that he called out from the belly of Sheol and was heard. He remembered Yahweh when his soul fainted, and his prayer reached the holy temple. He promises sacrifice and thanksgiving and says that salvation belongs to Yahweh. Yahweh then speaks to the fish, and it vomits Jonah onto dry land.
JON 3:1-10	The word comes to Jonah a second time and he goes to Nineveh, a city three days' journey across. He walks one day into it and says that in forty days Nineveh will be overthrown. The people believe God at once, proclaim a fast and put on sackcloth from the greatest to the least. The king leaves his throne, sits in ashes, and orders that neither people nor animals eat or drink. God sees that they have turned from their evil way and does not do what he said.
JON 4:1-11	Jonah is furious the city was spared, and tells God this is exactly why he ran: he knew God was gracious and merciful and would relent. He asks to die, then camps east of the city to watch what happens. God grows a vine for shade, delighting him, then sends a worm to kill it and a hot wind, and Jonah again asks to die. God's closing question is whether he should not care about a city of more than a hundred and twenty thousand people, and also much livestock.
JUD 1:1-25	Jude says he meant to write about salvation but had to write about intruders instead: men who have slipped in, turned grace into licence, and deny the Master. He stacks up warnings, the unbelievers destroyed after Egypt, the angels who left their own dwelling and are kept in chains under darkness, and Sodom. Michael the archangel, arguing with the devil over the body of Moses, would not insult him. The intruders are waterless clouds and wandering stars. Enoch prophesied about them.
MAL 1:1-14	The book runs as a series of exchanges. God says he has loved them, and they ask how; the answer is Jacob and Esau, with Edom's rebuilding promised to be thrown down again. He then asks the priests where his honour is, and they ask how they have despised his name. The answer is polluted bread on the altar and blind, lame and sick animals offered in sacrifice. Try that on the governor, he says, and see how it goes. He would rather someone shut the temple doors.
MAL 2:1-17	The priests are warned that their blessings are already cursed, with the dung of their own feasts spread on their faces. The covenant with Levi was one of life and peace, and a priest's lips should keep knowledge, but these have turned aside and made many stumble. Judah is charged with treachery: marrying the daughter of a foreign god, and covering the altar with tears while betraying the wife of his youth. God says he hates divorce, and closes by charging them with wearying him with their words.
MAL 3:1-18	God will send his messenger to prepare the way, and the Lord will come suddenly to his temple, but nobody can endure the day: he is a refiner's fire and launderers' soap, and he will purify the sons of Levi. He will be a swift witness against sorcerers, adulterers, perjurers and those who withhold a labourer's wages or deny justice to the foreigner. Asked how they have robbed him, he answers, in tithes and offerings, and invites them to test him by bringing the whole tithe in.
MAL 4:1-6	The last chapter is short. A day is coming that burns like a furnace, leaving the proud and the wicked as stubble with neither root nor branch, while for those who fear God's name the sun of righteousness rises with healing in its wings and they go out leaping like calves let out of the stall. Israel is told to remember the law of Moses given at Horeb. God promises to send Elijah before the great and terrible day, to turn the hearts of parents and children to each other.
MIC 1:1-16	Micah of Moresheth speaks in the days of Jotham, Ahaz and Hezekiah about Samaria and Jerusalem. Yahweh comes down and treads the high places until the mountains melt like wax. Samaria will be poured into the valley as a heap of rubble, her idols smashed, and the wound reaches Judah and Jerusalem's gate. Micah says he will lament stripped and naked, howling like a jackal, and runs through a list of towns whose names match their fates. Shave your heads, he tells them, for the children are gone into captivity.
MIC 2:1-13	Woe to those who lie awake planning evil and carry it out at first light because they have the power to: they covet fields and seize them, dispossessing whole families. God is planning a disaster they will not shrug off, and they will end up singing their own lament about fields handed to traitors. They tell Micah not to prophesy. He says the prophet this people wants is one who promises wine and strong drink. The chapter ends by gathering the remnant like sheep in a fold.
MIC 3:1-12	Micah asks the rulers whether it is not their business to know justice, then accuses them of eating his people: tearing off skin, breaking bones, chopping the flesh into the pot. When they cry out, God will not answer. The prophets who cry peace to whoever feeds them will get night with no vision, and will cover their lips because no answer comes. Judges take bribes, priests teach for a price, and prophets tell fortunes for money, yet all lean on Yahweh. So Zion will be plowed like a field.
MIC 4:1-13	In the latter days the mountain of Yahweh's temple will be raised above the hills, and nations will stream to it to be taught his ways. They will beat swords into plowshares and spears into pruning hooks and learn war no more, and every man will sit under his own vine and fig tree unafraid.
