God will gather the lame and the driven away and make them a strong nation. Zion is told she must go out to the field and to Babylon, and be rescued there.
MIC 5:1-15	Under siege, with the judge of Israel struck on the cheek with a rod, the oracle turns to Bethlehem Ephrathah, small among the clans of Judah, from which a ruler will come whose origins are ancient. He will shepherd in the strength of Yahweh and be great to the ends of the earth, and he will be their peace when Assyria invades. The remnant of Jacob will be like dew among the nations and also like a lion among sheep. God will cut off horses, chariots, strongholds and idols.
MIC 6:1-16	Yahweh calls the mountains as witnesses and asks his people what he has done to them, recalling the exodus, Moses, Aaron and Miriam, and what Balak and Balaam attempted. The question comes back: what should be brought, burnt offerings, thousands of rams, rivers of oil, a firstborn child? The answer is that he has already shown what is good, to act justly, love mercy, and walk humbly with God. Short measures and false weights are named, and the sentence is eating without being satisfied and sowing without reaping.
MIC 7:1-20	Micah compares himself to a gleaner finding no grapes: the godly are gone, everyone hunts his brother, and judges take bribes. Trust no one, not even the wife in your arms, since a man's enemies are in his own house. But Micah will wait for God, and tells his enemy not to gloat, since he will rise when he falls. Walls will be rebuilt and people will come from Assyria and Egypt. The book ends asking who is a God like this one, who casts sins into the sea.
NAM 2:1-13	The attacker comes up against Nineveh and the city is told to man the walls, while Yahweh restores the excellency of Jacob. Shields are red, soldiers in scarlet, chariots flash and run like lightning through the streets. The river gates open, the palace dissolves, and the queen is carried away while her servants moan like doves. Plunder the silver and the gold, the poem says: there is no end of it. Nineveh is left empty and waste, and the old lions' den has no lions in it.
NAM 3:1-19	Woe to the bloody city, full of lies and plunder, with whips cracking, chariots bounding and corpses heaped so high that people stumble over them. God will lift her skirts over her face and make her a spectacle nobody mourns. Nineveh is asked whether she is better than Thebes, which had the sea for a wall and still fell, its children killed and its great men chained. Her fortresses will drop like ripe figs, her officials scatter like locusts in the sun, and her wound will not heal.
NEH 1:1-11	At the fortress of Susa in the twentieth year, Nehemiah asks his brother Hanani about the survivors in Judah and is told the remnant is in great trouble and disgrace, Jerusalem's wall broken and its gates burned. He weeps, mourns for days, and fasts and prays. His prayer confesses the sins of Israel, including his own family's, recalls the promise to Moses that God would gather the scattered if they returned, and asks for mercy before one particular man. The chapter ends noting he was the king's cupbearer.
NEH 2:1-20	Serving wine, Nehemiah lets his sadness show for the first time. The king asks why, and Nehemiah, afraid, explains that the city of his ancestors' graves lies in ruins. Asked what he wants, he prays and then requests leave to rebuild it, plus letters of passage and an order for timber from the royal forest. Escorted by cavalry, he reaches Jerusalem, inspects the ruined walls by night without telling anyone, then calls the people to build. Sanballat, Tobiah and Geshem mock them and accuse them of rebellion.
NEH 3:1-32	The wall is rebuilt gate by gate and section by section, each group named. The high priest and the priests start with the sheep gate, the men of Jericho next, the sons of Hassenaah on the fish gate. Goldsmiths and a perfumer take stretches of masonry, district rulers repair their own areas, and many work directly opposite their own houses. Shallum works with his daughters. The Tekoites take two sections, though their nobles refuse to stoop to the work. Goldsmiths and merchants finish the last stretch.
NEH 4:1-23	Sanballat mocks the builders as feeble and Tobiah says a fox climbing the wall would knock it down. Nehemiah prays against them, and the wall reaches half its height because the people had a mind to work. When Sanballat, Tobiah, Arabians, Ammonites and Ashdodites plot an attack, Nehemiah posts guards by family with swords, spears and bows, and tells them to remember the Lord and fight for their homes. From then on half work and half stand armed, builders carry a weapon in one hand, and nobody undresses at night.
NEH 5:1-19	The people cry out against their fellow Jews: to buy grain and pay the king's tribute they have mortgaged fields, vineyards and houses, and some have sold their children into slavery. Nehemiah is angry, confronts the nobles for charging interest on their own brothers, and holds a public assembly. They agree to return the fields, vineyards and houses and charge nothing, and he makes the priests swear to it. As governor for twelve years he never claimed the food allowance his predecessors took, though he fed a hundred fifty daily.
NEH 6:1-19	With the wall closed but the gates not hung, Sanballat and Geshem invite Nehemiah to meet at Ono; he refuses four times, saying he is doing a great work and cannot come down. A fifth message, an open letter, accuses him of planning to make himself king. Shemaiah urges him to hide in the temple, but Nehemiah sees he was hired to make him sin. The wall is finished in fifty-two days, and the nations lose confidence, though Judah's nobles keep up ties with Tobiah, related to them by marriage.
NEH 7:1-43	With the wall built and the doors set, Nehemiah appoints gatekeepers, singers and Levites, and puts his brother Hanani and Hananiah of the fortress in charge, ordering the gates kept shut until the sun is hot and watches posted near their own houses. The city is large but thinly populated, so he gathers everyone to be registered and finds the record of the first return: the men who came with Zerubbabel and Jeshua, family by family and town by town, then the four priestly houses and the seventy-four Levites.
NEH 7:44-73	The register continues with a hundred and forty-eight singers, a hundred and thirty-eight gatekeepers, and the temple servants and sons of Solomon's servants numbering three hundred and ninety-two. Those who could not prove their descent are recorded, including three priestly families barred from the holy food until a priest with Urim and Thummim should rule. The assembly totals 42,360, with servants, singers and livestock counted. The governor and the family heads give gold, silver and priests' garments toward the work, and the people settle in their towns.
NEH 8:1-18	The people gather at the water gate and ask Ezra to bring the book of the law. Standing on a wooden platform with thirteen men beside him, he reads from early morning until midday while the Levites explain the sense so the people understand. Everyone weeps, and Nehemiah and Ezra tell them not to mourn but to eat, drink, send portions to those with nothing, and take joy. The next day the leaders study further, find the command about booths, and keep the feast in shelters, undone since Joshua's day.
NEH 9:1-21	On the twenty-fourth day the people assemble fasting, in sackcloth and dust, separate themselves from foreigners and confess their own and their fathers' sins, a quarter of the day spent reading the law and a quarter confessing. The Levites lead a long prayer: God alone made heaven and earth, chose Abram, saw the affliction in Egypt, split the sea, gave law at Sinai, bread from the sky and water from the rock. Even after the golden calf the pillar stayed, and for forty years their clothes did not wear out.
NEH 9:22-38	The prayer continues through the conquest, the good land with its cisterns and vineyards, and the cycle that followed: rebellion, oppression, a cry for help, deliverance, rebellion again. Prophets were killed, warnings ignored for years, and the people finally handed to foreign powers, though God did not destroy them entirely. The confession ends by admitting God has been just and they have done wickedly, that they are servants in their own land, its produce going to foreign kings. So they make a binding covenant, written and sealed.
NEH 10:1-39	The sealed covenant lists its signatories: Nehemiah the governor, twenty-two priests, seventeen Levites and forty-four chiefs of the people. The rest of the community joins by oath to keep the law of Moses. They pledge not to intermarry with the surrounding peoples, not to buy goods on the Sabbath, to release debts every seventh year, and to charge themselves a third of a shekel yearly for temple service. Lots are cast for the wood offering, and firstfruits, firstborn and tithes are promised, with a priest present whenever the Levites collect.
NEH 11:1-36	The leaders already live in Jerusalem, and lots are cast so one family in ten moves into the city, the people blessing those who volunteer. The residents are then counted: four hundred sixty-eight men of Judah through Perez, nine hundred twenty-eight Benjaminites, over a thousand priests, two hundred eighty-four Levites and a hundred seventy-two gatekeepers, with temple servants in Ophel and royal provision for the singers. The remaining towns are named, Judah settled from Beersheba to the valley of Hinnom and Benjamin from Geba to Ono.
NEH 12:1-30	A list of the priests and Levites who returned with Zerubbabel and Jeshua, followed by the high priestly succession from Jeshua down to Jaddua, and the heads of priestly houses in Joiakim's day. The Levite chiefs are recorded as praising watch by watch according to David's command, with gatekeepers over the storehouses. For the dedication of the wall the Levites are sought out from wherever they live, and the singers, who had built villages around Jerusalem, gather. Priests and Levites purify themselves, the people, the gates and the wall.
NEH 12:31-47	Nehemiah puts two thanksgiving choirs on the wall to circle the city in opposite directions. One goes right toward the dung gate with Ezra at its head, the other left past the tower of the furnaces, the Ephraim gate, the fish gate and the towers of Hananel and Hammeah, Nehemiah following. They meet at the temple, where trumpets and singers sound and great sacrifices are offered, the joy of Jerusalem audible far off. Men are appointed over the storerooms for tithes and firstfruits, and singers and gatekeepers are supported.
NEH 13:1-15	A reading of Moses finds that no Ammonite or Moabite may enter the assembly, because they hired Balaam to curse Israel, so the mixed company is separated out. Nehemiah had gone back to the king in Artaxerxes' thirty-second year, and returns to find that Eliashib has given Tobiah a large room in the temple courts. He throws Tobiah's furniture out, has the rooms cleansed, and restores the vessels and offerings. Finding the Levites unpaid and gone back to their fields, he reinstates the tithe and appoints trustworthy treasurers.
NEH 13:16-31	Nehemiah finds men treading winepresses and hauling loads on the Sabbath, and Tyrian traders selling fish in Jerusalem. He rebukes the nobles, has the gates shut from dusk before the Sabbath until it ends, and threatens the merchants camping outside with force. He also finds Jews married to women of Ashdod, Ammon and Moab whose children cannot speak the language of Judah, curses and strikes some of them, and cites Solomon as the warning. A grandson of the high priest, son-in-law to Sanballat, is driven away.
OBA 1:1-21	Obadiah's single chapter is a vision against Edom. Edom lives in the clefts of the rock and thinks nothing can bring it down, but though its nest were among the stars God will pull it down. Thieves would leave something behind; Edom will be stripped completely, betrayed by its own allies. The charge is standing aside when strangers carried off Jerusalem and casting lots with them. As you have done, it will be done to you. Jacob becomes a fire and Esau the stubble.
REV 1:1-20	John, exiled on Patmos for the word of God, writes to seven assemblies in Asia. On the Lord's day he hears a voice like a trumpet behind him and turns to see seven golden lamp stands and among them a figure like a son of man: hair white as snow, eyes like fire, feet like burnished brass, seven stars in his right hand and a two-edged sword coming out of his mouth. John falls down as dead. The figure says he holds the keys of Death and Hades.
REV 2:1-29	Four letters. Ephesus is commended for endurance and for exposing false apostles, but has left its first love and risks losing its lamp stand. Smyrna is poor but rich, and warned of ten days of prison. Pergamum lives where Satan's throne is and held firm when Antipas was killed, but tolerates the teaching of Balaam. Thyatira's works are increasing, yet it tolerates a woman called Jezebel who calls herself a prophetess. Rewards include hidden manna, a white stone with a new name, and the morning star.
REV 3:1-22	Three more letters. Sardis has a reputation for being alive but is dead, though a few there have not defiled their garments and will walk in white. Philadelphia has little power but kept the word, and is given an open door no one can shut and a promise to be made a pillar in the temple. Laodicea is neither hot nor cold, so it will be spat out; it thinks itself rich but is poor, blind and naked, and is told to buy gold, white garments and eye salve.
REV 4:1-11	A door opens in heaven and John is called up. He sees a throne with someone on it looking like jasper and sardius, a rainbow like emerald around it, and twenty-four elders in white with gold crowns on twenty-four thrones. Lightning and thunder come from the throne, seven lamps burn before it, and there is a sea of glass. Four living creatures covered in eyes, like a lion, a calf, a man and an eagle, never stop saying holy, holy, holy. The elders throw down their crowns.
REV 5:1-14	In the right hand of the one on the throne is a scroll sealed with seven seals, and an angel asks who is worthy to open it. Nobody in heaven, earth or under the earth can, and John weeps until an elder tells him the Lion of Judah has overcome. What John then sees is a Lamb looking as though slain, with seven horns and seven eyes. The Lamb takes the scroll, and the creatures, elders and countless angels sing that he is worthy.
REV 6:1-17	The Lamb opens six seals. A white horse rides out to conquer, a red horse takes peace from the earth, a black horse carries scales while a voice prices wheat and barley and warns not to damage the oil and wine, and a pale horse named Death, with Hades following, is given a quarter of the earth. Under the altar the souls of the martyrs ask how long and are given white robes and told to wait. The sixth seal brings an earthquake, a black sun and a blood moon.
REV 7:1-17	Four angels hold back the four winds so no wind blows on land, sea or tree, while another angel seals the servants of God on their foreheads. The number sealed is a hundred and forty-four thousand, twelve thousand from each tribe, listed by name. Then John sees a crowd too large to count, from every nation, in white robes with palm branches, praising God and the Lamb. An elder explains they came out of the great tribulation and washed their robes white in the Lamb's blood.
REV 8:1-13	The seventh seal opens and heaven is silent for about half an hour. Seven angels are given trumpets, and another angel offers incense with the prayers of the saints, then fills the censer with altar fire and throws it to earth. Four trumpets sound: hail and fire burn a third of the earth, a burning mountain turns a third of the sea to blood, a star called Wormwood poisons a third of the rivers, and a third of the sun, moon and stars is darkened. An eagle cries woe.
REV 9:1-21	The fifth trumpet opens the pit of the abyss, and out of the smoke come locusts shaped like horses with human faces, women's hair, lions' teeth and scorpion tails. They may not touch vegetation, only the unsealed, and torment them for five months so that people seek death and cannot find it. Their king is Abaddon, or Apollyon. The sixth trumpet frees four angels at the Euphrates and an army of two hundred million horsemen whose horses breathe fire. Survivors still do not repent.
REV 10:1-11	A mighty angel comes down wrapped in a cloud, a rainbow on his head, face like the sun and feet like pillars of fire, with one foot on the sea and one on the land. He shouts like a lion and seven thunders answer, but John is told to seal up what they said and not write it. The angel swears there will be no more delay. John is told to take the little open book and eat it; it is sweet as honey and turns his stomach bitter.
REV 11:1-19	John is given a reed to measure the temple but told to leave out the outer court. Two witnesses in sackcloth prophesy for 1,260 days, with fire from their mouths and power to stop the rain. The beast from the abyss kills them, their bodies lie in the street for three and a half days while people exchange gifts, then they stand up and are called into heaven. An earthquake kills seven thousand. The seventh trumpet sounds and heaven declares the kingdom has changed hands.
REV 12:1-17	A woman clothed with the sun, the moon under her feet and twelve stars on her head, is in labour, while a red dragon with seven heads and ten horns sweeps a third of the stars down with his tail and waits to eat her child. The son is born and snatched up to the throne; the woman flees to the wilderness. Michael and his angels fight the dragon and throw him down to earth. Enraged, he chases the woman, and the earth swallows the river he spits after her.
REV 13:1-18	A beast rises from the sea with seven heads and ten horns, like a leopard with bear's feet and a lion's mouth, and the dragon hands it his authority. One head has a healed fatal wound, and the world marvels and worships. It blasphemes and makes war on the saints for forty-two months. A second beast comes up from the earth, works signs, makes an image that speaks, and marks everyone on hand or forehead so no one can buy or sell without it. The number is 666.
REV 14:1-20	The Lamb stands on Mount Zion with the hundred and forty-four thousand, who alone can learn the new song. Three angels fly overhead: one proclaiming eternal good news, one announcing that Babylon the great has fallen, and one warning that anyone who takes the beast's mark will drink undiluted wrath. A voice calls the dead who die in the Lord blessed. Then two harvests: one like a son of man reaps the earth with a sickle, and grapes are trodden in the winepress until blood runs to the horses' bridles.
REV 15:1-8	Seven angels appear with the seven last plagues, which complete God's wrath. Those who overcame the beast, its image and the number of its name stand on a sea of glass mixed with fire, holding harps and singing the song of Moses and the song of the Lamb, saying that all nations will come and worship. The heavenly temple opens and the seven angels come out in bright linen with golden sashes. A living creature hands them seven golden bowls, and smoke fills the temple so no one can enter.
REV 16:1-21	The seven bowls are poured out. Sores on those with the mark, the sea and then the rivers turned to blood, the sun scorching people with fire, darkness over the beast's throne so that they gnaw their tongues, and the Euphrates dried up for the kings from the east. Three unclean spirits like frogs come from the mouths of the dragon, the beast and the false prophet and gather the kings at Megiddo. The seventh bowl brings a voice saying it is done, an earthquake and hailstones weighing a talent.
REV 17:1-18	An angel takes John into the wilderness to see a woman on a scarlet beast with seven heads and ten horns. She wears purple and scarlet, holds a golden cup full of filth, and bears a name on her forehead: Babylon the great, drunk on the blood of martyrs. John is amazed, and the angel explains: the seven heads are seven mountains and seven kings, the ten horns are kings who hand their power to the beast, and they will make war on the Lamb and lose.
REV 18:1-24	An angel announces that Babylon has fallen and become a haunt of demons and unclean birds, and a voice from heaven tells God's people to come out of her. Her plagues arrive in one day. The kings who lived luxuriously with her stand far off and mourn the smoke; the merchants weep because nobody buys their cargo, listed from gold and silk down to bodies and souls; the sailors throw dust on their heads. An angel throws a millstone into the sea to show how she will be thrown down.
REV 19:1-21	Heaven shouts hallelujah over the prostitute's judgment and announces the marriage of the Lamb, whose bride is clothed in fine linen. John falls at the angel's feet and is told to stop, since the angel is a fellow servant. Heaven then opens and a rider called Faithful and True rides out on a white horse, eyes like fire, robe dipped in blood, followed by armies on white horses. Birds are invited to God's supper, and the beast and false prophet are thrown into the lake of fire.
REV 20:1-15	An angel with the key of the abyss and a chain binds the dragon for a thousand years so he cannot deceive the nations. Those beheaded for their testimony reign with Christ, called the first resurrection. Satan is then released, gathers Gog and Magog, surrounds the beloved city, and fire from heaven consumes them; he is thrown into the lake of fire. At a great white throne the dead are judged from books, the sea and Hades give up their dead, and Death and Hades follow him in.
REV 21:1-27	John sees a new heaven and new earth with no sea, and the New Jerusalem coming down like a bride. A voice announces God dwelling with people, every tear wiped away, and no more death or pain. An angel carries John to a mountain and measures the city: a cube of twelve thousand stadia, walls of jasper, twelve gates of single pearls named for the tribes, twelve foundations named for the apostles, streets of transparent gold. There is no temple and no sun, because God and the Lamb are both.
REV 22:1-21	A river of the water of life runs from the throne down the street, with the tree of life on both banks bearing fruit and leaves for healing the nations. There is no curse and no night. The angel tells John not to seal the book, and refuses worship a second time. Jesus says three times he is coming quickly. The Spirit and the bride say come, and anyone thirsty may take the water freely. A warning closes the book against adding or taking away.
ZEC 1:1-21	Zechariah begins in the second year of Darius, calling Judah to return to Yahweh, unlike the fathers who ignored the former prophets. Then the first night vision: a man on a red horse among myrtle trees, with red, brown and white horses behind, reporting the whole earth at rest. An angel asks how long Jerusalem will go without mercy, and God answers he is jealous for Zion and will rebuild his house. A second vision shows four horns that scattered Judah, and four craftsmen coming to cast them down.
ZEC 2:1-13	Zechariah sees a man setting out with a measuring line to take the length and width of Jerusalem. An angel runs to stop him: the city will be inhabited as villages without walls, since there will be too many people and livestock for walls, and God himself will be a wall of fire around it. Those still in Babylon are told to flee, since whoever touches them touches the apple of his eye. Many nations will join themselves to Yahweh, and all flesh is told to be silent.
ZEC 3:1-10	Joshua the high priest stands before the angel of Yahweh in filthy clothes, with Satan at his right hand to accuse him. Yahweh rebukes the accuser and calls Joshua a burning stick pulled out of the fire. The filthy garments are taken off, his iniquity is declared gone, and he is dressed in rich clothing with a clean turban. He is told that if he walks in God's ways he will govern the house. A servant called the Branch is promised, and a stone with seven eyes.
ZEC 4:1-14	The angel wakes Zechariah as if from sleep and shows him a solid gold lampstand with a bowl on top, seven lamps, and an olive tree on either side. Asked what it means, Zechariah admits he does not know. The word for Zerubbabel is that it will be done not by might nor by power but by God's Spirit; the mountain will become a plain and he will bring out the capstone. The two olive trees are the two anointed ones who stand by the Lord of the whole earth.
ZEC 5:1-11	Zechariah sees a scroll flying over the land, twenty cubits by ten, a curse against thieves on one side and against those who swear falsely on the other, settling inside their houses to destroy them. Then he sees an ephah basket with a lead weight on it, and inside it a woman named Wickedness, pushed back down and sealed under the lead. Two women with stork wings lift the basket and carry it off to Shinar, to build a house for it there.
ZEC 6:1-15	Four chariots come out from between two mountains of brass, drawn by red, black, white and dappled horses, and the angel explains they are the four winds of the sky, sent to patrol the earth. Those going north quiet God's spirit there. Zechariah is told to take silver and gold from three men newly arrived from Babylon, make crowns, and set them on Joshua the high priest, with a word about the man named the Branch, who will build the temple and be a priest on his throne.
ZEC 7:1-14	Men from Bethel come to ask the priests whether they should keep fasting in the fifth month as they have for years. God's answer is a question: in seventy years of fasting and mourning, was any of it done for him, and when they ate and drank, was that not for themselves? The former prophets asked for true judgment, kindness, and no oppression of the widow, the fatherless, the foreigner or the poor. That generation stopped its ears and hardened its heart, so God did not listen when they called.
ZEC 8:1-23	God says he has returned to Zion, which will be called the City of Truth. The picture is domestic: old men and women sitting in the streets with staffs, boys and girls playing in them. He promises to bring his people back from east and west, to make the ground and the heavens yield again, and to turn Judah from a curse into a blessing. The fasts of four months become feasts. Ten men from every language will take hold of a Jew's skirt to come along.
ZEC 9:1-17	An oracle against Hadrach, Damascus, Hamath, Tyre and Sidon: Tyre heaped up silver like dust and gold like street mud and will be burned anyway, and the Philistine cities will lose their kings and their pride. God will camp around his house so no oppressor passes through. Then Zion is told to rejoice, because her king comes lowly, riding on a donkey, and will cut off the chariot and the battle bow and speak peace to the nations. Prisoners are freed from a waterless pit.
ZEC 10:1-12	People are told to ask Yahweh for spring rain rather than trust household idols and diviners, whose dreams are false and whose comfort is empty, leaving the people wandering like sheep with no shepherd. God's anger is against the shepherds, and he will make Judah his majestic warhorse. From him come the cornerstone, the nail and the battle bow. He will strengthen Judah and bring back the house of Joseph, gathering them out of Egypt and Assyria into Gilead and Lebanon until there is not room enough for them.
ZEC 11:1-17	Lebanon is told to open its doors to fire, and the shepherds wail because their glory is destroyed. Zechariah is told to feed a flock marked for slaughter, whose sellers thank God for their profits. He takes two staffs, Favor and Union, cuts off three shepherds in one month, then quits. Weighed out thirty pieces of silver as wages, which God calls a handsome price, he throws it to the potter in the house of Yahweh, then breaks the second staff and plays a worthless shepherd.
ZEC 12:1-14	God, who stretches out the heavens and forms the spirit within a man, says he will make Jerusalem a cup that makes the surrounding peoples reel and a heavy stone that injures anyone who lifts it. Horses will be struck with terror and blindness, and Judah's chieftains will be like a firepan among wood. The feeblest in Jerusalem will be like David. Then God pours out a spirit of grace, and the people look on the one they have pierced and mourn as for an only son, family by family.
ZEC 13:1-9	A spring is opened for sin and uncleanness, and God says he will cut off the names of the idols and remove the prophets and the spirit of impurity from the land. Prophesying will become so shameful that a man's own parents will threaten him, and he will deny it, saying he is a farmer and explaining his wounds as ones received in the house of his friends. The sword is called against the shepherd so the sheep scatter, and a third of the land is refined like silver.
ZEC 14:1-21	A day of Yahweh comes: all nations gather against Jerusalem, the city is taken and half of it goes into captivity, and then God goes out to fight. His feet stand on the Mount of Olives, which splits east to west into a great valley. It will be a unique day, neither day nor night, with light at evening and living waters flowing east and west year round. The nations that fought Jerusalem are struck with a wasting plague. Afterwards even the bells of the horses read HOLY TO YAHWEH.
ZEP 1:1-18	Zephaniah speaks in the days of Josiah. God announces that he will sweep everything from the face of the earth, people, animals, birds and fish, and stretch out his hand against Judah and Jerusalem to cut off the remnant of Baal and the rooftop star-worshippers. The day of Yahweh is called a sacrifice with consecrated guests. God will search Jerusalem with lamps for those settled on their dregs who say he will do nothing at all. Silver and gold will not save them.
ZEP 2:1-15	The shameless nation is told to gather before the day of anger, and the humble of the land to seek righteousness and humility in the hope of being hidden. Then the neighbours: Gaza forsaken, Ashkelon desolate, the Philistine coast turned into sheep pasture for the remnant of Judah. Moab and Ammon, who insulted God's people, will become like Sodom and Gomorrah, all nettles and salt pits. Assyria will fall too, and Nineveh, the city that said there is no one besides me, will house pelicans and porcupines.
ZEP 3:1-20	Woe to the rebellious city whose princes are roaring lions, judges evening wolves that leave nothing till morning, prophets treacherous and priests profaning the sanctuary, while Yahweh within her brings his justice to light every morning. God says to wait for the day he gathers the nations. Then he will purify the peoples' lips, leave an afflicted and poor people who take refuge in his name, and remove the proud. The book ends with Zion told to sing, because God is among her and rejoices over her with singing.
3MA 5:1-28	The king orders Hermon, master of the elephants, to drug five hundred beasts with wine and frankincense and drive them onto the Jews the next morning, then banquets with the men who hate the Jews most. The prisoners are bound overnight in prayer. Hermon comes early with the elephants prepared, but God sends sleep on the king, who wakes only in mid afternoon. Told the day has passed, he blames Hermon and reschedules. The city gathers before dawn, and the king emerges having forgotten his purpose entirely.
3MA 5:29-51	Hermon reports the elephants ready, and the king, his mind confused, turns on him and says his own parents and children deserved the beasts sooner than these loyal Jews. The court disperses. At another banquet the king orders it again, and his kinsmen ask how long he means to keep changing his mind, since the city is in turmoil. He swears to crush them and burn Judea too. At dawn the elephants go out, and the Jews, hearing the crowd, embrace one another and cry out to God.
3MA 6:1-20	Eleazar, an aged and honoured priest, quiets the elders and prays. He recalls Pharaoh drowned with his chariots, Sennacherib demolished, the three friends cooled in the furnace, Daniel brought back unhurt from the lions, and Jonah recovered from the sea monster. He asks God to appear quickly, so the heathen cannot say their god failed to deliver them. As he finishes, the king arrives at the hippodrome with the beasts, and two dreadful angels come down, visible to all but the Jews, and bind the army with fear.
3MA 6:21-41	The elephants turn back on the troops behind them and trample them. The king weeps, rounds on his friends, accuses them of governing badly and plotting against his life, and orders the prisoners released and sent home as sons of the living God. He pays for a seven day feast, and the Jews turn the place meant for their burial into banqueting booths with dances and psalms, ordaining the days an annual commemoration. After fourteen days they ask to go home.
3MA 7:1-23	The king's letter says certain friends had urged him to destroy the Jews as haters of all other people and that they were brought in chains without inquiry. He found the God of heaven shielding them as a father fights for his sons. He acquits them, sends them home, and warns anyone wronging them answers to God. The Jews ask leave to punish those who had abandoned the law, killing more than three hundred. They feast seven days at Ptolemais and go home with their goods returned.
4MA 1:1-35	The author announces his thesis: religious reasoning is master of the passions, to be proved from Eleazar, seven brothers and their mother, who died for virtue and broke the tyranny over their nation. He defines reasoning as intellect preferring wisdom, taught by the law, with its forms as prudence, justice, manliness and temperance. Pleasure and pain are the two great passions, with lust and fear before them, joy and sorrow after. Reasoning does not destroy the passions but prunes them, as when a man refuses forbidden food.
4MA 2:1-24	Examples follow. Joseph, young and tempted, defeated desire by reasoning; the law forbids coveting, so reasoning can govern lust. A lover of money ruled by the law lends without interest and cancels debts at the Sabbath. Law overrules affection for parents, marriage and friendship when these conflict with virtue, and even enmity: an enemy's crops may not be cut down. Moses regulated his anger against Dathan and Abiram, and Jacob cursed the anger of Simeon and Levi. God enthroned the mind over the senses.
4MA 3:1-21	Reasoning does not eradicate the passions but opposes them: you cannot root out desire or anger, only refuse to be enslaved. The illustration is David, consumed by longing for water from the enemy camp after a day fighting the Philistines. Two young soldiers broke through the lines and filled a pitcher from the spring; David, though parched, reasoned the draught was reputed equal to blood, and poured it out to God. The author turns to history, when the Jews were at peace and Seleucus Nicanor funded their worship.
2ES 1:1-20	The book opens with Ezra's descent traced back through the priestly line to Aaron, placing him among the captives in Media in the reign of Artaxerxes. The word of the Lord comes to him: go and show the people their sins and their children's wickedness, because they have forgotten God and sacrificed to strange gods. The charge is a list of benefits repaid with murmuring, the way through the sea, Moses and Aaron, the pillar of fire, the quails, manna, and water from the rock.
2ES 1:21-40	The indictment continues. God recalls the bitter water made sweet and the land taken from the Canaanites, then declares that since the people have forsaken him he will forsake them, refusing their feasts, new moons and offerings, and requiring the blood of the prophets they killed. Their house is left desolate and their inheritance given to a people who never saw a sign yet believe. That coming people from the east is promised Abraham, Isaac and Jacob as leaders, along with the twelve minor prophets.
2ES 2:1-25	God speaks of the people's mother, a widow telling her children to go and beg mercy, and pronounces woe on Assur, recalling Sodom and Gomorrah. Ezra is told that the kingdom prepared for Israel will be given to others, with everlasting tabernacles and the tree of life. The mother is told not to fear: Isaiah and Jeremiah are sent to her, twelve fruit trees, springs of milk and honey and seven mountains of roses and lilies are prepared, and the dead will be raised.
2ES 2:26-48	The promises to the mother continue: her children will be sheltered in the day of trouble and brought back from the secret places of the earth. Ezra says he was charged at Horeb to go to Israel and was refused, so he turns to the nations and tells them to look for their shepherd. He then sees a great multitude on Mount Zion praising God, crowned by a young man taller than the rest. The angel says these confessed God's name, and the young man is the Son of God.
2ES 3:1-36	Thirty years after the fall of the city, Ezra lies troubled in Babylon, seeing Zion desolate while Babylon prospers. He rehearses the history back to Adam, who was given one commandment and broke it, leaving a wicked heart in all his descendants, then the flood and Noah, Abraham and the covenant, Sinai, and David's city, which was handed to its enemies when its people did as Adam did. He asks whether Babylon's deeds are any better, and demands that the two be weighed in a balance.
2ES 4:1-25	The angel Uriel answers Ezra by setting him three tasks: weigh fire, measure wind, or call back yesterday. Ezra admits he cannot, and Uriel says a man who cannot explain the things he lives among cannot expect to grasp the way of the Most High. He tells a parable in which the woods plan war on the sea and the waves plan to take the plain, and both plans fail. Ezra asks why he was given understanding at all, since Israel is a reproach and men pass away like grasshoppers.
2ES 4:26-52	Uriel says the world is hurrying to its end, and that a grain of evil seed sown in Adam's heart is still producing its harvest, which cannot be gathered before the threshing time. Told that the souls of the righteous ask the same question in their chambers, Ezra hears the archangel Jeremiel's answer, that the number must first be complete. A womb cannot delay a birth past its term. Two visions, an oven leaving smoke and a storm leaving drops, show that more time has passed than remains.
2ES 5:1-31	Uriel lists the signs: truth hidden, the land barren of faith, the sun shining at night and the moon by day, blood dripping from wood, stones speaking, the Dead Sea casting out fish, women bearing monsters, salt water found in fresh, and friends destroying one another. Ezra wakes trembling. Phaltiel the captain comes asking where he has been, and Ezra sends him away for seven days. Fasting, he complains that God chose one vine, one lily, one dove, one people, and then handed them to many.
2ES 5:32-56	Uriel asks whether Ezra loves Israel more than their maker does. Ezra says he speaks only from grief and wishes he had never been born to see it. The angel gives him more impossible tasks: count those not yet born, gather scattered drops, show the image of a voice. Judgment is likened to a ring, with no first and no last. A woman bears ten children in their turn, not at once, and creation is now aging, which is why each generation is smaller than the last.
2ES 6:1-30	The answer begins before everything: before the winds, the thunder, paradise, the earthquake and the host of angels, God alone made all of it and will alone end it. The turn of the ages is set between Jacob's hand and Esau's heel. A voice like many waters announces the signs, books opened before the firmament, infants speaking, children born early who live and dance, sown fields found unsown, storehouses empty, a trumpet, and springs standing still for three hours. The ground rocks beneath Ezra.
2ES 6:31-59	Told to fast another seven days and take comfort, Ezra completes three weeks and then speaks again. He recites the six days of creation in order: light out of darkness and silence, the firmament dividing the waters, the sea gathered into a seventh part while six parts dried, fruit and flowers, sun and moon and stars, then fish and birds, and Behemoth and Leviathan divided between the dry land and the deep. Since the world was made for Israel, he asks why other nations rule and devour them.
2ES 7:1-37	The angel gives two pictures: a vast sea reached only through a narrow inlet, and a rich city whose single approach runs between fire and water, wide enough for one man. Israel's inheritance is like that, narrow first and broad after, because of Adam. Ezra objects and is told he is no judge over God. The end is then described: the hidden city appears, God's son rejoices the survivors four hundred years, then all die, the world falls silent seven days, and the dead rise for judgment.
2ES 7:38-73	The day of judgment has no sun, moon, stars or weather, only the splendour of the Most High, and lasts about a week of years. Ezra protests that almost everyone has sinned, so the world to come will delight few and torment many. The angel answers that rare things are the precious ones, as gold is rarer than clay, and that he will rejoice over the few and not grieve for the rest. Ezra wishes the mind had never been made from dust, since beasts expect no judgment.
2ES 7:74-102	Ezra asks whether souls rest after death or are tormented at once. The angel describes seven ways in which the spirits of the scornful grieve, ending with shame before the glory they sinned against, and seven orders of rest for the just, ending with their eagerness to see the face of the one they served, their faces shining like the sun. Souls are given seven days to see all this before being gathered into their dwellings. Ezra then asks whether the righteous may intercede for the wicked at the judgment.
2ES 7:103-140	The angel says no one may pray for another on that day, since each bears his own righteousness or unrighteousness. Ezra objects with a list of intercessors, Abraham for Sodom, Moses, Joshua, Samuel, David, Solomon, Elijah and Hezekiah, and is told that intercession belongs to this age, not to the day that ends it. He answers that it would have been better if Adam had never been made. The angel calls this the condition of the battle, and Ezra closes by naming God merciful, longsuffering, bountiful and forgiving.
2ES 8:1-31	The angel says this world was made for many but the world to come for few, as the earth yields much clay and little of the dust gold comes from. Ezra answers with the labour God spends on one person: the body formed in the womb, carried nine months, fed milk, brought up under the law. If that is destroyed so quickly, he asks, why make it? He then prays for Israel, asking God to look at those who kept the covenant rather than at the rest.
2ES 8:32-63	Ezra argues that mercy is only mercy when shown to those with no good works to their name, since the righteous already have their reward. The angel grants part of this and answers with a sower: not everything planted comes up. Ezra replies that seed fails for want of rain, and that man is God's own image. He is told he cannot love the creation more than its maker, and is praised for humbling himself. For him paradise is opened, the city built, the tree of life planted.
2ES 9:1-24	The angel tells Ezra to measure the signs himself: earthquakes, peoples in tumult, nations plotting, rulers wavering. He is told to stop asking how the ungodly will be punished and ask instead how the righteous will be saved. Ezra insists more perish than are saved, as a wave is greater than a drop, and hears God kept one grape from a cluster and one plant from a whole forest. He is sent to a field to eat only flowers for seven days.
2ES 9:25-47	Ezra goes to the field called Ardat, lives on the flowers of the field for seven days, and then prays. God sowed the law in Israel; the law cannot perish, but those who received it did, and unlike a vessel that outlasts what it holds, the receivers are destroyed. Looking up, he sees a woman on his right, weeping with torn clothes and ashes on her head. She tells him she was barren thirty years, was given a son, raised him, and held a wedding feast for him.
2ES 10:1-27	The woman tells Ezra her son entered his wedding chamber and fell down dead, and that she has left the city to mourn in the field until she dies. Ezra answers angrily that Zion, mother of them all, has lost far more, and the earth mourns more children than she does. He lists the ruin of the sanctuary, the altar and the temple. As he speaks, her face blazes like lightning, she gives a great cry that shakes the earth, and where she stood a city stands instead.
2ES 10:28-59	Terrified, Ezra calls for Uriel, who finds him lying as if dead and sets him on his feet. The angel explains the vision: the woman is Zion. Her thirty barren years are the three thousand years before an offering was made in her; the son born to her is the city Solomon built, and his death on his wedding day is the destruction of Jerusalem. Ezra was sent to an empty field because no building of man could stand where God's city is shown.
2ES 11:1-24	On the second night Ezra dreams of an eagle rising from the sea with twelve feathered wings and three heads, spreading her wings over the whole earth so that nothing living speaks against her. A voice from the middle of her body orders the wings to reign one at a time and the heads to be kept for last. The wings rule in turn and vanish, one after another, some ruling briefly and some never ruling at all, until only the three resting heads and six little wings remain.
2ES 11:25-46	Two small wings try to reign and disappear quickly. As the last two plan the same, the largest head wakes, joins the other two, devours the two wings, and rules the whole earth with great oppression before vanishing itself. The right head then devours the left. A lion comes roaring out of the wood and speaks with a human voice, telling the eagle it is the fourth beast, that it judged the earth falsely and oppressed the meek, and that its times are ended and it must disappear.
2ES 12:1-27	The last head disappears, the two wings that joined it reign briefly over a small troubled kingdom, and the whole eagle burns. Ezra wakes exhausted and asks for the meaning. He is told the eagle is the fourth kingdom Daniel saw, though it was never explained to Daniel as it is now to him: the twelve wings are twelve kings, the eight little wings are eight short reigns, and the three heads are three kingdoms that will oppress the earth more than any before them.
2ES 12:28-51	The interpretation finishes: two heads fall by the sword, and the two remaining wings are the small troubled kingdom kept until the end. The roaring lion is the anointed one from David's line, who will confront the last kingdom with its own wickedness, destroy it, and deliver the rest in mercy. Ezra is told to write the vision in a book, hide it, and teach it only to the wise. When the people come out begging him not to abandon them, he sends them home.
2ES 13:1-27	In a third dream a wind stirs the sea and something like a man rises and flies with the clouds, and everything trembles at his look. An innumerable multitude gathers from the four winds to fight him. He carves out a great mountain, flies onto it, carries no weapon, and destroys the whole army with fire, flaming breath and sparks from his mouth, leaving only ash. He then calls a second, peaceful multitude to himself, and Ezra wakes and asks what it means.
2ES 13:28-58	The angel explains: nations will turn on one another, and then the Son will be revealed, standing on Mount Zion, which appears prepared and built like the mountain cut without hands. He destroys the assembled nations by the law, which is likened to fire. The peaceful multitude is the ten tribes taken by Shalmaneser, who crossed the stopped waters of the Euphrates into a far country called Arzareth to keep their law, and are now returning by the same means. Ezra is told to wait three days more.
2ES 14:1-23	On the third day a voice calls Ezra from a bush, as God once spoke to Moses, telling him what to publish and what to hide. Ezra is told to keep what he has seen, since he will be taken from among men to remain with the Son. The world has aged: ten and a half of twelve parts gone. He is to set his house in order and instruct the wise. He answers that the law was burned, and asks for the Spirit to write it again.
2ES 14:24-48	Ezra is told to prepare tablets and take five swift scribes, Sarea, Dabria, Selemia, Ethanus and Asiel. He gathers the people, recounts the exodus and their failure to keep the law, and tells them not to seek him for forty days. In the field a voice tells him to drink from a cup like water but the colour of fire. His understanding opens, and for forty days he dictates while the five write in unknown characters. Ninety-four books result: twenty-four are published, seventy kept for the wise.
2ES 15:1-32	Ezra is told to write down words of prophecy and not to fear those who disbelieve. God announces sword, famine, death and destruction on the whole earth, saying he will keep silence no longer while innocent blood cries out. Egypt is threatened with plagues as before, crops fail under blasting, hail and a terrible star, and men turn on their neighbours for bread. The kings of the earth are summoned against each other. A horror appears from the east: the dragons of Arabia with chariots, and the raging Carmonians.
2ES 15:33-63	The oracle continues with clouds full of wrath crashing together from every direction, pouring out a destroying star, fire, hail, flying swords and floods that level cities, mountains and forests. The armies press on to Babylon and destroy her, and her smoke goes up to heaven. Asia is then told that she copied Babylon and dressed her daughters for the same trade, and is promised widowhood, famine, sword and pestilence: her children starving, her cities broken, her people in the mountains reduced to eating their own flesh.
2ES 16:1-39	Woe is pronounced on Babylon, Asia, Egypt and Syria, told to put on sackcloth and mourn their children. A series of questions asks who can turn back a sword, quench a fire, or recall a shot arrow, and answers no one can. Famine, plague and war follow, and the dead are cast out unburied with no one left to till fields or gather grapes. Of a city ten survive, of a field two, like the few olives left on a tree. The end comes as suddenly as childbirth.
2ES 16:40-78	The people are told to live as pilgrims: sell as though fleeing, buy as though about to lose, build a house never to live in, sow a field never to reap, since strangers will take the harvest. The more a nation adorns itself, the more God hates its sin. He knows every thought, having made the stars and hung the earth upon the waters, so hidden sins will be produced as accusers. The elect are told they will be tried like gold in fire and delivered.
4MA 4:1-26	Simon, thwarted in his feud with the high priest Onias, tells the Syrian governor Apollonius that Jerusalem's treasury holds private money belonging to King Seleucus. Apollonius comes to seize it; the priests and the women and children pray, armoured angels on horseback appear, and he is left half dead until Onias prays for him. Seleucus then dies, Antiochus Epiphanes succeeds, sells the high priesthood to Jason, outlaws the ancestral law, and compels Jews by torture to eat forbidden meat.
4MA 5:1-38	Antiochus sits in state and orders every Hebrew compelled to eat swine's flesh or be tortured. The aged priest Eleazar is brought forward, and the king urges him to abandon what he calls superstition, arguing that nature made the animal good to eat and that any god would pardon a transgression made under compulsion. Eleazar answers that a small breach slights the law exactly as a great one does, and that his law teaches temperance, courage, justice and piety. He tells the king to prepare the wheels.
4MA 6:1-35	Eleazar is stripped, bound and scourged, and does not give way while a herald calls on him to obey the king. Some of the attendants, pitying his age, offer to bring meat he can pretend is swine's flesh, and he refuses that too, saying he will not leave the young a pattern of pretense. He is burned, and dying he asks God to accept his blood as a purification for the people. The author concludes that devout reasoning is master of the passions.
4MA 7:1-25	The author praises Eleazar at length, comparing his reasoning to a pilot who holds the rudder of piety through the breakers of torture until the ship reaches the harbour of victory, and to a besieged city that never falls. He is likened to his ancestor Aaron facing the fire with a censer. To the objection that not everyone masters the passions, the answer is that those who meditate wholly on religion can, and the chapter turns toward the young men who endured worse.
4MA 8:1-27	Antiochus orders more Hebrews brought, and seven brothers arrive with their aged mother. Impressed by their bearing, he offers them friendship, places of authority and the Greek way of life if they will abandon their national ordinance, and warns of torture if they refuse. The instruments are brought out: wheels, racks, hooks, cauldrons and iron hands. The author sets out the cowardly speech the brothers might have made, then reports that they said none of it and answered with one voice.
4MA 9:1-32	The brothers tell Antiochus they are readier to die than break their fathers' law, and that Eleazar's death should have taught him something. Enraged, he has the eldest scourged and broken on the wheel; the young man refuses the offer of release for eating, urges his brothers not to desert their station, and dies. The second is bound to the catapult with iron hands and torn, and tells the tyrant that his own defeat is the worse torment. He dies as well.
4MA 10:1-21	The third brother is urged to save himself and answers that he shares the same father, mother and teaching as those already dead, and that they can reach his body but not his soul. He is dismembered on the rack and dies declaring the tyrant's punishment. The fourth refuses in the same terms, so Antiochus orders his tongue cut out. He answers that God hears the silent, holds out his tongue, and says that cutting it off will not remove their reasoning.
4MA 11:1-27	The fifth brother steps forward on his own, asks what they have done besides worship the Maker of all things, and is broken on the catapult, saying the tyrant has done them a favour by giving them nobler sufferings. The sixth, still a boy, says that though youngest in years he is as old in understanding, and is racked and burned. Dying, he tells Antiochus that six young men have destroyed his tyranny, since his fire is cold to them and his violence harmless.
4MA 12:1-20	The seventh and youngest is brought forward in chains. Antiochus pities him, promises friendship and a charge over the kingdom if he obeys, and sends for his mother in the hope she will persuade him. After his mother speaks to him in Hebrew, the boy asks to be released so that he may address the king, and the guards gladly free him. He runs to the pans, denounces Antiochus for killing God's servants, prays for mercy on his people, and throws himself in.
4MA 13:1-27	With the seven dead, the author returns to his thesis: they were not conquered, so reasoning is master of the passions. Their shared resolve is compared to a ring of seven towers breaking the waves before a harbour. He records how they encouraged each other, invoking the three young men in the furnace and Isaac's willingness to be sacrificed. He then explains their bond by their shared womb, shared nursing and shared education in the law, which made watching each other die harder still.
4MA 14:1-20	The author praises the seven for urging one another on, saying they overcame even brotherly affection, and compares their number to the seven days of creation. He then turns to their mother, who watched every one of them tortured. To show what that cost, he surveys the love animals have for their young: birds defending fledglings on roofs and cliffs, circling and calling when they cannot fight, and bees stinging anyone who comes near the hive. The mother, he says, had the spirit of Abraham.
4MA 15:1-32	Offered her sons' lives on the tyrant's terms, the mother chose religion instead. The author dwells on how strong a mother's love is, made stronger here by seven childbirths and by sons who were just, brave and obedient to her, then describes plainly what she watched: her children burned and dismembered one after another, and she did not weep or turn them back. He pictures her soul as a council chamber holding two votes, and compares her to Noah's ark riding out a flood.
4MA 16:1-25	Since even an aged woman endured this, the author says, devout reasoning masters the passions. He writes out the lament she might have uttered, seven childbirths come to nothing, no grandchildren, no son to bury her, and reports she said none of it. Instead, standing with her sons while Eleazar was tortured, she spoke to them in Hebrew: the contest is noble, an old man is bearing it, remember Abraham and Isaac, Daniel among the lions, and the three men in the furnace.
4MA 17:1-24	Some of the guards said that when the mother was about to be seized she threw herself on the pyre rather than be touched. The author calls her a house built on the pillar of her children and sets her among the stars, then composes an epitaph for the tomb of the aged priest, the aged woman and her seven sons. He casts the whole thing as a contest watched by the world, and notes that Antiochus held up their endurance as an example to his own soldiers.
4MA 18:1-24	The author closes by urging Israel to keep the law, since devout reasoning rules the passions. The nation regained peace, and Antiochus, unable to force foreign customs on it, left Jerusalem to make war on the Persians. The mother's own words to her sons are given: she was a chaste wife, and their father taught them Cain and Abel, the binding of Isaac, Joseph, Phinehas, Daniel, the furnace, and lines from Isaiah, David, Solomon, Ezekiel and Moses. The book ends with a doxology.
